WordPress Plugin Development 20 chapters · 139 topics · 133 code samples · works offline

Welcome · WordPress Plugin Development

Scope and supported environmentThe plugin contractActivation and load sequenceHow WordPress loads a plugin fileAudience and prerequisites

This guide documents the production-grade plugin contract for WordPress 6.7 running on PHP 8.3. It covers the loader mechanics that decide when your code executes, the hook system every extension point is built on, and the subsystem APIs you must use correctly for shortcodes, blocks, settings, custom tables, queries, REST routes, background work, security and distribution.

Scope and supported environment

Every version claim in this document is pinned to a specific WordPress and PHP pair. Plugin code is executed inside a shared process with the site owner, other plugins and the theme, so the version floor is a hard compatibility contract rather than a preference.

The reference environment for this guide is WordPress 6.7 with PHP 8.3, MySQL 5.7 or later, or MariaDB 10.6 or later. WordPress 6.7 itself declares a minimum of PHP 7.2.24, but a plugin that only supports 7.2 forgoes typed properties, constructor promotion, enums, readonly classes and null-safe narrowing, and must branch its code paths accordingly.

ComponentPinned versionNotes
WordPress6.7Introduces the unified 6.7 block theme template resolution and the command palette for plugin commands.
PHP8.3Typed class constants and the #[\Override] attribute are available; dynamic property creation is deprecated.
MySQL5.7+Generated columns and JSON indexing are available; utf8mb4 is required for correct emoji storage.
MariaDB10.6+Use the MySQL driver path; do not rely on MySQL-only syntax without a guard.

What a plugin may do, and the two questions that bound the answer

A WordPress plugin can do almost anything PHP can do, and the platform's design assumes it will: it runs on every request, in the same process, with full access to the database and the filesystem, before any theme code has run. The first bounding question is when your code runs. A plugin is loaded early — before most of the platform is available — so a plugin that assumes a function exists at file scope can break on a request where that function has not been defined yet, which is a different failure from the one you would get calling it inside a hook. The discipline that follows is that the plugin file declares and nothing else: constants, the header block, the requires, and a call that registers hooks. Every function that touches the platform runs inside a callback. The second bounding question is what your code must not assume. It must not assume a particular WordPress version is running, because a plugin directory is a fact about your code and the WordPress version is a fact about the installation, and they are independent — the same plugin runs on 6.4 and 6.8, and a site can be upgraded without the plugin being touched. It must not assume a particular theme, because the theme is chosen by whoever built the site and a plugin that depends on a theme's functions will break when the theme changes, which is the single most common reason a plugin "works on my site". And it must not assume a particular set of plugins, because you do not control them. Then the three things that are conventions rather than rules but that a reviewer will check, and each is a requirement in practice. Never touch the database directly for a table you did not create — read through the platform's APIs where they exist, because they carry the caching, the format handling and the multisite awareness that a direct query does not. Never output anything without escaping, on the way out as well as on the way in; WordPress is not an input-validation framework and the escaping functions are opt-in, which means an unescaped value is escaped by nobody. And never assume a request is a browser request, because the same code runs for a cron job, a REST request, an XML-RPC call and a command-line invocation, and code that assumes a screen will break on all of them. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Declare your minimum WordPress and PHP versions in the header and enforce them, so that the plugin refuses to load on an unsupported installation rather than failing obscurely on the first function it calls that does not exist. And test on a bare installation and on a populated one, because the bare installation finds the missing-declaration failures and the populated one finds the collisions, and a plugin tested on only one of them will pass your machine and fail in the wild.

/* The shape of the permission. */
const PERMISSION = {
  fact: 'A WORDPRESS PLUGIN CAN DO ALMOST ANYTHING PHP CAN DO',
  and: 'the PLATFORMS DESIGN ASSUMES IT WILL',
  detail: ['it RUNS ON EVERY REQUEST', 'IN the SAME PROCESS', 'with FULL ACCESS TO the DATABASE and the FILESYSTEM',
    'BEFORE ANY THEME CODE HAS RUN'],
};

/* The two bounding questions. */
const QUESTION_1 = {
  q: 'WHEN DOES YOUR CODE RUN',
  fact: 'A PLUGIN IS LOADED EARLY - BEFORE MOST of the PLATFORM IS AVAILABLE',
  symptom: 'SO A PLUGIN THAT ASSUMES A FUNCTION EXISTS AT FILE SCOPE CAN BREAK ON A REQUEST WHERE THAT FUNCTION HAS NOT BEEN DEFINED YET',
  and: 'WHICH IS A DIFFERENT FAILURE from the ONE YOU WOULD GET CALLING IT INSIDE A HOOK',
  discipline: 'THE PLUGIN FILE DECLARES and NOTHING ELSE - CONSTANTS, the HEADER BLOCK, the REQUIRES, and A CALL THAT REGISTERS HOOKS',
  rule: 'EVERY FUNCTION THAT TOUCHES the PLATFORM RUNS INSIDE A CALLBACK',
};
const QUESTION_2 = {
  q: 'WHAT MUST YOUR CODE NOT ASSUME',
  mustNot: [
    { a: 'A PARTICULAR WORDPRESS VERSION IS RUNNING', why: 'BECAUSE A PLUGIN DIRECTORY IS A FACT about YOUR CODE and the WORDPRESS VERSION IS A FACT about the INSTALLATION, and THEY ARE INDEPENDENT - the SAME PLUGIN RUNS ON SIX-POINT-FOUR and SIX-POINT-EIGHT, and A SITE CAN BE UPGRADED WITHOUT the PLUGIN BEING TOUCHED' },
    { a: 'A PARTICULAR THEME', why: 'BECAUSE the THEME IS CHOSEN BY WHOEVER BUILT the SITE',
      symptom: 'and A PLUGIN THAT DEPENDS ON A THEMES FUNCTIONS WILL BREAK WHEN the THEME CHANGES',
      rank: 'WHICH IS the SINGLE MOST COMMON REASON A PLUGIN works on my site' },
    { a: 'A PARTICULAR SET of PLUGINS', why: 'BECAUSE YOU DO NOT CONTROL THEM' },
  ],
};

/* Three conventions a reviewer checks. */
const CONVENTIONS = [
  { c: 'NEVER TOUCH the DATABASE DIRECTLY for A TABLE YOU DID NOT CREATE',
    do: 'READ THROUGH the PLATFORMS APIS WHERE THEY EXIST',
    why: 'BECAUSE THEY CARRY the CACHING, the FORMAT HANDLING and the MULTISITE AWARENESS that A DIRECT QUERY DOES NOT' },
  { c: 'NEVER OUTPUT ANYTHING WITHOUT ESCAPING, ON the WAY OUT as WELL as ON the WAY IN',
    fact: 'WORDPRESS IS NOT AN INPUT-VALIDATION FRAMEWORK and the ESCAPING FUNCTIONS ARE OPT-IN',
    consequence: 'WHICH MEANS AN UNESCAPED VALUE IS ESCAPED BY NOBODY' },
  { c: 'NEVER ASSUME A REQUEST IS A BROWSER REQUEST',
    instances: ['A CRON JOB', 'A REST REQUEST', 'AN XML-RPC CALL', 'A COMMAND-LINE INVOCATION'],
    symptom: 'and CODE THAT ASSUMES A SCREEN WILL BREAK ON ALL of THEM' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'DECLARE YOUR MINIMUM WORDPRESS and PHP VERSIONS IN the HEADER and ENFORCE THEM',
    gain: 'SO THAT the PLUGIN REFUSES TO LOAD ON AN UNSUPPORTED INSTALLATION rather than FAILING OBSCURELY ON the FIRST FUNCTION IT CALLS THAT DOES NOT EXIST' },
  { p: 'TEST ON A BARE INSTALLATION and ON A POPULATED ONE',
    why: ['BECAUSE the BARE INSTALLATION FINDS the MISSING-DECLARATION FAILURES',
      'and the POPULATED ONE FINDS the COLLISIONS'],
    consequence: 'and A PLUGIN TESTED ON ONLY ONE of THEM WILL PASS YOUR MACHINE and FAIL IN the WILD' },
];

The header block and the runtime guard

The header comment block is the only part of a plugin file the platform parses rather than executes, and it is where the bounds described above are declared rather than assumed. It carries the plugin name, the version, the author, the text domain, and the minimum WordPress and PHP versions — and those last two are the ones that make the contract checkable. A minimum WordPress version declared in the header is a claim the plugin screen honours by refusing activation on an older installation, which converts a vague compatibility statement into an enforced one. A minimum PHP version is checked before the plugin is included at all, so a plugin that requires a language feature the running PHP lacks produces a clear message rather than a parse error two hundred lines in. Then the four fields that are load-bearing, and each has a job beyond documentation. The version is what your own upgrade routine compares against — a plugin whose version does not change cannot have an upgrade routine, because there is nothing to compare. The text domain must equal the plugin slug exactly and is what every translatable string is looked up by, so a mismatch produces strings that are silently not translated. The required PHP and WordPress versions, as above. And the network-only flag, which tells the plugin screen to prevent activation on a single-site installation where the plugin cannot work — a small thing that prevents a support ticket. Then the runtime guard, which is the second half of the same mechanism and the part the header cannot do. Check the versions again in your own code, because a plugin can arrive by copying files into the plugins directory, by a deployment script, or by an update that bypasses the plugin screen, and in all of those cases the header was never examined. The guard's job is to print an admin notice and to decline to register anything — never to fatal, because a fatal is a white screen for an administrator who was told the plugin installed cleanly. And the guard belongs on an admin-only hook, because a notice on the front end is a notice every visitor sees and a version check on every request is a version check you pay for on every request. Then the two things worth naming, and both are the difference between a declaration and a check. Declaring a dependency in the header does not check it at run time. The tag prevents activation in the plugin screen; it does not stop your code from loading if the dependency was deactivated later, so the runtime check is still required and its failure message should name the missing plugin. And declaring a minimum version does not protect you from a version newer than you have tested, because a deprecation can become a removal; the discipline is to test against the current release and to avoid functions marked for deprecation, since a plugin that works today can break on a WordPress update without you touching anything.

/* The header block: the only part of the file the platform PARSES
   rather than EXECUTES. Everything else in the file is code. */

const HEADER_FIELDS = [
  { f: 'Plugin Name',        job: 'THE NAME SHOWN IN the PLUGIN LIST' },
  { f: 'Version',            job: 'NOT DOCUMENTATION - IT IS WHAT YOUR UPGRADE ROUTINE COMPARES AGAINST',
    consequence: 'A PLUGIN WHOSE VERSION DOES NOT CHANGE CANNOT HAVE AN UPGRADE ROUTINE, BECAUSE THERE IS NOTHING TO COMPARE' },
  { f: 'Text Domain',        mustEqual: 'the PLUGIN SLUG, EXACTLY',
    job: 'WHAT EVERY TRANSLATABLE STRING IS LOOKED UP BY',
    symptom: 'A MISMATCH PRODUCES STRINGS THAT ARE SILENTLY NOT TRANSLATED' },
  { f: 'Requires at least',  job: 'MINIMUM WORDPRESS VERSION',
    enforced: 'the PLUGIN SCREEN REFUSES ACTIVATION ON AN OLDER INSTALLATION',
    value: 'WHICH CONVERTS A VAGUE COMPATIBILITY STATEMENT INTO AN ENFORCED ONE' },
  { f: 'Requires PHP',       job: 'MINIMUM PHP VERSION',
    enforced: 'CHECKED BEFORE the PLUGIN IS INCLUDED AT ALL',
    gain: 'SO A PLUGIN THAT REQUIRES A LANGUAGE FEATURE the RUNNING PHP LACKS PRODUCES A CLEAR MESSAGE rather than A PARSE ERROR TWO HUNDRED LINES IN' },
  { f: 'Network',            value: 'true or absent',
    job: 'PREVENTS ACTIVATION ON A SINGLE-SITE INSTALLATION WHERE the PLUGIN CANNOT WORK',
    note: 'A SMALL THING THAT PREVENTS A SUPPORT TICKET' },
];

/* The runtime guard: the half the header cannot do. A plugin can arrive
   by copying files in, by a deploy script, or by an update that
   bypasses the plugin screen. In all of those the header was never read. */

const GUARD = {
  does: 'CHECK the VERSIONS AGAIN IN YOUR OWN CODE',
  on: 'an ADMIN-ONLY HOOK',
  action: 'PRINT AN ADMIN NOTICE and DECLINE TO REGISTER ANYTHING',
  never: 'NEVER FATAL',
  why: 'BECAUSE A FATAL IS A WHITE SCREEN for an ADMINISTRATOR WHO WAS TOLD the PLUGIN INSTALLED CLEANLY',
  hookChoice: [
    'A NOTICE ON the FRONT END IS A NOTICE EVERY VISITOR SEES',
    'and A VERSION CHECK ON EVERY REQUEST IS A VERSION CHECK YOU PAY FOR ON EVERY REQUEST',
  ],
};

/* Declaration is not a check. */
const NOT_A_CHECK = [
  { d: 'A DEPENDENCY DECLARED IN the HEADER',
    enforcedWhere: 'the PLUGIN SCREEN, ON ACTIVATION',
    notEnforced: 'IT DOES NOT STOP YOUR CODE FROM LOADING IF the DEPENDENCY WAS DEACTIVATED LATER',
    therefore: 'SO the RUNTIME CHECK IS STILL REQUIRED',
    message: 'and ITS FAILURE MESSAGE SHOULD NAME the MISSING PLUGIN' },
  { d: 'A MINIMUM VERSION DECLARED IN the HEADER',
    notEnforced: 'IT DOES NOT PROTECT YOU FROM A VERSION NEWER THAN YOU HAVE TESTED',
    why: 'BECAUSE A DEPRECATION CAN BECOME A REMOVAL',
    discipline: ['TEST AGAINST the CURRENT RELEASE', 'AVOID FUNCTIONS MARKED for DEPRECATION'],
    symptom: 'and A PLUGIN THAT WORKS TODAY CAN BREAK ON A WORDPRESS UPDATE WITHOUT YOU TOUCHING ANYTHING' },
];

Deprecations you must respect. PHP 8.2 deprecates dynamic properties. Assigning an undeclared property on a class not marked #[\AllowDynamicProperties] emits a deprecation notice. Declare every property on your classes, or extend stdClass deliberately when you are building a plain data carrier.

The plugin contract

A plugin is a directory in wp-content/plugins containing at least one PHP file with a recognised header. Everything else — whether it is a single file or a full application architecture — is a choice you make about maintainability, not about whether WordPress will load it.

WordPress does not load arbitrary PHP. It scans the plugins directory, reads each top-level PHP file, parses the header comment block, and collects the file paths of the plugins it recognises. Activation is therefore a metadata operation on that registry, not a file system operation.

Three obligations define a well-behaved plugin. First, namespacing: all global symbols you declare must be uniquely prefixed, because a single redeclaration is a fatal error that takes down the entire site, not just your plugin. Second, idempotence: your code must be safe to run when WordPress re-registers hooks on repeated requests, and safe if the plugin is loaded twice by different mechanisms. Third, data ownership: anything you persist must be removable on uninstall, and must respect the privacy exporter and eraser.

The three obligations, and why each is a hard requirement

The plugin contract is three promises, and each of them exists because the alternative is a failure that takes down more than one plugin. The first is namespacing: every global symbol you declare must be uniquely prefixed. This is not a style rule — a redeclaration of a global function is a fatal error, and a fatal error during a request is a white screen that takes down every plugin on the site, not only yours. Two plugins that both declare a function called ledger_get_total will both install perfectly and then break the first time both are loaded, which is a report that arrives as "the site went down after I installed something unrelated" and is miserable to diagnose because the error names neither plugin. The second is that you must not run code at file scope. Everything a plugin file does besides declaring things is a side effect, and side effects at load time happen on every request, in an order you do not control, before the platform is ready. The third is that you must not output anything at file scope either, which is the same rule for output and has a worse failure because it corrupts a response that other code is also contributing to. Then the three things the contract requires that are not about symbols, and each is a mechanism. Do not assume WordPress is present. A plugin file that is included directly — by a test, by a static analyser, by another plugin's file — will execute, and any call to a platform function will fail. Do not assume the request is for a browser. A cron run, a REST call and a web request all load your plugin, and a plugin that assumes $_GET is set or that echoes something is broken on the other two. And do not exit early from a hook callback unless you mean to terminate the request, because a bare exit in a callback skips the remaining callbacks and leaves the platform half-way through what it was doing — which produces a truncated response and a database in an inconsistent state. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Prefix everything and namespace your classes, — a namespace for classes and functions, a prefix for constants and globals, and a text domain for translatable strings, all derived from the plugin slug so they are unique on wordpress.org. And keep the plugin file to the header and the bootstrap, so that the file's contents are reviewable at a glance and a reader can see what happens when the plugin is loaded without reading anything else.

/* The three promises. */
const CONTRACT = 'the PLUGIN CONTRACT IS THREE PROMISES, and EACH of THEM EXISTS BECAUSE the ALTERNATIVE IS A FAILURE THAT TAKES DOWN MORE THAN ONE PLUGIN';

const P1 = {
  c: 'NAMESPACING - EVERY GLOBAL SYMBOL YOU DECLARE MUST BE UNIQUELY PREFIXED',
  notAStyleRule: 'THIS IS NOT A STYLE RULE',
  why: 'A REDECLARATION of A GLOBAL FUNCTION IS A FATAL ERROR, and A FATAL ERROR DURING A REQUEST IS A WHITE SCREEN THAT TAKES DOWN EVERY PLUGIN ON the SITE, NOT ONLY YOURS',
  example: ['TWO PLUGINS THAT BOTH DECLARE A FUNCTION CALLED ledger_get_total WILL BOTH INSTALL PERFECTLY',
    'and THEN BREAK the FIRST TIME BOTH ARE LOADED'],
  symptom: 'WHICH IS A REPORT THAT ARRIVES AS the site went down after I installed something unrelated',
  miserable: 'and IS MISERABLE TO DIAGNOSE BECAUSE the ERROR NAMES NEITHER PLUGIN',
};
const P2 = {
  c: 'YOU MUST NOT RUN CODE AT FILE SCOPE',
  why: 'EVERYTHING A PLUGIN FILE DOES BESIDES DECLARING THINGS IS A SIDE EFFECT',
  detail: ['and SIDE EFFECTS AT LOAD TIME HAPPEN ON EVERY REQUEST',
    'IN AN ORDER YOU DO NOT CONTROL', 'BEFORE the PLATFORM IS READY'],
};
const P3 = {
  c: 'YOU MUST NOT OUTPUT ANYTHING AT FILE SCOPE EITHER',
  same: 'WHICH IS the SAME RULE for OUTPUT',
  worse: 'and HAS A WORSE FAILURE BECAUSE IT CORRUPTS A RESPONSE THAT OTHER CODE IS ALSO CONTRIBUTING TO',
};

/* Three non-symbol requirements. */
const ALSO = [
  { r: 'DO NOT ASSUME WORDPRESS IS PRESENT',
    detail: 'A PLUGIN FILE THAT IS INCLUDED DIRECTLY - BY A TEST, BY A STATIC ANALYSER, BY ANOTHER PLUGINS FILE - WILL EXECUTE',
    symptom: 'and ANY CALL TO A PLATFORM FUNCTION WILL FAIL' },
  { r: 'DO NOT ASSUME the REQUEST IS FOR A BROWSER',
    detail: 'A CRON RUN, A REST CALL and A WEB REQUEST ALL LOAD your PLUGIN',
    symptom: 'and A PLUGIN THAT ASSUMES A QUERY STRING IS SET or THAT ECHOES SOMETHING IS BROKEN ON the OTHER TWO' },
  { r: 'DO NOT EXIT EARLY FROM A HOOK CALLBACK UNLESS YOU MEAN TO TERMINATE the REQUEST',
    detail: 'BECAUSE A BARE exit IN A CALLBACK SKIPS the REMAINING CALLBACKS and LEAVES the PLATFORM HALF-WAY THROUGH WHAT IT WAS DOING',
    consequence: 'WHICH PRODUCES A TRUNCATED RESPONSE and A DATABASE IN AN INCONSISTENT STATE' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'PREFIX EVERYTHING and NAMESPACE YOUR CLASSES',
    detail: ['A NAMESPACE for CLASSES and FUNCTIONS', 'A PREFIX for CONSTANTS and GLOBALS',
      'and A TEXT DOMAIN for TRANSLATABLE STRINGS'],
    all: 'ALL DERIVED FROM the PLUGIN SLUG so THEY ARE UNIQUE ON the PLUGIN DIRECTORY' },
  { p: 'KEEP the PLUGIN FILE TO the HEADER and the BOOTSTRAP',
    gain: 'SO THAT the FILES CONTENTS ARE REVIEWABLE AT A GLANCE',
    and: 'A READER CAN SEE WHAT HAPPENS WHEN the PLUGIN IS LOADED WITHOUT READING ANYTHING ELSE' },
];

The three obligations in practice

Each of the three obligations has a concrete shape, and the file's whole design follows from them. The header, the constants, the requires, and one call that registers — that is the entire top level of the file, and everything else lives behind a hook. The constants carry the prefix that makes the symbols unique; the requires use a once-only include so a second inclusion cannot redeclare anything; and the single call hands control to a class in a namespace, which is where every function that touches the platform lives. Then the four requirements that follow, and each has a specific failure. Every filter callback must return a value on every path. A callback that forgets to return gives the platform null, which it substitutes for the value it was passed, so the filter becomes a silent no-op and the symptom appears somewhere else — usually as a value that is mysteriously the unfiltered original. Every output must be escaped at the point of output, not on the way in; escaping on input is the wrong mechanism and produces double-escaped markup that renders visibly wrong on screen. Every hook priority should reflect a real ordering requirement, and a plugin that uses an extreme priority to run early collides with every other plugin that made the same choice, which is a collision nobody can debug because neither plugin knows about the other. And every early return in a filter must return the incoming value unchanged, not null and not an empty string, because the value flows to the next callback and a filter that drops it removes it from the chain entirely. Then the two things worth naming, and each is a mechanism behind a real class of bug. A fatal error in one plugin's callback is a fatal for the whole request, which makes defensive coding an obligation to every other plugin on the site: a missing array key is a warning, but a method call on a value the platform may return as null, or a division by an empty result set, is a fatal, and it is your code that produced the fatal in somebody else's page. And a callback registered twice fires twice, which is the mechanism behind duplicated output and duplicated database writes; it happens when a bootstrap function is called from two places or when a file is included more than once, so the plugin file's real job is making that structurally impossible rather than relying on every future reader remembering. Then the practical consequence, which is the whole shape of the file: a class with one registration method and a file that calls it gives you a place to test the registration, a place to see every hook in one list, and a guarantee that a callback cannot be registered twice because registration happens in exactly one place.

/* The top level of a plugin file, in full. */

const TOP_LEVEL = [
  'the HEADER BLOCK',
  'the CONSTANTS - WHICH CARRY the PREFIX THAT MAKES the SYMBOLS UNIQUE',
  'the REQUIRES - USING A ONCE-ONLY INCLUDE SO A SECOND INCLUSION CANNOT REDECLARE ANYTHING',
  'and ONE CALL THAT REGISTERS',
];
const EVERYTHING_ELSE = 'and EVERYTHING ELSE LIVES BEHIND A HOOK';

/* Four requirements, each with its failure. */
const REQUIREMENTS = [
  { r: 'EVERY FILTER CALLBACK MUST RETURN A VALUE ON EVERY PATH',
    failure: 'A CALLBACK THAT FORGETS TO RETURN GIVES the PLATFORM null, WHICH IT SUBSTITUTES FOR the VALUE IT WAS PASSED',
    consequence: 'SO the FILTER BECOMES A SILENT NO-OP and the SYMPTOM APPEARS SOMEWHERE ELSE',
    symptom: 'USUALLY AS A VALUE THAT IS MYSTERIOUSLY the UNFILTERED ORIGINAL' },
  { r: 'EVERY OUTPUT MUST BE ESCAPED AT the POINT of OUTPUT, NOT ON the WAY IN',
    why: 'ESCAPING ON INPUT IS the WRONG MECHANISM',
    symptom: 'and PRODUCES DOUBLE-ESCAPED MARKUP THAT RENDERS VISIBLY WRONG ON SCREEN' },
  { r: 'EVERY HOOK PRIORITY SHOULD REFLECT A REAL ORDERING REQUIREMENT',
    symptom: 'and A PLUGIN THAT USES AN EXTREME PRIORITY TO RUN EARLY COLLIDES WITH EVERY OTHER PLUGIN THAT MADE the SAME CHOICE',
    and: 'WHICH IS A COLLISION NOBODY CAN DEBUG BECAUSE NEITHER PLUGIN KNOWS ABOUT the OTHER' },
  { r: 'EVERY EARLY RETURN IN A FILTER MUST RETURN the INCOMING VALUE UNCHANGED',
    not: ['null', 'AN EMPTY STRING'],
    why: 'BECAUSE the VALUE FLOWS TO the NEXT CALLBACK and A FILTER THAT DROPS IT REMOVES IT FROM the CHAIN ENTIRELY' },
];

/* Two mechanisms. */
const MECHANISMS = [
  { m: 'A FATAL ERROR IN ONE PLUGINS CALLBACK IS A FATAL FOR the WHOLE REQUEST',
    obligation: 'DEFENSIVE CODING IS AN OBLIGATION TO EVERY OTHER PLUGIN ON the SITE',
    detail: ['A MISSING ARRAY KEY IS A WARNING',
      'but A METHOD CALL ON A VALUE the PLATFORM MAY RETURN AS NULL, or A DIVISION BY AN EMPTY RESULT SET, IS A FATAL'],
    and: 'IT IS YOUR CODE THAT PRODUCED the FATAL IN SOMEBODY ELSES PAGE' },
  { m: 'A CALLBACK REGISTERED TWICE FIRES TWICE',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND DUPLICATED OUTPUT and DUPLICATED DATABASE WRITES',
    causes: ['A BOOTSTRAP FUNCTION CALLED FROM TWO PLACES', 'A FILE INCLUDED MORE THAN ONCE'],
    therefore: 'SO the PLUGIN FILES REAL JOB IS MAKING THAT STRUCTURALLY IMPOSSIBLE rather than RELYING ON EVERY FUTURE READER REMEMBERING' },
];

/* The practical consequence. */
const SHAPE = {
  is: 'A CLASS with ONE REGISTRATION METHOD and A FILE THAT CALLS IT',
  gains: [
    'A PLACE TO TEST the REGISTRATION',
    'A PLACE TO SEE EVERY HOOK IN ONE LIST',
    'and A GUARANTEE THAT A CALLBACK CANNOT BE REGISTERED TWICE BECAUSE REGISTRATION HAPPENS IN EXACTLY ONE PLACE',
  ],
};

Choose a vendor prefix and never deviate. If your company is Acme and your product is Ledger, use the namespace Acme\Ledger\ and the function prefix acme_ledger_. The prefix must appear in every global function, class, constant, option key, cron hook, REST namespace and database table name. This is the single most effective defence against the collision failures described in Chapter 1.

Activation and load sequence

Activation hooks run once, in a sandboxed request with no output buffering and a shortened timeout. Understanding which request is which prevents the classic mistake of shipping schema creation inside plugins_loaded and breaking it on every subsequent request.

A plugin request passes through a fixed order. muplugins_loaded fires once must-use plugins are included. plugins_loaded fires after all active plugins are included, which is the correct point to bind every add_action and add_filter call. init fires once per request, after the current user and rewrite rules are known, and is the correct point to register post types, taxonomies, shortcodes, REST routes and blocks. wp_loaded fires last, immediately before the query runs.

HookFiresUse it for
plugins_loadedAfter all active plugins are includedBinding all hooks; loading your autoloader
initOnce per request, after $wp_query constructionregister_post_type, register_taxonomy, add_shortcode, register_rest_route, register_block_type
admin_initAdmin requests only, after initAdmin menus, settings registration, screen setup
wp_loadedImmediately before the main queryCode that must observe every plugin has registered its hooks

The sequence, and what each phase is for

A plugin has three distinct moments in a site's life, and confusing them produces bugs that appear only on one of them — on a fresh install, on a re-activation, or after an update — which is why the bugs are reported as intermittent. Loading happens on every request where the plugin is active: WordPress reads the plugin file, the header block is parsed, and the file's top-level code runs. This is where you declare, require and register. Activation happens once, on the transition from inactive to active, and it is the only moment you can rely on for work that must happen exactly once: creating tables, adding options, scheduling events. Deactivation happens on the transition back, and it is the place to undo only what is safe to undo. Then the four things that follow from the sequence, and each is a requirement. Anything that must happen once goes in the activation hook, not in the load path, because the load path runs on every request and a table creation there is a query on every page view; the mechanism is that WordPress stores the activation callback and fires it only on the transition. Deactivation must not destroy data, because deactivation is reversible and a user who deactivates a plugin to test something expects to find their data when they reactivate — so the deactivation hook unschedules events and clears caches and leaves the tables. Activation code must be idempotent anyway, because activation can fire more than once — a user reactivating a plugin that is already active, or a bulk action — and a create-table that is not guarded by an existence check will fail on the second run. And the update path is a fourth moment that is easy to forget, because an update replaces the plugin's files without firing activation, so anything that must happen when the version changes — a new column, a new option — has to run from a version check on load or from an upgrade routine the plugin owns. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Make every one-shot task idempotent, so it can run twice safely; the test is whether the code would be correct if it ran a hundred times, which is cheap to write and removes a whole class of support ticket. And log the version and the transition in development, because the difference between a plugin that is loading and one that is activating is invisible from the outside and is the thing you will want to know when a hook fires an unexpected number of times.

/* Three moments. */
const MOMENTS = {
  why: 'CONFUSING THEM PRODUCES BUGS THAT APPEAR ONLY ON ONE of THEM - ON A FRESH INSTALL, ON A RE-ACTIVATION, or AFTER AN UPDATE',
  symptom: 'WHICH IS WHY the BUGS ARE REPORTED AS INTERMITTENT',
  phases: [
    { n: 'LOADING', when: 'ON EVERY REQUEST WHERE the PLUGIN IS ACTIVE',
      does: ['WORDPRESS READS the PLUGIN FILE', 'the HEADER BLOCK IS PARSED', 'and the FILES TOP-LEVEL CODE RUNS'],
      for: 'THIS IS WHERE YOU DECLARE, REQUIRE and REGISTER' },
    { n: 'ACTIVATION', when: 'ONCE, ON the TRANSITION FROM INACTIVE to ACTIVE',
      unique: 'IT IS the ONLY MOMENT YOU CAN RELY ON for WORK THAT MUST HAPPEN EXACTLY ONCE',
      instances: ['CREATING TABLES', 'ADDING OPTIONS', 'SCHEDULING EVENTS'] },
    { n: 'DEACTIVATION', when: 'ON the TRANSITION BACK',
      rule: 'and IT IS the PLACE TO UNDO ONLY WHAT IS SAFE TO UNDO' },
  ],
};

/* Four consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'ANYTHING THAT MUST HAPPEN ONCE GOES IN the ACTIVATION HOOK, NOT IN the LOAD PATH',
    why: 'BECAUSE the LOAD PATH RUNS ON EVERY REQUEST and A TABLE CREATION THERE IS A QUERY ON EVERY PAGE VIEW',
    mechanism: 'the MECHANISM IS THAT WORDPRESS STORES the ACTIVATION CALLBACK and FIRES IT ONLY ON the TRANSITION' },
  { c: 'DEACTIVATION MUST NOT DESTROY DATA',
    why: 'BECAUSE DEACTIVATION IS REVERSIBLE and A USER WHO DEACTIVATES A PLUGIN TO TEST SOMETHING EXPECTS TO FIND THEIR DATA WHEN THEY REACTIVATE',
    practice: 'SO the DEACTIVATION HOOK UNSCHEDULES EVENTS and CLEARS CACHES and LEAVES the TABLES' },
  { c: 'ACTIVATION CODE MUST BE IDEMPOTENT ANYWAY',
    why: 'BECAUSE ACTIVATION CAN FIRE MORE THAN ONCE - A USER REACTIVATING A PLUGIN THAT IS ALREADY ACTIVE, or A BULK ACTION',
    symptom: 'and A CREATE-TABLE THAT IS NOT GUARDED BY AN EXISTENCE CHECK WILL FAIL ON the SECOND RUN' },
  { c: 'the UPDATE PATH IS A FOURTH MOMENT THAT IS EASY TO FORGET',
    detail: 'BECAUSE AN UPDATE REPLACES the PLUGINS FILES WITHOUT FIRING ACTIVATION',
    consequence: 'SO ANYTHING THAT MUST HAPPEN WHEN the VERSION CHANGES - A NEW COLUMN, A NEW OPTION - HAS TO RUN FROM A VERSION CHECK ON LOAD or FROM AN UPGRADE ROUTINE the PLUGIN OWNS' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'MAKE EVERY ONE-SHOT TASK IDEMPOTENT', gain: 'SO IT CAN RUN TWICE SAFELY',
    test: 'the TEST IS WHETHER the CODE WOULD BE CORRECT IF IT RAN A HUNDRED TIMES',
    why: 'WHICH IS CHEAP TO WRITE and REMOVES A WHOLE CLASS of SUPPORT TICKET' },
  { p: 'LOG the VERSION and the TRANSITION IN DEVELOPMENT',
    why: 'BECAUSE the DIFFERENCE BETWEEN A PLUGIN THAT IS LOADING and ONE THAT IS ACTIVATING IS INVISIBLE FROM the OUTSIDE',
    and: 'IT IS the THING YOU WILL WANT TO KNOW WHEN A HOOK FIRES AN UNEXPECTED NUMBER of TIMES' },
];

One-shot work, idempotently

The activation hook is the only place a plugin can safely do work that must happen once, and it is safe there and unsafe everywhere else for one reason: it fires on a transition, not on every request. But it is the wrong place for anything after the first release, because an update does not fire activation — the files are replaced and the plugin is simply active on the next request. So the pattern that actually works is a version check in the load path that triggers an upgrade routine, and it is strictly more useful than the activation hook because it covers installation, update and a partially-applied previous upgrade with one mechanism. Store the installed version in an option, compare it on load, and run the routine that brings the data from the stored version to the current one. Then the four things, and each is a requirement. The upgrade routine must be a sequence of steps, not a jump, because an installation two versions behind must apply two steps in order and a routine that assumes it runs immediately after the previous version produces a database in a state no version expects — which surfaces later as a query that fails against a column that should exist. Each step must be individually idempotent, because a partially-applied upgrade that was interrupted will be re-run and a column add that is not guarded fails on the second attempt; the platform provides an idempotent schema-change helper for exactly this reason, and using it is the difference between an upgrade that survives an interruption and one that does not. The version must be recorded after the steps, not before, so an interrupted upgrade re-runs rather than being recorded as complete — and this is a one-line ordering that decides whether the mechanism is safe. And deactivation must leave the data, because deactivating and reactivating is a thing people do while testing and a plugin that drops its tables on deactivation destroys their data irreversibly, which is the kind of report that ends a relationship with a user. Then the two things worth naming, and both are real failure modes of this pattern. A routine that runs in the load path runs on every request until it succeeds, so a routine that fatals will fatal on every request until somebody fixes it and a routine that is slow makes every request slow; the mitigations are to keep each step fast and to record which steps completed so a re-run skips them. And on a multisite network, activation fires once for the network but each site has its own data and its own version, so a network-activated plugin needs the routine to run per site, and getting this wrong produces a network where some sites migrated and some did not — a bug that appears only on the sites nobody looked at.

/* Why the version check in the load path, not the activation hook. */

const WHY = {
  activationIsSafe: 'IT FIRES ON A TRANSITION, NOT ON EVERY REQUEST',
  butWrongForUpdates: 'AND IT IS the WRONG PLACE for ANYTHING AFTER the FIRST RELEASE',
  why: 'BECAUSE AN UPDATE DOES NOT FIRE ACTIVATION - the FILES ARE REPLACED and the PLUGIN IS SIMPLY ACTIVE ON the NEXT REQUEST',
  pattern: ['STORE the INSTALLED VERSION IN AN OPTION', 'COMPARE IT ON LOAD',
    'and RUN the ROUTINE THAT BRINGS the DATA FROM the STORED VERSION TO the CURRENT ONE'],
  covers: ['INSTALLATION', 'UPDATE', 'and A PARTIALLY-APPLIED PREVIOUS UPGRADE'],
  with: 'ONE MECHANISM',
};

/* Four requirements. */
const REQUIREMENTS = [
  { r: 'the ROUTINE MUST BE A SEQUENCE of STEPS, NOT A JUMP',
    why: 'BECAUSE AN INSTALLATION TWO VERSIONS BEHIND MUST APPLY TWO STEPS IN ORDER',
    symptom: 'and A ROUTINE THAT ASSUMES IT RUNS IMMEDIATELY AFTER the PREVIOUS VERSION PRODUCES A DATABASE IN A STATE NO VERSION EXPECTS',
    later: 'WHICH SURFACES LATER AS A QUERY THAT FAILS AGAINST A COLUMN THAT SHOULD EXIST' },
  { r: 'EACH STEP MUST BE INDIVIDUALLY IDEMPOTENT',
    why: 'BECAUSE A PARTIALLY-APPLIED UPGRADE THAT WAS INTERRUPTED WILL BE RE-RUN',
    symptom: 'and A COLUMN ADD THAT IS NOT GUARDED FAILS ON the SECOND ATTEMPT',
    mechanism: 'the PLATFORM PROVIDES AN IDEMPOTENT SCHEMA-CHANGE HELPER for EXACTLY THIS REASON',
    and: 'USING IT IS the DIFFERENCE BETWEEN AN UPGRADE THAT SURVIVES AN INTERRUPTION and ONE THAT DOES NOT' },
  { r: 'the VERSION MUST BE RECORDED AFTER the STEPS, NOT BEFORE',
    gain: 'SO AN INTERRUPTED UPGRADE RE-RUNS rather than BEING RECORDED AS COMPLETE',
    note: 'and THIS IS A ONE-LINE ORDERING THAT DECIDES WHETHER the MECHANISM IS SAFE' },
  { r: 'DEACTIVATION MUST LEAVE the DATA',
    why: 'BECAUSE DEACTIVATING and REACTIVATING IS A THING PEOPLE DO WHILE TESTING',
    consequence: 'and A PLUGIN THAT DROPS ITS TABLES ON DEACTIVATION DESTROYS THEIR DATA IRREVERSIBLY',
    and: 'WHICH IS the KIND of REPORT THAT ENDS A RELATIONSHIP WITH A USER' },
];

/* Two failure modes. */
const FAILURE = [
  { f: 'A ROUTINE THAT RUNS IN the LOAD PATH RUNS ON EVERY REQUEST UNTIL IT SUCCEEDS',
    detail: 'SO A ROUTINE THAT FATALS WILL FATAL ON EVERY REQUEST UNTIL SOMEBODY FIXES IT',
    and: 'A ROUTINE THAT IS SLOW MAKES EVERY REQUEST SLOW',
    mitigations: ['KEEP EACH STEP FAST', 'RECORD WHICH STEPS COMPLETED so A RE-RUN SKIPS THEM'] },
  { f: 'ON A MULTISITE NETWORK, ACTIVATION FIRES ONCE for the NETWORK but EACH SITE HAS ITS OWN DATA and ITS OWN VERSION',
    requirement: 'SO A NETWORK-ACTIVATED PLUGIN NEEDS the ROUTINE TO RUN PER SITE',
    symptom: 'and GETTING THIS WRONG PRODUCES A NETWORK WHERE SOME SITES MIGRATED and SOME DID NOT',
    and: 'A BUG THAT APPEARS ONLY ON the SITES NOBODY LOOKED AT' },
];

Activation hooks are one-shot. register_activation_hook stores its callback in the activate_ option and fires only on the transition from inactive to active. Adding schema creation there is correct; adding it there and nowhere else is also correct, because the hook genuinely does not run again. Never rely on it for upgrades — that is what register_uninstall_hook and a versioned upgrade routine are for.

How WordPress loads a plugin file

Loading is a three-stage pipeline: file discovery, header parsing, then inclusion in alphabetical order. Each stage has consequences for how you may structure a plugin, and two of them are routinely misunderstood.

Discovery walks wp-content/plugins one level deep and reads every .php file it finds there. Subdirectories are not scanned. A plugin whose entry point lives in a subdirectory is therefore invisible unless a thin top-level stub file carries the header. Header parsing is regex-based against the first 8 kilobytes of the file, so the header comment must appear near the top and must not be separated from the opening <?php tag by more than one blank line.

Inclusion happens in the order the active_plugins option lists, not alphabetical order. That array is an ordered list, and WordPress preserves it. Plugin load order is therefore a property of activation history, not a constant.

What happens before your code runs, and why it constrains you

Understanding the load sequence is what turns "it works on my site" into "it works", because every constraint on plugin code comes from a step that happens before it. WordPress loads the core bootstrap, then the active plugins. It reads each active plugin's header comment to decide whether it is active, then includes the file. Only after the plugins are loaded is the theme included, and only after that is init fired. That ordering is the source of most plugin load-order bugs and it is worth having precisely in mind: your plugin file is included before the theme's functions exist, and before most of the platform's own subsystems are ready. Then the four things this implies, and each is a specific consequence. You cannot call a theme function at file scope, because the theme has not been loaded. You cannot rely on a function from another plugin at file scope, because plugins load in the order the active_plugins option lists, which is the order they were activated and which you do not control on somebody else's site. You cannot assume the request is authenticated, because the authentication cookies have not been parsed at load time — which is why the capability checks belong in the callback rather than in a guard. And you cannot assume a query result exists, because no query has run. Then the two things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism worth knowing because both produce the intermittent reports. Calling a function that a later-loaded plugin defines, which works on a site where that plugin happens to be loaded first and produces a fatal error on a site where it is not — and since activation order is per-site, the same plugin breaks for one user and not another. And a guard that checks a capability at file scope, which returns false on every request because no user is authenticated yet, so the plugin silently does nothing on every page including the admin pages where it would have worked. Then the three practices, and each is a discipline. Do all your work on hooks, and register them at file scope. That means the file's top-level code is only a require and a registration call, and everything that touches the platform is inside a callback that the platform invokes when it is ready. Declare dependencies explicitly — the header tag that names the plugins you need, and a runtime check that reports the problem clearly rather than fataling, because a plugin that dies with a clear message is debuggable and one that dies with a type error is not. And when two plugins must interact, publish a hook rather than calling their code, so the dependency is at the right end and neither plugin has to know about the other's load order.

/* The sequence. */
const SEQUENCE = [
  'WORDPRESS LOADS the CORE BOOTSTRAP',
  'THEN the ACTIVE PLUGINS - IT READS EACH ACTIVE PLUGINS HEADER COMMENT to DECIDE WHETHER IT IS ACTIVE, THEN INCLUDES the FILE',
  'ONLY AFTER the PLUGINS ARE LOADED IS the THEME INCLUDED',
  'and ONLY AFTER THAT IS THE INIT ACTION FIRED',
};
const WHY_MATTERS = 'THAT ORDERING IS the SOURCE of MOST PLUGIN LOAD-ORDER BUGS';
const PRECISE = 'YOUR PLUGIN FILE IS INCLUDED BEFORE the THEMES FUNCTIONS EXIST, and BEFORE MOST of the PLATFORMS OWN SUBSYSTEMS ARE READY';

/* Four implications. */
const IMPLIES = [
  { i: 'YOU CANNOT CALL A THEME FUNCTION AT FILE SCOPE', why: 'BECAUSE the THEME HAS NOT BEEN LOADED' },
  { i: 'YOU CANNOT RELY ON A FUNCTION FROM ANOTHER PLUGIN AT FILE SCOPE',
    why: 'BECAUSE PLUGINS LOAD IN the ORDER the ACTIVE PLUGINS OPTION LISTS, WHICH IS the ORDER THEY WERE ACTIVATED',
    and: 'and WHICH YOU DO NOT CONTROL ON SOMEBODY ELSES SITE' },
  { i: 'YOU CANNOT ASSUME the REQUEST IS AUTHENTICATED',
    why: 'BECAUSE the AUTHENTICATION COOKIES HAVE NOT BEEN PARSED AT LOAD TIME',
    consequence: 'WHICH IS WHY the CAPABILITY CHECKS BELONG IN the CALLBACK rather than IN A GUARD' },
  { i: 'YOU CANNOT ASSUME A QUERY RESULT EXISTS', why: 'BECAUSE NO QUERY HAS RUN' },
];

/* Two things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'CALLING A FUNCTION THAT A LATER-LOADED PLUGIN DEFINES',
    detail: 'WHICH WORKS ON A SITE WHERE THAT PLUGIN HAPPENS TO BE LOADED FIRST',
    symptom: 'and PRODUCES A FATAL ERROR ON A SITE WHERE IT IS NOT',
    why: 'and SINCE ACTIVATION ORDER IS PER-SITE, the SAME PLUGIN BREAKS FOR ONE USER and NOT ANOTHER' },
  { f: 'A GUARD THAT CHECKS A CAPABILITY AT FILE SCOPE',
    symptom: 'WHICH RETURNS FALSE ON EVERY REQUEST BECAUSE NO USER IS AUTHENTICATED YET',
    consequence: 'SO the PLUGIN SILENTLY DOES NOTHING ON EVERY PAGE INCLUDING the ADMIN PAGES WHERE IT WOULD HAVE WORKED' },
];

/* Three practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'DO ALL YOUR WORK ON HOOKS, and REGISTER THEM AT FILE SCOPE',
    detail: 'THAT MEANS the FILES TOP-LEVEL CODE IS ONLY A REQUIRE and A REGISTRATION CALL',
    and: 'EVERYTHING THAT TOUCHES the PLATFORM IS INSIDE A CALLBACK THAT the PLATFORM INVOKES WHEN IT IS READY' },
  { p: 'DECLARE DEPENDENCIES EXPLICITLY',
    detail: ['the HEADER TAG THAT NAMES the PLUGINS YOU NEED', 'A RUNTIME CHECK THAT REPORTS the PROBLEM CLEARLY rather than FATALING'],
    why: 'BECAUSE A PLUGIN THAT DIES with A CLEAR MESSAGE IS DEBUGGABLE and ONE THAT DIES with A TYPE ERROR IS NOT' },
  { p: 'WHEN TWO PLUGINS MUST INTERACT, PUBLISH A HOOK rather than CALLING THEIR CODE',
    gain: ['SO the DEPENDENCY IS AT the RIGHT END', 'and NEITHER PLUGIN HAS TO KNOW ABOUT the OTHERS LOAD ORDER'] },
];

Registering, depending, and the early hook

The registration method is where a plugin's behaviour begins, and its shape is worth stating precisely because the alternative — doing the work at file scope — is both the most common defect and the most damaging. A registration method adds actions and filters and does nothing else. No queries, no output, no reading of request data, no capability checks. The callbacks it names do the work, and each of them begins by testing the conditions under which it should run — the capability, the screen, the request method, the nonce — and returns immediately when they are not met. Then the four things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Hook early for what other plugins depend on, and late for what depends on other plugins. A hook that registers a post type must run before anything that queries for one; a hook that prints admin markup must run after the menu exists. The default priority is a convention rather than a requirement, and using the same extreme priority for both is a collision waiting to happen. A dependency between two plugins must be a hook, not a call. If your plugin needs another plugin's output, publish a filter and let the other plugin pass its value into it; a direct call couples the two plugins' load order together and produces a fatal on any site where they happen to load in the other order — which is a per-site fact nobody controls. A capability check belongs in the callback, not in a guard, because a guard runs at load time when no user is authenticated and is therefore always false, which is why a plugin that guards on a capability appears to do nothing at all. And a dependency declared in the header is not a dependency checked at run time, so a plugin deactivated after yours was activated produces a fatal unless your callbacks check — and that check belongs on an admin notice rather than as a load-time exit, so the site keeps working. Then the two things worth naming, and both are about order. The order of registration is not the order of execution, and two callbacks on the same hook at the same priority run in registration order, which is a per-site fact — so a dependency between two of your own callbacks is a bug even though both are yours and you know the code. And the earliest hook you can safely use is the one where the subsystems you need already exist, which is why the answer to "which hook" is nearly always "the earliest one after the thing I depend on is loaded" rather than "the earliest one"; the pattern for a plugin that must run before others is to publish your own registration on the earliest hook and let the others attach to that, which inverts the dependency and makes the ordering your own problem rather than a shared one.

/* The registration method does exactly one thing. */

const REGISTRATION = {
  does: 'ADD ACTIONS and FILTERS and NOTHING ELSE',
  notDoes: ['QUERIES', 'OUTPUT', 'READING OF REQUEST DATA', 'CAPABILITY CHECKS'],
  callbacksDo: 'the WORK',
  andEach: ['TESTS the CONDITIONS UNDER WHICH IT SHOULD RUN - the CAPABILITY, the SCREEN, the REQUEST METHOD, the NONCE',
    'and RETURNS IMMEDIATELY WHEN THEY ARE NOT MET'],
};

/* Four mechanisms. */
const MECHANISMS = [
  { m: 'HOOK EARLY for WHAT OTHER PLUGINS DEPEND ON, and LATE for WHAT DEPENDS ON OTHER PLUGINS',
    detail: ['A HOOK THAT REGISTERS A POST TYPE MUST RUN BEFORE ANYTHING THAT QUERIES FOR ONE',
      'A HOOK THAT PRINTS ADMIN MARKUP MUST RUN AFTER the MENU EXISTS'],
    priority: 'the DEFAULT PRIORITY IS A CONVENTION rather than A REQUIREMENT',
    collision: 'and USING the SAME EXTREME PRIORITY for BOTH IS A COLLISION WAITING TO HAPPEN' },
  { m: 'A DEPENDENCY BETWEEN TWO PLUGINS MUST BE A HOOK, NOT A CALL',
    how: 'PUBLISH A FILTER and LET the OTHER PLUGIN PASS ITS VALUE INTO IT',
    consequence: 'A DIRECT CALL COUPLES the TWO PLUGINS LOAD ORDER TOGETHER and PRODUCES A FATAL ON ANY SITE WHERE THEY HAPPEN TO LOAD IN the OTHER ORDER',
    and: 'WHICH IS A PER-SITE FACT NOBODY CONTROLS' },
  { m: 'A CAPABILITY CHECK BELONGS IN the CALLBACK, NOT IN A GUARD',
    why: 'BECAUSE A GUARD RUNS AT LOAD TIME WHEN NO USER IS AUTHENTICATED and IS THEREFORE ALWAYS FALSE',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS WHY A PLUGIN THAT GUARDS ON A CAPABILITY APPEARS TO DO NOTHING AT ALL' },
  { m: 'A DEPENDENCY DECLARED IN the HEADER IS NOT A DEPENDENCY CHECKED AT RUN TIME',
    symptom: 'SO A PLUGIN DEACTIVATED AFTER YOURS WAS ACTIVATED PRODUCES A FATAL UNLESS YOUR CALLBACKS CHECK',
    and: 'that CHECK BELONGS ON AN ADMIN NOTICE rather than AS A LOAD-TIME EXIT, SO the SITE KEEPS WORKING' },
];

/* Two things about order. */
const ORDER = [
  { t: 'the ORDER of REGISTRATION IS NOT the ORDER of EXECUTION',
    detail: 'and TWO CALLBACKS ON the SAME HOOK AT the SAME PRIORITY RUN IN REGISTRATION ORDER, WHICH IS A PER-SITE FACT',
    consequence: 'SO A DEPENDENCY BETWEEN TWO of YOUR OWN CALLBACKS IS A BUG EVEN THOUGH BOTH ARE YOURS and YOU KNOW the CODE' },
  { t: 'the EARLIEST HOOK YOU CAN SAFELY USE IS the ONE WHERE the SUBSYSTEMS YOU NEED ALREADY EXIST',
    answer: 'WHICH IS WHY the ANSWER TO which hook IS NEARLY ALWAYS the EARLIEST ONE AFTER the THING I DEPEND ON IS LOADED rather than the EARLIEST ONE',
    pattern: ['PUBLISH YOUR OWN REGISTRATION ON the EARLIEST HOOK and LET the OTHERS ATTACH TO THAT',
      'WHICH INVERTS the DEPENDENCY and MAKES the ORDERING YOUR OWN PROBLEM rather than A SHARED ONE'],
  },
];

Never rely on load order. Binding a hook that another plugin may not have registered yet fails silently. If you need another plugin, declare it in your header with Requires Plugins: (WordPress 6.5 and later) so the dependency UI can enforce it, and guard every cross-plugin call with function_exists() or class_exists(). Your code must also never call a function from a theme, because themes change without notice.

Audience and prerequisites

This is a reference, not a tutorial. It assumes you can write PHP, have read the WordPress coding standards, and want the exact contract for an API rather than a worked example with it explained.

The bar for contributing a plugin to wordpress.org is a single reviewer with full source access. A plugin that cannot be audited against this guide will not pass review, and a plugin that fails review damages the author’s standing with the community. Read Chapters 16 and 19 in full before submitting anything.

  • Assumed. PHP 8.3 syntax including enums and constructor promotion, Composer autoloading, and Git.
  • Assumed. The WordPress coding standards for naming, file layout and Yoda conditions.
  • Not covered. Theme development, WooCommerce extension authoring, and multisite network administration beyond the summary in Chapter 11.
  • Not covered. JavaScript build tooling for editor assets beyond the wp-scripts command surface used in Chapter 5.

Why local development is assumed, and how to get it

Every exercise in this guide involves running the code, and that requirement is the reason the guide assumes a local environment: a plugin developed by editing files on a live site is developed in the slowest and most dangerous way available, because the feedback loop is a browser refresh over a network round trip to somebody else's server, and because the site you are changing is the site somebody is using. A local environment is a WordPress installation running on your own machine, which means three things must be running: a web server or its built-in equivalent, a database, and PHP with the extensions WordPress needs. The commonest approach on a machine that already has the other pieces is a container, because it packages the web server, the database and the PHP version into something reproducible, and because "it works on my machine" becomes a specification you can state rather than a description of your operating system. The alternative is a native stack installed directly, which gives faster file access and easier debugging and which is worth the setup on a machine that will develop WordPress full time. Then the three things that make a local environment workable, and each is a specific configuration. Enable error display, because WordPress suppresses errors by default in production-like configurations and a plugin that fatals on your machine should say so loudly rather than render a blank page; the setting is in the configuration file and the display function has a companion that only administrators see. Enable the debug log, which is the mechanism that records errors the display setting would otherwise hide, and which is where you will actually find the error when it happens inside a hook rather than at file scope. And set the environment constant, which is the platform's marker that this is not production, and which several plugins and most developer tools use to decide whether to enable their own verbose behaviour. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Testing on the live site, which risks a fatal error on a site with real users and which produces the difference between "it works for me" and a report. Not knowing where the error log is, which is the single most time-wasting failure when a plugin does nothing and produces no visible error — the debug log is where a warning that did not become an error is recorded. And developing with a different PHP version from production, which produces code that uses a feature the production PHP does not have, or that avoids one the local PHP deprecates; the version is a number in the plugin header and it should be the number your production server runs. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Keep the site in version control, including its configuration and its content, because a local environment that takes an hour to rebuild is an environment you will stop using, and because a configuration change you cannot reproduce is a configuration you cannot debug. And make reinstalling disposable — a script that fetches WordPress, creates the database and installs it in one command — because the fastest way to a reproducible environment is to throw it away and rebuild it regularly.

/* Why a local environment is assumed. */

const WHY = {
  everyExercise: 'EVERY EXERCISE IN this GUIDE INVOLVES RUNNING the CODE',
  liveDevelopment: 'A PLUGIN DEVELOPED BY EDITING FILES ON A LIVE SITE IS DEVELOPED IN the SLOWEST and MOST DANGEROUS WAY AVAILABLE',
  slow: 'BECAUSE the FEEDBACK LOOP IS A BROWSER REFRESH OVER A NETWORK ROUND TRIP to SOMEBODY ELSES SERVER',
  and: 'and BECAUSE the SITE YOU ARE CHANGING IS the SITE SOMEBODY IS USING',
  needs: ['A WEB SERVER or ITS BUILT-IN EQUIVALENT', 'A DATABASE', 'and PHP with the EXTENSIONS WORDPRESS NEEDS'],
  containerApproach: 'the COMMONEST APPROACH ON A MACHINE THAT ALREADY HAS the OTHER PIECES IS A CONTAINER',
  why: 'BECAUSE IT PACKAGES the WEB SERVER, the DATABASE and the PHP VERSION INTO SOMETHING REPRODUCIBLE',
  and2: 'and BECAUSE it works on my machine BECOMES A SPECIFICATION YOU CAN STATE rather than A DESCRIPTION of YOUR OPERATING SYSTEM',
  nativeApproach: 'the ALTERNATIVE IS A NATIVE STACK INSTALLED DIRECTLY',
  nativeGood: 'WHICH GIVES FASTER FILE ACCESS and EASIER DEBUGGING and WHICH IS WORTH the SETUP ON A MACHINE THAT WILL DEVELOP WORDPRESS FULL TIME',
};

/* Three things that make it workable. */

What to read first, and what to skip

A guide of this size has an order and a shape, and knowing both saves a reader a day. Read the orientation chapter in order, then chapter one, then whichever of chapters two to seven describes the thing you are building. The orientation chapter is not introductory material in the usual sense — it establishes the constraints that everything after it assumes, and skipping it produces code that violates them in ways that are not obvious until much later. Chapter one is the file, the header, the symbols and the assets, and it is the part that every plugin needs regardless of what the plugin does. Chapters two and three are hooks and the request lifecycle, and they are the foundation of everything a plugin does; a plugin that reads only those two can be written correctly, if not ambitiously. Chapters four to seven are the domain — settings, database, assets in the browser, and the security and deployment material — and the right order among them is the order of what you need first, which is usually settings, then the database, then the rest. Then the three things worth saying about the exercises, and each is a deliberate property of how they are written. Every exercise produces something that runs. There is no exercise that ends with "you should now understand how hooks work"; each one ends with code that does something observable on a real site, because the feedback loop of running it is what makes the concept stick. Every exercise is scoped to the chapter it is in, so an exercise in chapter four assumes the chapter-one file structure and the chapter-two hook registration, and no more. And every exercise is written to be done in order within the chapter, because they build on each other — the second exercise in a chapter usually extends the first, and doing them out of order produces a plugin that does not run. Then the two things that are worth knowing about the code in the exercises, and both are about why it is shaped the way it is. The code is written to be read rather than run to completion, which means it is compact, it omits the parts that are not the subject, and it will not survive a real deployment unchanged — the text around each sample says what it is eliding and why. And the security decisions in the samples are real, — escaping, capability checks, nonce verification and prepared statements are present in every sample where they would be needed, because an exercise that teaches the pattern without the security is teaching something nobody should ship.

/* Reading order. */

const ORDER = [
  { part: 'the ORIENTATION CHAPTER, IN ORDER', then: 'CHAPTER ONE', then: 'CHAPTERS TWO to SEVEN',
    notIntro: 'IT IS NOT INTRODUCTORY MATERIAL IN the USUAL SENSE',

    consequence: 'and SKIPPING IT PRODUCES CODE THAT VIOLATES THEM IN WAYS THAT ARE NOT OBVIOUS UNTIL MUCH LATER' },

];
