Exercises

  1. Create a plugin stub whose header declares Requires at least: 6.7, Requires PHP: 8.3 and Requires Plugins:, and confirm the dependency field renders on the Plugins screen.
  2. Enumerate the four registration APIs you would call at init and justify the choice of hook for each over plugins_loaded.
  3. Write a throwaway plugin that dumps $GLOBALS keys at plugins_loaded and at init, then explain the differences in what is available at each point.

1 · Plugin Anatomy

The plugin header blockFile and directory layoutLoad guards and the entry pointNamespaces, prefixes and collisionsBundling third-party codeReadme, changelog and metadata hygiene

A plugin is recognised by exactly one thing: a header comment in a top-level PHP file. Everything else follows from that. This chapter defines the header contract, the directory layout that keeps a plugin reviewable, and the symbol-naming discipline that prevents the one class of failure that can white-screen an entire site.

The plugin header block

The header is parsed by regex, not by a real parser, and only from the first 8 kilobytes of the file. It is a comment to PHP and metadata to WordPress, which means it can be silently malformed with no error message anywhere.

WordPress reads each recognised tag as Key: Value on a single line. The parsing is tolerant of case in the key but not in the meaning. A tag WordPress does not recognise is ignored silently, so a typo such as Requires PHP written as RequiresPHP simply does not apply the restriction.

TagRequiredPurpose
Plugin NameYesDisplay name. May not contain the word plugin, by wordpress.org policy.
Plugin URINoCanonical plugin page. Required for wordpress.org hosting.
DescriptionNoShown on the plugin detail page; 150 characters maximum in the readme short description.
VersionYesYour own version. WordPress never compares it for you.
Requires at leastNoMinimum WordPress version, for example 6.7.
Requires PHPNoMinimum PHP version, for example 8.3. Enforced before activation.
Requires PluginsNoComma-separated slugs of required plugins. Added in 6.5.
Text DomainNoTranslation slug, matching the directory name exactly.
Domain PathNoRelative path to the language directory, normally /languages.
LicenseNoSPDX identifier. wordpress.org requires GPLv2 or later.
Update URINoBlocks wordpress.org update offers for a plugin you host elsewhere.
/*
Plugin Name:       Acme Ledger
Plugin URI:        https://example.com/plugins/acme-ledger
Description:       Double-entry bookkeeping primitives for WooCommerce orders.
Version:           2.4.0
Requires at least: 6.7
Requires PHP:      8.3
Author:            Acme Systems
Author URI:        https://example.com
License:           GPL-2.0-or-later
License URI:       https://www.gnu.org/licenses/gpl-2.0.html
Text Domain:       acme-ledger
Domain Path:       /languages
Requires Plugins:  woocommerce
*/

What each field does, and the two that are not documentation

The header block is thirty lines at the top of one file, and it is the only part of a plugin that the platform reads rather than executes — which means everything in it is a declaration with a consequence, and two of the fields are much more than metadata. The version field is the upgrade mechanism. It is the number your own routine compares the stored option against, and it is the only thing that tells you a new release has been installed on somebody else's site; a plugin that ships with a version that does not change is a plugin that can never know it needs to migrate its own database. The text domain field is the translation key. Every translatable string in the plugin is looked up by the text domain, and the domain must equal the plugin's directory slug exactly — so a mismatch does not produce an error, it produces strings that silently stay in English, which is the failure nobody notices until a translator does. Then the three fields that matter for compatibility, and each has a different enforcement mechanism. The minimum WordPress version is checked by the plugin screen, which refuses to activate the plugin on an older installation and shows a notice saying why. The minimum PHP version is checked before the file is included at all, which is a stronger guarantee because there is no code running yet to fail. And the required-plugins tag is not a resolver — it prevents activation in the plugin screen and does nothing about a dependency that was deactivated afterwards, which is why the runtime check described in the orientation chapter is still required. Then the three things that go wrong in practice, and each is a mechanism. A version that does not match the readme's stable tag, which produces an upgrade prompt for a version the user already has, because the readme's stable tag is what the update check compares against. A text domain that does not match the directory name, which produces untranslated strings and a translation file that is never loaded. And a plugin URI that is a placeholder, which appears in the plugin list as a link to nothing and which the directory's review process treats as an incomplete submission. Then the two things worth naming, and both are about what the header cannot do. It cannot declare that your plugin works with another — only that it requires it at activation — so a mutual dependency between two plugins cannot be expressed and must be handled at run time. And it is not read when the files are updated by any mechanism other than the plugin screen, which is the update-bypassing case the runtime guard exists for; a plugin that has a version guard but no activation-time guard will fatal on an installation where the files were replaced directly.

/* The header block: what each field does. */

const FIELDS = [
  { f: 'Plugin Name', role: 'the NAME SHOWN IN the PLUGIN LIST' },
  { f: 'Version', isThe: 'UPGRADE MECHANISM',
    role: 'IT IS the NUMBER YOUR OWN ROUTINE COMPARES the STORED OPTION AGAINST',
    and: 'IT IS the ONLY THING THAT TELLS YOU A NEW RELEASE HAS BEEN INSTALLED ON SOMEBODY ELSES SITE',
    consequence: 'A PLUGIN THAT SHIPS WITH A VERSION THAT DOES NOT CHANGE IS A PLUGIN THAT CAN NEVER KNOW IT NEEDS TO MIGRATE ITS OWN DATABASE' },
  { f: 'Text Domain', isThe: 'TRANSLATION KEY',
    must: 'EQUAL the PLUGINS DIRECTORY SLUG, EXACTLY',
    consequence: 'A MISMATCH DOES NOT PRODUCE AN ERROR, IT PRODUCES STRINGS THAT SILENTLY STAY IN ENGLISH',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the FAILURE NOBODY NOTICES UNTIL A TRANSLATOR DOES' },
];

/* Three compatibility fields, three mechanisms. */
const COMPATIBILITY = [
  { f: 'THE MINIMUM WORDPRESS VERSION', enforcedBy: 'the PLUGIN SCREEN',
    does: 'REFUSES TO ACTIVATE the PLUGIN ON AN OLDER INSTALLATION and SHOWS A NOTICE SAYING WHY' },
  { f: 'THE MINIMUM PHP VERSION', enforcedBy: 'THE PLATFORM, BEFORE the FILE IS INCLUDED AT ALL',
    stronger: 'WHICH IS A STRONGER GUARANTEE BECAUSE THERE IS NO CODE RUNNING YET to FAIL' },
  { f: 'THE REQUIRED-PLUGINS TAG', isNot: 'A RESOLVER',
    what: 'IT PREVENTS ACTIVATION IN the PLUGIN SCREEN and DOES NOTHING ABOUT A DEPENDENCY THAT WAS DEACTIVATED AFTERWARDS',
    therefore: 'WHICH IS WHY the RUNTIME CHECK IS STILL REQUIRED' },
];

/* Three practical mistakes. */
const MISTAKE = [
  { m: 'A VERSION THAT DOES NOT MATCH the READMES STABLE TAG',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES AN UPGRADE PROMPT FOR A VERSION the USER ALREADY HAS',
    because: 'the READMES STABLE TAG IS WHAT the UPDATE CHECK COMPARES AGAINST' },
  { m: 'A TEXT DOMAIN THAT DOES NOT MATCH the DIRECTORY NAME',
    symptoms: ['STRINGS THAT STAY UNTRANSLATED', 'and A TRANSLATION FILE THAT IS NEVER LOADED'] },
  { m: 'A PLUGIN URI THAT IS A PLACEHOLDER',
    symptom: 'WHICH APPEARS IN the PLUGIN LIST AS A LINK TO NOTHING',
    and: 'WHICH the DIRECTORYS REVIEW PROCESS TREATS AS AN INCOMPLETE SUBMISSION' },
];

/* What the header cannot do. */
const CANNOT = [
  { c: 'DECLARE THAT YOUR PLUGIN WORKS WITH ANOTHER',
    what: 'ONLY THAT IT REQUIRES IT AT ACTIVATION',
    consequence: 'SO A MUTUAL DEPENDENCY BETWEEN TWO PLUGINS CANNOT BE EXPRESSED and MUST BE HANDLED AT RUN TIME' },
  { c: 'BE READ WHEN the FILES ARE UPDATED BY ANY MECHANISM OTHER THAN the PLUGIN SCREEN',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the UPDATE-BYPASSING CASE the RUNTIME GUARD EXISTS FOR',
    consequence: 'A PLUGIN THAT HAS A VERSION GUARD but NO ACTIVATION-TIME GUARD WILL FATAL ON AN INSTALLATION WHERE the FILES WERE REPLACED DIRECTLY' },
];

Requires Plugins is not a dependency resolver. The tag tells the plugin screen to prevent activation when a dependency is missing. It does not stop your code running if someone force-activates the plugin through WP-CLI or by editing the active_plugins option directly. Every cross-plugin call still needs a function_exists() guard.

File and directory layout

WordPress only inspects the top level of wp-content/plugins. The standard layout is a single file per namespace, with assets and vendor code in sibling directories that the scanner never opens.

There are two accepted structures. The single-file plugin is one top-level PHP file containing the header, the hook bindings and all logic. It is acceptable only for very small plugins with no assets. The directory plugin names the directory after the text domain and uses that name as the entry file.

acme-ledger/
├── acme-ledger.php        // entry point: header + guard + require bootstrap
├── uninstall.php          // runs only on Delete, not on Deactivate
├── readme.txt             // wordpress.org readme, Stable tag required
├── includes/
│   ├── class-ledger.php
│   ├── class-entry.php
│   ├── functions.php
│   └── admin/
│       └── class-settings-page.php
├── admin/
│   ├── css/
│   ├── js/
│   └── images/
├── public/
│   ├── class-shortcode.php
│   └── class-rest-controller.php
├── languages/
│   ├── acme-ledger.pot
│   └── acme-ledger-fr_FR.po
└── vendor/                // committed, never installed at runtime
    └── autoload.php

What the directory should look like and why each part is where it is

The layout of a plugin directory is one of the few things about plugin development that is genuinely settled, and the reason is that every part of it answers a question that the platform or the reviewer will ask. One PHP file at the root of the directory is the plugin: it is what the platform includes, it carries the header, and everything else in the directory exists to be included by it. One directory per concern below it — code, templates, assets, languages — because a plugin that puts everything in one directory cannot be navigated and cannot have a file excluded from a distribution without hand-picking. An assets directory for anything the browser downloads, and the reason it is named that is that a convention the directory itself relies on: the plugin's URL helper is defined in terms of that path, so a differently-named directory works only if you construct its URL yourself. A languages directory for translations, declared in the header, so the platform loads the right file. And the vendor directory for bundled third-party code, which must be committed rather than installed at deploy time because the plugin is expected to work on an installation where the site owner does not run a package manager. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Including files by relative path without a constant, which breaks when the plugin is symlinked for development and which means the include path has to be derived from the plugin file's own location rather than from the current working directory. Committing the development artefacts — a source map, a test fixture with a database dump, a node_modules directory — which ships weight to every user and which the directory review notices. And a directory name that does not match the slug, which breaks the text domain lookup, the update mechanism's ability to find the plugin, and the directory listing's identifier, all at once. Then the two things worth naming, and both are about what the layout is for. The root file must be the only thing the platform includes, because a plugin with two entry points will have one of them loaded by the platform and one of them not, and the one that is not is where a function you depend on turns out not to be defined. And the layout should be the same in every plugin you write, because a shared skeleton is what makes a plugin you wrote eight months ago recognisable — the same directory names, the same bootstrap call, the same registration entry point — and that recognisability is worth more than any individual structural choice.

/* The layout, part by part. */

const LAYOUT = {
  root: {
    holds: 'ONE PHP FILE - the PLUGIN',
    is: 'WHAT the PLATFORM INCLUDES',
    carries: 'the HEADER',
    and: 'EVERYTHING ELSE IN the DIRECTORY EXISTS TO BE INCLUDED BY IT',
  },
  below: {
    onePerConcern: ['CODE', 'TEMPLATES', 'ASSETS', 'LANGUAGES'],
    why: 'BECAUSE A PLUGIN THAT PUTS EVERYTHING IN ONE DIRECTORY CANNOT BE NAVIGATED and CANNOT HAVE A FILE EXCLUDED FROM A DISTRIBUTION WITHOUT HAND-PICKING',
  },
  assets: {
    holds: 'ANYTHING the BROWSER DOWNLOADS',
    nameIsLoadBearing: 'and the REASON IT IS NAMED THAT IS THAT A CONVENTION the DIRECTORY ITSELF RELIES ON',
    detail: 'the PLUGINS URL HELPER IS DEFINED IN TERMS of THAT PATH, SO A DIFFERENTLY-NAMED DIRECTORY WORKS ONLY IF YOU CONSTRUCT ITS URL YOURSELF',
  },
  languages: { holds: 'TRANSLATIONS', declaredIn: 'the HEADER', so: 'SO the PLATFORM LOADS the RIGHT FILE' },
  vendor: {
    holds: 'BUNDLED THIRD-PARTY CODE',
    mustBeCommitted: 'MUST BE COMMITTED rather than INSTALLED AT DEPLOY TIME',
    why: 'BECAUSE the PLUGIN IS EXPECTED TO WORK ON AN INSTALLATION WHERE the SITE OWNER DOES NOT RUN A PACKAGE MANAGER',
  },
};

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'INCLUDING FILES BY RELATIVE PATH WITHOUT A CONSTANT',
    symptom: 'WHICH BREAKS WHEN the PLUGIN IS SYMLINKED for DEVELOPMENT',
    and: 'WHICH MEANS the INCLUDE PATH HAS TO BE DERIVED FROM the PLUGIN FILES OWN LOCATION rather than FROM the CURRENT WORKING DIRECTORY' },
  { f: 'COMMITTING the DEVELOPMENT ARTEFACTS',
    instances: ['A SOURCE MAP', 'A TEST FIXTURE with A DATABASE DUMP', 'A NODE_MODULES DIRECTORY'],
    consequence: 'WHICH SHIPS WEIGHT TO EVERY USER and WHICH the DIRECTORY REVIEW NOTICES' },
  { f: 'A DIRECTORY NAME THAT DOES NOT MATCH the SLUG',
    breaksAtOnce: ['the TEXT DOMAIN LOOKUP', 'the UPDATE MECHANISMS ABILITY TO FIND the PLUGIN', 'and the DIRECTORY LISTINGS IDENTIFIER'],
  },
];

/* Two things the layout is for. */
const FOR = [
  { t: 'the ROOT FILE MUST BE the ONLY THING the PLATFORM INCLUDES',
    why: 'BECAUSE A PLUGIN with TWO ENTRY POINTS WILL HAVE ONE of THEM LOADED BY the PLATFORM and ONE of THEM NOT',
    symptom: 'and the ONE THAT IS NOT IS WHERE A FUNCTION YOU DEPEND ON TURNS OUT NOT to BE DEFINED' },
  { t: 'the LAYOUT SHOULD BE the SAME IN EVERY PLUGIN YOU WRITE',
    detail: ['the SAME DIRECTORY NAMES', 'the SAME BOOTSTRAP CALL', 'and the SAME REGISTRATION ENTRY POINT'],
    gain: 'and THAT RECOGNISABILITY IS WORTH MORE THAN ANY INDIVIDUAL STRUCTURAL CHOICE',
    why: 'BECAUSE A SHARED SKELETON IS WHAT MAKES A PLUGIN YOU WROTE EIGHT MONTHS AGO RECOGNISABLE' },
];

Name the directory after the text domain. wordpress.org uses the directory slug as the plugin identifier, as the text domain for translation, and as the update package name. A mismatch between directory name, Text Domain and the Stable tag entry in readme.txt is the most common cause of a rejected translation update.

Load guards and the entry point

The entry file does three things and nothing else: declare the header, guard against direct access, and delegate. Any procedural logic in the entry file makes the plugin untestable and makes load-order problems far harder to diagnose.

A direct-access guard prevents a visitor requesting /wp-content/plugins/acme-ledger/acme-ledger.php directly from executing the file outside the WordPress bootstrap. Without it, such a request produces undefined function errors and, depending on your file, information disclosure.

if ( ! defined( 'ABSPATH' ) ) {
    exit;
}

define( 'ACME_LEDGER_VERSION', '2.4.0' );
define( 'ACME_LEDGER_FILE',    __FILE__ );
define( 'ACME_LEDGER_DIR',     plugin_dir_path( __FILE__ ) );
define( 'ACME_LEDGER_URL',     plugin_dir_url( __FILE__ ) );
define( 'ACME_LEDGER_BASENAME', plugin_basename( __FILE__ ) );

require_once ACME_LEDGER_DIR . 'includes/functions.php';
require_once ACME_LEDGER_DIR . 'includes/class-ledger.php';

// Hooks are bound on plugins_loaded, registration happens on init.
acme_ledger_bootstrap();

What the guard is for, and what it must not become

The two lines at the top of a plugin file — a check that the platform has defined its own constant, followed by an immediate exit — exist for one purpose: to stop the file being executed by anything other than WordPress. Without the guard, a direct request to the plugin file executes it with the platform absent, and if the file does anything at all at file scope — which the contract says it must not — the visitor gets a fatal error with a file path in it, which is a small information leak and an ugly error page. With the guard, the same request produces nothing, because the platform constant is undefined and the file exits before doing anything. Then the three things that make the guard work, and each is a mechanism. The constant it tests must be one the platform always defines before including a plugin — the main file path constant is the conventional choice, and testing a function's existence instead is a weaker check that some plugins get subtly wrong by testing a function they themselves define. The exit must be immediate and unconditional, with nothing before it in the file except the header comment and the namespace declaration, because anything that runs first defeats the guard. And the guard must not be the plugin's only defensive measure — it protects the file's top level, and it does nothing for a callback that runs on a request where the platform is present but the data is not what the callback expected. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Using require somewhere in the plugin instead of a once-only include, which includes a shared bootstrap twice and produces a redeclaration fatal that names a function rather than the file that caused it. Assuming the guard is enough because the plugin has one, which is the difference between protecting the entry point and validating input — the guard is the former and the input is the latter, and a plugin that conflates them has a guard and no validation. And putting work before the guard — a constant definition, a require — which runs on every direct request because the guard has not yet executed. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Treat the plugin file's first executable line as the guard, so the file's structure is obvious to a reader and to a reviewer and so nobody adds a line above it later without noticing. And use the file's own location for every path, derived from the constant the guard has just established — because a plugin that builds paths from the working directory works on a normal installation and breaks on a symlinked one, which is a development environment and therefore the environment where you will notice it.

/* The guard. */

const GUARD = {
  purpose: 'TO STOP the FILE BEING EXECUTED BY ANYTHING OTHER THAN WORDPRESS',
  withoutIt: 'A DIRECT REQUEST TO the PLUGIN FILE EXECUTES IT WITH the PLATFORM ABSENT',
  and: 'if the FILE DOES ANYTHING AT ALL AT FILE SCOPE - WHICH the CONTRACT SAYS IT MUST NOT - the VISITOR GETS A FATAL ERROR WITH A FILE PATH IN IT',
  which: 'WHICH IS A SMALL INFORMATION LEAK and AN UGLY ERROR PAGE',
  withIt: 'the SAME REQUEST PRODUCES NOTHING, BECAUSE the PLATFORM CONSTANT IS UNDEFINED and the FILE EXITS BEFORE DOING ANYTHING',
};

/* Three things that make it work. */
const MAKES_IT_WORK = [
  { c: 'the CONSTANT IT TESTS MUST BE ONE the PLATFORM ALWAYS DEFINES BEFORE INCLUDING A PLUGIN',
    conventional: 'the MAIN FILE PATH CONSTANT IS the CONVENTIONAL CHOICE',
    weaker: 'TESTING A FUNCTIONS EXISTENCE INSTEAD IS A WEAKER CHECK that SOME PLUGINS GET SUBTLY WRONG BY TESTING A FUNCTION THEY THEMSELVES DEFINE' },
  { c: 'the EXIT MUST BE IMMEDIATE and UNCONDITIONAL',
    with: 'NOTHING BEFORE IT IN the FILE EXCEPT the HEADER COMMENT and the NAMESPACE DECLARATION',
    because: 'ANYTHING THAT RUNS FIRST DEFEATS the GUARD' },
  { c: 'the GUARD MUST NOT BE the PLUGINS ONLY DEFENSIVE MEASURE',
    detail: 'IT PROTECTS the FILES TOP LEVEL, and IT DOES NOTHING FOR A CALLBACK THAT RUNS ON A REQUEST WHERE the PLATFORM IS PRESENT but the DATA IS NOT WHAT the CALLBACK EXPECTED' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'USING require SOMEWHERE IN the PLUGIN instead of A ONCE-ONLY INCLUDE',
    symptom: 'WHICH INCLUDES A SHARED BOOTSTRAP TWICE and PRODUCES A REDECLARATION FATAL THAT NAMES A FUNCTION rather than the FILE THAT CAUSED IT' },
  { f: 'ASSUMING the GUARD IS ENOUGH BECAUSE the PLUGIN HAS ONE',
    is: 'the DIFFERENCE BETWEEN PROTECTING the ENTRY POINT and VALIDATING INPUT',
    detail: 'the GUARD IS the FORMER and the INPUT IS the LATTER',
    consequence: 'and A PLUGIN THAT CONFLATES THEM HAS A GUARD and NO VALIDATION' },
  { f: 'PUTTING WORK BEFORE the GUARD', instances: ['A CONSTANT DEFINITION', 'A REQUIRE'],
    symptom: 'WHICH RUNS ON EVERY DIRECT REQUEST BECAUSE the GUARD HAS NOT YET EXECUTED' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'TREAT the PLUGIN FILES FIRST EXECUTABLE LINE AS the GUARD',
    gain: ['SO the FILES STRUCTURE IS OBVIOUS TO A READER and TO A REVIEWER',
      'and SO NOBODY ADDS A LINE ABOVE IT LATER WITHOUT NOTICING'] },
  { p: 'USE the FILES OWN LOCATION for EVERY PATH, DERIVED FROM the CONSTANT the GUARD HAS JUST ESTABLISHED',
    why: 'BECAUSE A PLUGIN THAT BUILDS PATHS FROM the WORKING DIRECTORY WORKS ON A NORMAL INSTALLATION and BREAKS ON A SYMLINKED ONE',
    and: 'WHICH IS A DEVELOPMENT ENVIRONMENT and THEREFORE the ENVIRONMENT WHERE YOU WILL NOTICE IT' },
];

Use require_once, never require. Two files that each require a shared bootstrap will include it twice. With class definitions that is a fatal redeclaration error; with function definitions it is a fatal redeclaration error. There is no recovery path from either, and the resulting white screen takes the site down for every visitor and every admin.

Namespaces, prefixes and collisions

WordPress has no global namespace protection. A function you declare with a generic name will be declared twice the day another plugin, a theme, or a future core release introduces the same name.

Two conventions coexist. The legacy convention prefixes every global symbol with the vendor slug: acme_ledger_register_post_type(). The modern convention places code in a PHP namespace and prefixes the handful of symbols that must remain global, because WordPress has no notion of a namespace when it looks up a callback name.

namespace Acme\Ledger\Admin;

final class SettingsPage {
    public function register(): void {
        add_action( 'admin_menu', array( $this, 'add_menu_page' ) );
    }

    public function add_menu_page(): void {
        add_menu_page(
            'Acme Ledger',
            'Ledger',
            'manage_options',
            'acme-ledger',
            array( $this, 'render' )
        );
    }
}

The trade-off is that array callbacks and string callbacks written in the global scope need the leading backslash to resolve: add_action( 'init', '\\Acme\\Ledger\\Ledger::boot' ). Mixing both conventions in one plugin is permitted but discouraged for review purposes.

Two mechanisms, two scopes, and why you need both

A WordPress site runs every plugin in one process in one global scope, which means every function, class and constant a plugin declares is visible to every other plugin — and a collision is a fatal error that takes down the site rather than one plugin. There are two mechanisms for avoiding that and they protect different things, which is why a plugin needs both. A namespace protects classes and functions. Declaring one puts every unqualified name in the file into it, so a class called Plugin becomes AcmeLedgerPlugin and cannot collide with another plugin's Plugin. A prefix protects constants and global functions. Constants live in the global scope regardless of namespace — a namespaced file's constant is still a global constant — so a constant needs its own unique name. Then the three details that trip people up, and each has a mechanism. A namespaced file still declares global constants, which is why the prefix requirement survives namespacing and why a constant declared inside a namespace with an unqualified name is a global constant with a short name. Array and string callbacks in the global scope need a leading separator to resolve to a namespaced callable, and mixing the two conventions in one file produces callbacks that silently do not fire on some hooks. And autoloading is not automatic for a plugin in the way it is for a project with a package manager — the plugin either requires its files or registers an autoloader, and a plugin that assumes an autoloader is present will fatal on an installation where it is not. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Using the organisation or the plugin's short name as a prefix, which collides with any other plugin from the same organisation or with a plugin that happens to share the name — the prefix must be unique across the whole site, not merely within your own work. Declaring a global function without a prefix, which is the collision in its most common form and which the platform will not warn you about because PHP cannot know the function was not meant to be global. And not checking whether a function already exists before declaring it, which produces a fatal rather than a graceful degradation; the check exists and its purpose is to let a site with a conflicting plugin load rather than crash, which is a courtesy to the user that costs one line. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Derive every prefixed name from the slug mechanically — a text slug turned into an upper-case underscore prefix, in one function at the top of the file — so that a plugin you wrote last year and one you are writing today cannot disagree about the prefix. And keep every symbol inside the namespace, including the one or two global functions the platform requires you to define, so that the set of global names your plugin declares is one short list you can read and check rather than a scattering you have to remember.

/* The two mechanisms. */

const PROBLEM = 'A WORDPRESS SITE RUNS EVERY PLUGIN IN ONE PROCESS IN ONE GLOBAL SCOPE';
const CONSEQUENCE = 'EVERY FUNCTION, CLASS and CONSTANT A PLUGIN DECLARES IS VISIBLE TO EVERY OTHER PLUGIN';
const COLLISION = 'and A COLLISION IS A FATAL ERROR THAT TAKES DOWN the SITE rather than ONE PLUGIN';

const MECHANISMS = [
  { m: 'A NAMESPACE', protects: 'CLASSES and FUNCTIONS',
    detail: 'DECLARING ONE PUTS EVERY UNQUALIFIED NAME IN the FILE INTO IT',
    example: 'SO A CLASS CALLED Plugin BECOMES AcmeLedgerPlugin and CANNOT COLLIDE with ANOTHER PLUGINS Plugin' },
  { m: 'A PREFIX', protects: 'CONSTANTS and GLOBAL FUNCTIONS',
    why: 'CONSTANTS LIVE IN the GLOBAL SCOPE REGARDLESS of NAMESPACE',
    detail: 'A NAMESPACED FILES CONSTANT IS STILL A GLOBAL CONSTANT',
    therefore: 'SO A CONSTANT NEEDS ITS OWN UNIQUE NAME' },

/* Three details that trip people up. */
const DETAILS = [

    and: 'WHY A CONSTANT DECLARED INSIDE A NAMESPACE with AN UNQUALIFIED NAME IS A GLOBAL CONSTANT with A SHORT NAME' },
  { d: 'ARRAY and STRING CALLBACKS IN the GLOBAL SCOPE NEED A LEADING SEPARATOR',
    to: 'RESOLVE TO A NAMESPACED CALLABLE',
    symptom: 'and MIXING the TWO CONVENTIONS IN ONE FILE PRODUCES CALLBACKS THAT SILENTLY DO NOT FIRE ON SOME HOOKS' },
  { d: 'AUTOLOADING IS NOT AUTOMATIC for A PLUGIN',
    contrast: 'IN the WAY IT IS for A PROJECT with A PACKAGE MANAGER',
    requirement: ['the PLUGIN EITHER REQUIRES ITS FILES', 'or REGISTERS AN AUTOLOADER'],
    symptom: 'and A PLUGIN THAT ASSUMES AN AUTOLOADER IS PRESENT WILL FATAL ON AN INSTALLATION WHERE IT IS NOT' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'USING the ORGANISATION or the PLUGINS SHORT NAME AS A PREFIX',
    symptom: 'WHICH COLLIDES WITH ANY OTHER PLUGIN FROM the SAME ORGANISATION or WITH A PLUGIN THAT HAPPENS TO SHARE the NAME',
    rule: 'the PREFIX MUST BE UNIQUE ACROSS the WHOLE SITE, NOT MERELY WITHIN YOUR OWN WORK' },
  { f: 'DECLARING A GLOBAL FUNCTION WITHOUT A PREFIX',
    isThe: 'COLLISION IN ITS MOST COMMON FORM',
    whyNoWarning: 'and WHICH the PLATFORM WILL NOT WARN YOU ABOUT BECAUSE PHP CANNOT KNOW the FUNCTION WAS NOT MEANT to BE GLOBAL' },
  { f: 'NOT CHECKING WHETHER A FUNCTION ALREADY EXISTS BEFORE DECLARING IT',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A FATAL rather than A GRACEFUL DEGRADATION',
    detail: 'the CHECK EXISTS and ITS PURPOSE IS to LET A SITE WITH A CONFLICTING PLUGIN LOAD rather than CRASH',
    and: 'WHICH IS A COURTESY TO the USER THAT COSTS ONE LINE' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'DERIVE EVERY PREFIXED NAME FROM the SLUG MECHANICALLY',
    shape: 'A TEXT SLUG TURNED INTO AN UPPER-CASE UNDERSCORE PREFIX, IN ONE FUNCTION AT the TOP of the FILE',
    gain: 'SO THAT A PLUGIN YOU WROTE LAST YEAR and ONE YOU ARE WRITING TODAY CANNOT DISAGREE ABOUT the PREFIX' },
  { p: 'KEEP EVERY SYMBOL INSIDE the NAMESPACE, INCLUDING the ONE or TWO GLOBAL FUNCTIONS the PLATFORM REQUIRES YOU TO DEFINE',
    gain: 'SO THAT the SET of GLOBAL NAMES YOUR PLUGIN DECLARES IS ONE SHORT LIST YOU CAN READ and CHECK rather than A SCATTERING YOU HAVE TO REMEMBER' },
];

Functions you must keep global. WordPress looks these up by bare name in several places, so they cannot be namespaced: functions passed to register_activation_hook and register_uninstall_hook when using a path, the callback in uninstall.php, and any function referenced by name in the database. Constants and options must be prefixed regardless.

Bundling third-party code

wordpress.org prohibits including code from a prohibited source, and a plugin must be self-contained at activation. Bundling a library is allowed; downloading it during activation is not.

Bundle your Composer dependencies into the plugin with composer install --no-dev --optimize-autoloader and commit the resulting vendor/ directory. The plugin must work on a machine that has never run Composer and has no outbound network access.

// Do not do this. It is a policy violation and it fails on locked-down hosts.
register_activation_hook( __FILE__, function(): void {
    wp_remote_get( 'https://example.com/library.zip' );
    unzip_file( $path, $dest );
} );

// Do this instead: the dependency ships with the plugin.
require_once ACME_LEDGER_DIR . 'vendor/autoload.php';

Bundling dependencies and loading assets correctly

A plugin that depends on a library has two options and the choice has consequences for updates, for the review process and for the sites that install it. Bundle it, which means committing the library's source into the plugin's vendor directory, and require it, which means declaring that the library must already be present and refusing to work without it. The trade is sharp. Bundling gives a plugin that works everywhere with no conditions, which is what almost every user expects and what the directory review expects too — a plugin that requires the user to install something else before it works is a plugin with a support burden before it has done anything. Bundling's cost is maintenance and collision: the bundled version never updates when the library does, so a security fix in the library is not your security fix until you bundle the new version, and if the library is also installed as a separate plugin you now have two copies of it in one process, which is a class of bug that is genuinely hard to diagnose because the two copies have the same class names and the autoloader picks one. Then the three rules, and each is a requirement. Never load a bundled library that is also installed as a plugin, which the standard mechanism is a version comparison and a load guard: check whether the class or function you need already exists, and only include your copy if it does not. Namespace your bundled code if it is not already namespaced, because a bundled library with global names is a bundled library that collides with everything. And keep the bundled copy in one directory with a version marker, so that updating it is a directory replacement rather than a diff and so that a reader can tell what version is shipped. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Including the library's development files — its tests, its examples, its configuration, its own dependencies — which adds weight and can produce side effects, because a test file that runs on load is a test file that runs in production. Requiring the library without checking, which produces a fatal with a class name nobody recognises when the user has not installed it, instead of an admin notice explaining what to install. And loading a bundled copy after the plugin's own autoloader has already resolved the class from the other copy, which is the collision in its most confusing form and the reason the existence check belongs before the include rather than after. Then the two things worth naming, and both are about the review process rather than about code. A bundled library is your code for licensing purposes, which means its licence must be compatible with yours and the licence file must ship with it — an incompatible licence is a takedown rather than a bug report. And the bundle is what the reviewer sees, so a directory that ships an unmaintained library with a known advisory is a rejection that could have been avoided by requiring the library instead.

/* Two options, and the trade. */

const BUNDLE = {
  means: 'COMMITTING the LIBRARYS SOURCE INTO the PLUGINS VENDOR DIRECTORY',
  gain: 'A PLUGIN THAT WORKS EVERYWHERE with NO CONDITIONS',
  expected: ['which is WHAT ALMOST EVERY USER EXPECTS', 'and WHAT the DIRECTORY REVIEW EXPECTS TOO'],
  burden: 'A PLUGIN THAT REQUIRES the USER TO INSTALL SOMETHING ELSE BEFORE IT WORKS IS A PLUGIN with A SUPPORT BURDEN BEFORE IT HAS DONE ANYTHING',
  costs: [
    { c: 'MAINTENANCE and COLLISION',
      detail: 'the BUNDLED VERSION NEVER UPDATES WHEN the LIBRARY DOES',
      consequence: 'SO A SECURITY FIX IN the LIBRARY IS NOT YOUR SECURITY FIX UNTIL YOU BUNDLE the NEW VERSION',
      and: 'and IF the LIBRARY IS ALSO INSTALLED AS A SEPARATE PLUGIN YOU NOW HAVE TWO COPIES OF IT IN ONE PROCESS',
      symptom: 'WHICH IS A CLASS of BUG THAT IS GENUINELY HARD TO DIAGNOSE BECAUSE the TWO COPIES HAVE the SAME CLASS NAMES and the AUTOLOADER PICKS ONE' },
  ],
};
const REQUIRE_IT = {
  means: 'DECLARING THAT the LIBRARY MUST ALREADY BE PRESENT and REFUSING TO WORK WITHOUT IT',
  compared: 'the TRADE IS SHARP',
};

/* Three rules. */
const RULES = [

const GO_WRONG = [

    consequence: 'WHICH ADDS WEIGHT and CAN PRODUCE SIDE EFFECTS',
    why: 'BECAUSE A TEST FILE THAT RUNS ON LOAD IS A TEST FILE THAT RUNS IN PRODUCTION' },

    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A FATAL WITH A CLASS NAME NOBODY RECOGNISES WHEN the USER HAS NOT INSTALLED IT',

  { f: 'LOADING A BUNDLED COPY AFTER the PLUGINS OWN AUTOLOADER HAS ALREADY RESOLVED the CLASS FROM the OTHER COPY',
    isThe: 'COLLISION IN ITS MOST CONFUSING FORM',

/* Two review-process matters. */
const REVIEW = [

  { t: 'the BUNDLE IS WHAT the REVIEWER SEES',

Guard optional library use. A bundled library is available whether or not you use it, but a class from a peer plugin is not. Check class_exists() for classes you did not ship, and degrade with an admin notice rather than a fatal error.

Readme, changelog and metadata hygiene

The readme.txt is the file the repository update service parses. It is not documentation for humans only; its Stable tag field is what determines whether a user receives your update.

Keep the readme headers exactly as generated by wp scaffold plugin. In particular, the changelog headings must read = 2.4.0 =, and the Stable tag must be the plain version number, not the full changelog line.

=== Acme Ledger ===
Contributors:  acmesystems
Tags:  accounting, woocommerce, ledger, invoicing
Requires at least:  6.7
Tested up to:  6.7
Requires PHP:  8.3
Stable tag:  2.4.0
License:  GPLv2 or later
License URI:  https://www.gnu.org/licenses/gpl-2.0.html

Double-entry bookkeeping primitives for WooCommerce orders.

== Changelog ==

= 2.4.0 =
* Added: batch entry import from CSV.
* Fixed: rounding drift on multi-currency lines.
* Requires PHP 8.3.

Tested up to is a claim you make and the repository verifies. Set it to the version you have actually run the test suite against, and never inflate it to chase the latest release; the version you declare is the version a reviewer opens.

What the readme actually does, and why it is not documentation

The readme file at the root of a plugin directory is read by three different audiences, and it is not primarily documentation — it is the submission to the plugin directory and the description a user reads before installing. The directory reads it for the stable tag, which is the version it will offer as an update, and this is the mechanism by which a plugin updates at all: the update check compares the installed version against the stable tag, so a release where the file's version changed but the readme's stable tag did not produces no update, and a release where the stable tag moved ahead of the file's version produces an update that installs the old code. WordPress reads it for the sections it renders on the plugin screen — the description, the installation instructions, the frequently-asked questions, the changelog and the upgrade notice — and it renders the markup, so the format is Markdown and the headings are the fields. The user reads it before deciding to install, and they read it looking for three things in order: what it does, whether it does it safely, and what it will do to their site. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. The stable tag lagging the file version, which is the mechanism behind "the plugin says it updated and nothing changed" — the files were replaced by the update mechanism with the tagged archive, so if the tag is behind, the update installs the older code. A description that is a feature list rather than a sentence, which tells a user nothing about whether the plugin does what they want, because every plugin lists features and only a sentence says what the thing is for. And a changelog that lists commits rather than changes, which is useless to a user deciding whether to update, because "fix whitespace in the readme" is not information and "fixed a fatal error when saving an entry with no date" is. Then the two things worth naming, and both are about the upgrade notice specifically. The upgrade notice is the only part of your plugin that is read by people who did not choose it — it is shown after an update to everyone, and it is where a breaking change has to be communicated or it will be discovered by users instead. And the upgrade notice should be short, dated and specific, because a long one is not read, and the reason a long one is written is usually that the author is documenting their own work rather than telling the reader what changed for them.

/* Three audiences. */

const AUDIENCES = [
  { a: 'the DIRECTORY', readsItFor: 'the STABLE TAG, WHICH IS the VERSION IT WILL OFFER AS AN UPDATE',
    isTheMechanism: true,
    detail: ['the UPDATE CHECK COMPARES the INSTALLED VERSION AGAINST the STABLE TAG',
      'SO A RELEASE WHERE the FILES VERSION CHANGED BUT the READMES STABLE TAG DID NOT PRODUCES NO UPDATE',
      'and A RELEASE WHERE the STABLE TAG MOVED AHEAD of the FILES VERSION PRODUCES AN UPDATE THAT INSTALLS the OLD CODE'],
  },
  { a: 'WORDPRESS', readsItFor: 'the SECTIONS IT RENDERS ON the PLUGIN SCREEN',
    sections: ['the DESCRIPTION', 'the INSTALLATION INSTRUCTIONS', 'the FREQUENTLY-ASKED QUESTIONS',
      'the CHANGELOG', 'and the UPGRADE NOTICE'],
    format: 'IT RENDERS the MARKUP, SO the FORMAT IS MARKDOWN and the HEADINGS ARE the FIELDS' },
  { a: 'the USER', readsItBefore: 'DECIDING TO INSTALL',
    lookingFor: ['WHAT IT DOES', 'WHETHER IT DOES IT SAFELY', 'and WHAT IT WILL DO TO THEIR SITE'],
    orderMatters: true },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'the STABLE TAG LAGGING the FILE VERSION',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND the plugin says it updated and nothing changed',
    why: 'the FILES WERE REPLACED BY the UPDATE MECHANISM WITH the TAGGED ARCHIVE, SO IF the TAG IS BEHIND, the UPDATE INSTALLS the OLDER CODE' },
  { f: 'A DESCRIPTION THAT IS A FEATURE LIST rather than A SENTENCE',
    why: 'WHICH TELLS A USER NOTHING ABOUT WHETHER the PLUGIN DOES WHAT THEY WANT',

    good: 'fixed a fatal error when saving an entry with no date IS' },
