/* The upgrade notice. */
const UPGRADE_NOTICE = {
  unique: 'IT IS the ONLY PART of YOUR PLUGIN THAT IS READ BY PEOPLE WHO DID NOT CHOOSE IT',
  when: 'IT IS SHOWN AFTER AN UPDATE to EVERYONE',
  job: 'and IT IS WHERE A BREAKING CHANGE HAS TO BE COMMUNICATED or IT WILL BE DISCOVERED BY USERS INSTEAD',
  shape: ['SHORT', 'DATED', 'SPECIFIC'],
  whyShort: 'BECAUSE A LONG ONE IS NOT READ',
  whyLong: 'and the REASON A LONG ONE IS WRITTEN IS USUALLY THAT the AUTHOR IS DOCUMENTING THEIR OWN WORK rather than TELLING the READER WHAT CHANGED FOR THEM',
};

Exercises 1

  1. Write a plugin header with every tag in the table above, then confirm each one is parsed by reading the plugin_data() return for your slug.
  2. Convert a single-file plugin into the directory layout, moving logic into includes/ and leaving the entry file with exactly four statements.
  3. Prove the fatal collision: create two plugins that each declare acme_ledger_boot() and explain precisely where in the request the failure surfaces.
  4. List every global symbol in your current plugin and verify each is either prefixed or namespaced.

2 · Hooks in Depth

Actions and filtersPriority and the ordering modelAccepted args and arityStatic and class callbacksDynamic hooks and removalHook hazards

The hook system is the only extension mechanism WordPress offers, and every other API in this guide is a thin layer over it. Understanding priority, accepted-argument propagation and callback identity precisely is the difference between a plugin that composes and one that breaks other plugins.

Actions and filters

An action announces that something happened. A filter transforms a value as it passes through. Both are stored in the same array structure; the difference is purely in what the return value means and in the obligation of the callback.

An action callback receives arguments for context and its return value is discarded by do_action(). A filter callback receives a value, must return a value, and that return value replaces the value for every subsequent callback at the same or lower priority. Returning nothing from a filter callback silently converts the value to null, which is the single most common filter bug.

// Action: notify. Return value is ignored.
do_action( 'acme_ledger_entry_created', $entry_id, $entry );

// Filter: transform. Return value replaces the argument.
$rows = apply_filters( 'acme_ledger_query_rows', $rows, $args );

Naming follows a convention that matters for ecosystem health. Use your prefix on every hook you fire or filter, put the subject first, and separate the specific event with an underscore. acme_ledger_entry_created is a good name. acme_do_thing is not.

Actions, filters, and the asymmetry between them

The two hook types are the same mechanism with different return conventions, and the convention is the whole difference. An action is a notification: callbacks are invoked and their return values are ignored. A filter is a transformation: each callback receives a value, returns a value, and the next callback receives what the previous one returned. Everything else — how they are registered, how they are prioritised, how they are removed — is identical, which is why learning one learns the other. Then the three things that follow from the asymmetry, and each has a real consequence. A filter callback must return a value on every path, because the platform substitutes the incoming value when a callback returns nothing, which means a callback with an early return and no return turns the filter into a no-op on exactly the paths where you wanted it to do something. An action's return value is discarded, which means an action cannot communicate a result and a plugin that needs a result must use a filter — this is the reason a "get my value" method on a plugin object is usually a filter and not an action. And the number of arguments matters, because the platform passes only as many as you declared: registering a callback that takes three arguments to a hook that fires with one produces a PHP error at call time, and registering one that takes one to a hook that fires with three is silently fine because the extra arguments are ignored. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Calling an action's callback list and expecting a return, which is the confusion above and which produces a plugin whose filter always returns the unmodified value. Firing an action with a payload other plugins are not expecting, which is a compatibility break: the payload is your API, and changing it breaks every extension that reads it, which is the same rule as any public interface. And using a filter for something that should be an action, which produces a chain of transformations where one was intended, and the symptom is a value that is subtly wrong and a chain that is impossible to reason about. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Fire your own hooks with a prefixed name and a documented payload, because they are the extension points other plugins will use and because a prefix is what makes them yours; and prefer an action for notification and a filter for transformation, because choosing correctly is what keeps the call graph comprehensible — an action that transforms a value is surprising and a filter that only notifies is confusing.

/* Two types, one mechanism. */

const TYPES = {
  action: { is: 'A NOTIFICATION', convention: 'CALLBACKS ARE INVOKED and THEIR RETURN VALUES ARE IGNORED' },
  filter: { is: 'A TRANSFORMATION', convention: 'EACH CALLBACK RECEIVES A VALUE, RETURNS A VALUE, and the NEXT CALLBACK RECEIVES WHAT the PREVIOUS ONE RETURNED' },
  identical: 'EVERYTHING ELSE - HOW THEY ARE REGISTERED, HOW THEY ARE PRIORITISED, HOW THEY ARE REMOVED - IS IDENTICAL',
  therefore: 'WHICH IS WHY LEARNING ONE LEARNS the OTHER',
};

/* Three consequences of the asymmetry. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A FILTER CALLBACK MUST RETURN A VALUE ON EVERY PATH',
    mechanism: 'the PLATFORM SUBSTITUTES the INCOMING VALUE WHEN A CALLBACK RETURNS NOTHING',
    symptom: 'WHICH MEANS A CALLBACK WITH AN EARLY RETURN and NO RETURN TURNS the FILTER INTO A NO-OP on EXACTLY the PATHS WHERE YOU WANTED IT to DO SOMETHING' },
  { c: 'AN ACTIONS RETURN VALUE IS DISCARDED',
    consequence: 'WHICH MEANS AN ACTION CANNOT COMMUNICATE A RESULT',
    and: 'A PLUGIN THAT NEEDS A RESULT MUST USE A FILTER',
    why: 'WHICH IS the REASON A get my value METHOD ON A PLUGIN OBJECT IS USUALLY A FILTER and NOT AN ACTION' },
  { c: 'the NUMBER of ARGUMENTS MATTERS',
    detail: 'the PLATFORM PASSES ONLY AS MANY AS YOU DECLARED',
    tooMany: 'REGISTERING A CALLBACK THAT TAKES THREE ARGUMENTS TO A HOOK THAT FIRES WITH ONE PRODUCES A PHP ERROR AT CALL TIME',
    tooFew: 'and REGISTERING ONE THAT TAKES ONE TO A HOOK THAT FIRES WITH THREE IS SILENTLY FINE BECAUSE the EXTRA ARGUMENTS ARE IGNORED' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'CALLING AN ACTIONS CALLBACK LIST and EXPECTING A RETURN',
    isThe: 'CONFUSION ABOVE',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A PLUGIN WHOSE FILTER ALWAYS RETURNS the UNMODIFIED VALUE' },
  { f: 'FIRING AN ACTION with A PAYLOAD OTHER PLUGINS ARE NOT EXPECTING',
    isAPublicInterface: true,
    consequence: 'the PAYLOAD IS YOUR API, and CHANGING IT BREAKS EVERY EXTENSION THAT READS IT',
    rule: 'WHICH IS the SAME RULE AS ANY PUBLIC INTERFACE' },
  { f: 'USING A FILTER for SOMETHING THAT SHOULD BE AN ACTION',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A CHAIN of TRANSFORMATIONS WHERE ONE WAS INTENDED',
    and: 'the SYMPTOM IS A VALUE THAT IS SUBTLY WRONG and A CHAIN THAT IS IMPOSSIBLE TO REASON ABOUT' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'FIRE YOUR OWN HOOKS with A PREFIXED NAME and A DOCUMENTED PAYLOAD',
    why: ['BECAUSE THEY ARE the EXTENSION POINTS OTHER PLUGINS WILL USE', 'and BECAUSE A PREFIX IS WHAT MAKES THEM YOURS'] },
  { p: 'PREFER AN ACTION for NOTIFICATION and A FILTER for TRANSFORMATION',
    why: 'BECAUSE CHOOSING CORRECTLY IS WHAT KEEPS the CALL GRAPH COMPREHENSIBLE',
    examples: ['AN ACTION THAT TRANSFORMS A VALUE IS SURPRISING', 'and A FILTER THAT ONLY NOTIFIES IS CONFUSING'] },
];

Prefer did_action over a boolean option. A global flag that records that an event happened cannot be reset, cannot carry arguments, and cannot be observed by anyone who registered late. did_action( 'acme_ledger_entry_created' ) answers the same question correctly.

Priority and the ordering model

Callbacks are stored per hook name in a numerically indexed array keyed by priority, and the array is walked with ksort() before execution. Insertion order breaks ties, so priority alone does not fully determine order.

The default priority is 10. Core reserves the low numbers: 1 is used for early initialisation, 9 for late dependency checks, and 10 for the large mass of ordinary callbacks. Numbers between 10 and 100 are considered reserved for plugins and themes that want to run inside core’s processing window.

PriorityReserved for
1Very early initialisation, before most callbacks are registered.
9Late dependency checks; used by WordPress to detect missing components.
10The default. Ordinary plugin and theme callbacks.
20 – 100Core handlers that must run after most third-party code.
PHP_INT_MAXDeliberate last, used by core to halt or finalise processing.

How ordering actually works, and why priority is not a schedule

A hook's callbacks run in a defined order and the definition has three parts, and the part that surprises people is that priority is a sort key rather than a delay. Callbacks are grouped by priority, from lowest to highest, and every callback at one priority runs before any callback at the next. Within a priority, callbacks run in the order they were added, which is a per-site fact that depends on which plugins are active and in what order they were activated. And the default priority is ten, so a callback added without specifying one is in a large group with every other default-priority callback on the site. Then the three things that follow, and each has a consequence. A priority is an ordering relationship, not a time. A priority of five runs before ten and a priority of twenty runs after, but there is no priority that means "after everything else" unless you pick a number above every other plugin's — which is exactly the coupling you were trying to avoid. Within a priority you have no control beyond your own code, because the order is the order the plugins were loaded, so two callbacks at ten from two plugins run in an order neither author chose. And a filter's chain means priority changes the value, not just the timing, so moving a callback from ten to twenty does not merely run it later — it runs it on a different input. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Using an extreme priority to run first, which collides with every other plugin that did the same and produces an order that depends on which of the two was activated first. Depending on a numeric gap — registering at five because something else is at ten — which works until the other plugin changes its priority, at which point the assumption breaks silently. And assuming that being early is always safe, which is false for filters: running early on a filter means receiving the unmodified input, which for a transformation that assumes a previous transformation has run is wrong. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Use the default priority unless you have a specific, articulable reason not to, because a callback at ten is compatible with every other callback at ten and a callback at five is not. And express an ordering requirement against your own hooks rather than against another plugin's — fire your own hook at the priority you want and let consumers attach to it — because that inverts the dependency and makes the ordering your problem rather than a negotiation between two authors who never spoke.

/* The ordering rule. */

const RULE = [
  { part: 'GROUPING BY PRIORITY', says: 'CALLBACKS ARE GROUPED BY PRIORITY, FROM LOWEST to HIGHEST', then: 'and EVERY CALLBACK AT ONE PRIORITY RUNS BEFORE ANY CALLBACK AT the NEXT' },
  { part: 'WITHIN A PRIORITY', says: 'CALLBACKS RUN IN the ORDER THEY WERE ADDED', isAPerSiteFact: true, because: 'WHICH DEPENDS ON WHICH PLUGINS ARE ACTIVE and IN WHAT ORDER THEY WERE ACTIVATED' },
  { part: 'the DEFAULT', value: 'TEN', consequence: 'SO A CALLBACK ADDED WITHOUT SPECIFYING ONE IS IN A LARGE GROUP WITH EVERY OTHER DEFAULT-PRIORITY CALLBACK ON the SITE' },
];

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A PRIORITY IS AN ORDERING RELATIONSHIP, NOT A TIME',
    detail: ['A PRIORITY OF FIVE RUNS BEFORE TEN and A PRIORITY of TWENTY RUNS AFTER',
      'BUT THERE IS NO PRIORITY THAT MEANS after everything else UNLESS YOU PICK A NUMBER ABOVE EVERY OTHER PLUGINS'],
    and: 'WHICH IS EXACTLY the COUPLING YOU WERE TRYING TO AVOID' },
  { c: 'WITHIN A PRIORITY YOU HAVE NO CONTROL BEYOND YOUR OWN CODE',
    why: 'BECAUSE the ORDER IS the ORDER the PLUGINS WERE LOADED',
    consequence: 'SO TWO CALLBACKS AT TEN FROM TWO PLUGINS RUN IN AN ORDER NEITHER AUTHOR CHOSE' },
  { c: 'A FILTERS CHAIN MEANS PRIORITY CHANGES the VALUE, NOT JUST the TIMING',
    detail: 'SO MOVING A CALLBACK FROM TEN to TWENTY DOES NOT MERELY RUN IT LATER - IT RUNS IT ON A DIFFERENT INPUT' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'USING AN EXTREME PRIORITY TO RUN FIRST',
    symptom: 'WHICH COLLIDES WITH EVERY OTHER PLUGIN THAT DID the SAME',
    consequence: 'and PRODUCES AN ORDER THAT DEPENDS ON WHICH of the TWO WAS ACTIVATED FIRST' },
  { f: 'DEPENDING ON A NUMERIC GAP', example: 'REGISTERING AT FIVE BECAUSE SOMETHING ELSE IS AT TEN',
    symptom: 'WHICH WORKS UNTIL the OTHER PLUGIN CHANGES ITS PRIORITY, AT WHICH POINT the ASSUMPTION BREAKS SILENTLY' },
  { f: 'ASSUMING THAT BEING EARLY IS ALWAYS SAFE', falseFor: 'FILTERS',
    why: 'RUNNING EARLY ON A FILTER MEANS RECEIVING the UNMODIFIED INPUT',
    symptom: 'WHICH FOR A TRANSFORMATION THAT ASSUMES A PREVIOUS TRANSFORMATION HAS RUN IS WRONG' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'USE the DEFAULT PRIORITY UNLESS YOU HAVE A SPECIFIC, ARTICULABLE REASON NOT TO',
    why: 'BECAUSE A CALLBACK AT TEN IS COMPATIBLE WITH EVERY OTHER CALLBACK AT TEN and A CALLBACK AT FIVE IS NOT' },
  { p: 'EXPRESS AN ORDERING REQUIREMENT AGAINST YOUR OWN HOOKS rather than AGAINST ANOTHER PLUGINS',
    how: ['FIRE YOUR OWN HOOK AT the PRIORITY YOU WANT and LET CONSUMERS ATTACH TO IT'],
    gain: 'BECAUSE THAT INVERTS the DEPENDENCY and MAKES the ORDERING YOUR PROBLEM rather than A NEGOTIATION BETWEEN TWO AUTHORS WHO NEVER SPOKE' },
];

Choosing a priority, and the four reasons to leave the default

Choosing a priority is a decision about what must happen before what, and the reason it feels harder than it is that most plugins pick numbers for reasons that are not ordering reasons. The four legitimate reasons to move off the default are narrow, and knowing them makes the decision mechanical. You must run before something that consumes your output, where "something" is your own code — the case where a filter has to normalise a value before the callback at the default priority reads it. You must run after something that produces it, where a filter must transform what an earlier callback produced and therefore has to run later. You must run before the platform does something destructive, which is the case where you need to intercept a value before a default callback mangles it. And you need to run after a third party has done its work, which is the only case involving another plugin and the only one that couples you to them. Then the three things that follow from that list, and each is a mechanism. Three of the four reasons are about your own code, which means the correct priority is often one you control entirely and can express by putting your own callback in the right order relative to your own other callback. The fourth is a negotiation, and a negotiation between two authors who have not spoken is resolved by whoever picked the more extreme number, which is not a rule. And a priority you chose once becomes a dependency the moment another plugin observes it, so a priority chosen carelessly is a compatibility risk that nobody documented. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Choosing a priority from a tutorial, which is a number that made sense in someone else's filter chain and means nothing in yours. Using a large gap between your own callbacks — ten and twenty rather than ten and eleven — which reserves space in the ordering you do not control and which makes your plugin harder to interleave with others. And removing a callback with the wrong priority, which fails silently because removal matches on priority as well as name and callback, and the callback then fires when you thought it would not — which is the same mechanism as the removal chapter's mismatch case and produces a duplicated action rather than a missing one. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Use adjacent priorities for your own ordered callbacks — ten and eleven, twenty and twenty-one — so that other plugins can slot between them, and so that the ordering you intended is still visible in the code. And document every non-default priority in the code with a comment saying what it depends on, because a bare number in a registration is a decision nobody will revisit, and a number with a reason next to it is a decision a reader can evaluate when the thing it depended on changes.

/* The four legitimate reasons to move off the default. */

const REASONS = [
  { r: 'YOU MUST RUN BEFORE SOMETHING THAT CONSUMES YOUR OUTPUT',
    whose: 'WHERE something IS YOUR OWN CODE',
    instance: 'the CASE WHERE A FILTER HAS to NORMALISE A VALUE BEFORE the CALLBACK AT the DEFAULT PRIORITY READS IT' },
  { r: 'YOU MUST RUN AFTER SOMETHING THAT PRODUCES IT',
    instance: 'WHERE A FILTER MUST TRANSFORM WHAT AN EARLIER CALLBACK PRODUCED and THEREFORE HAS to RUN LATER' },
  { r: 'YOU MUST RUN BEFORE the PLATFORM DOES SOMETHING DESTRUCTIVE',
    instance: 'WHICH IS the CASE WHERE YOU NEED TO INTERCEPT A VALUE BEFORE A DEFAULT CALLBACK MANGLES IT' },
  { r: 'YOU NEED TO RUN AFTER A THIRD PARTY HAS DONE ITS WORK',
    onlyOne: true,
    why: 'WHICH IS the ONLY CASE INVOLVING ANOTHER PLUGIN and the ONLY ONE THAT COUPLES YOU TO THEM' },
];

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'THREE of the FOUR REASONS ARE ABOUT YOUR OWN CODE',
    gain: 'WHICH MEANS the CORRECT PRIORITY IS OFTEN ONE YOU CONTROL ENTIRELY and CAN EXPRESS BY PUTTING YOUR OWN CALLBACK IN the RIGHT ORDER RELATIVE TO YOUR OWN OTHER CALLBACK' },
  { c: 'the FOURTH IS A NEGOTIATION',
    symptom: 'and A NEGOTIATION BETWEEN TWO AUTHORS WHO HAVE NOT SPOKEN IS RESOLVED BY WHOEVER PICKED the MORE EXTREME NUMBER',
    and: 'WHICH IS NOT A RULE' },
  { c: 'A PRIORITY YOU CHOSE ONCE BECOMES A DEPENDENCY the MOMENT ANOTHER PLUGIN OBSERVES IT',
    consequence: 'SO A PRIORITY CHOSEN CARELESSLY IS A COMPATIBILITY RISK THAT NOBODY DOCUMENTED' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'CHOOSING A PRIORITY FROM A TUTORIAL',
    why: 'WHICH IS A NUMBER THAT MADE SENSE in SOMEBODY ELSES FILTER CHAIN and MEANS NOTHING in YOURS' },
  { f: 'USING A LARGE GAP BETWEEN YOUR OWN CALLBACKS',
    bad: ['TEN and TWENTY rather than TEN and ELEVEN'],
    consequences: ['WHICH RESERVES SPACE IN the ORDERING YOU DO NOT CONTROL',
      'and WHICH MAKES YOUR PLUGIN HARDER TO INTERLEAVE WITH OTHERS'] },
  { f: 'REMOVING A CALLBACK WITH the WRONG PRIORITY',
    detail: 'WHICH FAILS SILENTLY BECAUSE REMOVAL MATCHES ON PRIORITY as WELL as NAME and CALLBACK',
    symptom: 'and the CALLBACK THEN FIRES WHEN YOU THOUGHT IT WOULD NOT',
    connects: 'WHICH IS the SAME MECHANISM as the REMOVAL CHAPTERS MISMATCH CASE and PRODUCES A DUPLICATED ACTION rather than A MISSING ONE' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'USE ADJACENT PRIORITIES for YOUR OWN ORDERED CALLBACKS', example: 'TEN and ELEVEN, TWENTY and TWENTY-ONE',
    gain: ['SO THAT OTHER PLUGINS CAN SLOT BETWEEN THEM', 'and SO THAT the ORDERING YOU INTENDED IS STILL VISIBLE IN the CODE'] },
  { p: 'DOCUMENT EVERY NON-DEFAULT PRIORITY IN the CODE with A COMMENT SAYING WHAT IT DEPENDS ON',
    why: 'BECAUSE A BARE NUMBER IN A REGISTRATION IS A DECISION NOBODY WILL REVISIT',
    and: 'and A NUMBER with A REASON NEXT TO IT IS A DECISION A READER CAN EVALUATE WHEN the THING IT DEPENDED ON CHANGES' },
];

Never rely on insertion order against another plugin. If two callbacks share a priority, the one registered first runs first, and registration order depends on the active_plugins ordering, which depends on activation history. If your callback must run after another plugin’s, either require that plugin and coordinate with it, or run at a documented priority and accept the documented contract.

Accepted args and arity

The fourth argument to add_action() and add_filter() caps how many arguments are passed to your callback. It exists because passing a large object graph to hundreds of callbacks that ignore it is measurable overhead.

Declare the number of arguments you actually use. Declaring 1 when you use 0 causes a fatal error under PHP 8, because a callback with no parameters cannot accept one. The default is 1.

// Correct: the callback uses no arguments, and accepts none.
add_action( 'save_post', array( $this, 'clear_cache' ), 10, 0 );

// Correct: the callback uses two of the four arguments save_post passes.
add_action( 'save_post', array( $this, 'stamp_version' ), 10, 2 );

add_action(
    'acme_ledger_entry_created',
    array( $this, 'sync_remote' ),
    20,
    2
);

Filters are stricter. A filter callback that declares fewer parameters than are passed is fine; a callback that declares more parameters than accepted_args allows is an ArgumentCountError. If you need all four arguments of a filter, you must declare accepted_args as 4, and you must return a value on every path.

add_filter( 'the_content', array( $this, 'append_notice' ), 10, 1 );

public function append_notice( string $content ): string {
    if ( ! is_singular( 'product' ) ) {
        return $content;
    }

    return $content . $this->render_notice();
}

Arity, the mismatch error, and how to accept a variable number

Every hook has a fixed number of arguments that the platform passes, and a callback declares how many of them it accepts, and the two numbers do not have to match — which produces one silent case and one loud case. A callback that accepts fewer arguments than the hook fires is fine, because the platform passes exactly what the callback declared and discards the rest; this is how a callback that only needs the second of three arguments is written. A callback that declares more arguments than the hook fires is an error at call time, and it is a PHP error about too few arguments to the function rather than anything about hooks, which makes it a genuinely confusing first experience. Then the three things that follow, and each has a mechanism. You cannot know a third party's hook's arity without reading it, so a filter callback you are registering against somebody else's hook must be written to accept the documented count, and "documented" means their documentation rather than your assumption. A callback's declared count is the contract, so changing it is a breaking change for every consumer that registered against it, which means the count belongs in the documentation the moment the hook is public. And a variadic callback is possible and is a mistake for a public hook, because it accepts anything and therefore documents nothing, and the first consumer to rely on a fourth argument has silently coupled itself to an implementation detail. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Declaring one argument for a hook that fires three, which produces a callback that works for the first argument and is wrong for the other two — and it works in testing if your test only exercises the first. Declaring three for a hook that fires one, which is the loud failure and which is at least diagnosable. And changing a public hook's arity in a major release, which is a breaking change that produces the second failure across every consumer at once, which is the same rule as any interface. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Document the arity of every hook you fire, next to its name, because the arity is the part of the contract a consumer has to match and it is invisible at the call site. And prefer a payload object to a long argument list once a hook needs more than about three arguments, because an object can gain fields without a signature change and an argument list cannot — which is the same extensibility argument as the payload rule the previous section made.

/* The two cases. */

const FEWER = {
  case: 'A CALLBACK THAT ACCEPTS FEWER ARGUMENTS THAN the HOOK FIRES IS FINE',
  mechanism: 'the PLATFORM PASSES EXACTLY WHAT the CALLBACK DECLARED and DISCARDS the REST',
  use: 'WHICH IS HOW A CALLBACK THAT ONLY NEEDS the SECOND of THREE ARGUMENTS IS WRITTEN',
};
const MORE = {
  case: 'A CALLBACK THAT DECLARES MORE ARGUMENTS THAN the HOOK FIRES IS AN ERROR AT CALL TIME',
  symptom: ['it IS A PHP ERROR ABOUT TOO FEW ARGUMENTS TO the FUNCTION rather than ANYTHING ABOUT HOOKS'],
  effect: 'WHICH MAKES IT A GENUINELY CONFUSING FIRST EXPERIENCE',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'YOU CANNOT KNOW A THIRD PARTYS HOOKS ARITY WITHOUT READING IT',
    consequence: 'SO A FILTER CALLBACK YOU ARE REGISTERING AGAINST SOMEBODY ELSES HOOK MUST BE WRITTEN TO ACCEPT the DOCUMENTED COUNT',
    and: 'AND DOCUMENTED MEANS THEIR DOCUMENTATION rather than YOUR ASSUMPTION' },
  { c: 'A CALLBACKS DECLARED COUNT IS the CONTRACT',
    consequence: 'SO CHANGING IT IS A BREAKING CHANGE for EVERY CONSUMER THAT REGISTERED AGAINST IT',
    therefore: 'WHICH MEANS the COUNT BELONGS IN the DOCUMENTATION the MOMENT the HOOK IS PUBLIC' },
  { c: 'A VARIADIC CALLBACK IS POSSIBLE and IS A MISTAKE for A PUBLIC HOOK',
    why: ['BECAUSE IT ACCEPTS ANYTHING and THEREFORE DOCUMENTS NOTHING',
      'and the FIRST CONSUMER TO RELY ON A FOURTH ARGUMENT HAS SILENTLY COUPLED ITSELF TO AN IMPLEMENTATION DETAIL'] },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'DECLARING ONE ARGUMENT for A HOOK THAT FIRES THREE',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A CALLBACK THAT WORKS FOR the FIRST ARGUMENT and IS WRONG FOR the OTHER TWO',
    and: 'and IT WORKS IN TESTING IF YOUR TEST ONLY EXERCISES the FIRST' },
  { f: 'DECLARING THREE for A HOOK THAT FIRES ONE', isThe: 'LOUD FAILURE', and: 'WHICH IS AT LEAST DIAGNOSABLE' },
  { f: 'CHANGING A PUBLIC HOOKS ARITY IN A MAJOR RELEASE',
    consequence: 'WHICH IS A BREAKING CHANGE THAT PRODUCES the SECOND FAILURE ACROSS EVERY CONSUMER AT ONCE',
    rule: 'WHICH IS the SAME RULE AS ANY INTERFACE' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'DOCUMENT the ARITY of EVERY HOOK YOU FIRE, NEXT TO ITS NAME',
    why: 'BECAUSE the ARITY IS the PART of the CONTRACT A CONSUMER HAS TO MATCH and IT IS INVISIBLE AT the CALL SITE' },
  { p: 'PREFER A PAYLOAD OBJECT TO A LONG ARGUMENT LIST ONCE A HOOK NEEDS MORE THAN ABOUT THREE ARGUMENTS',
    gain: ['BECAUSE AN OBJECT CAN GAIN FIELDS WITHOUT A SIGNATURE CHANGE and AN ARGUMENT LIST CANNOT'],
    connects: 'WHICH IS the SAME EXTENSIBILITY ARGUMENT as the PAYLOAD RULE the PREVIOUS SECTION MADE' },
];

Static and class callbacks

Use a static method wherever a callback has no instance state. It avoids retaining an object for the life of the request, and it makes the callback identity independent of the instance.

Callback identity for removal purposes is resolved by _wp_filter_build_unique_id(). A plain function name is the identity. An array callback of object, method uses the object hash followed by the method. A Class::method string uses Class::method. Two different instances of the same class produce two different identities, which matters when you attempt removal.

class Ledger {
    public function handle(): void {}

    public static function handle_static(): void {}
}

// Identifiable and removable without an instance reference.
add_action( 'init', 'Acme\\Ledger\\Ledger::handle_static' );

// Same method name, different identity, because the object differs.
$a = new Ledger();
add_action( 'init', array( $a, 'handle' ) );
add_action( 'init', array( new Ledger(), 'handle' ) );

return;

Store the instance in a property of your main class if you need to remove the callback later, and remove it by passing the identical callback value. Removal of a callback registered with a fresh new expression will not work.

Callbacks that resolve to objects, and why identity is the hard part

A hook callback can be a plain function name, an array naming a class and a method, an object and a method name, or a closure — and the platform has to be able to compare a callback you registered against the callback you are trying to remove, which is where the difficulty lives. A string callback is compared by string, so a static method name is the easiest to register and the easiest to remove. An array callback is compared by its two values, so removing one requires the same class name and the same method name. An object callback is compared by object identity, which means the object you pass to remove must be the same object instance you passed to register — and constructing a second instance of the same class to remove with it silently does nothing. That asymmetry is the mechanism behind the single most common complaint about hook removal, and it is worth knowing before you hit it. Then the three things that follow, and each is a consequence. A method array requires the method to be public, because the platform invokes it from outside the object and a private method cannot be called — and the failure is a PHP visibility error at call time rather than a registration error. A callback that is a closure cannot be removed unless you kept a reference to it, because the platform holds the only reference and there is no name to match on, which means a closure registered inside a method that runs more than once accumulates callbacks, each firing, with the symptom being duplicated output that grows every time you visit the screen. And a static method callback is resolvable by name at all times, which makes it the only form that can be removed by a plugin that did not create it — which is the reason it is the right form for anything you want other plugins to be able to detach. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Registering an object callback and removing it with a new instance of the same class, which fails silently because identity differs. Registering a closure from a method that is called on every request, which registers again on every request and produces output that grows without bound across a site — and this is the mechanism behind "my output appears twice after I reloaded the page". And using an array callback to a private method, which registers cleanly and fails at call time. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Store the object you registered in a property if you need to remove the callback, so that removal uses the identical instance — one line of storage removes an entire category of leak. And register closures once, in a bootstrap that runs once, because a bootstrap that runs once is what makes a closure safe; and where a callback must be registered per-view, register and remove it together so the counts stay balanced.

/* The four forms and how each is compared. */

const FORMS = [
  { f: 'A STRING', compared: 'BY STRING', ease: 'the EASIEST TO REGISTER and the EASIEST TO REMOVE' },
  { f: 'AN ARRAY', compared: 'BY ITS TWO VALUES', ease: 'SO REMOVING ONE REQUIRES the SAME CLASS NAME and the SAME METHOD NAME' },
  { f: 'AN OBJECT', compared: 'BY OBJECT IDENTITY',
    consequence: 'WHICH MEANS the OBJECT YOU PASS TO REMOVE MUST BE the SAME OBJECT INSTANCE YOU PASSED TO REGISTER',
    fail: 'and CONSTRUCTING A SECOND INSTANCE of the SAME CLASS TO REMOVE WITH IT SILENTLY DOES NOTHING' },
  { f: 'A CLOSURE', comparedByNothing: true,
    consequence: 'the PLATFORM HOLDS the ONLY REFERENCE and there IS NO NAME TO MATCH ON',
    therefore: 'WHICH MEANS A CLOSURE REGISTERED INSIDE A METHOD THAT RUNS MORE THAN ONCE ACCUMULATES CALLBACKS, EACH FIRING' },
];
const ASYMMETRY = 'and IT IS THE MECHANISM BEHIND the SINGLE MOST COMMON COMPLAINT ABOUT HOOK REMOVAL';

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A METHOD ARRAY REQUIRES the METHOD to BE PUBLIC',
    why: 'BECAUSE the PLATFORM INVOKES IT FROM OUTSIDE the OBJECT',
    failure: 'and A PRIVATE METHOD CANNOT BE CALLED',
    symptom: 'and the FAILURE IS A PHP VISIBILITY ERROR AT CALL TIME rather than A REGISTRATION ERROR' },
  { c: 'A CALLBACK THAT IS A CLOSURE CANNOT BE REMOVED UNLESS YOU KEPT A REFERENCE TO IT',
    symptom: 'WHICH MEANS A CLOSURE REGISTERED INSIDE A METHOD THAT RUNS MORE THAN ONCE ACCUMULATES CALLBACKS',
    and: 'WITH the SYMPTOM BEING DUPLICATED OUTPUT THAT GROWS EVERY TIME YOU VISIT the SCREEN' },
  { c: 'A STATIC METHOD CALLBACK IS RESOLVABLE BY NAME AT ALL TIMES',
    gain: 'WHICH MAKES IT the ONLY FORM THAT CAN BE REMOVED BY A PLUGIN THAT DID NOT CREATE IT',
    why: 'WHICH IS the REASON IT IS the RIGHT FORM for ANYTHING YOU WANT OTHER PLUGINS to BE ABLE TO DETACH' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'REGISTERING AN OBJECT CALLBACK and REMOVING IT WITH A NEW INSTANCE of the SAME CLASS',
    symptom: 'WHICH FAILS SILENTLY BECAUSE IDENTITY DIFFERS' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING A CLOSURE FROM A METHOD THAT IS CALLED ON EVERY REQUEST',
    detail: 'WHICH REGISTERS AGAIN ON EVERY REQUEST and PRODUCES OUTPUT THAT GROWS WITHOUT BOUND ACROSS A SITE',
    symptom: 'and THIS IS the MECHANISM BEHIND my output appears twice after I reloaded the page' },
  { f: 'USING AN ARRAY CALLBACK TO A PRIVATE METHOD',
    detail: 'WHICH REGISTERS CLEANLY and FAILS AT CALL TIME' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'STORE the OBJECT YOU REGISTERED IN A PROPERTY IF YOU NEED TO REMOVE the CALLBACK',
    gain: ['SO THAT REMOVAL USES the IDENTICAL INSTANCE',
      'ONE LINE of STORAGE REMOVES AN ENTIRE CATEGORY of LEAK'] },
  { p: 'REGISTER CLOSURES ONCE, IN A BOOTSTRAP THAT RUNS ONCE',
    why: 'BECAUSE A BOOTSTRAP THAT RUNS ONCE IS WHAT MAKES A CLOSURE SAFE',
    and: 'and WHERE A CALLBACK MUST BE REGISTERED PER-VIEW, REGISTER and REMOVE IT TOGETHER so the COUNTS STAY BALANCED' },
];

Dynamic hooks and removal

You may fire a hook whose name contains a variable, for example "acme_ledger_status_{$status}". This enables targeted extension points, but it also means the set of hook names is not knowable by static analysis, and removal requires the same string you fired.

$hook = 'acme_ledger_status_' . $status;
do_action( $hook, $entry, $status );

$value = apply_filters( $hook, $value, $entry );

// Removal uses the resolved name, not a pattern.
remove_filter( $hook, array( $this, 'adjust_value' ), 10 );
remove_action( $hook, array( $this, 'on_status' ), 10 );

Removal must match on name, callback and priority. If any one differs, the removal silently does nothing. There is no error and no return value indicating failure, which is why removing hooks is a common source of callbacks that appear to persist after a plugin is deactivated within the same request.

Hooks that are decided at run time, and the registration pattern

A dynamic hook is one whose name includes something decided when the plugin runs rather than written in the source — a post type name, an object identifier, a user-supplied key — and it is the mechanism behind almost every plugin's extensibility and behind almost every plugin's accidental coupling to another's internals. The platform fires a dynamic action by building the name and calling the corresponding function, which means the hook exists only if something fired it, and a consumer that registers against a dynamic hook the producer never fires registers a callback that silently never runs. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. A dynamic hook's name is an API and has to be documented exactly, because a consumer cannot discover it and must be told the format, which means a change to the format is a breaking change for every consumer who guessed right. The producer must fire the hook, not the consumer assume it, because a callback on a hook nobody fires is not an error and not a warning — it is a line of code that never runs, which is the hardest kind of bug to find. And the arguments a dynamic hook fires with are part of the contract, and the same arity rule from the arguments section applies with less tolerance because the consumer cannot read the producer's source. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Registering on a hook that may never fire, which is correct as a defensive measure and wrong as an assumption — the pattern is to register anyway and let it not fire, not to try to detect whether it will. Firing a dynamic hook with the object rather than its identifier, which couples every consumer to the class and prevents you from changing the class without breaking every consumer. And using a dynamic hook for something that should be a filter, which loses the chaining and the return value and leaves a consumer that transforms nothing. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Give your dynamic hook a prefix, the subject, and a fixed argument shape — a prefix so it is yours, the subject so it is sortable in a list of hooks, and a fixed shape so a consumer knows what it receives without reading your source. And fire the hook at a defined point in your own lifecycle and document that point, because a hook fired at the wrong time is a hook a consumer cannot use even when its callback is perfect.

/* What a dynamic hook is. */

const DYNAMIC = {
  is: 'A HOOK WHOSE NAME INCLUDES SOMETHING DECIDED WHEN the PLUGIN RUNS rather than WRITTEN IN the SOURCE',
  decidedBy: ['A POST TYPE NAME', 'AN OBJECT IDENTIFIER', 'A USER-SUPPLIED KEY'],
  whyItExists: 'IT IS the MECHANISM BEHIND ALMOST EVERY PLUGINS EXTENSIBILITY and BEHIND ALMOST EVERY PLUGINS ACCIDENTAL COUPLING to ANOTHERS INTERNALS',
  mechanism: 'the PLATFORM FIRES A DYNAMIC ACTION BY BUILDING the NAME and CALLING the CORRESPONDING FUNCTION',
  consequence: 'WHICH MEANS the HOOK EXISTS ONLY IF SOMETHING FIRED IT',
  and: 'A CONSUMER THAT REGISTERS AGAINST A DYNAMIC HOOK the PRODUCER NEVER FIRES REGISTERS A CALLBACK THAT SILENTLY NEVER RUNS',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A DYNAMIC HOOKS NAME IS AN API and HAS TO BE DOCUMENTED EXACTLY',
    why: 'BECAUSE A CONSUMER CANNOT DISCOVER IT and MUST BE TOLD the FORMAT',
    consequence: 'WHICH MEANS A CHANGE TO the FORMAT IS A BREAKING CHANGE for EVERY CONSUMER WHO GUESSED RIGHT' },
  { c: 'the PRODUCER MUST FIRE the HOOK, NOT the CONSUMER ASSUME IT',
    because: 'A CALLBACK ON A HOOK NOBODY FIRES IS NOT AN ERROR and NOT A WARNING - IT IS A LINE of CODE THAT NEVER RUNS',
    which: 'WHICH IS the HARDEST KIND of BUG TO FIND' },
  { c: 'the ARGUMENTS A DYNAMIC HOOK FIRES WITH ARE PART of the CONTRACT',
    rule: 'and the SAME ARITY RULE FROM the ARGUMENTS SECTION APPLIES WITH LESS TOLERANCE',
    why: 'BECAUSE the CONSUMER CANNOT READ the PRODUCERS SOURCE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'REGISTERING ON A HOOK THAT MAY NEVER FIRE',
    detail: 'WHICH IS CORRECT AS A DEFENSIVE MEASURE and WRONG AS AN ASSUMPTION',
    pattern: 'the PATTERN IS to REGISTER ANYWAY and LET IT NOT FIRE, NOT to TRY TO DETECT WHETHER IT WILL' },
  { f: 'FIRING A DYNAMIC HOOK with the OBJECT rather than ITS IDENTIFIER',
    consequence: 'WHICH COUPLES EVERY CONSUMER TO the CLASS and PREVENTS YOU FROM CHANGING the CLASS WITHOUT BREAKING EVERY CONSUMER' },
  { f: 'USING A DYNAMIC HOOK for SOMETHING THAT SHOULD BE A FILTER',
    consequence: 'WHICH LOSES the CHAINING and the RETURN VALUE and LEAVES A CONSUMER THAT TRANSFORMS NOTHING' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'GIVE YOUR DYNAMIC HOOK A PREFIX, the SUBJECT, and A FIXED ARGUMENT SHAPE',
    detail: ['A PREFIX so IT IS YOURS', 'the SUBJECT so IT IS SORTABLE IN A LIST of HOOKS',
      'and A FIXED SHAPE so A CONSUMER KNOWS WHAT IT RECEIVES WITHOUT READING YOUR SOURCE'] },
  { p: 'FIRE the HOOK AT A DEFINED POINT IN YOUR OWN LIFECYCLE and DOCUMENT THAT POINT',
    why: 'BECAUSE A HOOK FIRED AT the WRONG TIME IS A HOOK A CONSUMER CANNOT USE EVEN WHEN ITS CALLBACK IS PERFECT' },
];

Deactivation does not unload code. register_deactivation_hook runs your callback, but the file is already included and the hooks are already registered. Your callbacks remain live for the remainder of the request. This is why activation and deactivation hooks must not be used for runtime behaviour.

Hook hazards

Five failure patterns account for most hook-related bugs in production plugins. Each has a documented mitigation, and all five are avoidable by writing the callback before choosing the hook name.

HazardSymptomMitigation
Filter returns nothingContent or value becomes null downstreamType the return and return the input on every early-exit path.
Callback registered too lateCallback never firesBind at plugins_loaded, never at init or later.
Priority arithmeticCallback runs earlier than intendedPHP_INT_MAX is the last, not the first. Never use 0 to mean first; use 1.
Recursive hookStack overflow or infinite loopGuard re-entry with a static flag or an in_progress property.
Unfiltered superglobalSecurity vulnerabilityNever filter $_GET or $_POST. Read them with the input API and validate.
private bool $syncing = false;

public function on_entry_saved( int $entry_id ): void {
    if ( $this->syncing ) {
        return;
    }

    $this->syncing = true;

    try {
        $this->sync_remote( $entry_id );
    } finally {
        $this->syncing = false;
    }
}

Five hazards, and the one inspection that finds them

The hook system's failure modes are few and each of them produces a symptom that looks like something else, which is why they are worth enumerating together. The first is the callback that never fires, because it was registered against a hook nobody fires or with a name that differs by a character; the symptom is a feature that simply does not appear and no error anywhere. The second is the callback that fires twice, because it was registered twice — most often by a closure registered inside a method that runs more than once — and the symptom is output that grows each time the user visits the screen. The third is the callback that fires when you removed it, because removal matched on priority and the priority differed; the symptom is a duplicated action rather than a missing one, which is harder to attribute because the thing you removed is still there. The fourth is the filter that returns nothing, which the platform replaces with the incoming value, so the filter is a no-op on exactly the paths where you expected it to do something — and which is worst on the paths you did not test. And the fifth is the ordering assumption, which is not a runtime failure at all but a design fault: code that assumes its callback at priority five runs before another plugin's, which is true on your machine and not on anybody else's. Then the three things that go wrong in practice, and each is a mechanism. Registering hooks before the platform is ready, which means the callback is added to a hook that has already fired, so it waits for the next occurrence of that hook — which for a one-shot hook means never. Removing a callback from a different instance, which is the identity comparison the previous section described and which fails silently. And assuming a hook fires once, which is false for anything in a loop: a hook fired per item in a query fires once per item, and a callback that assumes a single invocation accumulates state or emits a message per item. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Assert your registrations in a test: after registering, ask the platform whether the callback is attached, and fail loudly if it is not, because the whole class of "it does nothing" failures becomes a test failure rather than a support ticket. And treat every hook you register as a contract you have written — name it, document it, count how many times it should fire and check that it does — because a hook registration is code that runs on somebody else's schedule and the only defence is that you have decided what it should do.

/* Five hazards. */

const HAZARDS = [
  { h: 'the CALLBACK THAT NEVER FIRES',

  { h: 'the CALLBACK THAT FIRES TWICE',
    cause: 'IT WAS REGISTERED TWICE - MOST OFTEN BY A CLOSURE REGISTERED INSIDE A METHOD THAT RUNS MORE THAN ONCE',
    symptom: 'the SYMPTOM IS OUTPUT THAT GROWS EACH TIME the USER VISITS the SCREEN' },
  { h: 'the CALLBACK THAT FIRES WHEN YOU REMOVED IT',

Exercises 2

  1. Instrument apply_filters() with a filter that logs every callback name and priority, then run a full page load and rank the callbacks by execution time.
  2. Register two callbacks at priority 10 and demonstrate that reordering the active_plugins option changes execution order without changing priority.
  3. Write a filter callback that returns the wrong type and trace the resulting error through the_content rendering.
  4. Prove that remove_action() with a mismatched priority silently fails, and show the has_action() check that would have caught it.

3 · The Plugin API

The four function signaturesRegistering hooks at plugins_loadedFiring your own hooksCore hooks a plugin consumesLifecycle and condition hooksThe current-screen and current-query context

This chapter covers the registration and firing surface in full: the exact signatures, the correct time to bind, how to expose a documented extension point, and which core hooks a production plugin actually consumes.

The four function signatures

The four signatures, and which one you actually need

The platform exposes four registration functions and the difference between them is smaller than it looks, which is worth saying precisely because choosing the wrong one produces no error. add_action and add_filter are the same function — the action form is an alias that calls the filter form — and the only difference is what happens to the return value: a filter's return value becomes the next callback's input and an action's is discarded. do_action and apply_filters are the two firing counterparts, and again the difference is only the return value. So the whole system is two functions with four names. Then the three things that follow, and each has a consequence. Using the wrong name is a documentation error rather than a runtime error, because the alias means it works — but the code reads as if the callback returns something meaningful when it does not, and the next person to maintain it will act on that belief. The removal functions follow the same rule, so a callback added with the action name is removed with either name, which is one fewer thing to get wrong. And the firing functions differ in what they return, which is the only functional difference in the four names and which matters if you are choosing between them. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Firing an action and expecting a return, which returns nothing and which is the symptom of a caller that should have used a filter. Passing arguments positionally when the hook's contract is a single object, which produces a callback receiving an object where it expected a scalar. And registering on a hook name that does not exist yet, which is legal and silent — the platform does not validate hook names — so a typo produces a callback that never fires and no warning. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Choose by return semantics, not by name: if anything downstream consumes the value, it is a filter; if it is a notification, it is an action. And register against the platform's own hooks with their documented names copied rather than typed, because the platform will not tell you a hook name is wrong and a typo is indistinguishable from a hook that does not fire for another reason.

/* Four names, two functions. */

const FUNCTIONS = [
  { f: 'add_action and add_filter', relation: 'THE SAME FUNCTION',
    detail: 'the ACTION FORM IS AN ALIAS THAT CALLS the FILTER FORM',
    onlyDifference: 'WHAT HAPPENS TO the RETURN VALUE',
    filterReturns: 'A FILTERS RETURN VALUE BECOMES the NEXT CALLBACKS INPUT',
    actionReturns: 'and AN ACTIONS IS DISCARDED' },
  { f: 'do_action and apply_filters', relation: 'the TWO FIRING COUNTERPARTS',
    onlyDifference: 'AGAIN ONLY the RETURN VALUE' },
];
const BOTTOM_LINE = 'SO the WHOLE SYSTEM IS TWO FUNCTIONS WITH FOUR NAMES';

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'USING the WRONG NAME IS A DOCUMENTATION ERROR rather than A RUNTIME ERROR',
    because: 'BECAUSE the ALIAS MEANS IT WORKS',
    but: 'the CODE READS AS IF the CALLBACK RETURNS SOMETHING MEANINGFUL WHEN IT DOES NOT',
    consequence: 'and the NEXT PERSON TO MAINTAIN IT WILL ACT ON THAT BELIEF' },
  { c: 'the REMOVAL FUNCTIONS FOLLOW the SAME RULE',
    detail: 'SO A CALLBACK ADDED WITH the ACTION NAME IS REMOVED WITH EITHER NAME',
    gain: 'WHICH IS ONE FEWER THING to GET WRONG' },
  { c: 'the FIRING FUNCTIONS DIFFER IN WHAT THEY RETURN',
    isTheOnlyFunctionalDifference: true,
    and: 'WHICH MATTERS IF YOU ARE CHOOSING BETWEEN THEM' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'FIRING AN ACTION and EXPECTING A RETURN', symptom: 'WHICH RETURNS NOTHING and WHICH IS the SYMPTOM of A CALLER THAT SHOULD HAVE USED A FILTER' },
  { f: 'PASSING ARGUMENTS POSITIONALLY WHEN the HOOKS CONTRACT IS A SINGLE OBJECT',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A CALLBACK RECEIVING AN OBJECT WHERE IT EXPECTED A SCALAR' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING ON A HOOK NAME THAT DOES NOT EXIST YET', isLegal: true, silent: true,
    detail: 'the PLATFORM DOES NOT VALIDATE HOOK NAMES',
    symptom: 'SO A TYPO PRODUCES A CALLBACK THAT NEVER FIRES and NO WARNING' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'CHOOSE BY RETURN SEMANTICS, NOT BY NAME',
    rule: 'IF ANYTHING DOWNSTREAM CONSUMES the VALUE, IT IS A FILTER; IF IT IS A NOTIFICATION, IT IS AN ACTION' },
  { p: 'REGISTER AGAINST the PLATFORMS OWN HOOKS with THEIR DOCUMENTED NAMES COPIED rather than TYPED',
    why: 'BECAUSE the PLATFORM WILL NOT TELL YOU A HOOK NAME IS WRONG',
    symptom: 'and A TYPO IS INDISTINGUISHABLE FROM A HOOK THAT DOES NOT FIRE FOR ANOTHER REASON' },
];

Four functions constitute the entire registration and firing API. Their signatures have been stable for many major releases, and knowing them precisely is a prerequisite for the rest of this guide.

function add_action( string $hook_name, callable $callback, int $priority = 10, int $accepted_args = 1 ): true;
function add_filter( string $hook_name, callable $callback, int $priority = 10, int $accepted_args = 1 ): true;
function do_action( string $hook_name, mixed ...$arg ): void;
function apply_filters( string $hook_name, mixed $value, mixed ...$arg ): mixed;

add_action and add_filter are functionally identical; both call add_filter() internally. Using add_filter for an action or add_action for a filter is a readability error only, and has no runtime consequence.

Two related inspection functions complete the surface. has_action( $hook, $callback = false ) and has_filter() return the priority at which a callback is registered, or false if it is not registered. has_filter( $hook ) with no callback returns the priority of the first callback, which is a reliable test for whether anything is listening at all.

Registering hooks at plugins_loaded

Binding every hook in one place, called from plugins_loaded at priority 10, gives you a single readable inventory of your extension points and guarantees no callback is registered after the hook it serves has already fired.

add_action( 'plugins_loaded', 'acme_ledger_bootstrap', 10, 0 );

function acme_ledger_bootstrap(): void {
    acme_ledger_load_textdomain();

    add_action( 'init', 'acme_ledger_register_content_types', 5 );
    add_action( 'init', 'acme_ledger_register_shortcodes', 5 );
    add_action( 'rest_api_init', 'acme_ledger_register_routes' );
    add_action( 'widgets_init', 'acme_ledger_register_widget' );
    add_filter( 'acme_ledger_entry_amount', 'acme_ledger_round_amount', 10, 2 );
    add_action( 'acme_ledger_entry_created', 'acme_ledger_on_entry_created', 10, 2 );
}

Registration helpers that themselves call add_action must be bound at init, not at plugins_loaded, because the registry they populate does not exist until init has run. This is the reason register_post_type() throws a doing-it-wrong notice if called too early.

Registering at plugins_loaded, and why it is the right moment

There is one hook at which a plugin should register its callbacks, and it is not the earliest one, and the reason is a consequence of what the other plugins' files have done by that point. plugins_loaded fires after every plugin's main file has been included but before init. At that moment every plugin's declarations exist — classes, functions, constants — so a plugin can depend on another plugin's code being present without depending on its load order. And it fires before init, which is the point: registering at plugins_loaded means your callbacks are in place before the platform fires any of the hooks it fires during initialisation. Then the three things that follow, and each has a mechanism. Registering at file scope is wrong because the platform is not ready, — functions you need are not defined, so a file-scope call to one is a fatal on every request rather than only on the requests you care about. Registering at init is too late for anything init itself triggers, which is the mechanism behind a plugin that registers on init and then misses its own init-fired work. And registration that itself populates a registry must be bound to the hook where that registry exists, which is the case the section's callout describes: a helper that adds callbacks to your own hooks must be added at init, not at plugins_loaded, because the registry it fills does not exist yet. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Registering at plugins_loaded a callback that calls a function another plugin defines in its own plugins_loaded handler, which works only when yours runs second and fatals when it does not. Assuming your callback will run for a hook that already fired, which is the case where you register at a hook later than the one you are registering for, and the callback waits for the next occurrence — which for a one-shot hook is never. And registering everything at the default priority, which is correct but loses the ability to reason about ordering, and the fix is not a different priority but a comment saying what the callback assumes. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Register in exactly one place, a single method whose only job is registration, because a plugin with three registration sites has three chances to register the same callback twice and no way to see them all. And make registration idempotent by construction — check whether the callback is already attached before adding it — because idempotence is the property that makes the rest of the lifecycle chapter's one-shot reasoning hold when a plugin is loaded twice by a test harness or by a plugin that includes your file.

/* Why this hook. */

const TIMING = {
  fires: 'AFTER EVERY PLUGINS MAIN FILE HAS BEEN INCLUDED but BEFORE init',
  givesYou: ['EVERY PLUGINS DECLARATIONS EXIST - CLASSES, FUNCTIONS, CONSTANTS'],
  consequence: 'SO A PLUGIN CAN DEPEND ON ANOTHER PLUGINS CODE BEING PRESENT WITHOUT DEPENDING ON ITS LOAD ORDER',
  and: 'IT FIRES BEFORE init, WHICH IS the POINT',
  why: 'REGISTERING AT plugins_loaded MEANS YOUR CALLBACKS ARE IN PLACE BEFORE the PLATFORM FIRES ANY of the HOOKS IT FIRES DURING INITIALISATION',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'REGISTERING AT FILE SCOPE IS WRONG BECAUSE the PLATFORM IS NOT READY',
    symptom: 'FUNCTIONS YOU NEED ARE NOT DEFINED, SO A FILE-SCOPE CALL TO ONE IS A FATAL ON EVERY REQUEST rather than ONLY ON the REQUESTS YOU CARE ABOUT' },
  { c: 'REGISTERING AT init IS TOO LATE for ANYTHING init ITSELF TRIGGERS',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND A PLUGIN THAT REGISTERS ON init and THEN MISSES ITS OWN init-FIRED WORK' },
  { c: 'REGISTRATION THAT ITSELF POPULATES A REGISTRY MUST BE BOUND TO the HOOK WHERE THAT REGISTRY EXISTS',
    detail: 'A HELPER THAT ADDS CALLBACKS TO YOUR OWN HOOKS MUST BE ADDED AT init, NOT AT plugins_loaded',
    because: 'BECAUSE the REGISTRY IT FILLS DOES NOT EXIST YET' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'REGISTERING AT plugins_loaded A CALLBACK THAT CALLS A FUNCTION ANOTHER PLUGIN DEFINES IN ITS OWN plugins_loaded HANDLER',
    symptom: 'WHICH WORKS ONLY WHEN YOURS RUNS SECOND and FATALS WHEN IT DOES NOT' },
  { f: 'ASSUMING YOUR CALLBACK WILL RUN FOR A HOOK THAT ALREADY FIRED',
    detail: 'WHICH IS the CASE WHERE YOU REGISTER AT A HOOK LATER THAN the ONE YOU ARE REGISTERING FOR',
    consequence: 'and the CALLBACK WAITS FOR the NEXT OCCURRENCE - WHICH for A ONE-SHOT HOOK IS NEVER' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING EVERYTHING AT the DEFAULT PRIORITY',
    isCorrect: true, butLoses: 'THE ABILITY TO REASON ABOUT ORDERING',
    fix: 'and the FIX IS NOT A DIFFERENT PRIORITY but A COMMENT SAYING WHAT the CALLBACK ASSUMES' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'REGISTER IN EXACTLY ONE PLACE',
    shape: 'A SINGLE METHOD WHOSE ONLY JOB IS REGISTRATION',
    why: 'BECAUSE A PLUGIN with THREE REGISTRATION SITES HAS THREE CHANCES TO REGISTER the SAME CALLBACK TWICE and NO WAY TO SEE THEM ALL' },
  { p: 'MAKE REGISTRATION IDEMPOTENT BY CONSTRUCTION',
    how: 'CHECK WHETHER the CALLBACK IS ALREADY ATTACHED BEFORE ADDING IT',
    gain: ['BECAUSE IDEMPOTENCE IS the PROPERTY THAT MAKES the REST of the LIFECYCLE CHAPTERS ONE-SHOT REASONING HOLD',
      'WHEN A PLUGIN IS LOADED TWICE BY A TEST HARNESS or BY A PLUGIN THAT INCLUDES YOUR FILE'] },
];

_doing_it_wrong() is not decorative. WordPress emits notices when you register content types before init, add hooks after plugins_loaded, or translate a string before the text domain is loaded. Enable WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY while developing and treat every notice as a defect, not as noise.

Firing your own hooks

Firing a hook is a promise to third parties. The name, the argument list and the timing become a public contract that you will be expected to keep across major versions.

Fire actions after the state change has completed, not before, so callbacks observe a consistent database. Fire filters on values you own, and document the accepted type in the argument position. Return the filtered value; discarding it is a defect that only surfaces when another plugin depends on the extension point.

function acme_ledger_record_entry( int $account_id, float $amount, string $memo ): int {
    global $wpdb;

    $amount = apply_filters( 'acme_ledger_pre_record_amount', $amount, $account_id, $memo );
    $memo   = apply_filters( 'acme_ledger_pre_record_memo', $memo, $account_id );

    $wpdb->insert(
        $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_ledger_entries',
        array(
            'account_id' => $account_id,
            'amount'     => $amount,
            'memo'       => $memo,
        ),
        array( '%d', '%f', '%s' )
    );

    $entry_id = ( int ) $wpdb->insert_id;

    do_action( 'acme_ledger_entry_created', $entry_id, $account_id, $amount );

    return $entry_id;
}

Firing your own hooks, and designing them as an interface

Hooks you fire yourself are the extension points other plugins will build on, and once one does, the shape of what you fire is a public interface that you cannot change. Firing an action announces that something happened; firing a filter asks the world to transform a value. The choice between them is the choice between "here is a fact" and "here is a value you may change", and it is worth being deliberate because a consumer who wants to transform a value cannot do it on an action and a consumer who only wants to know something cannot use a filter without risking changing it. Then the three things that make a hook you fire usable, and each is a requirement. The name must be prefixed, and the subject must come first, — a prefix so it is identifiable as yours and cannot collide, the subject first so a list of your hooks is sortable and readable, and the specific event separated by an underscore so the name describes what happened rather than what object it happened to. The payload must be documented, and the payload is the interface: its keys are the contract, its values are the data, and a consumer reads the keys. And the hook must be fired at a defined point, not merely somewhere in your function — because a consumer attaching to "after the entry was saved" can only attach if you fire after the save and not before it. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Firing a filter with no callback that changes the value, which returns the value unchanged and which is harmless but means the filter's existence is not tested by anything. Firing a hook inside a loop, which fires once per item and which every consumer must therefore handle in a loop-shaped way; if you mean "after all of them", that is a different hook with a different name. And firing a hook whose name includes data that changes — a post identifier in the hook name rather than in the payload — which multiplies the number of hooks and makes it impossible for a consumer to attach to the general case. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Fire a pair: a pre-filter and a post-action, so a consumer can transform the value before it is stored and observe the result after — which is the pattern that makes a plugin extensible rather than merely observable, and it is what the section's callout describes. And fire the action after the work has completed, not before, because a consumer that reacts to your hook by querying your data will find nothing if you fire first, and the resulting bug is reported as "your plugin says it saved but my listener never saw it".

/* Choosing between them. */

const CHOICE = {
  action: 'ANNOUNCES THAT SOMETHING HAPPENED',
  filter: 'ASKS the WORLD TO TRANSFORM A VALUE',
  why: 'the CHOICE BETWEEN THEM IS the CHOICE BETWEEN here is a fact AND here is a value you may change',
  deliberate: 'WHICH IS WORTH BEING DELIBERATE BECAUSE A CONSUMER WHO WANTS TO TRANSFORM A VALUE CANNOT DO IT ON AN ACTION and A CONSUMER WHO ONLY WANTS TO KNOW SOMETHING CANNOT USE A FILTER WITHOUT RISKING CHANGING IT',
};

/* Three requirements. */
const REQUIREMENTS = [
  { r: 'the NAME MUST BE PREFIXED, and the SUBJECT MUST COME FIRST',
    detail: ['A PREFIX so IT IS IDENTIFIABLE AS YOURS and CANNOT COLLIDE',
      'the SUBJECT FIRST so A LIST of YOUR HOOKS IS SORTABLE and READABLE',
      'and the SPECIFIC EVENT SEPARATED BY AN UNDERSCORE so the NAME DESCRIBES WHAT HAPPENED rather than WHAT OBJECT IT HAPPENED TO'] },
  { r: 'the PAYLOAD MUST BE DOCUMENTED',
    isTheInterface: true,
    detail: ['ITS KEYS ARE the CONTRACT', 'ITS VALUES ARE the DATA', 'and A CONSUMER READS the KEYS'] },
  { r: 'the HOOK MUST BE FIRED AT A DEFINED POINT, NOT MERELY SOMEWHERE IN YOUR FUNCTION',
    example: 'BECAUSE A CONSUMER ATTACHING TO after the entry was saved CAN ONLY ATTACH IF YOU FIRE AFTER the SAVE and NOT BEFORE IT' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'FIRING A FILTER with NO CALLBACK THAT CHANGES the VALUE',
    symptom: 'WHICH RETURNS the VALUE UNCHANGED and WHICH IS HARMLESS',
    but: 'and WHICH MEANS the FILTERS EXISTENCE IS NOT TESTED BY ANYTHING' },
  { f: 'FIRING A HOOK INSIDE A LOOP',
    detail: 'WHICH FIRES ONCE PER ITEM and WHICH EVERY CONSUMER MUST THEREFORE HANDLE IN A LOOP-SHAPED WAY',
    instead: 'IF YOU MEAN after all of them, THAT IS A DIFFERENT HOOK with A DIFFERENT NAME' },
  { f: 'FIRING A HOOK WHOSE NAME INCLUDES DATA THAT CHANGES', example: 'A POST IDENTIFIER IN the HOOK NAME rather than IN the PAYLOAD',
    consequence: 'WHICH MULTIPLIES the NUMBER of HOOKS and MAKES IT IMPOSSIBLE for A CONSUMER TO ATTACH TO the GENERAL CASE' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'FIRE A PAIR - A PRE-FILTER and A POST-ACTION',
    detail: ['SO A CONSUMER CAN TRANSFORM the VALUE BEFORE IT IS STORED', 'and OBSERVE the RESULT AFTER'],
    gain: 'WHICH IS the PATTERN THAT MAKES A PLUGIN EXTENSIBLE rather than MERELY OBSERVABLE' },
  { p: 'FIRE the ACTION AFTER the WORK HAS COMPLETED, NOT BEFORE',
    why: 'BECAUSE A CONSUMER THAT REACTS TO YOUR HOOK BY QUERYING YOUR DATA WILL FIND NOTHING IF YOU FIRE FIRST',
    symptom: 'and the RESULTING BUG IS REPORTED AS your plugin says it saved but my listener never saw it' },
];

Pre-action and post-action pairs. The pattern of acme_ledger_pre_record_amount (filter) and acme_ledger_entry_created (action) gives integrators control and notification respectively. Document the return type of every filter in the readme; integrators cannot use an extension point they cannot reason about.

Core hooks a plugin consumes

A short list of core hooks covers most plugin needs. Knowing which exists prevents the common mistake of reimplementing behaviour that core already exposes.

The core hooks a plugin consumes, grouped by what they are for

The platform fires a large number of hooks and a plugin needs perhaps a dozen of them, and the useful way to learn them is by what question they answer rather than alphabetically. Lifecycle hooks answer "when is the platform doing what". Content hooks answer "what is this thing made of". Request and conditional hooks answer "what kind of request is this and what is true right now". Admin hooks answer "is this an administration screen and what does it need". Then the three things that matter when choosing a hook, and each is a mechanism. A hook's firing point determines what state exists when your callback runs, which is the single most important fact about hook selection: a hook that fires before the post is saved does not have the saved identifier, and one that fires before the current user is determined cannot check a capability. Some hooks fire more than once per request, sometimes many times, so a callback on one of them must be written to be called repeatedly — which is the mechanism behind a callback that appends to a string and produces it four times. And some hooks fire only in a specific context, like the admin-only or REST-only variants, and registering on the wrong one means your callback never runs in the context you care about. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Hooking too late for what you need, which produces a callback that runs after the thing it wanted to influence has already happened — the mechanism behind "my filter does not change the output". Hooking a loop-shaped hook without thinking about the loop, which produces per-item side effects where one was intended. And assuming a hook exists in every version you support, which produces a callback that fires on new installations and not on old ones, and the minimum-version declaration is what makes that a supported difference rather than a bug. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Read the hook's documentation for its firing point and its argument list before writing the callback, because both are part of the contract and both are documented, and a callback written against a guessed signature is a callback that breaks on the next argument added. And group the hooks you consume by question in your own notes, because the set you actually need is small and having it in one list is what lets you see that a callback and a filter are both doing the same job twice.

/* The four groups. */

const GROUPS = [
  { g: 'LIFECYCLE HOOKS', answer: 'WHEN IS the PLATFORM DOING WHAT' },
  { g: 'CONTENT HOOKS', answer: 'WHAT IS this THING MADE OF' },
  { g: 'REQUEST and CONDITIONAL HOOKS', answer: 'WHAT KIND of REQUEST IS THIS and WHAT IS TRUE RIGHT NOW' },
  { g: 'ADMIN HOOKS', answer: 'IS THIS AN ADMINISTRATION SCREEN and WHAT DOES IT NEED' },
];
const SCALE = 'the PLATFORM FIRES A LARGE NUMBER of HOOKS and A PLUGIN NEEDS PERHAPS A DOZEN of THEM';
const LEARN_BY = 'and the USEFUL WAY TO LEARN THEM IS BY WHAT QUESTION THEY ANSWER rather than ALPHABETICALLY';

/* Three things about choosing a hook. */
const CHOOSING = [
  { c: 'A HOOKS FIRING POINT DETERMINES WHAT STATE EXISTS WHEN YOUR CALLBACK RUNS',

HookTypePurpose
initActionRegister post types, taxonomies, shortcodes, REST routes, blocks.
wp_enqueue_scriptsActionEnqueue front-end assets. Fires once per request, on demand.
admin_enqueue_scriptsActionEnqueue admin assets; receives the hook suffix as argument 1.
save_post_{$post_type}ActionRun after a specific post type is saved; receives post ID, post, update flag.
wp_insert_post_dataFilterAlter title, slug, content and status before a post is written.
the_contentFilterPost content on the front end. Return the content unchanged by default.
rest_api_initActionRegister REST routes. Fires once per REST request.
widgets_initActionRegister widgets; fires once when the widget registry is built.

There is one important asymmetry in save_post. It fires for revisions, autosaves, and for quick edits made through the bulk-edit path, and the DOING_AUTOSAVE constant is not a reliable guard for all of them. Check wp_is_post_revision() and wp_is_post_autosave() explicitly.

add_action( 'save_post_acme_entry', array( $this, 'on_save' ), 10, 3 );

public function on_save( int $post_id, WP_Post $post, bool $update ): void {
    if ( defined( 'DOING_AUTOSAVE' ) && DOING_AUTOSAVE ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( wp_is_post_revision( $post_id ) || wp_is_post_autosave( $post_id ) ) {
        return;
    }

    $this->rebuild_balances( $post_id );
}

Lifecycle and condition hooks

WordPress fires a documented set of hooks for activation, deactivation, uninstall, updates, cron and multisite. Each has an exact signature, and misuse of any of them produces silent no-ops.

HookSignatureNotes
activate_{$plugin}()Single file, admin request only. Never run on a frontend request.
deactivate_{$plugin}()Does not flush rewrite rules; you must call flush_rewrite_rules() yourself.
upgrader_process_complete( $upgrader, $hook_extra )Fires for plugin, theme and core updates. Inspect $hook_extra['type'].
deleted_post( $postid, $post )Fires for each post; prefer before_delete_post for cleanup you own.
transition_post_status( $new, $old, $post )Covers every status change, including auto-draft to draft.
wp_mail_failed( $error )WP_Error from PHPMailer. The only way to log mail failures.
add_action( 'upgrader_process_complete', array( $this, 'on_update' ), 10, 2 );

public function on_update( WP_Upgrader $upgrader, array $hook_extra ): void {
    if ( ( $hook_extra['type'] ?? '' ) !== 'plugin' ) {
        return;
    }

    $previous = ( array ) ( $hook_extra['plugins'] ?? array() );

    foreach ( $previous as $file ) {
        if ( $file === 'acme-ledger/acme-ledger.php' ) {
            $this->run_upgrade_routine();
        }
    }
}

The hooks that bracket a request, and what exists at each point

A request passes through a fixed sequence and each step has a hook, and knowing which state exists at which point is what makes a plugin's behaviour predictable rather than intermittent. Plugins are included, then plugins are loaded, then the current user is determined, then initialisation runs, then the main query runs, then the request is dispatched to the template, then the response is sent. Between each of those there is a hook, and the value of the list is that it tells you what you can rely on. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Some state exists only after a specific point, which is the whole reason the sequence matters: the current user does not exist before the user is determined, the main query does not exist before it runs, and the theme's functions do not exist before the theme is loaded. Some hooks fire once per request and some fire many times, and the ones that fire many times are the per-item and per-post ones, which is the loop hazard from the previous section. And some hooks never fire in a given context — the admin hooks never fire on a front-end request, the REST hooks never fire in a normal page load — so a plugin that registers only on an admin hook has no effect on the front end and one that registers only on a front-end hook has no effect in the admin. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Doing work at plugins_loaded that needs the current user, which runs before the user exists and produces either an anonymous user or an error, depending on the call. Doing work at init that needs the main query, which runs before the query and produces an empty result. And firing an action on shutdown and assuming it always runs, which it does not if the request was terminated by a fatal or a timeout — which is exactly the case where you would most want the cleanup to happen. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Put each piece of work at the earliest point where everything it needs exists, and no earlier, because earlier means more requests pay for it and later means it may be too late. And make anything that must always happen robust to an early termination, by writing its state to storage before the risky operation rather than after it, because a shutdown hook is not a guarantee.

/* The sequence. */

const SEQUENCE = [
  'PLUGINS ARE INCLUDED',
  'THEN PLUGINS ARE LOADED',
  'THEN the CURRENT USER IS DETERMINED',
  'THEN INITIALISATION RUNS',
  'THEN the MAIN QUERY RUNS',
  'THEN the REQUEST IS DISPATCHED TO the TEMPLATE',
  'THEN the RESPONSE IS SENT',
];
const VALUE = 'BETWEEN EACH of THOSE THERE IS A HOOK, and the VALUE of the LIST IS THAT IT TELLS YOU WHAT YOU CAN RELY ON';

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'SOME STATE EXISTS ONLY AFTER A SPECIFIC POINT',
    isTheReason: 'WHICH IS the WHOLE REASON the SEQUENCE MATTERS',
    detail: ['the CURRENT USER DOES NOT EXIST BEFORE the USER IS DETERMINED',
      'the MAIN QUERY DOES NOT EXIST BEFORE IT RUNS',
      'and the THEMES FUNCTIONS DO NOT EXIST BEFORE the THEME IS LOADED'] },
  { c: 'SOME HOOKS FIRE ONCE PER REQUEST and SOME FIRE MANY TIMES',
    manyTimes: ['the PER-ITEM HOOKS', 'the PER-POST HOOKS'],
    which: 'WHICH IS the LOOP HAZARD from the PREVIOUS SECTION' },
  { c: 'SOME HOOKS NEVER FIRE IN A GIVEN CONTEXT', instances: ['the ADMIN HOOKS NEVER FIRE ON A FRONT-END REQUEST', 'the REST HOOKS NEVER FIRE IN A NORMAL PAGE LOAD'],
    consequence: 'SO A PLUGIN THAT REGISTERS ONLY ON AN ADMIN HOOK HAS NO EFFECT ON the FRONT END and ONE THAT REGISTERS ONLY ON A FRONT-END HOOK HAS NO EFFECT IN the ADMIN' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'DOING WORK AT plugins_loaded THAT NEEDS the CURRENT USER', symptom: 'WHICH RUNS BEFORE the USER EXISTS', consequence: 'and PRODUCES EITHER AN ANONYMOUS USER or AN ERROR, DEPENDING ON the CALL' },
  { f: 'DOING WORK AT init THAT NEEDS the MAIN QUERY', symptom: 'WHICH RUNS BEFORE the QUERY and PRODUCES AN EMPTY RESULT' },
  { f: 'FIRING AN ACTION ON SHUTDOWN and ASSUMING IT ALWAYS RUNS', symptom: 'WHICH IT DOES NOT IF the REQUEST WAS TERMINATED BY A FATAL or A TIMEOUT',
    and: 'WHICH IS EXACTLY the CASE WHERE YOU WOULD MOST WANT the CLEANUP TO HAPPEN' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'PUT EACH PIECE OF WORK AT the EARLIEST POINT WHERE EVERYTHING IT NEEDS EXISTS, and NO EARLIER',
    why: 'BECAUSE EARLIER MEANS MORE REQUESTS PAY FOR IT and LATER MEANS IT MAY BE TOO LATE' },
  { p: 'MAKE ANYTHING THAT MUST ALWAYS HAPPEN ROBUST TO AN EARLY TERMINATION',
    how: 'BY WRITING ITS STATE TO STORAGE BEFORE the RISKY OPERATION rather than AFTER IT',
    because: 'BECAUSE A SHUTDOWN HOOK IS NOT A GUARANTEE' },
];

The current-screen and current-query context

Two pieces of ambient context let you condition behaviour on where your code is running. Both are request-scoped globals rather than parameters, and both must be read defensively because they are not always populated.

get_current_screen() returns a WP_Screen object in the admin, or null elsewhere. Use its id property, which is a stable identifier such as dashboard, plugins, or acme_page_acme-ledger for your own pages.

$wp_query is the main query object during a frontend request. is_main_query() and is_admin() together identify the canonical context, and the pair you almost always want is is_main_query() && ! is_admin().

public function on_enqueue(): void {
    if ( is_admin() ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( ! is_main_query() || ! is_singular( 'acme_entry' ) ) {
        return;
    }

    wp_enqueue_script( 'acme-ledger-view', ACME_LEDGER_URL . 'public/js/view.js', array(), ACME_LEDGER_VERSION, true );
}

Reading the request context, and why the globals are unsafe

A plugin often needs to know what request it is serving — which screen, which query, which user — and the platform exposes that in several ways, of which exactly one is worth using. Condition tags answer questions about the request as a set of named booleans, and there is one for almost everything: which screen, which post type, which taxonomy, whether the request is the main one, whether a query has run. They are read-only, they are available at the right point in the sequence, and they are the platform's own answer to "what kind of request is this". The superglobals answer the same questions less safely — the query string is in a superglobal, the current user is a global — and using them couples the plugin to the request representation rather than to the platform, which means a REST request, a cron run and a command-line invocation all present differently and all break the code. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. A condition tag tells you the request's shape; it does not tell you the answer to your question. "Is this the single-post screen" is not "is this the post I was asked about" — you still need the object, and the object comes from the query that ran or from a lookup by identifier, not from the condition. A request-context change fires the same hook as any other, so a plugin that listens for the post having been set is listening for a hook that can fire several times per request in the admin and is the mechanism behind a callback that runs when you expected a page load. And the context is a snapshot, not a live view, so a value read from it early is not updated when the request moves on. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Reading the current post from a global rather than from the query, which gives a different post in the admin than on the front end and in a loop than outside one. Assuming the main query has run, which is true on a front-end page load and false in an admin request, in a REST handler, and in a cron callback, and produces an empty result or an error. And registering a hook that fires per-request and treating it as a per-page event, which is the mechanism behind duplicated work in the admin and none on the front end. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Read the context from the condition tags and the query, and pass what you need into your functions as arguments, because a function that reads the request is a function that cannot be tested without a request.
