And write one entry point that works out what the request is and dispatches to it, so that the place where you make assumptions about the request is one function you can read in thirty seconds rather than scattered across every callback.

/* Three ways to ask, one worth using. */

const CONDITION_TAGS = {
  answers: 'QUESTIONS ABOUT the REQUEST AS A SET of NAMED BOOLEANS',
  covers: ['WHICH SCREEN', 'WHICH POST TYPE', 'WHICH TAXONOMY', 'WHETHER the REQUEST IS the MAIN ONE', 'WHETHER A QUERY HAS RUN'],
  properties: ['READ-ONLY', 'AVAILABLE AT the RIGHT POINT IN the SEQUENCE', 'the PLATFORMS OWN ANSWER TO what kind of REQUEST IS this'],
  use: 'THESE ARE the ONES WORTH USING',
};
const SUPERGLOBALS = {
  answers: 'the SAME QUESTIONS LESS SAFELY',
  detail: 'the QUERY STRING IS IN A SUPERGLOBAL, the CURRENT USER IS A GLOBAL',
  whyUnsafe: 'USING THEM COUPLES the PLUGIN TO the REQUEST REPRESENTATION rather than TO the PLATFORM',
  consequence: ['WHICH MEANS A REST REQUEST, A CRON RUN and A COMMAND-LINE INVOCATION ALL PRESENT DIFFERENTLY',
  'and ALL BREAK the CODE'],
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A CONDITION TAG TELLS YOU the REQUESTS SHAPE; IT DOES NOT TELL YOU the ANSWER to YOUR QUESTION',
    detail: 'is this the single-post screen IS NOT is this the post I was asked about',
    how: 'YOU STILL NEED the OBJECT, and the OBJECT COMES FROM the QUERY THAT RAN or FROM A LOOKUP BY IDENTIFIER, NOT FROM the CONDITION' },
  { c: 'A REQUEST-CONTEXT CHANGE FIRES the SAME HOOK AS ANY OTHER',
    detail: 'SO A PLUGIN THAT LISTENS FOR the POST HAVING BEEN SET IS LISTENING FOR A HOOK THAT CAN FIRE SEVERAL TIMES PER REQUEST IN the ADMIN',
    symptom: 'and IS the MECHANISM BEHIND A CALLBACK THAT RUNS WHEN YOU EXPECTED A PAGE LOAD' },
  { c: 'the CONTEXT IS A SNAPSHOT, NOT A LIVE VIEW',
    detail: 'SO A VALUE READ FROM IT EARLY IS NOT UPDATED WHEN the REQUEST MOVES ON' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'READING the CURRENT POST FROM A GLOBAL rather than FROM the QUERY',
    symptom: 'WHICH GIVES A DIFFERENT POST IN the ADMIN THAN ON the FRONT END and IN A LOOP THAN OUTSIDE ONE' },
  { f: 'ASSUMING the MAIN QUERY HAS RUN',
    trueWhen: 'TRUE ON A FRONT-END PAGE LOAD',
    falseWhen: ['IN AN ADMIN REQUEST', 'IN A REST HANDLER', 'IN A CRON CALLBACK'],
    symptom: 'and PRODUCES AN EMPTY RESULT or AN ERROR' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING A HOOK THAT FIRES PER-REQUEST and TREATING IT AS A PER-PAGE EVENT',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND DUPLICATED WORK IN the ADMIN and NONE ON the FRONT END' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'READ the CONTEXT FROM the CONDITION TAGS and the QUERY, and PASS WHAT YOU NEED INTO YOUR FUNCTIONS AS ARGUMENTS',
    why: 'BECAUSE A FUNCTION THAT READS the REQUEST IS A FUNCTION THAT CANNOT BE TESTED WITHOUT A REQUEST' },
  { p: 'WRITE ONE ENTRY POINT THAT WORKS OUT WHAT the REQUEST IS and DISPATCHES TO IT',
    gain: 'SO THAT the PLACE WHERE YOU MAKE ASSUMPTIONS ABOUT the REQUEST IS ONE FUNCTION YOU CAN READ IN THIRTY SECONDS rather than SCATTERED ACROSS EVERY CALLBACK' },
];

Do not branch on is_admin() for data logic. It tells you about the request, not about the actor. A capability check answers who, and is the only correct guard for anything that mutates state. The pair is_admin() for presentation and capability checks for behaviour is the pattern to follow throughout.

Exercises 3

  1. Write a bootstrap function that registers exactly twenty hooks and produce the corresponding integration-test list from the returned $wp_filter array.
  2. Fire a filter that accepts an object and a string and returns a string, then fire an action with the same name to demonstrate the naming collision risk.
  3. Add a pre_get_posts observer that logs every variable the main query carries at pre_get_posts time versus after parse_query.
  4. Implement on_update() and confirm it does not fire for a theme update by inspecting $hook_extra['type'].

4 · Shortcodes

Registration and the shortcode APIAttributes, defaults and typingEnclosing form and nested contentEscaping and output bufferingDynamic shortcodes and the data flowShortcode pitfalls

Shortcodes are a text-level API that survives the transition to the block editor but does not participate in it. Correct implementation means strict attribute handling, deterministic output, and escaping discipline that matches the surrounding content.

Registration and the shortcode API

Registration, the fixed signature, and why the callback shape is not yours to choose

The shortcode API is the oldest extensibility mechanism in the platform and it has one unusual property: the callback's signature is fixed by the platform and your attributes are parsed before your code runs. You register a shortcode with a tag, a callback and, optionally, a default for each attribute. When the platform finds the tag in content it parses the attributes into an array, applies your defaults for any that are absent, and calls your callback with that array plus the enclosed content and the tag. The callback always receives three arguments in that order, and the content argument is empty rather than null for a self-closing shortcode, which is a distinction that matters if you treat empty content as a meaningful value. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The attribute array is a string-keyed array with every key present — absent attributes have been filled with your defaults, so a callback never has to test whether a key exists, and a default of null and a default of the empty string are different values that produce different output. Attribute values are strings, always, because the shortcode is parsed out of post content, which is text; the callback is responsible for casting and validating, and a plugin that assumes an integer will work with the string that came out of a content editor is a plugin that breaks when somebody types a number with a space in it. And the callback's return value is substituted into the content verbatim, which means everything it returns must be escaped by the callback, because the platform does not escape it and the content is going straight into a page. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A tag that collides with another plugin's, which produces whichever callback was registered second — the platform does not warn about duplicate tags, so the second registration silently wins and the first plugin's feature disappears. A callback that echoes its attribute values unescaped, which is an injection point that works because a shortcode is authored by somebody with editing rights, but editing rights are shared and a lower-privileged author can therefore run script in a higher-privileged author's context. And assuming the tag appears once, which is false — a shortcode can appear many times in one post and the callback runs each time, so a callback that accumulates state produces output that depends on how many times the tag appears. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Prefix your tag, exactly as you prefix your hooks, because the tag namespace is global and shared with every plugin on the site. And treat the callback as a pure function of its three arguments, so that it produces the same output for the same input and can be tested by calling it directly — which is both faster than a rendering test and possible without one.

/* Registration. */

const REGISTER = {
  takes: ['A TAG', 'A CALLBACK', 'and, OPTIONALLY, A DEFAULT for EACH ATTRIBUTE'],
  flow: ['WHEN the PLATFORM FINDS the TAG IN CONTENT IT PARSES the ATTRIBUTES INTO AN ARRAY',
    'APPLIES YOUR DEFAULTS for ANY THAT ARE ABSENT',
    'and CALLS YOUR CALLBACK with THAT ARRAY PLUS the ENCLOSED CONTENT and the TAG'],
  signature: 'YOUR CALLBACK ALWAYS RECEIVES THREE ARGUMENTS IN THAT ORDER',
  contentArgument: 'and the CONTENT ARGUMENT IS EMPTY rather THAN NULL FOR A SELF-CLOSING SHORTCODE',
  whyItMatters: 'WHICH IS A DISTINCTION THAT MATTERS IF YOU TREAT EMPTY CONTENT AS A MEANINGFUL VALUE',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the ATTRIBUTE ARRAY IS A STRING-KEYED ARRAY with EVERY KEY PRESENT',
    gain: 'ABSENT ATTRIBUTES HAVE BEEN FILLED WITH YOUR DEFAULTS, SO A CALLBACK NEVER HAS TO TEST WHETHER A KEY EXISTS',
    and: 'A DEFAULT OF NULL and A DEFAULT OF the EMPTY STRING ARE DIFFERENT VALUES THAT PRODUCE DIFFERENT OUTPUT' },
  { c: 'ATTRIBUTE VALUES ARE STRINGS, ALWAYS',
    why: 'BECAUSE the SHORTCODE IS PARSED OUT OF POST CONTENT, WHICH IS TEXT',
    responsibility: 'the CALLBACK IS RESPONSIBLE for CASTING and VALIDATING',
    symptom: 'and A PLUGIN THAT ASSUMES AN INTEGER WILL WORK with the STRING THAT CAME OUT OF A CONTENT EDITOR IS A PLUGIN THAT BREAKS WHEN SOMEBODY TYPES A NUMBER with A SPACE IN IT' },
  { c: 'the CALLBACKS RETURN VALUE IS SUBSTITUTED INTO the CONTENT VERBATIM',
    consequence: 'WHICH MEANS EVERYTHING IT RETURNS MUST BE ESCAPED BY the CALLBACK',
    why: 'BECAUSE the PLATFORM DOES NOT ESCAPE IT and the CONTENT IS GOING STRAIGHT INTO A PAGE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'A TAG THAT COLLIDES with ANOTHER PLUGINS', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES WHICHEVER CALLBACK WAS REGISTERED SECOND',
    and: 'the PLATFORM DOES NOT WARN ABOUT DUPLICATE TAGS, SO the SECOND REGISTRATION SILENTLY WINS and the FIRST PLUGINS FEATURE DISAPPEARS' },
  { f: 'A CALLBACK THAT ECHOES ITS ATTRIBUTE VALUES UNESCAPED',
    is: 'AN INJECTION POINT',
    reasoning: 'WHICH WORKS BECAUSE A SHORTCODE IS AUTHORED BY SOMEBODY with EDITING RIGHTS',
    but: 'EDITING RIGHTS ARE SHARED and A LOWER-PRIVILEGED AUTHOR CAN THEREFORE RUN SCRIPT IN A HIGHER-PRIVILEGED AUTHORS CONTEXT' },
  { f: 'ASSUMING the TAG APPEARS ONCE',
    detail: 'WHICH IS FALSE - A SHORTCODE CAN APPEAR MANY TIMES IN ONE POST and the CALLBACK RUNS EACH TIME',
    symptom: 'SO A CALLBACK THAT ACCUMULATES STATE PRODUCES OUTPUT THAT DEPENDS ON HOW MANY TIMES the TAG APPEARS' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'PREFIX YOUR TAG', exactlyAs: 'YOU PREFIX YOUR HOOKS', because: 'BECAUSE the TAG NAMESPACE IS GLOBAL and SHARED WITH EVERY PLUGIN ON the SITE' },
  { p: 'TREAT the CALLBACK AS A PURE FUNCTION of ITS THREE ARGUMENTS',
    gain: ['SO THAT IT PRODUCES the SAME OUTPUT FOR the SAME INPUT', 'and CAN BE TESTED BY CALLING IT DIRECTLY'],
    and: 'WHICH IS BOTH FASTER THAN A RENDERING TEST and POSSIBLE WITHOUT ONE' },
];

A shortcode is registered on init with a name that is a bare tag, not an enclosing tag. Registration returns nothing and there is no unregister function; removal is done with remove_shortcode() using the same name.

function acme_ledger_register_shortcodes(): void {
    add_shortcode( 'acme_ledger_balance', 'acme_ledger_balance_shortcode' );
    add_shortcode( 'acme_ledger_entries', array( Ledger_Shortcode::class, 'render_entries' ) );
}

function shortcode_atts_typed(): array {
    return array(
        'account' => 0,
        'limit'   => 10,
        'format'  => 'table',
    );
}

The callback signature is fixed: function( $atts, $content = null, $tag = '' ). The $content argument is null for a self-closing shortcode, and the $tag argument is the registered name, which lets one function serve several tags.

Always call shortcode_atts( $pairs, $atts, $tag ) as the first operation. It merges defaults, casts known values, and returns only keys present in your defaults array, which strips attacker-supplied extras. Passing an unfiltered $atts array directly into a query is a common source of unrecognised parameter injection.

Attributes, defaults and typing

The parser hands your callback an array of strings. Every value is a string, including numbers, and a numeric attribute with no value arrives as an empty string. Cast explicitly and validate ranges.

function acme_ledger_balance_shortcode( array $atts, ?string $content = null, string $tag = '' ): string {
    $atts = shortcode_atts(
        array(
            'account' => 0,
            'columns' => 2,
            'title'   => '',
        ),
        $atts,
        $tag
    );

    $account_id = absint( $atts['account'] );
    $columns    = max( 1, min( 4, absint( $atts['columns'] ) ) );
    $title      = sanitize_text_field( $atts['title'] );

    if ( 0 === $account_id ) {
        return '<p>' . esc_html__( 'A valid account is required.', 'acme-ledger' ) . '</p>';
    }

    $balance = Ledger::get_balance( $account_id );

    ob_start();
    ?>
    <div class="acme-balance acme-balance--columns-<?= (int) $columns ?>">
        <?php if ( '' !== $title ) : ?>
            <h3 class="acme-balance__title"><?= esc_html( $title ) ?></h3>
        <?php endif; ?>
        
            <?= esc_html( Ledger::format_amount( $balance ) ) ?>
        
    </div>
    <?php
    return ( string ) ob_get_clean();
}

Parsing, defaults, and what a shortcode attribute really is

Shortcode attributes look like function arguments and are not: they are a bag of strings parsed out of text by a regular expression, and almost every surprise in this area follows from that one fact. Every attribute value arrives as a string, so an attribute written as a number is a string, a boolean written as an attribute name with no value is the string "true", and anything with a space must be quoted — and the quoting rules are the platform's, not PHP's. A default is a string too, which means a default of false and a default of an empty string are the same value and a callback cannot distinguish "the author wrote nothing" from "the author wrote an empty value" except by the presence of the key, which the defaults have filled. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Because the parse is textual, an unquoted attribute cannot contain a space, which is why the platform's quoting is required for anything human-readable and why a value containing a quote character needs escaping in a way that is the parser's convention rather than PHP's. Because every key is present, a callback's branching on an attribute is branching on a value the author chose, and a value that is not in your documented set should be handled explicitly rather than falling through to a default that silently does the wrong thing. And because the callback re-runs for every occurrence, an attribute-driven query runs once per occurrence — which is the mechanism behind a page with twenty tags issuing twenty identical queries. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Casting without validating, which turns the string "12abc" into the integer twelve and produces a result for input the author did not mean. Using an attribute value in a query without preparing it, which is a SQL injection reachable by anybody who can edit the content — which on most sites is not only the administrator. And trusting a default to mean "not specified", which breaks the moment the author writes the default value explicitly and the plugin behaves as though the author had chosen nothing. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Normalise and validate at the top of the callback and fail visibly, so a bad attribute produces a message in the content rather than a wrong number in a table. And document your attributes with their types, their defaults and whether they are required, because the author of a shortcode in a content editor has no way to discover any of that and will type what the name suggests.

/* What an attribute actually is. */

const REALITY = {
  notThis: 'SHORTCODE ATTRIBUTES LOOK LIKE FUNCTION ARGUMENTS and ARE NOT',
  is: 'A BAG of STRINGS PARSED OUT OF TEXT BY A REGULAR EXPRESSION',
  consequence: 'and ALMOST EVERY SURPRISE IN THIS AREA FOLLOWS FROM THAT ONE FACT',
  facts: [
    'EVERY ATTRIBUTE VALUE ARRIVES AS A STRING',
    'SO AN ATTRIBUTE WRITTEN AS A NUMBER IS A STRING',
    'A BOOLEAN WRITTEN AS AN ATTRIBUTE NAME with NO VALUE IS the STRING true',
    'and ANYTHING with A SPACE MUST BE QUOTED - and the QUOTING RULES ARE the PLATFORMS, NOT PHPs',
  ],
  defaults: 'A DEFAULT IS A STRING TOO',
  consequence2: ['WHICH MEANS A DEFAULT OF FALSE and A DEFAULT OF the EMPTY STRING ARE the SAME VALUE',
    'and A CALLBACK CANNOT DISTINGUISH the AUTHOR WROTE NOTHING FROM the AUTHOR WROTE AN EMPTY VALUE',
    'EXCEPT BY the PRESENCE OF the KEY, WHICH the DEFAULTS HAVE FILLED'],
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'BECAUSE the PARSE IS TEXTUAL, AN UNQUOTED ATTRIBUTE CANNOT CONTAIN A SPACE',
    detail: 'WHICH IS WHY the PLATFORMS QUOTING IS REQUIRED for ANYTHING HUMAN-READABLE',
    and: 'WHY A VALUE CONTAINING A QUOTE CHARACTER NEEDS ESCAPING IN A WAY THAT IS the PARSERS CONVENTION rather than PHPs' },
  { c: 'BECAUSE EVERY KEY IS PRESENT, A CALLBACKS BRANCHING ON AN ATTRIBUTE IS BRANCHING ON A VALUE the AUTHOR CHOSE',
    requirement: ['A VALUE THAT IS NOT IN YOUR DOCUMENTED SET SHOULD BE HANDLED EXPLICITLY',
      'rather than FALLING THROUGH TO A DEFAULT THAT SILENTLY DOES the WRONG THING'] },
  { c: 'BECAUSE the CALLBACK RE-RUNS for EVERY OCCURRENCE, AN ATTRIBUTE-DRIVEN QUERY RUNS ONCE PER OCCURRENCE',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND A PAGE with TWENTY TAGS ISSUING TWENTY IDENTICAL QUERIES' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'CASTING WITHOUT VALIDATING', example: 'WHICH TURNS the STRING twelve-abc INTO the INTEGER TWELVE',
    consequence: 'and PRODUCES A RESULT FOR INPUT the AUTHOR DID NOT MEAN' },
  { f: 'USING AN ATTRIBUTE VALUE IN A QUERY WITHOUT PREPARING IT',
    is: 'A SQL INJECTION',
    reachableBy: 'ANYBODY WHO CAN EDIT the CONTENT',
    and: 'WHICH ON MOST SITES IS NOT ONLY the ADMINISTRATOR' },
  { f: 'TRUSTING A DEFAULT to MEAN not specified',
    symptom: 'WHICH BREAKS the MOMENT the AUTHOR WRITES the DEFAULT VALUE EXPLICITLY and the PLUGIN BEHAVES AS THOUGH the AUTHOR HAD CHOSEN NOTHING' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'NORMALISE and VALIDATE AT the TOP of the CALLBACK and FAIL VISIBLY',
    gain: 'SO A BAD ATTRIBUTE PRODUCES A MESSAGE IN the CONTENT rather than A WRONG NUMBER IN A TABLE' },
  { p: 'DOCUMENT YOUR ATTRIBUTES with THEIR TYPES, THEIR DEFAULTS and WHETHER THEY ARE REQUIRED',
    why: 'BECAUSE the AUTHOR of A SHORTCODE IN A CONTENT EDITOR HAS NO WAY TO DISCOVER ANY of THAT and WILL TYPE WHAT the NAME SUGGESTS' },
];

Output inside a shortcode must be escaped exactly as elsewhere. The shortcode is expanded inside post content, inside widgets, and inside theme templates. A shortcode that returns raw interpolated values is a stored cross-site scripting vector whenever the underlying data is user-controlled. Escape at output, every time, and do not rely on a downstream filter to do it for you.

Enclosing form and nested content

Enclosed content, and why nesting is a depth problem

A shortcode can enclose content, which arrives as the third-relevant argument and which is where shortcodes become compositional — and it is also where the two failure modes live. Enclosed content arrives as whatever was between the opening and closing tags, with any nested shortcodes inside it left unexpanded, and the expansion of those is the callback's problem. That is the key fact: the platform does not recursively expand shortcodes for you, so a callback that outputs the enclosed content unchanged will produce literal nested tags in the page rather than the rendered thing, which is the mechanism behind "my nested shortcode shows as text". Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Nested expansion must be requested explicitly, by calling the platform's expansion function on the enclosed content, and the function's output then replaces the content in your own output. The enclosed content is untrusted text by default, because it came out of a content editor, so anything a callback does with it — trimming, parsing, extracting attributes from it — is operating on input an author supplied. And nesting depth is capped by the parser, and deeply nested shortcodes are a denial-of-service vector because expansion is recursive, so a plugin that accepts arbitrary nesting inherits a recursion cost on input it did not limit. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Not expanding the enclosed content, which produces literal shortcode text on the page and is the most common nested-shortcode bug. Wrapping the enclosed content in output without escaping it, which is an injection point and which is worse here than elsewhere because the content is by definition author-supplied and may itself contain a shortcode whose output is markup. And calling the expansion function on content the callback did not receive — on a value it constructed or fetched — which expands shortcodes in text that was never in a post and can produce output that depends on the request rather than on the content. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Escape the expanded content when you put it in an attribute, because an attribute value containing markup will be escaped by the browser's parser in the wrong place; and keep the enclosed content in the body of your output rather than in an attribute, where it needs no escaping at all. And decide what your shortcode does when the enclosed content is empty — because the platform treats a self-closing tag and an empty pair almost identically and a callback that assumes content exists will emit an empty container where the author intended nothing.

/* What enclosed content is. */

const CONTENT = {
  arrivesAs: 'WHATEVER WAS BETWEEN the OPENING and CLOSING TAGS, WITH ANY NESTED SHORTCODES INSIDE IT LEFT UNEXPANDED',
  isWhere: 'SHORTCODES BECOME COMPOSITIONAL',
  and: 'and IT IS ALSO WHERE the TWO FAILURE MODES LIVE',
  keyFact: 'the PLATFORM DOES NOT RECURSIVELY EXPAND SHORTCODES FOR YOU',
  symptom: 'SO A CALLBACK THAT OUTPUTS the ENCLOSED CONTENT UNCHANGED WILL PRODUCE LITERAL NESTED TAGS IN the PAGE rather than the RENDERED THING',
  which: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND my nested shortcode shows as text',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'NESTED EXPANSION MUST BE REQUESTED EXPLICITLY',
    how: ['BY CALLING the PLATFORMS EXPANSION FUNCTION ON the ENCLOSED CONTENT',
      'and the FUNCTIONS OUTPUT THEN REPLACES the CONTENT IN YOUR OWN OUTPUT'] },
  { c: 'the ENCLOSED CONTENT IS UNTRUSTED TEXT BY DEFAULT',
    why: 'BECAUSE IT CAME OUT OF A CONTENT EDITOR',
    consequence: 'SO ANYTHING A CALLBACK DOES WITH IT - TRIMMING, PARSING, EXTRACTING ATTRIBUTES FROM IT - IS OPERATING ON INPUT AN AUTHOR SUPPLIED' },
  { c: 'NESTING DEPTH IS CAPPED BY the PARSER',
    dos: 'and DEEPLY NESTED SHORTCODES ARE A DENIAL-OF-SERVICE VECTOR BECAUSE EXPANSION IS RECURSIVE',
    consequence: 'SO A PLUGIN THAT ACCEPTS ARBITRARY NESTING INHERITS A RECURSION COST ON INPUT IT DID NOT LIMIT' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'NOT EXPANDING the ENCLOSED CONTENT', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES LITERAL SHORTCODE TEXT ON the PAGE', rank: 'and IS the MOST COMMON NESTED-SHORTCODE BUG' },
  { f: 'WRAPPING the ENCLOSED CONTENT IN OUTPUT WITHOUT ESCAPING IT', is: 'AN INJECTION POINT',
    worse: 'WHICH IS WORSE HERE THAN ELSEWHERE BECAUSE the CONTENT IS BY DEFINITION AUTHOR-SUPPLIED and MAY ITSELF CONTAIN A SHORTCODE WHOSE OUTPUT IS MARKUP' },
  { f: 'CALLING the EXPANSION FUNCTION ON CONTENT the CALLBACK DID NOT RECEIVE', detail: 'ON A VALUE IT CONSTRUCTED or FETCHED',
    symptom: 'WHICH EXPANDS SHORTCODES IN TEXT THAT WAS NEVER IN A POST',
    and: 'and CAN PRODUCE OUTPUT THAT DEPENDS ON the REQUEST rather than ON the CONTENT' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'ESCAPE the EXPANDED CONTENT WHEN YOU PUT IT IN AN ATTRIBUTE',
    why: 'BECAUSE AN ATTRIBUTE VALUE CONTAINING MARKUP WILL BE ESCAPED BY the BROWSERS PARSER IN the WRONG PLACE',
    instead: 'and KEEP the ENCLOSED CONTENT IN the BODY of YOUR OUTPUT rather than IN AN ATTRIBUTE, WHERE IT NEEDS NO ESCAPING AT ALL' },
  { p: 'DECIDE WHAT YOUR SHORTCODE DOES WHEN the ENCLOSED CONTENT IS EMPTY',
    why: 'BECAUSE the PLATFORM TREATS A SELF-CLOSING TAG and AN EMPTY PAIR ALMOST IDENTICALLY',
    symptom: 'and A CALLBACK THAT ASSUMES CONTENT EXISTS WILL EMIT AN EMPTY CONTAINER WHERE the AUTHOR INTENDED NOTHING' },
];

A shortcode written as [tag]content[/tag] receives the inner markup in $content already processed for nested shortcodes. Your job is to decide whether to render it, filter it, or discard it.

// Author: [acme_ledger_note account="12"]Open the [acme_ledger_link]ledger[/acme_ledger_link][/acme_note]

add_shortcode( 'acme_ledger_note', 'acme_ledger_note_shortcode' );

function acme_ledger_note_shortcode( array $atts, ?string $content = null ): string {
    $atts = shortcode_atts( array( 'account' => 0, 'tone' => 'note' ), $atts );

    if ( null === $content || '' === trim( $content ) ) {
        return '';
    }

    $tone = in_array( $atts['tone'], array( 'note', 'warn' ), true )
        ? $atts['tone']
        : 'note';

    return sprintf(
        '<div class="acme-note acme-note--%1$s">%2$s</div>',
        esc_attr( $tone ),
        wp_kses_post( $content )
    );
}

Nesting depth is capped at 16 levels by the shortcode parser, and deeply nested shortcodes are a denial-of-service vector because expansion is recursive. Do not build a shortcode that accepts arbitrary shortcodes in its attributes.

Escaping and output buffering

Every value that reaches HTML goes through an escaping function matched to its context: esc_html for text, esc_attr for attributes, esc_url for URLs, esc_js for inline JavaScript strings, and wp_kses_post for post-authorised HTML.

// Context: text node.
esc_html( $memo );

// Context: HTML attribute value.
esc_attr( $title );

// Context: href/src. Also enforces allowed protocols.
?><a href="<?= esc_url( $permalink ) ?>">
    <?= esc_html( get_the_title() ) ?>
</a><?php

// Context: post content supplied by an author.
wp_kses_post( $content );

// Context: inline script. Use wp_json_encode, not esc_js, for data.
<script>
    window.acmeLedger = <?= wp_json_encode( $config ) ?>;
</script>

// Context: SQL. Prepare, never escape.
$rows = $wpdb->get_results( $wpdb->prepare( "SELECT * FROM {$table} WHERE account_id = %d", $account_id ) );

Escaping at each boundary, and why one escape function is not enough

A shortcode sits between two trust boundaries and each has its own escaping function, and using the wrong one produces either broken output or a hole. Output that goes into HTML structure must be escaped for HTML, which turns the markup-significant characters into entities so that author-supplied text cannot become tags. Output that goes into an attribute must additionally be escaped for the attribute's context, because a value inside an attribute can break out of it with a quote, and HTML escaping alone does not stop that because the entity is not what terminates the attribute — the character is. Output that goes into JavaScript, a URL or a style attribute needs its own escaping again, because each of those contexts has its own syntax with its own breakout character, and a value escaped for HTML is not safe in any of them. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Escaping is a property of the destination, not of the value, so the same string needs three different treatments for three destinations and there is no function that is correct for all of them. Escaping late is correct and escaping early is wrong, because escaping on the way into your function produces a value that is escaped twice by the time it is output and renders as visible entities. And double-escaping is a common symptom of getting the boundary wrong, so if a value shows as literal & entities in the page, the value was escaped twice rather than once. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Echoing an attribute value into an attribute without context escaping, which is a breakout: an author who types a quote and a handler name into an attribute gets script in the page. Building a URL by concatenating an attribute, which permits a scheme that is not the one you intended and is the mechanism behind a link that can point anywhere. And outputting HTML you built by concatenating author-supplied strings, which is the same hole as the first one wearing a different hat — the string that is concatenated is the author's and the markup around it is yours. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Use the platform's escaping functions and choose the one that matches the destination, because they handle the character sets and the double-encoding cases that a hand-written replacement will get wrong. And build your output with a template that escapes by default rather than assembling it as strings, because a template that escapes unless told otherwise makes the unsafe case the exception you have to notice, and string concatenation makes it the default.

/* Two trust boundaries, three destinations. */

const BOUNDARIES = [
  { b: 'HTML STRUCTURE', needs: 'ESCAPING for HTML', does: 'TURNS the MARKUP-SIGNIFICANT CHARACTERS INTO ENTITIES SO THAT AUTHOR-SUPPLIED TEXT CANNOT BECOME TAGS' },
  { b: 'AN ATTRIBUTE', needs: 'ADDITIONALLY, ESCAPING for the ATTRIBUTES CONTEXT',
    why: 'BECAUSE A VALUE INSIDE AN ATTRIBUTE CAN BREAK OUT OF IT with A QUOTE',
    and: 'HTML ESCAPING ALONE DOES NOT STOP THAT BECAUSE the ENTITY IS NOT WHAT TERMINATES the ATTRIBUTE - the CHARACTER IS' },
  { b: 'JAVASCRIPT, A URL or A STYLE ATTRIBUTE', needs: 'ITS OWN ESCAPING AGAIN',
    why: 'BECAUSE EACH of THOSE CONTEXTS HAS ITS OWN SYNTAX with ITS OWN BREAKOUT CHARACTER',
    and: 'A VALUE ESCAPED for HTML IS NOT SAFE IN ANY of THEM' },
];

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'ESCAPING IS A PROPERTY of the DESTINATION, NOT of the VALUE',
    consequence: ['SO the SAME STRING NEEDS THREE DIFFERENT TREATMENTS for THREE DESTINATIONS',
      'and THERE IS NO FUNCTION THAT IS CORRECT for ALL of THEM'] },
  { c: 'ESCAPING LATE IS CORRECT and ESCAPING EARLY IS WRONG',
    why: 'BECAUSE ESCAPING ON the WAY INTO YOUR FUNCTION PRODUCES A VALUE THAT IS ESCAPED TWICE BY the TIME IT IS OUTPUT and RENDERS AS VISIBLE ENTITIES' },
  { c: 'DOUBLE-ESCAPING IS A COMMON SYMPTOM of GETTING the BOUNDARY WRONG',
    diagnosis: 'SO IF A VALUE SHOWS AS LITERAL ENTITIES IN the PAGE, the VALUE WAS ESCAPED TWICE rather than ONCE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'ECHOING AN ATTRIBUTE VALUE INTO AN ATTRIBUTE WITHOUT CONTEXT ESCAPING', is: 'A BREAKOUT',
    exploit: 'AN AUTHOR WHO TYPES A QUOTE and A HANDLER NAME INTO AN ATTRIBUTE GETS SCRIPT IN the PAGE' },
  { f: 'BUILDING A URL BY CONCATENATING AN ATTRIBUTE',
    consequence: 'WHICH PERMITS A SCHEME THAT IS NOT the ONE YOU INTENDED',
    symptom: 'and IS the MECHANISM BEHIND A LINK THAT CAN POINT ANYWHERE' },
  { f: 'OUTPUTTING HTML YOU BUILT BY CONCATENATING AUTHOR-SUPPLIED STRINGS',
    detail: 'WHICH IS the SAME HOLE as the FIRST ONE WEARING A DIFFERENT HAT',
    why: 'the STRING THAT IS CONCATENATED IS the AUTHORS and the MARKUP AROUND IT IS YOURS' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'USE the PLATFORMS ESCAPING FUNCTIONS and CHOOSE the ONE THAT MATCHES the DESTINATION',
    why: 'BECAUSE THEY HANDLE the CHARACTER SETS and the DOUBLE-ENCODING CASES that A HAND-WRITTEN REPLACEMENT WILL GET WRONG' },
  { p: 'BUILD YOUR OUTPUT with A TEMPLATE THAT ESCAPES BY DEFAULT rather than ASSEMBLING IT AS STRINGS',
    gain: 'BECAUSE A TEMPLATE THAT ESCAPES UNLESS TOLD OTHERWISE MAKES the UNSAFE CASE the EXCEPTION YOU HAVE TO NOTICE',
    and: 'and STRING CONCATENATION MAKES IT the DEFAULT' },
];

Pass arrays to sprintf, never concatenation. The %1$s and %2$s forms with an argument array guarantee each escaped value lands in exactly the position you intended, and they are immune to the %-in-value problem that breaks naive concatenation into str_replace.

Dynamic shortcodes and the data flow

Dynamic shortcodes, and where the cache invalidation has to hook in

A dynamic shortcode computes its output at the moment the page is rendered rather than from data already in the post, and that single difference turns a simple API into one with a caching obligation. The platform does not know your shortcode's output depends on data. It knows the post content, and a page is assembled from post content; if the output of your shortcode was computed when the page was last built, a change to your data does not appear until something rebuilds the page. So a dynamic shortcode must declare its own dependencies and arrange for the page to be rebuilt when they change — and that arrangement is not automatic, because the page cache is the platform's and your data is not. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. A dynamic shortcode's output is not stored in the post, so there is nothing to invalidate the page cache from — the mechanism is to tell the platform that this post depends on your data, and to trigger a rebuild of the affected pages when that data changes. A shortcode's data dependency must be enumerable, because you have to be able to name which posts are affected when a record changes, and a query that depends on "the current date" or "the most recent three posts" is not enumerable in the way the invalidation mechanism requires. And the cache-busting is a notification, not a guarantee, — the platform will rebuild the page on the next request, so the change becomes visible on the second visitor rather than the first, and an application that needs the change visible immediately has to purge rather than notify. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A dynamic shortcode with no invalidation, which produces stale output that users report as "my change is not showing" and which is invisible in development because there is no cache there. Registering a cache dependency on every post, which makes every change rebuild every page, which is a denial of service against your own site rather than against anybody else's. And assuming the page cache knows about your data, which is the same first failure stated as a belief. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Register the dependency narrowly and at write time, naming the post types and the identifier of the record that changed, because the cost of invalidation is proportional to its breadth and a narrow registration is cheap. And keep the computed value in your own table with the post's identifier, so that the invalidation question becomes "is the cached copy for this post still current" — a question you can answer — rather than "does the page cache know", which is a question about the platform's internals you cannot.

/* What makes it different. */

const DYNAMIC = {
  is: 'COMPUTES ITS OUTPUT AT the MOMENT the PAGE IS RENDERED rather than FROM DATA ALREADY IN the POST',
  consequence: 'THAT SINGLE DIFFERENCE TURNS A SIMPLE API INTO ONE with A CACHING OBLIGATION',
  why: ['the PLATFORM DOES NOT KNOW your SHORTCODES OUTPUT DEPENDS ON DATA',
    'IT KNOWS the POST CONTENT, and A PAGE IS ASSEMBLED FROM POST CONTENT',
    'SO IF the OUTPUT of YOUR SHORTCODE WAS COMPUTED WHEN the PAGE WAS LAST BUILT, A CHANGE TO YOUR DATA DOES NOT APPEAR UNTIL SOMETHING REBUILDS the PAGE'],
  therefore: 'A DYNAMIC SHORTCODE MUST DECLARE ITS OWN DEPENDENCIES and ARRANGE FOR the PAGE to BE REBUILT WHEN THEY CHANGE',
  and: 'and THAT ARRANGEMENT IS NOT AUTOMATIC, BECAUSE the PAGE CACHE IS the PLATFORMS and YOUR DATA IS NOT',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A DYNAMIC SHORTCODES OUTPUT IS NOT STORED IN the POST',
    consequence: 'SO THERE IS NOTHING TO INVALIDATE the PAGE CACHE FROM',
    mechanism: ['the MECHANISM IS to TELL the PLATFORM THAT this POST DEPENDS ON YOUR DATA',
      'and to TRIGGER A REBUILD of the AFFECTED PAGES WHEN THAT DATA CHANGES'] },
  { c: 'A SHORTCODES DATA DEPENDENCY MUST BE ENUMERABLE',
    why: 'BECAUSE YOU HAVE TO BE ABLE TO NAME WHICH POSTS ARE AFFECTED WHEN A RECORD CHANGES',
    notEnumerable: ['the CURRENT DATE', 'the MOST RECENT THREE POSTS'],
    and: 'WHICH ARE NOT ENUMERABLE IN the WAY the INVALIDATION MECHANISM REQUIRES' },
  { c: 'the CACHE-BUSTING IS A NOTIFICATION, NOT A GUARANTEE',
    detail: 'the PLATFORM WILL REBUILD the PAGE ON the NEXT REQUEST',
    consequence: 'SO the CHANGE BECOMES VISIBLE ON the SECOND VISITOR rather than the FIRST',
    and: 'AN APPLICATION THAT NEEDS the CHANGE VISIBLE IMMEDIATELY HAS TO PURGE rather than NOTIFY' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'A DYNAMIC SHORTCODE with NO INVALIDATION',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES STALE OUTPUT THAT USERS REPORT AS my change is not showing',
    invisible: 'and WHICH IS INVISIBLE IN DEVELOPMENT BECAUSE THERE IS NO CACHE THERE' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING A CACHE DEPENDENCY ON EVERY POST', notEveryPostButBroad: true,
    consequence: 'WHICH MAKES EVERY CHANGE REBUILD EVERY PAGE',
    detail: 'WHICH IS A DENIAL of SERVICE AGAINST YOUR OWN SITE rather than AGAINST ANYBODY ELSES' },
  { f: 'ASSUMING the PAGE CACHE KNOWS ABOUT YOUR DATA', detail: 'WHICH IS the SAME FIRST FAILURE STATED AS A BELIEF' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'REGISTER the DEPENDENCY NARROWLY and AT WRITE TIME', how: 'NAMING the POST TYPES and the IDENTIFIER of the RECORD THAT CHANGED',
    why: 'BECAUSE the COST of INVALIDATION IS PROPORTIONAL TO ITS BREADTH and A NARROW REGISTRATION IS CHEAP' },
  { p: 'KEEP the COMPUTED VALUE IN YOUR OWN TABLE with the POSTS IDENTIFIER',
    gain: ['SO THAT the INVALIDATION QUESTION BECOMES is the CACHED COPY FOR this POST STILL CURRENT, A QUESTION YOU CAN ANSWER',
      'rather than DOES the PAGE CACHE KNOW, WHICH IS A QUESTION ABOUT the PLATFORMS INTERNALS YOU CANNOT'] },
];

A dynamic shortcode resolves its argument to data at request time rather than at save time. This is the correct design for anything that must reflect current state, and it interacts with caching in ways that must be understood.

A static shortcode stores its output in post_content the first time it is rendered. If your shortcode is dynamic, the stored post content still contains the shortcode tag, and the output is regenerated on every request. When a page is cached, the cached HTML is stale until the cache is purged. Purge on the events that change the data, using a filter on acme_ledger_entry_created or on save_post_acme_entry.

// A dynamic shortcode: resolved at render time, safe to cache with a purge hook.
add_shortcode( 'acme_ledger_balance', 'acme_ledger_balance_shortcode' );

// The editor cannot preview dynamic output. Supply a server-side preview.
add_shortcode( 'acme_ledger_balance', 'acme_ledger_balance_shortcode', 10 );
add_filter(
    'no-shortcode-content',
    static function(): bool {
        return false;
    }
);

// Purge the page cache whenever an entry changes.
add_action(
    'save_post_acme_entry',
    function(): void {
        if ( function_exists( 'wp_cache_flush' ) ) {
            function_exists( 'rocket_clean_domain' ) && rocket_clean_domain();
        }
    }
);

Shortcode pitfalls

Six documented failure modes account for the majority of shortcode bugs in the plugin directory. Each is cheap to avoid and expensive to discover after release.

PitfallSymptomFix
Skipping shortcode_attsUnknown attributes pass through to a queryAlways call it first; it whitelists keys.
Unescaped interpolationStored XSS in post contentEscape by output context at the point of output.
Returning nullSilent empty output, hard to debugDeclare a string return type and return ''.
Static outputStale data after the first renderReturn fresh HTML each time; do not rewrite post content.
Missing init registrationShortcode appears literally in contentRegister on init, not at file scope.
Query inside a shortcodeUnbounded memory on a loop pageCache per request in a static, and cap the limit attribute.
private static ?array $cache = null;

public static function render_entries( array $atts, ?string $content = null, string $tag = '' ): string {
    $atts = shortcode_atts( array( 'account' => 0, 'limit' => 5 ), $atts, $tag );

    $account_id = absint( $atts['account'] );
    $limit      = min( 50, max( 1, absint( $atts['limit'] ) ) );
    $key        = "{$account_id}:{$limit}";

    if ( isset( self::$cache[ $key ] ) ) {
        return self::$cache[ $key ];
    }

    self::$cache[ $key ] = '';

    return self::$cache[ $key ];
}

The six pitfalls, and the one that produces the rest

Shortcode pitfalls are few, they repeat, and most of them share one root cause: a shortcode is a function that runs during page rendering, and everything about rendering makes things that were fine at write time wrong. The value in enumerating them is that each has a distinctive symptom, so a report about shortcodes is usually diagnostic. Then the six, and each with its symptom. Unescaped output, which shows as broken markup or as somebody else's HTML in your output. An unvalidated attribute used in a query, which shows as an error on unusual input or as a security report. A tag collision, which shows as your feature disappearing when somebody installs a second plugin. Assuming the tag appears once, which shows as output that depends on how many times an author used it. A dynamic shortcode with no invalidation, which shows as stale data that refreshes on its own schedule. And output that depends on the request in a way the cache cannot see — a shortcode that renders differently for a logged-in user, or that embeds a timestamp — which shows as one user seeing something different from another and neither believing the other. Then the three that go wrong in a way the others cause, and each is a mechanism. A shortcode that queries in a loop over post content, which produces a page whose query count scales with its tag count and which is invisible until a post with fifty tags exists. A shortcode that renders markup which is itself inside a shortcode's output, where the inner output has already been escaped and is now double-escaped, or not escaped and is now a hole. And a shortcode whose callback has a side effect — writing a record, sending a request — which runs once per rendering and therefore once per visitor on an uncached page, which is the mechanism behind "my analytics counter is wrong by a factor of a hundred". Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Treat a shortcode callback as a pure, escaped function of its arguments, because every one of the six pitfalls is either a purity violation or an escaping violation and this one rule excludes both classes. And decide, per shortcode, whether it may run in a loop and whether its output may be cached, and write that decision in the registration, because both questions have to be answered anyway and answering them in the code makes them reviewable.

/* The root cause. */

const ROOT = 'A SHORTCODE IS A FUNCTION THAT RUNS DURING PAGE RENDERING';
const CONSEQUENCE = 'and EVERYTHING ABOUT RENDERING MAKES THINGS THAT WERE FINE AT WRITE TIME WRONG';
const VALUE = 'the VALUE in ENUMERATING THEM IS THAT EACH HAS A DISTINCTIVE SYMPTOM, SO A REPORT ABOUT SHORTCODES IS USUALLY DIAGNOSTIC';

/* Six pitfalls. */
const SIX = [
  { p: 'UNESCAPED OUTPUT', symptom: 'WHICH SHOWS AS BROKEN MARKUP or AS SOMEBODY ELSES HTML IN YOUR OUTPUT' },
  { p: 'AN UNVALIDATED ATTRIBUTE USED IN A QUERY', symptom: 'WHICH SHOWS AS AN ERROR ON UNUSUAL INPUT or AS A SECURITY REPORT' },
  { p: 'A TAG COLLISION', symptom: 'WHICH SHOWS AS YOUR FEATURE DISAPPEARING WHEN SOMEBODY INSTALLS A SECOND PLUGIN' },
  { p: 'ASSUMING the TAG APPEARS ONCE', symptom: 'WHICH SHOWS AS OUTPUT THAT DEPENDS ON HOW MANY TIMES AN AUTHOR USED IT' },
  { p: 'A DYNAMIC SHORTCODE with NO INVALIDATION', symptom: 'WHICH SHOWS AS STALE DATA THAT REFRESHES ON ITS OWN SCHEDULE' },
  { p: 'OUTPUT THAT DEPENDS ON the REQUEST IN A WAY the CACHE CANNOT SEE', instances: ['A SHORTCODE THAT RENDERS DIFFERENTLY FOR A LOGGED-IN USER', 'ONE THAT EMBEDS A TIMESTAMP'],
    symptom: 'WHICH SHOWS AS ONE USER SEEING SOMETHING DIFFERENT FROM ANOTHER and NEITHER BELIEVING the OTHER' },
];

/* Three that compound. */
const COMPOUND = [
  { f: 'A SHORTCODE THAT QUERIES IN A LOOP OVER POST CONTENT',
    consequence: 'WHICH PRODUCES A PAGE WHOSE QUERY COUNT SCALES WITH ITS TAG COUNT',
    symptom: 'and WHICH IS INVISIBLE UNTIL A POST with FIFTY TAGS EXISTS' },
  { f: 'A SHORTCODE THAT RENDERS MARKUP WHICH IS ITSELF INSIDE A SHORTCODES OUTPUT',
    detail: ['WHERE the INNER OUTPUT HAS ALREADY BEEN ESCAPED and IS NOW DOUBLE-ESCAPED',
      'OR NOT ESCAPED and IS NOW A HOLE'] },
  { f: 'A SHORTCODE WHOSE CALLBACK HAS A SIDE EFFECT', instances: ['WRITING A RECORD', 'SENDING A REQUEST'],
    detail: 'WHICH RUNS ONCE PER RENDERING and THEREFORE ONCE PER VISITOR ON AN UNCACHED PAGE',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND my analytics counter is wrong by a factor of a hundred' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'TREAT A SHORTCODE CALLBACK AS A PURE, ESCAPED FUNCTION of ITS ARGUMENTS',
    gain: 'BECAUSE EVERY ONE of the SIX PITFALLS IS EITHER A PURITY VIOLATION or AN ESCAPING VIOLATION and THIS ONE RULE EXCLUDES BOTH CLASSES' },
  { p: 'DECIDE, PER SHORTCODE, WHETHER IT MAY RUN IN A LOOP and WHETHER ITS OUTPUT MAY BE CACHED',
    where: 'and WRITE THAT DECISION IN the REGISTRATION',
    why: 'BECAUSE BOTH QUESTIONS HAVE TO BE ANSWERED ANYWAY and ANSWERING THEM IN the CODE MAKES THEM REVIEWABLE' },
];

Exercises 4

  1. Implement [acme_ledger_balance] with three typed attributes, per-request memoisation and correct escaping in all five output contexts.
  2. Write a test that passes an unrecognised attribute to your shortcode and assert that it does not reach the underlying query.
  3. Build an enclosing shortcode and demonstrate nested shortcode expansion, then measure the recursion cost of 10 levels of nesting.
  4. Convert a static shortcode to a dynamic one and enumerate the cache-invalidation events you must handle.

5 · Widgets, Blocks and the Block Editor

Classic widgets and WP_WidgetWidget forms and instance updatesThe block.json metadata modelServer-rendered blocksEditor assets and script translationsView scripts, styles and the 6.7 feature set

Two rendering surfaces coexist. Classic widgets remain supported and are still the pragmatic choice for a sidebar widget, while the block editor requires metadata-driven blocks registered from a block.json file. This chapter covers both and the asset pipeline that connects them.

Classic widgets and WP_Widget

The widget contract, and why the sidebar supplies the markup

A classic widget is a small piece of configuration attached to a sidebar, and the platform has a specific contract with it that is worth understanding because the parts look arbitrary until you know why each exists. A widget class extends the platform's widget base and declares two methods. One produces the configuration form the editor renders; the other produces the output the sidebar renders. The form method returns a description of the fields, not the fields themselves, and the platform builds the markup from that description — which is why a widget's form looks like every other widget's form rather than like whatever the plugin felt like, and why a widget cannot produce a custom-styled form control. The output method receives the stored instance and the sidebar's own arguments, and the second of those is the theme's contribution: the sidebar passes the wrapper markup its design uses, so that emitting it verbatim is what makes a widget look correct in every theme rather than only in the theme it was designed against. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The form is described in data, so the widget cannot know how it is being displayed — it cannot add a conditional field, cannot validate, and cannot react to another field's value, which is the mechanism behind "I want this field to appear only when that one is checked". The output method is called during page rendering, so anything expensive it does is paid on every request that includes the sidebar, whether or not the widget is visible. And the instance is stored per widget, so two instances of the same widget on the same page have independent configuration and a widget that assumes a single instance is a widget that misbehaves on a page with two. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Emitting your own wrapper instead of the sidebar's, which makes the widget look wrong in every theme except the one you tested in — the single most common widget complaint. Querying in the output method without caching, which produces a query per request per widget and is invisible until a site with six widgets and a sidebar on every page notices. And assuming there is one instance, which produces output that overwrites the first instance's configuration when a second is added. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Output the sidebar's arguments verbatim and let the theme own the wrapper, because that is what makes the widget portable and because the alternative is a widget that only works in one theme. And make the output method cheap and idempotent — fetch or compute once, cache it, and let the cache expire — so that a sidebar with your widget in it costs a lookup rather than a query on every page.

/* The contract. */

const CONTRACT = {
  extends: 'THE PLATFORMS WIDGET BASE',
  methods: ['ONE PRODUCES the CONFIGURATION FORM the EDITOR RENDERS', 'ONE PRODUCES the OUTPUT the SIDEBAR RENDERS'],
  formMethod: ['RETURNS A DESCRIPTION of the FIELDS, NOT the FIELDS THEMSELVES',
    'and the PLATFORM BUILDS the MARKUP FROM that DESCRIPTION'],
  whyFormIsUniform: 'WHICH IS WHY a WIDGETS FORM LOOKS LIKE EVERY OTHER WIDGETS FORM rather than LIKE WHATEVER the PLUGIN FELT LIKE',
  and: 'and WHY A WIDGET CANNOT PRODUCE A CUSTOM-STYLED FORM CONTROL',
  outputReceives: ['the STORED INSTANCE', 'and the SIDEBARS OWN ARGUMENTS'],
  secondArgIsTheThemes: ['the SIDEBAR PASSES the WRAPPER MARKUP ITS DESIGN USES',
    'SO THAT EMITTING IT VERBATIM IS WHAT MAKES A WIDGET LOOK CORRECT IN EVERY THEME rather than ONLY IN the THEME IT WAS DESIGNED AGAINST'],
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the FORM IS DESCRIBED IN DATA, SO the WIDGET CANNOT KNOW HOW IT IS BEING DISPLAYED',
    cannot: ['ADD A CONDITIONAL FIELD', 'VALIDATE', 'and REACT TO ANOTHER FIELDS VALUE'],
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND I want this field to appear only when that one is checked' },
  { c: 'the OUTPUT METHOD IS CALLED DURING PAGE RENDERING',
    consequence: 'SO ANYTHING EXPENSIVE IT DOES IS PAID ON EVERY REQUEST THAT INCLUDES the SIDEBAR, WHETHER OR NOT the WIDGET IS VISIBLE' },
  { c: 'the INSTANCE IS STORED PER WIDGET',
    detail: 'SO TWO INSTANCES of the SAME WIDGET ON the SAME PAGE HAVE INDEPENDENT CONFIGURATION',
    symptom: 'and A WIDGET THAT ASSUMES A SINGLE INSTANCE IS A WIDGET THAT MISBEHAVES ON A PAGE with TWO' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'EMITTING YOUR OWN WRAPPER instead of the SIDEBARS', symptom: 'WHICH MAKES the WIDGET LOOK WRONG IN EVERY THEME EXCEPT the ONE YOU TESTED IN', rank: 'the SINGLE MOST COMMON WIDGET COMPLAINT' },
  { f: 'QUERYING IN the OUTPUT METHOD WITHOUT CACHING', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A QUERY PER REQUEST PER WIDGET', invisible: 'and WHICH IS INVISIBLE UNTIL A SITE with SIX WIDGETS and A SIDEBAR ON EVERY PAGE NOTICES' },
  { f: 'ASSUMING THERE IS ONE INSTANCE', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES OUTPUT THAT OVERWRITES the FIRST INSTANCES CONFIGURATION WHEN A SECOND IS ADDED' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'OUTPUT the SIDEBARS ARGUMENTS VERBATIM and LET the THEME OWN the WRAPPER',
    why: 'BECAUSE THAT IS WHAT MAKES the WIDGET PORTABLE',
    and: 'and BECAUSE the ALTERNATIVE IS A WIDGET THAT ONLY WORKS IN ONE THEME' },
  { p: 'MAKE the OUTPUT METHOD CHEAP and IDEMPOTENT', how: ['FETCH OR COMPUTE ONCE', 'CACHE IT', 'and LET the CACHE EXPIRE'],
    gain: 'SO THAT A SIDEBAR WITH YOUR WIDGET IN IT COSTS A LOOKUP rather than A QUERY ON EVERY PAGE' },
];

A widget is an instance of a class extending WP_Widget, registered on widgets_init. Registration is what places the class in the registry that the block and classic widget screens read from.

add_action( 'widgets_init', 'acme_ledger_register_widgets' );

function acme_ledger_register_widgets(): void {
    register_widget( Acme_Ledger_Balance_Widget::class );
}

final class Acme_Ledger_Balance_Widget extends WP_Widget {
    public function __construct() {
        parent::__construct(
            'acme_ledger_balance',
            __( 'Ledger balance', 'acme-ledger' ),
            array(
                'description'           => __( 'Shows the running balance of one account.', 'acme-ledger' ),
                'customize_selective_refresh' => true,
            )
        );
    }

    public function widget( array $args, array $instance ): void {
        $account_id = isset( $instance['account_id'] ) ? absint( $instance['account_id'] ) : 0;
        $title      = isset( $instance['title'] ) ? $instance['title'] : '';

        echo $args['before_widget'];

        if ( '' !== $title ) {
            echo $args['before_title'] . esc_html( $title ) . $args['after_title'];
        }

        echo Ledger::render_balance_summary( $account_id );

        echo $args['after_widget'];
    }
}

The $args array is supplied by the sidebar and supplies the theme’s own wrapper markup. Emitting it verbatim is what makes a widget theme-agnostic. Emitting your own wrapper instead breaks every theme that styles its sidebars.

Widget forms and instance updates

Saving, sanitising, and the serialisation you did not choose

A widget's configuration is saved as one serialised array in one option row, which means three things about saving are true whether you intended them or not, and each is worth stating because each produces a bug that is hard to trace. The whole instance array is serialised at once, so the size of a widget's configuration is bounded by what a single option can hold and by what is sensible to put in a database row. The platform sanitises form input on save according to field types it recognises, so a text field is sanitised as text and a number field as a number — which means the sanitisation happens for you if you declare the types correctly and does not happen at all if you declare them loosely. And the value is not validated against anything but its declared type, so a checkbox that is unchecked arrives as an absent key rather than as false, and a number field that was left blank arrives as an empty string. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. A missing key is not the same as a false value, which is the mechanism behind a checkbox that unchecks itself — the form omits the key, the callback treats the missing key as not-present-and-therefore-true, and the checkbox stays on. An empty string is not the same as zero, so a numeric field that was cleared arrives as a string and a strict comparison against zero fails. And the instance is unserialised on every read, so an instance containing an object is unserialised into a fresh object every request, which is why storing an object works and storing a closure or a resource does not. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Storing an object in the instance, which survives one save and one read and then behaves differently from the object you stored, because what comes back is a new instance of the class with the same field values and none of the identity. Not sanitising the output, which is the widget equivalent of the escaping section's rule: the form is sanitised on the way in and the output must be escaped on the way out, because the value in between is not trusted just because the form produced it. And trusting the declared field type to sanitise, which works for the types the platform recognises and not for your own validation — a field declared as text with a value that must be a number will be sanitised as text and will reach your code as text. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Normalise the instance at the top of every method that receives it, filling absent keys with their defaults and casting the rest, so that the rest of the code never has to test for absence and a schema change has exactly one place to be handled. And store primitives and identifiers, not objects, because the instance is a database row and what you put in it is what you get back — an identifier you can look up in a table is a better design than an object in an option.

/* Three things true about saving. */

const SAVING = {
  is: 'A WIDGETS CONFIGURATION IS SAVED AS ONE SERIALISED ARRAY IN ONE OPTION ROW',
  facts: [
    { f: 'the WHOLE INSTANCE ARRAY IS SERIALISED AT ONCE', consequence: 'SO the SIZE of a WIDGETS CONFIGURATION IS BOUNDED BY WHAT A SINGLE OPTION CAN HOLD and BY WHAT IS SENSIBLE TO PUT IN A DATABASE ROW' },
    { f: 'the PLATFORM SANITISES FORM INPUT ON SAVE ACCORDING TO FIELD TYPES IT RECOGNISES', detail: 'SO A TEXT FIELD IS SANITISED AS TEXT and A NUMBER FIELD AS A NUMBER', and: 'WHICH MEANS the SANITISATION HAPPENS FOR YOU IF YOU DECLARE the TYPES CORRECTLY and DOES NOT HAPPEN AT ALL IF YOU DECLARE THEM LOOSELY' },
    { f: 'the VALUE IS NOT VALIDATED AGAINST ANYTHING BUT ITS DECLARED TYPE', detail: ['SO A CHECKBOX THAT IS UNCHECKED ARRIVES AS AN ABSENT KEY rather than AS FALSE',
      'and A NUMBER FIELD THAT WAS LEFT BLANK ARRIVES AS AN EMPTY STRING'] },
  ],
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A MISSING KEY IS NOT the SAME AS A FALSE VALUE',
    mechanism: 'THE FORM OMITS the KEY, the CALLBACK TREATS the MISSING KEY AS NOT-PRESENT-AND-THEREFORE-TRUE',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND A CHECKBOX THAT UNCHECKS ITSELF' },
  { c: 'AN EMPTY STRING IS NOT the SAME AS ZERO',
    detail: 'SO A NUMERIC FIELD THAT WAS CLEARED ARRIVES AS A STRING and A STRICT COMPARISON AGAINST ZERO FAILS' },
  { c: 'the INSTANCE IS UNSERIALISED ON EVERY READ',
    detail: 'SO AN INSTANCE CONTAINING AN OBJECT IS UNSERIALISED INTO A FRESH OBJECT EVERY REQUEST',
    consequence: 'WHICH IS WHY STORING AN OBJECT WORKS and STORING A CLOSURE or A RESOURCE DOES NOT' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'STORING AN OBJECT IN the INSTANCE', behaviour: 'SURVIVES ONE SAVE and ONE READ and THEN BEHAVES DIFFERENTLY FROM the OBJECT YOU STORED',
    because: 'BECAUSE WHAT COMES BACK IS A NEW INSTANCE of the CLASS with the SAME FIELD VALUES and NONE of the IDENTITY' },
  { f: 'NOT SANITISING the OUTPUT', connects: 'WHICH IS the WIDGET EQUIVALENT of the ESCAPING SECTIONS RULE',
    detail: ['the FORM IS SANITISED ON the WAY IN and the OUTPUT MUST BE ESCAPED ON the WAY OUT',
      'BECAUSE the VALUE IN BETWEEN IS NOT TRUSTED JUST BECAUSE the FORM PRODUCED IT'] },
  { f: 'TRUSTING the DECLARED FIELD TYPE to SANITISE', worksFor: 'the TYPES the PLATFORM RECOGNISES', notFor: 'YOUR OWN VALIDATION',
    symptom: 'A FIELD DECLARED AS TEXT with A VALUE THAT MUST BE A NUMBER WILL BE SANITISED AS TEXT and WILL REACH YOUR CODE AS TEXT' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'NORMALISE the INSTANCE AT the TOP of EVERY METHOD THAT RECEIVES IT',
    how: ['FILLING ABSENT KEYS WITH THEIR DEFAULTS', 'and CASTING the REST'],
    gain: ['SO THAT the REST of the CODE NEVER HAS TO TEST FOR ABSENCE',
      'and A SCHEMA CHANGE HAS EXACTLY ONE PLACE to BE HANDLED'] },
  { p: 'STORE PRIMITIVES and IDENTIFIERS, NOT OBJECTS',
    why: 'BECAUSE the INSTANCE IS A DATABASE ROW and WHAT YOU PUT IN IT IS WHAT YOU GET BACK',
    and: 'AN IDENTIFIER YOU CAN LOOK UP IN A TABLE IS A BETTER DESIGN THAN AN OBJECT IN AN OPTION' },
];

Two methods complete a widget: form() renders the settings UI and update() converts the submitted values into a stored instance. Both run in the admin, and both receive untrusted input.

public function form( array $instance ): void {
    $title      = $instance['title']      ?? '';
    $account_id = $instance['account_id'] ?? 0;
    ?>
    <p>
        <label for="<?= esc_attr( $this->get_field_id( 'title' ) ) ?>">
            <?= esc_html_e( 'Title:', 'acme-ledger' ) ?>
        </label>
        <input class="widefat"
            type="text"
            name="<?= esc_attr( $this->get_field_name( 'title' ) ) ?>"
            value="<?= esc_attr( $title ) ?>">
    </p>
    <p>
        <label for="<?= esc_attr( $this->get_field_id( 'account_id' ) ) ?>">
            <?= esc_html_e( 'Account:', 'acme-ledger' ) ?>
        </label>
        <?php wp_dropdown_categories( array(
            'taxonomy'        => 'acme_account',
            'name'            => $this->get_field_name( 'account_id' ),
            'selected'        => $account_id,
            'show_option_none' => __( '— Select —', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'hide_empty'      => false,
            'class'            => 'widefat',
        ) ); ?>
    </p>
    <?php
}

public function update( array $new_instance, array $old_instance ): array {
    return array(
        'title'      => sanitize_text_field( $new_instance['title'] ?? '' ),
        'account_id' => absint( $new_instance['account_id'] ?? 0 ),
    );
}

Widgets are saved through update_option(), which means the whole instance array is serialised into one option. Store primitives, not objects. An instance containing an object will be reconstructed as stdClass on the way back out.

The block.json metadata model

Since WordPress 5.5 a block may be fully described by a block.json file with no PHP registration beyond a single register_block_type() call. This is the model wordpress.org review expects and the one that enables server-side rendering without a build step.

// build/entry/block.json
{
  "$schema": "https://schemas.wp.org/trunk/block.json",
  "apiVersion": 3,
  "name": "acme-ledger/entry",
  "title": "Ledger entry",
  "category": "widgets",
  "icon": "money-alt",
  "description": "Displays a single ledger entry.",
  "textdomain": "acme-ledger",
  "supports": {
    "html": false,
    "align": ["wide", "full"],
    "spacing": { "margin": true, "padding": true }
  },
  "attributes": {
    "entryId": { "type": "number", "default": 0 },
    "showMemo": { "type": "boolean", "default": true }
  },
  "example": {
    "attributes": { "entryId": 42, "showMemo": false }
  }
}
add_action( 'init', 'acme_ledger_register_blocks' );

function acme_ledger_register_blocks'(): void {
    register_block_type( ACME_LEDGER_DIR . 'build/entry', ACME_LEDGER_URL . 'build/entry' );
    register_block_type( ACME_LEDGER_DIR . 'build/entries', ACME_LEDGER_URL . 'build/entries' );
}

Passing the second argument is what makes viewScript and viewStyle files resolve, and it is what tells WordPress where the compiled editor asset lives. Omit it and only the metadata path is available.

The metadata file, and why one file describes a block

A block is described by a metadata file in the plugin's own language and by a registration call, and the split between them is the first thing to understand because it decides where a property lives. The metadata file declares the block's name, title, description, category, icon, keywords and its support flags, and it also declares the editor script and style that render its form and the view script and style that render its output on the front end. The registration call declares the render callback and the block-type location. The consequence is that everything an editor needs to build the form is in the metadata file and the editor can load it without loading your plugin's PHP — which is the mechanism behind the editor being fast and behind the requirement that a block's form not depend on server state. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The metadata file's paths are relative to the plugin root and are resolved against a base you pass to the registration call, which is why a block registered without the base argument has a metadata path but no view script — the editor will render the block and nothing will happen on the front end. The support flags are declarations the editor enforces, so declaring that a block does not support custom class names or multiple instances removes the control from the editor rather than merely ignoring it later. And attributes are declared in the metadata file but their types are not enforced at save time, so a number attribute can be saved as a string and the render callback receives whatever the editor sent — which is the same untrusted-input property as a shortcode attribute and requires the same normalisation. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Registering without the base path, which produces a block that appears in the editor and renders as a comment on the front end — the symptom that most often gets reported as "my block is not saving". Changing a metadata file and not invalidating the editor's cache, which produces an editor that still shows the old form until the cache is cleared. And assuming the attribute types are validated, which produces a render callback that receives a string where it expected a number and arithmetic that silently produces the wrong result. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Keep every attribute's normalisation in the render callback, because the editor can be in a different version from the front end and an old editor may not send a field a new one requires, so a missing attribute is normal rather than exceptional. And treat the metadata file as the block's public interface, because renaming an attribute breaks saved content — a post saved with the old attribute name loses its value, and nothing warns you.

/* The split. */

const METADATA_FILE = {
  declares: ['the BLOCKS NAME', 'TITLE', 'DESCRIPTION', 'CATEGORY', 'ICON', 'KEYWORDS', 'and ITS SUPPORT FLAGS'],
  alsoDeclares: ['the EDITOR SCRIPT and STYLE THAT RENDER ITS FORM', 'and the VIEW SCRIPT and STYLE THAT RENDER ITS OUTPUT ON the FRONT END'],
};
const REGISTRATION = { declares: ['the RENDER CALLBACK', 'and the BLOCK-TYPE LOCATION'] };
const CONSEQUENCE = {
  is: 'EVERYTHING AN EDITOR NEEDS TO BUILD the FORM IS IN the METADATA FILE',
  gain: ['and the EDITOR CAN LOAD IT WITHOUT LOADING YOUR PLUGINS PHP'],
  and: 'which IS the MECHANISM BEHIND the EDITOR BEING FAST',
  also: 'and BEHIND the REQUIREMENT THAT A BLOCKS FORM NOT DEPEND ON SERVER STATE',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const THREE = [
  { c: 'the METADATA FILES PATHS ARE RELATIVE TO the PLUGIN ROOT and ARE RESOLVED AGAINST A BASE YOU PASS TO the REGISTRATION CALL',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS WHY A BLOCK REGISTERED WITHOUT the BASE ARGUMENT HAS A METADATA PATH but NO VIEW SCRIPT',
    and: 'THE EDITOR WILL RENDER the BLOCK and NOTHING WILL HAPPEN ON the FRONT END' },
  { c: 'the SUPPORT FLAGS ARE DECLARATIONS the EDITOR ENFORCES',
    detail: 'SO DECLARING THAT A BLOCK DOES NOT SUPPORT CUSTOM CLASS NAMES or MULTIPLE INSTANCES REMOVES the CONTROL FROM the EDITOR rather than MERELY IGNORING IT LATER' },
  { c: 'ATTRIBUTES ARE DECLARED IN the METADATA FILE but THEIR TYPES ARE NOT ENFORCED AT SAVE TIME',
    consequence: 'SO A NUMBER ATTRIBUTE CAN BE SAVED AS A STRING and the RENDER CALLBACK RECEIVES WHATEVER the EDITOR SENT',
    connects: 'WHICH IS the SAME UNTRUSTED-INPUT PROPERTY as A SHORTCODE ATTRIBUTE and REQUIRES the SAME NORMALISATION' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'REGISTERING WITHOUT the BASE PATH', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A BLOCK THAT APPEARS IN the EDITOR and RENDERS AS A COMMENT ON the FRONT END', and: 'THE SYMPTOM THAT MOST OFTEN GETS REPORTED AS my block is not saving' },
  { f: 'CHANGING A METADATA FILE and NOT INVALIDATING the EDITORS CACHE', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES AN EDITOR THAT STILL SHOWS the OLD FORM UNTIL the CACHE IS CLEARED' },
  { f: 'ASSUMING the ATTRIBUTE TYPES ARE VALIDATED', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A RENDER CALLBACK THAT RECEIVES A STRING WHERE IT EXPECTED A NUMBER and ARITHMETIC THAT SILENTLY PRODUCES the WRONG RESULT' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'KEEP every ATTRIBUTES NORMALISATION IN the RENDER CALLBACK',
    why: 'BECAUSE the EDITOR CAN BE IN A DIFFERENT VERSION FROM the FRONT END and AN OLD EDITOR MAY NOT SEND A FIELD A NEW ONE REQUIRES',
    consequence: 'SO A MISSING ATTRIBUTE IS NORMAL rather than EXCEPTIONAL' },
  { p: 'TREAT the METADATA FILE AS the BLOCKS PUBLIC INTERFACE',
    why: 'BECAUSE RENAMING AN ATTRIBUTE BREAKS SAVED CONTENT',
    symptom: 'A POST SAVED WITH the OLD ATTRIBUTE NAME LOSES ITS VALUE, and NOTHING WARNS YOU' },
];

API version 3 is the floor for a new block in 6.7. Version 1 stored all attributes in comment delimiters; version 2 introduced separate comment delimiters per attribute; version 3 stores a single JSON payload. Use 3 unless a specific interop requirement forces otherwise.

Server-rendered blocks

Rendering on the server, and why the callback is where the trust boundary is

A server-rendered block's render callback receives the saved attributes, the block's own content, and the block instance, and it returns markup — which makes it the point where stored, author-supplied data becomes output. The callback is called during page rendering for every occurrence of the block, so anything it does is done per occurrence, on the request, which is the same loop hazard the shortcode chapter described arriving with a different API. It receives attributes as a plain array, with only the keys the editor sent and no defaults applied, so a block that reads an attribute must decide what a missing one means — and must do it in the callback, because the metadata file's declared attributes are a description to the editor and not a schema enforced on save. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The attributes are untrusted on the front end even though the editor produced them, because the block instance is also a saved representation that can be produced by an import, a REST request, a programmatic insert, or an older version of the editor, and every one of those paths bypasses the editor's validation. The render callback runs during rendering, so it may query, and a block that queries per occurrence multiplies its cost by the number of occurrences — which is the mechanism behind a page of sixty identical blocks issuing sixty identical queries. And the render callback must escape, everything it outputs, because the attributes are strings that came from an editor and the editor is not a security boundary. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Not applying defaults, which produces a notice or a warning on the front end for any block saved before an attribute was added — and it appears only on old content, which is why it is reported as "some blocks are broken". Returning unescaped attribute values, which is an injection point whose severity depends on who can create posts. And querying inside the callback with no caching, which is the per-occurrence cost above and which is invisible in the editor because the editor does not run the render callback. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Apply defaults and escape at the top of the callback, in that order, so the rest of the function can assume a complete, safe set of values and there is one place to check when a new attribute is added. And keep the render callback free of queries where you can, by having the view script fetch what it needs from a small endpoint — because a block that fetches in the browser costs nothing on cached pages and a block that queries on the server costs a query on every uncached request for every occurrence.

/* What the callback receives and returns. */

const CALLBACK = {
  receives: ['the SAVED ATTRIBUTES', 'the BLOCKS OWN CONTENT', 'and the BLOCK INSTANCE'],
  returns: 'MARKUP',
  isTheTrustBoundary: 'WHICH MAKES IT the POINT WHERE STORED, AUTHOR-SUPPLIED DATA BECOMES OUTPUT',
  when: 'IT IS CALLED DURING PAGE RENDERING for EVERY OCCURRENCE of the BLOCK',
  consequence: 'SO ANYTHING IT DOES IS DONE PER OCCURRENCE, ON the REQUEST',
  connects: 'WHICH IS the SAME LOOP HAZARD the SHORTCODE CHAPTER DESCRIBED ARRIVING WITH A DIFFERENT API',
  attributes: 'IT RECEIVES ATTRIBUTES AS A PLAIN ARRAY, WITH ONLY the KEYS the EDITOR SENT and NO DEFAULTS APPLIED',
  therefore: ['SO A BLOCK THAT READS AN ATTRIBUTE MUST DECIDE WHAT A MISSING ONE MEANS',
    'and MUST DO IT IN the CALLBACK'],
  whyNotMetadata: 'BECAUSE the METADATA FILES DECLARED ATTRIBUTES ARE A DESCRIPTION TO the EDITOR and NOT A SCHEMA ENFORCED ON SAVE',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the ATTRIBUTES ARE UNTRUSTED ON the FRONT END EVEN THOUGH the EDITOR PRODUCED THEM',
    because: 'the BLOCK INSTANCE IS ALSO A SAVED REPRESENTATION THAT CAN BE PRODUCED BY [' + ['AN IMPORT', 'A REST REQUEST', 'A PROGRAMMATIC INSERT', 'AN OLDER VERSION of the EDITOR'] + ']',
    and: 'EVERY ONE of THOSE PATHS BYPASSES the EDITORS VALIDATION' },
  { c: 'the RENDER CALLBACK RUNS DURING RENDERING, SO IT MAY QUERY',
    symptom: 'and A BLOCK THAT QUERIES PER OCCURRENCE MULTIPLIES ITS COST BY the NUMBER of OCCURRENCES',
    example: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND A PAGE of SIXTY IDENTICAL BLOCKS ISSUING SIXTY IDENTICAL QUERIES' },
  { c: 'the RENDER CALLBACK MUST ESCAPE, EVERYTHING IT OUTPUTS',
    why: 'BECAUSE the ATTRIBUTES ARE STRINGS THAT CAME FROM AN EDITOR and the EDITOR IS NOT A SECURITY BOUNDARY' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'NOT APPLYING DEFAULTS', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A NOTICE or A WARNING ON the FRONT END for ANY BLOCK SAVED BEFORE AN ATTRIBUTE WAS ADDED', and: 'IT APPEARS ONLY ON OLD CONTENT, WHICH IS WHY IT IS REPORTED AS some blocks are broken' },
  { f: 'RETURNING UNESCAPED ATTRIBUTE VALUES', is: 'AN INJECTION POINT', severity: 'WHOSE SEVERITY DEPENDS ON WHO CAN CREATE POSTS' },
  { f: 'QUERYING INSIDE the CALLBACK with NO CACHING', symptom: 'WHICH IS the PER-OCCURRENCE COST ABOVE and WHICH IS INVISIBLE IN the EDITOR BECAUSE the EDITOR DOES NOT RUN the RENDER CALLBACK' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'APPLY DEFAULTS and ESCAPE AT the TOP of the CALLBACK, IN THAT ORDER',
    gain: ['SO THAT the REST of the FUNCTION CAN ASSUME A COMPLETE, SAFE SET of VALUES',
      'and THERE IS ONE PLACE TO CHECK WHEN A NEW ATTRIBUTE IS ADDED'] },
  { p: 'KEEP the RENDER CALLBACK FREE of QUERIES WHERE YOU CAN',
    how: 'BY HAVING the VIEW SCRIPT FETCH WHAT IT NEEDS FROM A SMALL ENDPOINT',
    reason: ['BECAUSE A BLOCK THAT FETCHES IN the BROWSER COSTS NOTHING ON CACHED PAGES',
      'and A BLOCK THAT QUERIES ON the SERVER COSTS A QUERY ON EVERY UNCACHED REQUEST for EVERY OCCURRENCE'] },
];

A block with a render property in block.json delegates markup generation to PHP. The rendered wrapper element must carry the block attributes so client-side styles and the block supports system work correctly.

// build/entry/render.php
<?php
declare( strict_types=1 );

$entry_id = isset( $attributes['entryId'] ) ? intval( $attributes['entryId'] ) : 0;
$show_memo = ! empty( $attributes['showMemo'] );

if ( $entry_id <= 0 ) {
    return '';
}

$entry = Ledger::get_entry( $entry_id );

if ( ! $entry ) {
    return '<div class="acme-entry acme-entry--missing">'
         . esc_html__( 'Entry not found.', 'acme-ledger' )
         . '</div>';
}
?>
<div <?= get_block_wrapper_attributes( array( 'class' => 'acme-entry--posted' ) ) ?>>
    <?= esc_html( $entry['posted_on'] ) ?>
    <?= esc_html( $entry['amount'] ) ?>
    <?php if ( $show_memo && $entry['memo'] ) : ?>
        <p class="acme-entry__memo"><?= esc_html( $entry['memo'] ) ?></p>
    <?php endif; ?>
</div>

The alternative to a render file is a dynamic block registered purely in PHP with a render_callback, filtered in through render_block_{$name}. The callback receives the parsed block array, the WP_Block instance, and an instance of WP_Block_Editor_Context, so you can vary output for the editor, the front end and the REST endpoint.

add_filter( 'render_block_acme-ledger/entries', array( $this, 'render_entries_block' ), 10, 3 );

public function render_entries_block( string $content, array $block, WP_Block $instance ): string {
    $context = $instance->context;

    if ( ( $context['name'] ?? '' ) === 'core/edit-post' ) {
        return $content;
    }

    $limit = min( 25, max( 1, (int) ( $block['attrs']['limit'] ?? 5 ) ) );

    return Ledger::render_entry_list( array( 'limit' => $limit ) );
}

Editor assets and script translations

Compiled editor JavaScript is enqueued with enqueue_block_editor_assets, and its __() strings must be published as translation files or the editor shows English inside a translated interface.

add_action( 'enqueue_block_editor_assets', function(): void {
    $asset = require ACME_LEDGER_DIR . 'build/entry/index.asset.php';

    wp_enqueue_script(
        'acme-ledger-entry',
        plugins_url( 'build/entry/index.js', ACME_LEDGER_FILE ),
        $asset['dependencies'],
        $asset['version'],
        true
    );

    wp_set_script_translations(
        'acme-ledger-entry',
        'acme-ledger',
        ACME_LEDGER_DIR . 'languages'
    );
} );

The index.asset.php file is generated by @wordpress/scripts and declares the exact dependency list and content hash. Reading it is mandatory: a hand-written dependency list will be wrong the moment a core package changes its peer dependency.

Editor scripts, translations, and the four things that break in the editor

The editor loads a block's form from a compiled script that the metadata file names, and getting that to work involves four separate mechanisms that fail independently. The script must be registered with the editor, which tells the editor a file exists and gives it a handle; the metadata file must name the same file, which is how the editor knows to load it for your block; the script must declare its dependency on the editor's own script handle, so it is not evaluated before the API it calls exists; and the script must be translatable, which means its strings must be extracted for translation rather than written as literals. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Translation is extraction, not lookup, so a string written as a literal in the editor script is never offered to a translator and never appears in a translation file; the mechanism is a translation function whose presence the extractor recognises, which means the call must be in a form the extractor recognises — a literal concatenation defeats it. The script handle is a dependency declaration and it is enforced, so a script that runs before the editor's API is available fails on an undefined function rather than silently doing nothing, which at least is diagnosable. And the editor script and the view script are different files with different lifetimes, so sharing one file between them means loading a form-only bundle on every page view and an editor-only bundle nowhere. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Untranslated strings in the editor, which is the symptom of a literal where the extraction function should be, and which produces an editor in one language for a translated site. A script that loads but whose block does not register, which is usually a dependency handle mismatch or a name mismatch between the metadata file and the script's internal block name — and the editor's console is where both are visible. And registering the same block name twice, from two plugins or two build outputs, where the second registration wins silently and the first plugin's block disappears. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines.
