Use the platform's text-domain-aware translation function everywhere in the editor script, and treat a literal string in editor code as a defect rather than a shortcut — because the editor is part of the product and it is the part users see most. And build the editor and view scripts as separate bundles from a shared source, so the shared parts are shared in the source rather than in a file that both bundles load, which keeps the front-end bundle free of editor-only code and the editor bundle free of view-only code.

/* Four independent mechanisms. */

const MECHANISMS = [
  { m: 'the SCRIPT MUST BE REGISTERED WITH the EDITOR', gives: 'IT TELLS the EDITOR A FILE EXISTS and GIVES IT A HANDLE' },
  { m: 'the METADATA FILE MUST NAME the SAME FILE', how: 'WHICH IS HOW the EDITOR KNOWS TO LOAD IT FOR YOUR BLOCK' },
  { m: 'the SCRIPT MUST DECLARE ITS DEPENDENCY ON the EDITORS OWN SCRIPT HANDLE', why: 'SO IT IS NOT EVALUATED BEFORE the API IT CALLS EXISTS' },
  { m: 'the SCRIPT MUST BE TRANSLATABLE', how: 'WHICH MEANS ITS STRINGS MUST BE EXTRACTED for TRANSLATION rather than WRITTEN AS LITERALS' },
];

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'TRANSLATION IS EXTRACTION, NOT LOOKUP',
    symptom: 'SO A STRING WRITTEN AS A LITERAL IN the EDITOR SCRIPT IS NEVER OFFERED TO A TRANSLATOR and NEVER APPEARS IN A TRANSLATION FILE',
    mechanism: ['the MECHANISM IS A TRANSLATION FUNCTION WHOSE PRESENCE the EXTRACTOR RECOGNISES',
      'WHICH MEANS the CALL MUST BE IN A FORM the EXTRACTOR RECOGNISES - A LITERAL CONCATENATION DEFEATS IT'] },
  { c: 'the SCRIPT HANDLE IS A DEPENDENCY DECLARATION and IT IS ENFORCED',
    symptom: 'SO A SCRIPT THAT RUNS BEFORE the EDITORS API IS AVAILABLE FAILS ON AN UNDEFINED FUNCTION rather than SILENTLY DOING NOTHING',
    which: 'WHICH AT LEAST IS DIAGNOSABLE' },
  { c: 'the EDITOR SCRIPT and the VIEW SCRIPT ARE DIFFERENT FILES with DIFFERENT LIFETIMES',
    symptom: 'SO SHARING ONE FILE BETWEEN THEM MEANS LOADING A FORM-ONLY BUNDLE ON EVERY PAGE VIEW and AN EDITOR-ONLY BUNDLE NOWHERE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'UNTRANSLATED STRINGS IN the EDITOR', symptom: 'WHICH IS the SYMPTOM OF A LITERAL WHERE the EXTRACTION FUNCTION SHOULD BE', and: 'WHICH PRODUCES AN EDITOR IN ONE LANGUAGE FOR A TRANSLATED SITE' },
  { f: 'A SCRIPT THAT LOADS but WHOSE BLOCK DOES NOT REGISTER', causes: ['A DEPENDENCY HANDLE MISMATCH', 'A NAME MISMATCH BETWEEN the METADATA FILE and the SCRIPTS INTERNAL BLOCK NAME'], and: 'the EDITORS CONSOLE IS WHERE BOTH ARE VISIBLE' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING the SAME BLOCK NAME TWICE', from: ['TWO PLUGINS', 'TWO BUILD OUTPUTS'], symptom: 'WHERE the SECOND REGISTRATION WINS SILENTLY and the FIRST PLUGINS BLOCK DISAPPEARS' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'USE the PLATFORMS TEXT-DOMAIN-AWARE TRANSLATION FUNCTION EVERYWHERE IN the EDITOR SCRIPT',
    rule: 'and TREAT A LITERAL STRING IN EDITOR CODE AS A DEFECT rather than A SHORTCUT',
    why: 'BECAUSE the EDITOR IS PART of the PRODUCT and IT IS the PART USERS SEE MOST' },
  { p: 'BUILD the EDITOR and VIEW SCRIPTS AS SEPARATE BUNDLES FROM A SHARED SOURCE',
    gain: ['SO THE SHARED PARTS ARE SHARED IN the SOURCE rather than IN A FILE THAT BOTH BUNDLES LOAD'],
    consequence: 'WHICH KEEPS the FRONT-END BUNDLE FREE of EDITOR-ONLY CODE and the EDITOR BUNDLE FREE of VIEW-ONLY CODE' },
];

Register a block category rather than using the default. The category must be registered on init with block_categories_all (plural, since 5.8). For a single-block plugin, block_categories still works but is deprecated for new code.

add_filter( 'block_categories_all', function( array $categories ): array {
    foreach ( $categories as $category ) {
        if ( 'acme-ledger' === $category['slug'] ) {
            return $categories;
        }
    }

    $categories[] = array(
        'slug'  => 'acme-ledger',
        'title' => __( 'Ledger', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'icon'  => null,
    );

    return $categories;
} );

View scripts, styles and the 6.7 feature set

WordPress 6.5 introduced declarative viewScript and viewStyle fields, and 6.7 extended block metadata with variations, example and template lock support. These reduce the amount of PHP a block needs to ship.

{
  "$schema": "https://schemas.wp.org/trunk/block.json",
  "apiVersion": 3,
  "name": "acme-ledger/chart",
  "title": "Ledger chart",
  "category": "acme-ledger",
  "textdomain": "acme-ledger",
  "viewScript": "file:./view.js",
  "viewStyle": "file:./view.css",
  "attributes": {
    "accountIds": { "type": "array", "default": [], "items": { "type": "number" } },
    "period": { "type": "string", "default": "month" }
  },
  "usesContext": [ "postId" ],
  "variations": [
    { "name": "line", "title": "Line", "isDefault": true },
    { "name": "bar",  "title": "Bar" }
  ],
  "editorScript": "file:./index.js"
}

A view script is enqueued on the front end only when the block is actually present in the rendered content, which WordPress determines by scanning for the block comment delimiters. This is the correct mechanism for interactive behaviour and is considerably better than enqueuing globally on wp_enqueue_scripts.

Choosing what runs on the front end, and what runs in the editor

Every block has two bundles with two jobs and two loads, and most block bugs are a consequence of putting code in the wrong one. The editor bundle runs only inside the editor and is where the form lives: the React component, its controls, its data fetching for the form's own options. The view bundle runs on every page view where the block appears and is where the front-end behaviour lives: the interactivity, the fetch of block data, the animations. The two have genuinely different constraints, which is why one bundle cannot serve both. The editor bundle may assume the editor's API exists, because it only runs where it does. The view bundle may assume nothing about the page — it runs in the same global scope as every other script, alongside every other plugin's script, with no namespace and no module system unless you bring one. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The view bundle is a global script and its top-level names are global names, so two blocks that both declare a helper function collide — and the collision is a fatal or a silent overwrite depending on order, which is the same prefix rule the plugin contract imposes, applied to JavaScript. The view bundle may be loaded more than once on a page, once per block instance, so its own top-level code must be idempotent — a script that attaches a listener at top level attaches it twice per instance. And the view bundle runs on cached pages with no server available, so it cannot assume an inline data blob is present unless the block declared a dependency on one. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Importing the editor's API in the view bundle, which fails at run time on the front end with an undefined global, and which a build step may not catch because the editor bundle resolves it fine. Shipping the whole editor bundle as the view bundle because they share a source, which means every page view downloads the form code and every block instance executes code it never uses. And rendering data server-side and also fetching it client-side, which is a double request on every view and a race between the two sources, and whose symptom is content that changes between the first paint and a second later. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Decide per block whether the front end needs data at all, and render it in the callback if it does not, because a static block that fetches is a block that fails offline and on a slow connection. And give the view bundle a namespace and an idempotent entry point, so that the names it declares cannot collide and so that loading it twice is harmless — which is also what lets you ship the same bundle for every instance of the block.

/* Two bundles, two jobs, two loads. */

const BUNDLES = [
  { b: 'the EDITOR BUNDLE', runsWhere: 'ONLY INSIDE the EDITOR',
    holds: ['the REACT COMPONENT', 'ITS CONTROLS', 'ITS DATA FETCHING for the FORMS OWN OPTIONS'],
    mayAssume: 'the EDITORS API EXISTS, BECAUSE IT ONLY RUNS WHERE IT DOES' },
  { b: 'the VIEW BUNDLE', runsWhere: 'ON EVERY PAGE VIEW WHERE the BLOCK APPEARS',
    holds: ['the INTERACTIVITY', 'the FETCH of BLOCK DATA', 'the ANIMATIONS'],
    mayAssume: 'NOTHING ABOUT the PAGE',
    detail: ['IT RUNS IN the SAME GLOBAL SCOPE AS EVERY OTHER SCRIPT',      'ALONGSIDE EVERY OTHER PLUGINS SCRIPT',      'with NO NAMESPACE and NO MODULE SYSTEM UNLESS YOU BRING ONE'],
  },
];
const WHY = 'the TWO HAVE GENUINELY DIFFERENT CONSTRAINTS, WHICH IS WHY ONE BUNDLE CANNOT SERVE BOTH';/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the VIEW BUNDLE IS A GLOBAL SCRIPT and ITS TOP-LEVEL NAMES ARE GLOBAL NAMES',
    symptom: 'SO TWO BLOCKS THAT BOTH DECLARE A HELPER FUNCTION COLLIDE',
    collision: 'and the COLLISION IS A FATAL or A SILENT OVERWRITE DEPENDING ON ORDER',    connects: 'WHICH IS the SAME PREFIX RULE the PLUGIN CONTRACT IMPOSES, APPLIED to JAVASCRIPT' },
  { c: 'the VIEW BUNDLE MAY BE LOADED MORE THAN ONCE ON A PAGE',
    detail: 'ONCE PER BLOCK INSTANCE',    consequence: "SO ITS OWN TOP-LEVEL CODE MUST BE IDEMPOTENT",    symptom: 'A SCRIPT THAT ATTACHES A LISTENER AT TOP LEVEL ATTACHES IT TWICE PER INSTANCE' },
  { c: 'the VIEW BUNDLE RUNS ON CACHED PAGES with NO SERVER AVAILABLE',
    consequence: 'SO IT CANNOT ASSUME AN INLINE DATA BLOB IS PRESENT UNLESS the BLOCK DECLARED A DEPENDENCY ON ONE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'IMPORTING the EDITORS API IN the VIEW BUNDLE',
    symptom: 'WHICH FAILS AT RUN TIME ON the FRONT END with AN UNDEFINED GLOBAL',
    whyNotCaught: 'and WHICH A BUILD STEP MAY NOT CATCH BECAUSE the EDITOR BUNDLE RESOLVES IT FINE' },
  { f: 'SHIPPING the WHOLE EDITOR BUNDLE AS the VIEW BUNDLE', because: 'BECAUSE THEY SHARE A SOURCE',
    consequence: 'WHICH MEANS EVERY PAGE VIEW DOWNLOADS the FORM CODE and EVERY BLOCK INSTANCE EXECUTES CODE IT NEVER USES' },
  { f: 'RENDERING DATA SERVER-SIDE and ALSO FETCHING IT CLIENT-SIDE',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS A DOUBLE REQUEST ON EVERY VIEW and A RACE BETWEEN the TWO SOURCES',
    and: 'WHOSE SYMPTOM IS CONTENT THAT CHANGES BETWEEN the FIRST PAINT and A SECOND LATER' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'DECIDE PER BLOCK WHETHER the FRONT END NEEDS DATA AT ALL',
    ifNot: 'RENDER IT IN the CALLBACK',
    why: 'BECAUSE A STATIC BLOCK THAT FETCHES IS A BLOCK THAT FAILS OFFLINE and ON A SLOW CONNECTION' },
  { p: 'GIVE the VIEW BUNDLE A NAMESPACE and AN IDEMPOTENT ENTRY POINT',
    gain: ['SO THAT the NAMES IT DECLARES CANNOT COLLIDE', 'and SO THAT LOADING IT TWICE IS HARMLESS'],
    and: 'WHICH IS ALSO WHAT LETS YOU SHIP the SAME BUNDLE for EVERY INSTANCE of the BLOCK' },
];

Blocks are not a substitute for shortcodes. A shortcode can be placed anywhere including inside a classic widget, a reusable block and a template part that predates the block editor. Choosing a block where a shortcode would work removes a placement surface integrators depend on. Provide both if both are relevant, and document the equivalence.

Exercises 5

  1. Register a metadata-driven block from block.json with no PHP registration call beyond register_block_type and confirm the attributes survive a save and reload cycle.
  2. Implement a classic widget whose form() survives a theme change without losing its stored instance.
  3. Add viewScript and prove it is absent from a page that contains no instance of the block.
  4. Build the same balance display three ways — shortcode, classic widget and server-rendered block — and diff the HTML each produces.

6 · Settings, Options and the Settings API

The options APIThe autoload decisionThe Settings APIRendering and submitting a formSanitisation callbacksErrors, notices and outside-the-API options

Options are the standard key-value store for plugin configuration. The Settings API wraps them with a registration contract, a sanitisation hook and a form renderer, all of which cooperate with WordPress core’s own permission and nonce handling.

The options API

Reading and writing options, and why the return value lies to you

The options API is the smallest thing in the platform and has three behaviours that surprise people, all of them about the difference between "the option has a value" and "the option has been written". Reading an option returns its stored value, or the default you passed, and the default is returned whether the option is absent or present-and-equal-to-the-default — which means you cannot distinguish those two cases by reading. Writing an option only writes when the value differs from what is stored,, so a write with the same value does not update the modification time, does not fire the update hook, and returns false. And the update hook fires only on a real change, which is correct and which means a hook callback is not a reliable way to notice that a particular function ran. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The write function returns false in three distinct situations — the option did not exist and the new value equals the default, the value is unchanged, or a filter rejected it — and it does not distinguish between them, so code that treats a false return as an error produces errors on the most common path of all. Options are autoloaded, or they are not, and which one is a declaration made at write time and cannot be changed afterwards without an explicit migration — which is the autoload section's subject and the reason the decision is hard to reverse. And an option is one row, so a large option is a large row, which interacts with the caching layer's behaviour and with the backup size in a way a table does not. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Treating a false write as a failure, which is the three-way ambiguity above and which produces an error message on a successful save. Writing a serialised structure with no version marker, which means the reader has to guess which shape it is in and cannot migrate it when the shape changes. And using an option as a cache, which is the right shape and the wrong use of this API — the platform's own cache object exists for that, it handles serialisation and expiry and invalidation, and an option used as a cache is a cache that never expires and is not invalidated by anything the platform does. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Store a version inside the value and check it on read, so a schema change is a branch on a number rather than a guess at a shape, and so an old value can be migrated rather than discarded. And write through one function, which applies the version marker, checks the type and normalises the value — because an option written from three places is an option whose shape is decided by whichever code ran last.

/* Three behaviours. */

const BEHAVIOURS = [
  { b: 'READING AN OPTION RETURNS ITS STORED VALUE, OR the DEFAULT YOU PASSED',
    consequence: ['and the DEFAULT IS RETURNED WHETHER the OPTION IS ABSENT or PRESENT-AND-EQUAL-TO-the DEFAULT',
      'WHICH MEANS YOU CANNOT DISTINGUISH THOSE TWO CASES BY READING'] },
  { b: 'WRITING AN OPTION ONLY WRITES WHEN the VALUE DIFFERS FROM WHAT IS STORED',
    consequences: ['SO A WRITE WITH the SAME VALUE DOES NOT UPDATE the MODIFICATION TIME', 'DOES NOT FIRE the UPDATE HOOK', 'and RETURNS FALSE'] },
  { b: 'the UPDATE HOOK FIRES ONLY ON A REAL CHANGE', correct: true,
    and: 'WHICH MEANS A HOOK CALLBACK IS NOT A RELIABLE WAY TO NOTICE THAT A PARTICULAR FUNCTION RAN' },
];

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the WRITE FUNCTION RETURNS FALSE IN THREE DISTINCT SITUATIONS',
    list: ['the OPTION DID NOT EXIST and the NEW VALUE EQUALS the DEFAULT', 'the VALUE IS UNCHANGED', 'OR A FILTER REJECTED IT'],
    and: 'IT DOES NOT DISTINGUISH BETWEEN THEM',
    symptom: 'SO CODE THAT TREATS A FALSE RETURN AS AN ERROR PRODUCES ERRORS ON the MOST COMMON PATH of ALL' },
  { c: 'OPTIONS ARE AUTOLOADED, OR THEY ARE NOT',
    detail: ['and WHICH ONE IS A DECLARATION MADE AT WRITE TIME and CANNOT BE CHANGED AFTERWARDS WITHOUT AN EXPLICIT MIGRATION'],
    connects: 'WHICH IS the AUTOLOAD SECTIONS SUBJECT and the REASON the DECISION IS HARD TO REVERSE' },
  { c: 'AN OPTION IS ONE ROW, SO A LARGE OPTION IS A LARGE ROW',
    detail: 'WHICH INTERACTS WITH the CACHING LAYERS BEHAVIOUR and WITH the BACKUP SIZE IN A WAY A TABLE DOES NOT' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'TREATING A FALSE WRITE AS A FAILURE', detail: 'WHICH IS the THREE-WAY AMBIGUITY ABOVE', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES AN ERROR MESSAGE ON A SUCCESSFUL SAVE' },
  { f: 'WRITING A SERIALISED STRUCTURE with NO VERSION MARKER',
    consequence: 'WHICH MEANS the READER HAS TO GUESS WHICH SHAPE IT IS IN and CANNOT MIGRATE IT WHEN the SHAPE CHANGES' },
  { f: 'USING AN OPTION AS A CACHE', isTheRightShape: true, wrongUse: true,
    detail: ['the PLATFORMS OWN CACHE OBJECT EXISTS for THAT', 'IT HANDLES SERIALISATION and EXPIRY and INVALIDATION'],
    symptom: 'and AN OPTION USED AS A CACHE IS A CACHE THAT NEVER EXPIRES and IS NOT INVALIDATED BY ANYTHING the PLATFORM DOES' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'STORE A VERSION INSIDE the VALUE and CHECK IT ON READ',
    gain: ['SO A SCHEMA CHANGE IS A BRANCH ON A NUMBER rather than A GUESS AT A SHAPE',
      'and SO AN OLD VALUE CAN BE MIGRATED rather than DISCARDED'] },
  { p: 'WRITE THROUGH ONE FUNCTION', how: ['WHICH APPLIES the VERSION MARKER', 'CHECKS the TYPE', 'and NORMALISES the VALUE'],
    why: 'BECAUSE AN OPTION WRITTEN FROM THREE PLACES IS AN OPTION WHOSE SHAPE IS DECIDED BY WHICHEVER CODE RAN LAST' },
];

Four functions cover the whole surface: get_option, update_option, add_option and delete_option. Each fires a filter, and the option_{$name} filters are the documented extension point for other plugins.

$value = get_option( 'acme_ledger_options', array() );

update_option( 'acme_ledger_options', $value, false );

add_option( 'acme_ledger_installed_at', time(), '', false );

if ( delete_option( 'acme_ledger_options' ) ) {
    // Removed.
}

update_option returns false in three distinct situations: the option did not exist and the new value equals the default, the value is unchanged, or a filter rejected it. It does not distinguish between them, so never treat a false return as failure without checking the value independently.

Options are stored as strings in a single wp_options row and serialised automatically. The serialisation is safe only for arrays and objects. A raw string containing a semicolon-prefixed serialised value is a genuine injection vector; maybe_serialize() escapes the leading semicolon for this reason.

The autoload decision

Autoloaded options are loaded into memory on every single request, on every page, for every visitor. A plugin that autoloads a 2 MB array of cached data is a measurable site-wide performance defect, not a local one.

The third argument to update_option() controls autoloading. WordPress 6.6 generalised it: pass true to force, false to suppress, or 'auto' to let WordPress decide by measuring the value size against wp_max_autoloaded_option_size.

// Small, always needed on every request: safe to autoload.
update_option( 'acme_ledger_currency', 'EUR', true );

// Large or rarely needed: never autoload, read it only where needed.
update_option( 'acme_ledger_exchange_rates', $rates, false );

// Let the size heuristic decide. 150000 bytes, 1 second, 128 MB.
update_option( 'acme_ledger_cache', $cache, 'auto' );

if ( ! function_exists( 'wp_autoload_values_to_autoload' ) ) {
    require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/option.php';
}

foreach ( wp_autoload_values_to_autoload() as $value ) {
    error_log( $value );
}

The autoload decision, and why it is hard to reverse

An option is either autoloaded — loaded on every request before your code runs — or it is fetched when you ask for it. That one flag is the most consequential decision a plugin makes about its own data, and it is worth understanding exactly what it costs because the costs are asymmetric. An autoloaded option is loaded on every request of every kind, including requests that never touch your plugin: a front-end page, a cron run, a REST call, a login request, an update check. A non-autoloaded option is loaded only when something reads it, and it is cached in the object cache after the first read within a request. Then the three consequences, and each is a mechanism. The autoload cost is paid by every request on the site, not by your plugin's requests, so the cost appears in a form nobody attributes to you: the site is slightly slower everywhere and the plugin's own pages look fine. The flag cannot be changed by writing the option again, because the autoload value is a property of the option row set at creation, so changing it requires a deliberate migration — which is what makes it a decision rather than a default. And an autoloaded option that grows becomes a per-request cost on every page, so a plugin that autoloads a list and then appends to it degrades the whole site rather than its own feature. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Autoloading per-request data, — a per-user token, a computed total — because "it is always available" is true and "it is available on every request from every user" is the cost, and the mechanism behind "the site got slow after I installed a plugin". Autoloading a large value, which pays the serialisation and the load on every request for a value most requests do not need. And autoloading something that should be recomputed, which produces an option that is stale by construction and which nobody refreshes because refreshing it would mean writing it, which does not fire the hook. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Autoload only small, site-wide configuration — version numbers, feature flags that everything reads, a toggle everybody needs — and let everything else be fetched on demand. And write a migration that changes the flag, before you ship the version that needs it, because a plugin that decides in version three that a setting should not be autoloaded and does not migrate it is paying the cost forever on every site that installed version one.

/* The decision. */

const DECISION = {
  is: 'AN OPTION IS EITHER AUTOLOADED - LOADED ON EVERY REQUEST BEFORE YOUR CODE RUNS - OR IT IS FETCHED WHEN YOU ASK FOR IT',
  mostConsequential: 'THAT ONE FLAG IS the MOST CONSEQUENTIAL DECISION A PLUGIN MAKES ABOUT ITS OWN DATA',
  autoloaded: ['LOADED ON EVERY REQUEST OF EVERY KIND', 'INCLUDING REQUESTS THAT NEVER TOUCH YOUR PLUGIN: A FRONT-END PAGE, A CRON RUN, A REST CALL, A LOGIN REQUEST, AN UPDATE CHECK'],
  notAutoloaded: ['LOADED ONLY WHEN SOMETHING READS IT', 'and IT IS CACHED IN the OBJECT CACHE AFTER the FIRST READ WITHIN A REQUEST'],
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the AUTOLOAD COST IS PAID BY EVERY REQUEST ON the SITE, NOT BY YOUR PLUGINS REQUESTS',
    symptom: 'SO the COST APPEARS IN A FORM NOBODY ATTRIBUTES TO YOU - the SITE IS SLIGHTLY SLOWER EVERYWHERE and the PLUGINS OWN PAGES LOOK FINE' },
  { c: 'the FLAG CANNOT BE CHANGED BY WRITING the OPTION AGAIN',
    why: 'BECAUSE the AUTOLOAD VALUE IS A PROPERTY of the OPTION ROW SET AT CREATION',
    therefore: 'SO CHANGING IT REQUIRES A DELIBERATE MIGRATION - WHICH IS WHAT MAKES IT A DECISION rather than A DEFAULT' },
  { c: 'AN AUTOLOADED OPTION THAT GROWS BECOMES A PER-REQUEST COST ON EVERY PAGE',
    symptom: 'SO A PLUGIN THAT AUTOLOADS A LIST and THEN APPENDS TO IT DEGRADES the WHOLE SITE rather than ITS OWN FEATURE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'AUTOLOADING PER-REQUEST DATA', instances: ['A PER-USER TOKEN', 'A COMPUTED TOTAL'],
    reasoning: ['BECAUSE it is always available IS TRUE', 'and it is AVAILABLE ON EVERY REQUEST FROM EVERY USER IS the COST'],
    symptom: 'and the MECHANISM BEHIND the site got slow after I installed a plugin' },
  { f: 'AUTOLOADING A LARGE VALUE', detail: 'WHICH PAYS the SERIALISATION and the LOAD ON EVERY REQUEST for A VALUE MOST REQUESTS DO NOT NEED' },
  { f: 'AUTOLOADING SOMETHING THAT SHOULD BE RECOMPUTED', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES AN OPTION THAT IS STALE BY CONSTRUCTION', why: 'and WHICH NOBODY REFRESHES BECAUSE REFRESHING IT WOULD MEAN WRITING IT, WHICH DOES NOT FIRE the HOOK' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'AUTOLOAD ONLY SMALL, SITE-WIDE CONFIGURATION', instances: ['VERSION NUMBERS', 'FEATURE FLAGS THAT EVERYTHING READS', 'A TOGGLE EVERYBODY NEEDS'], and: 'and LET EVERYTHING ELSE BE FETCHED ON DEMAND' },
  { p: 'WRITE A MIGRATION THAT CHANGES the FLAG, BEFORE YOU SHIP the VERSION THAT NEEDS IT',
    why: 'BECAUSE A PLUGIN THAT DECIDES IN VERSION THREE THAT A SETTING SHOULD NOT BE AUTOLOADED and DOES NOT MIGRATE IT IS PAYING the COST FOREVER ON EVERY SITE THAT INSTALLED VERSION ONE' },
];

Never autoload per-request data. A common pattern stores a per-user token or a computed total in an autoloaded option so that “it is always available”. This defeats the entire autoload mechanism. Use transients for time-limited values and user meta or a keyed cache for per-user values.

The Settings API

What the Settings API actually does, and what it decides for you

The Settings API is a layer over the options API that trades control for consistency, and knowing exactly what it decides for you is what tells you whether it is the right layer for a given screen. You declare a group of settings, the platform builds the page around them, and it owns the form's plumbing. It emits the form wrapper, the nonce, the option-group field, the submit button's name and the reference to the handler that saves. You describe each field as a type and a label and an argument list, and the platform renders the appropriate control, escapes the label, and remembers the value. That is the whole mechanism, and it is worth being precise about because the consequence is that your screen's markup is the platform's, not yours — you cannot add a description under a field, cannot put two controls on one row, cannot change the order without changing the registration order. Then the three things that follow, and each is a consequence. Each field belongs to exactly one option, which means the API's model is one setting per row and a setting that logically belongs to a small structure has to be either split across rows or stored as one serialised row and edited through one field — and the second is a design decision the API cannot make for you. The field type determines the input control and some of the sanitisation, so choosing a type is choosing both a control and a class of validation, and choosing the wrong one produces either a control the user cannot use or a sanitiser that does not match the data. And the save handler receives the whole group at once, so saving is a batch operation whose per-field validation must be done field by field inside one function, which is where the error-reporting discipline the next sections describe comes from. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Registering fields outside the group's section, which produces a field that renders nowhere and saves nothing, because the section assignment is what places it. Using one field for a list of things, which produces a single control editing a serialised array and a user who cannot see what is in the array. And registering the same field twice, which produces two controls for one value that overwrite each other on save, in an order determined by which one the browser submitted last. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Register settings in a function that runs once and returns them, so the declaration is a value you can inspect and test rather than a sequence of calls with side effects. And keep the group to one screen's worth of settings, because the API's model is a group that is saved together and validated together, and a group that spans two screens means a save that validates fields the user cannot see.

/* What it does. */

const DOES = {
  declaration: ['YOU DECLARE A GROUP of SETTINGS', 'the PLATFORM BUILDS the PAGE AROUND THEM', 'and IT OWNS the FORMS PLUMBING'],
  owns: ['the FORM WRAPPER', 'the NONCE', 'the OPTION-GROUP FIELD', 'the SUBMIT BUTTONS NAME', 'and the REFERENCE TO the HANDLER THAT SAVES'],
  fieldDescription: ['YOU DESCRIBE EACH FIELD AS A TYPE and A LABEL and AN ARGUMENT LIST',    'and the PLATFORM RENDERS the APPROPRIATE CONTROL, ESCAPES the LABEL, and REMEMBERS the VALUE'],
  consequence: 'YOUR SCREENS MARKUP IS the PLATFORMS, NOT YOURS',
  cannot: ['ADD A DESCRIPTION UNDER A FIELD', 'PUT TWO CONTROLS ON ONE ROW', 'CHANGE the ORDER WITHOUT CHANGING the REGISTRATION ORDER'],
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'EACH FIELD BELONGS TO EXACTLY ONE OPTION',
    detail: 'WHICH MEANS the APIS MODEL IS ONE SETTING PER ROW',
    problem: 'and A SETTING THAT LOGICALLY BELONGS TO A SMALL STRUCTURE HAS TO BE EITHER SPLIT ACROSS ROWS or STORED AS ONE SERIALISED ROW and EDITED THROUGH ONE FIELD',
    and: 'the SECOND IS A DESIGN DECISION the API CANNOT MAKE for YOU' },
  { c: 'the FIELD TYPE DETERMINES the INPUT CONTROL and SOME of the SANITISATION',
    detail: 'SO CHOOSING A TYPE IS CHOOSING BOTH A CONTROL and A CLASS of VALIDATION',
    symptom: 'CHOOSING the WRONG ONE PRODUCES EITHER A CONTROL the USER CANNOT USE or A SANITISER THAT DOES NOT MATCH the DATA' },
  { c: 'the SAVE HANDLER RECEIVES the WHOLE GROUP AT ONCE',
