];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'REGISTERING FIELDS OUTSIDE the GROUPS SECTION',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A FIELD THAT RENDERS NOWHERE and SAVES NOTHING',
    because: 'BECAUSE the SECTION ASSIGNMENT IS WHAT PLACES IT' },
  { f: 'USING ONE FIELD for A LIST of THINGS',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A SINGLE CONTROL EDITING A SERIALISED ARRAY',
    and: 'A USER WHO CANNOT SEE WHAT IS IN the ARRAY' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING the SAME FIELD TWICE',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES TWO CONTROLS FOR ONE VALUE THAT OVERWRITE EACH OTHER ON SAVE',
    order: 'IN AN ORDER DETERMINED BY WHICH ONE the BROWSER SUBMITTED LAST' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'REGISTER SETTINGS IN A FUNCTION THAT RUNS ONCE and RETURNS THEM',
    gain: 'SO the DECLARATION IS A VALUE YOU CAN INSPECT and TEST rather than A SEQUENCE of CALLS with SIDE EFFECTS' },
  { p: 'KEEP the GROUP TO ONE SCREENS WORTH of SETTINGS',
    why: 'BECAUSE the APIS MODEL IS A GROUP THAT IS SAVED TOGETHER and VALIDATED TOGETHER',
    symptom: 'and A GROUP THAT SPANS TWO SCREENS MEANS A SAVE THAT VALIDATES FIELDS the USER CANNOT SEE' },
];

The Settings API separates declaration from rendering. You declare sections and fields once on admin_init, and WordPress renders the markup, handles the nonce, verifies the capability and dispatches the sanitisation callback.

add_action( 'admin_init', 'acme_ledger_register_settings' );

function acme_ledger_register_settings(): void {
    register_setting(
        'acme_ledger_group',
        'acme_ledger_options',
        array(
            'type'              => 'object',
            'default'           => array(
                'currency'        => 'EUR',
                'decimal_separator' => ',',
                'decimals'        => 2,
                'strict_mode'     => false,
            ),
            'sanitize_callback' => 'acme_ledger_sanitize_options',
            'show_in_rest'      => true,
        )
    );

    add_settings_section(
        'acme_ledger_formatting',
        __( 'Amount formatting', 'acme-ledger' ),
        static function(): void {
            echo <p class="description">
                <?= esc_html__( 'Affects every amount rendered by this plugin.', 'acme-ledger' ) ?>
            </p>;
        },
        'acme-ledger'
    );

    add_settings_field(
        'acme_ledger_currency',
        __( 'Currency code', 'acme-ledger' ),
        static function(): void {
            $value = Ledger_Settings::get( 'currency' );
            ?>
            <input type="text"
                name="acme_ledger_options[currency]"
                value="<?= esc_attr( $value ) ?>"
                maxlength="3" pattern="[A-Za-z]{3}" required>
            <?php
        },
        'acme_ledger_formatting',
        'acme_ledger_currency',
        array( 'label_for' => 'acme_ledger_options_currency' )
    );
}

The label_for argument causes WordPress to render the <label for> attribute for you. Supplying a unique id on your input and referencing it here is what makes the form pass an accessibility audit; WordPress does not generate the id for you.

Rendering and submitting a form

The submit path, and the four things that must happen on it

A settings screen has two halves that meet at one submit, and the half that receives the submit is the security boundary and the half that renders the form is not. The form must emit the fields the platform's own submit handler expects — the option-group name, the action, and the nonce — which is what the platform's own form-emission function does, and which must not be assembled by hand because the nonce field names are part of the contract with the handler. The handler must verify the nonce before anything else, because a handler that validates input before verifying the nonce has already acted on unauthenticated data. The handler must verify the referer for the same reason, and the platform's referer check is what ties the submission to a page the user actually loaded. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The nonce is what makes the request unforgeable for a third party, and it is not what makes it unique for the user — a nonce is created for a session and action, not for a request, so replaying it within its lifetime is possible and is why the handler should be idempotent. The capability check belongs in the handler, not in the render, because the handler is the only code that runs when the request is actually made — a page that hides the form still posts to the handler. And a redirect after saving is what makes the notice survive, because a message set in the handler is consumed by the next request and a redirect is what produces that next request — which is why a handler that renders the form again loses its own success message. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Checking the nonce after sanitising, which acts on attacker-controlled input first, and which is the exact ordering the platform's own examples demonstrate to be wrong. Omitting the redirect, which produces a success message that never appears and a browser warning about resubmission. And saving only the fields the form contains, which means a field the user cleared is not saved at all because it was absent from the submission — so a checkbox that is unchecked disappears from the data rather than becoming false. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Write the handler as a fixed sequence — nonce, referer, capability, validate, save, redirect — in that order, and do not add a step before the nonce for any reason. And treat every field as submitted on every save, filling absent fields with their defaults before validation, so that clearing a field is a saveable state rather than a deletion.

/* The two halves. */

const HALVES = {
  render: 'the HALF THAT RENDERS the FORM IS NOT the SECURITY BOUNDARY',
  receive: 'the HALF THAT RECEIVES the SUBMIT IS the SECURITY BOUNDARY',
};

/* What the form must emit. */
const FORM_EMITS = [
  'the OPTION-GROUP NAME',
  'the ACTION',
  'and the NONCE',
];
const WHY_NOT_BY_HAND = 'WHICH IS WHAT the PLATFORMS OWN FORM-EMISSION FUNCTION DOES, and WHICH MUST NOT BE ASSEMBLED BY HAND BECAUSE the NONCE FIELD NAMES ARE PART of the CONTRACT WITH the HANDLER';

/* Three things that must happen on the handler. */
const MUST = [
  { s: 'VERIFY the NONCE BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE',
    why: 'BECAUSE A HANDLER THAT VALIDATES INPUT BEFORE VERIFYING the NONCE HAS ALREADY ACTED ON UNAUTHENTICATED DATA' },
  { s: 'VERIFY the REFERER FOR the SAME REASON',
    and: 'the PLATFORMS REFERER CHECK IS WHAT TIES the SUBMISSION TO A PAGE the USER ACTUALLY LOADED' },
  { s: 'VERIFY the CAPABILITY',
    where: 'IN the HANDLER, NOT IN the RENDER',
    why: 'BECAUSE the HANDLER IS the ONLY CODE THAT RUNS WHEN the REQUEST IS ACTUALLY MADE',    symptom: 'A PAGE THAT HIDES the FORM STILL POSTS TO the HANDLER' },
];

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the NONCE IS WHAT MAKES the REQUEST UNFORGEABLE FOR A THIRD PARTY',
    isNot: 'IT IS NOT WHAT MAKES IT UNIQUE FOR the USER',
    detail: 'A NONCE IS CREATED FOR A SESSION and ACTION, NOT FOR A REQUEST',    consequence: 'SO REPLAYING IT WITHIN ITS LIFETIME IS POSSIBLE',    therefore: 'and IS WHY the HANDLER SHOULD BE IDEMPOTENT' },
  { c: 'A REDIRECT AFTER SAVING IS WHAT MAKES the NOTICE SURVIVE',
    detail: 'BECAUSE A MESSAGE SET IN the HANDLER IS CONSUMED BY the NEXT REQUEST and A REDIRECT IS WHAT PRODUCES THAT NEXT REQUEST',
    symptom: 'WHICH IS WHY A HANDLER THAT RENDERS the FORM AGAIN LOSES ITS OWN SUCCESS MESSAGE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'CHECKING the NONCE AFTER SANITISING', detail: 'WHICH ACTS ON ATTACKER-CONTROLLED INPUT FIRST', and: 'WHICH IS the EXACT ORDERING the PLATFORMS OWN EXAMPLES DEMONSTRATE to BE WRONG' },
  { f: 'OMITTING the REDIRECT', symptoms: ['A SUCCESS MESSAGE THAT NEVER APPEARS', 'A BROWSER WARNING ABOUT RESUBMISSION'] },
  { f: 'SAVING ONLY the FIELDS the FORM CONTAINS',
    detail: 'WHICH MEANS A FIELD the USER CLEARED IS NOT SAVED AT ALL BECAUSE IT WAS ABSENT FROM the SUBMISSION',    symptom: 'SO A CHECKBOX THAT IS UNCHECKED DISAPPEARS FROM the DATA rather than BECOMING FALSE' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'WRITE the HANDLER AS A FIXED SEQUENCE - NONCE, REFERER, CAPABILITY, VALIDATE, SAVE, REDIRECT',
    detail: 'IN THAT ORDER',
    rule: 'and DO NOT ADD A STEP BEFORE the NONCE for ANY REASON' },
  { p: 'TREAT EVERY FIELD AS SUBMITTED ON EVERY SAVE',
    how: 'FILLING ABSENT FIELDS WITH THEIR DEFAULTS BEFORE VALIDATION',
    gain: 'SO THAT CLEARING A FIELD IS A SAVABLE STATE rather than A DELETION' },
];

The form posts to options.php, which is the only endpoint permitted to write settings. Three function calls produce the nonces, the option page slug and the section output.

<form method="post" action="<?= esc_url( admin_url( 'options.php' ) ) ?>">
    <?php
    settings_fields( 'acme_ledger_group' );
    do_settings_sections( 'acme-ledger' );
    submit_button();
    ?>
</form>

settings_fields() emits the option_page hidden field, the action hidden field and both nonce fields. check_admin_referer( $option_page . '-options' ) is what options.php performs; the naming convention is {$option_page}-options and you must not construct the nonce yourself.

WordPress 6.7 renders an additional option_page derived nonce for options that use show_in_rest. No action is required, but the behaviour means an options group can be saved from the REST settings endpoint as well as from the form.

Sanitisation callbacks

The sanitisation callback is the only place where a submitted value is trusted, and it is the plugin’s responsibility. Returning unsanitised input from this callback is the most common Settings API defect.

The callback receives the merged input array and must return a fully normalised array. It is not a validation layer that can fail gracefully; it is a transformation that cannot fail. Every key must be present in the return value, because update_option() replaces rather than merges.

function acme_ledger_sanitize_options( array $input ): array {
    $defaults = array(
        'currency'         => 'EUR',
        'decimal_separator' => ',',
        'decimals'         => 2,
        'strict_mode'      => false,
    );

    $currency = strtoupper( sanitize_text_field( $input['currency'] ?? '' ) );

    if ( ! preg_match( '/^[A-Z]{3}$/', $currency ) ) {
        add_settings_error(
            'acme_ledger_options',
            'acme_ledger_currency_invalid',
            __( 'Currency must be a three-letter ISO code.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'error'
        );

        $currency = $defaults['currency'];
    }

    $separator = ( $input['decimal_separator'] ?? ',' ) === '.' ? '.' : ',';

    return array(
        'currency'         => $currency,
        'decimal_separator' => $separator,
        'decimals'         => max( 0, min( 6, (int) ( $input['decimals'] ?? 2 ) ) ),
        'strict_mode'      => ! empty( $input['strict_mode'] ),
    );
}

Sanitising, and the difference between cleaning and validating

Sanitisation and validation are different operations with different purposes, and conflating them produces either data that was silently changed or errors the user did not expect. Sanitisation makes a value safe to store and to output; it never rejects. A sanitiser takes whatever it is given and returns a version of it that cannot cause harm — text with markup removed, a number cast, a value restricted to a set. Validation decides whether a value is acceptable, and it rejects. A validator returns a result saying yes or no, and the screen must then decide what to do about the no. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Sanitisation cannot be the only defence for output, because it is applied once at write time and the value can be modified between write and read by another plugin, by a direct database edit, or by an import — which is why the output must be escaped at the point of output regardless of how carefully it was sanitised on the way in. Validation must produce a message the user can act on, and a validator that returns a boolean without saying what was wrong produces a screen that rejects the submission with no explanation, which is the same defect as the escaping section's. And the two compose, in that order: validate the shape of the input, then sanitise the value you are about to store, and never use a sanitiser to decide whether the input was acceptable. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Sanitising silently and reporting success, which is defensible for a field whose sanitised value is equivalent and indefensible for one that is not — a field where the sanitiser truncates at the maximum length should tell the user it truncated. Validating with a rule the database cannot enforce, which produces data that passed validation and cannot be stored, which surfaces as a database error on a submission the user was told was fine. And reusing one sanitiser for two fields with different shapes, which produces a field that is over-sanitised — a markup-stripping sanitiser applied to a field that legitimately contains angle brackets — and which is invisible because the data is merely wrong rather than broken. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Have a sanitiser per field, registered as that field's sanitiser, so that the mapping between a field and its cleaning rule is a declaration you can read and so that a new field cannot be added without one. And make every validator return a reason, not a boolean, because a reason can be shown to the user and a boolean cannot, and the one-line difference is the difference between a rejected form and an understood one.

/* Two operations. */

const OPERATIONS = [
  { o: 'SANITISATION', makes: 'A VALUE SAFE TO STORE and TO OUTPUT', neverRejects: true,
    takes: 'WHATEVER IT IS GIVEN and RETURNS A VERSION of IT THAT CANNOT CAUSE HARM',
    examples: ['TEXT with MARKUP REMOVED', 'A NUMBER CAST', 'A VALUE RESTRICTED TO A SET'] },
  { o: 'VALIDATION', decides: 'WHETHER A VALUE IS ACCEPTABLE', rejects: true,
    returns: 'A RESULT SAYING YES or NO',
    and: 'the SCREEN MUST THEN DECIDE WHAT TO DO ABOUT the NO' },
];
/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'SANITISATION CANNOT BE the ONLY DEFENCE for OUTPUT',
    why: 'BECAUSE IT IS APPLIED ONCE AT WRITE TIME',    couldChange: ['ANOTHER PLUGIN', 'A DIRECT DATABASE EDIT', 'AN IMPORT'],    therefore: 'WHICH IS WHY the OUTPUT MUST BE ESCAPED AT the POINT of OUTPUT REGARDLESS of HOW CAREFULLY IT WAS SANITISED ON the WAY IN' },
  { c: 'VALIDATION MUST PRODUCE A MESSAGE the USER CAN ACT ON',
    symptom: 'and A VALIDATOR THAT RETURNS A BOOLEAN WITHOUT SAYING WHAT WAS WRONG PRODUCES A SCREEN THAT REJECTS the SUBMISSION with NO EXPLANATION',
    connects: 'WHICH IS the SAME DEFECT as the ESCAPING SECTIONS' },
  { c: 'the TWO COMPOSE, IN THAT ORDER',
    sequence: ['VALIDATE the SHAPE of the INPUT', 'then SANITISE the VALUE YOU ARE ABOUT TO STORE'],
    rule: 'and NEVER USE A SANITISER to DECIDE WHETHER the INPUT WAS ACCEPTABLE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'SANITISING SILENTLY and REPORTING SUCCESS',
    defensibleFor: 'A FIELD WHOSE SANITISED VALUE IS EQUIVALENT',
    indefensibleFor: 'ONE THAT IS NOT - A FIELD WHERE the SANITISER TRUNCATES AT the MAXIMUM LENGTH',
    requirement: 'SHOULD TELL the USER IT TRUNCATED' },
  { f: 'VALIDATING with A RULE the DATABASE CANNOT ENFORCE',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES DATA THAT PASSED VALIDATION and CANNOT BE STORED',
    and: 'WHICH SURFACES AS A DATABASE ERROR ON A SUBMISSION the USER WAS TOLD WAS FINE' },
  { f: 'REUSING ONE SANITISER for TWO FIELDS with DIFFERENT SHAPES',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A FIELD THAT IS OVER-SANITISED - A MARKUP-STRIPPING SANITISER APPLIED TO A FIELD THAT LEGITIMATELY CONTAINS ANGLE BRACKETS',
    invisible: 'and WHICH IS INVISIBLE BECAUSE the DATA IS MERELY WRONG rather than BROKEN' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'HAVE A SANITISER PER FIELD, REGISTERED AS that FIELDS SANITISER',
    gain: ['SO THAT the MAPPING BETWEEN A FIELD and ITS CLEANING RULE IS A DECLARATION YOU CAN READ',      'and SO THAT A NEW FIELD CANNOT BE ADDED WITHOUT ONE'] },
  { p: 'MAKE EVERY VALIDATOR RETURN A REASON, NOT A BOOLEAN',
    why: 'BECAUSE A REASON CAN BE SHOWN TO the USER and A BOOLEAN CANNOT',
    verdict: 'and the ONE-LINE DIFFERENCE IS the DIFFERENCE BETWEEN A REJECTED FORM and AN UNDERSTOOD ONE' },
];

Validate and report, do not silently coerce. Falling back to a default when the input is invalid is defensible only if the user is told. add_settings_error() messages are displayed automatically on the same page after a save, which makes a rejected field visible without any extra code.

Errors, notices and outside-the-API options

Not every value belongs in the options table. Transient data, per-user state and large caches have dedicated stores, and choosing the wrong one produces either a performance defect or a correctness defect.

DataStoreReason
Plugin configurationOption via register_settingSettings API provides nonce, capability and sanitisation.
Computed cache, expiringTransientSelf-expiring, not autoloaded, invalidatable.
Per-user preferenceUser metaScoped to the user, not loaded for everyone.
Per-post dataPost metaScoped to the post, queryable, revisionable.
Bulk or relational dataCustom tableNormalised, indexed, joined in SQL. See Chapter 8.
Network-wide configurationSite optionSee Chapter 11 for the multisite API.
// Transient: expires on its own, never autoloaded.
set_transient( 'acme_ledger_rates_2026_01', $rates, DAY_IN_SECONDS );
$rates = get_transient( 'acme_ledger_rates_2026_01' );

// User meta: per user, per capability to serialise.
update_user_meta( get_current_user_id(), '_acme_ledger_currency', 'GBP' );
$currency = get_user_meta( get_current_user_id(), '_acme_ledger_currency', true );

// Cache group invalidation for a query your code ran.
wp_cache_delete( 'acme_ledger_totals', 'acme-ledger' );

Reporting a failure without losing the user's work

A validation failure has an awkward constraint that most implementations get wrong in one direction or the other: the screen must tell the user what was wrong, and it must not destroy what the user typed to tell them. The submission is a request that carries the form's contents. When the handler rejects it, the contents are still in that request and are gone when the response is sent — so the screen that re-renders must have been given them again, either by storing them temporarily or by the browser resubmitting the form. The platform's mechanism is a transient: a short-lived stored value keyed by the user. The handler puts the submitted values and the error messages into a transient, redirects to the settings screen, and the screen's render reads the transient back and consumes it. That round trip is what lets the message survive, and it is the same mechanism the notices chapter uses for one-shot messages. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. A transient must be consumed exactly once, because a value left in place reappears on the next page load, which is the mechanism behind "my error message is still there after I fixed it". Errors must be keyed by field, not concatenated into one message, because the screen needs to place each message next to the field it belongs to and a user cannot act on "some fields are invalid". And the submitted values must be re-rendered escaped, because they are user input and the screen that shows them back is the one place a value the user supplied meets the page unescaped. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Rendering the form with defaults instead of the submitted values, which discards everything the user typed, and which is the most common variant because re-rendering with defaults is the easy path. Reporting success after a partial save, which produces a screen that says saved while one field was rejected, and which is the same bug as the field-error case at a coarser grain. And storing errors in a global rather than per user, which produces one user's error message appearing on another user's screen — a genuine data leak and one that happens whenever a shared cache holds a per-user value. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Report errors per field and highlight the field, because a message next to the input is actionable and a summary at the top is not, and because the association is what a user needs. And always re-render the submitted values, escaped, rather than the stored ones — the stored ones are the last save, which is not what the user is looking at, and using them silently discards their edits.

/* The constraint. */

const CONSTRAINT = {
  must: ['TELL the USER WHAT WAS WRONG', 'and NOT DESTROY WHAT the USER TYPED TO TELL THEM'],
  fact: 'the SUBMISSION IS A REQUEST THAT CARRIES the FORMS CONTENTS',
  consequence: 'WHEN the HANDLER REJECTS IT, the CONTENTS ARE STILL IN that REQUEST and ARE GONE WHEN the RESPONSE IS SENT',
  therefore: 'SO the SCREEN THAT RE-RENDERS MUST HAVE BEEN GIVEN THEM AGAIN',  how: ['EITHER BY STORING THEM TEMPORARILY', 'or BY the BROWSER RESUBMITTING the FORM'],
};
const MECHANISM = {
  is: 'A TRANSIENT - A SHORT-LIVED STORED VALUE KEYED BY the USER',
  flow: ['the HANDLER PUTS the SUBMITTED VALUES and the ERROR MESSAGES INTO A TRANSIENT',    'REDIRECTS TO the SETTINGS SCREEN',    'and the SCREENS RENDER READS the TRANSIENT BACK and CONSUMES IT'],
  why: 'THAT ROUND TRIP IS WHAT LETS the MESSAGE SURVIVE',
  connects: 'and IT IS the SAME MECHANISM the NOTICES CHAPTER USES for ONE-SHOT MESSAGES',
};
/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A TRANSIENT MUST BE CONSUMED EXACTLY ONCE',
    symptom: 'BECAUSE A VALUE LEFT IN PLACE REAPPEARS ON the NEXT PAGE LOAD',
    which: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND my error message is still there after I fixed it' },
  { c: 'ERRORS MUST BE KEYED BY FIELD, NOT CONCATENATED INTO ONE MESSAGE',
    why: 'BECAUSE the SCREEN NEEDS TO PLACE EACH MESSAGE NEXT TO the FIELD IT BELONGS TO',
    symptom: 'and A USER CANNOT ACT ON some fields are invalid' },
  { c: 'the SUBMITTED VALUES MUST BE RE-RENDERED ESCAPED',
    why: 'BECAUSE THEY ARE USER INPUT and the SCREEN THAT SHOWS THEM BACK IS the ONE PLACE A VALUE the USER SUPPLIED MEETS the PAGE UNESCAPED' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'RENDERING the FORM with DEFAULTS instead of the SUBMITTED VALUES',
    symptom: 'WHICH DISCARDS EVERYTHING the USER TYPED',
    rank: 'and WHICH IS the MOST COMMON VARIANT BECAUSE RE-RENDERING WITH DEFAULTS IS the EASY PATH' },
  { f: 'REPORTING SUCCESS AFTER A PARTIAL SAVE',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A SCREEN THAT SAYS SAVED WHILE ONE FIELD WAS REJECTED',
    which: 'and WHICH IS the SAME BUG as the FIELD-ERROR CASE AT A COARSER GRAIN' },
  { f: 'STORING ERRORS IN A GLOBAL rather than PER USER',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES ONE USERS ERROR MESSAGE APPEARING ON ANOTHER USERS SCREEN',
    is: 'A GENUINE DATA LEAK',    when: 'AND IT HAPPENS WHENEVER A SHARED CACHE HOLDS A PER-USER VALUE' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'REPORT ERRORS PER FIELD and HIGHLIGHT the FIELD',
    why: 'BECAUSE A MESSAGE NEXT TO the INPUT IS ACTIONABLE and A SUMMARY AT the TOP IS NOT',    and: 'BECAUSE the ASSOCIATION IS WHAT A USER NEEDS' },
  { p: 'ALWAYS RE-RENDER the SUBMITTED VALUES, ESCAPED, rather than the STORED ONES',
    why: 'the STORED ONES ARE the LAST SAVE, WHICH IS NOT WHAT the USER IS LOOKING AT',    symptom: 'and USING THEM SILENTLY DISCARDS THEIR EDITS' },
];

Meta keys beginning with an underscore are protected. In the post, user and term meta APIs, a key starting with _ is hidden from the custom fields UI. Use the convention for internal plugin data so that an editor cannot delete it by accident from the Custom Fields panel.

Exercises 6

  1. Build a settings group with four fields, a sanitisation callback that rejects a malformed ISO code, and an error message that renders after a failed save.
  2. Measure the effect of autoloading versus not autoloading a 500 KB array across 100 simulated page loads.
  3. Prove that a sanitisation callback returning a partial array deletes the omitted keys, and document the behaviour in a comment.
  4. Replace an autoloaded per-user option with user meta and confirm memory use drops on a page served to an anonymous visitor.

7 · Admin Pages, Notices and Menus

The admin menu APIPage capabilities and the access guardAdmin noticesDismissible and stateful noticesPer-screen asset loadingForm handlers and admin-post

The admin is a separate application surface with its own registration API, its own security model and its own performance characteristics. This chapter covers menu registration, the capability gate that must accompany every page, the notice system, and the correct way to handle a submitted form.

The admin menu API

Menu registration happens on admin_menu. Each function takes a capability as an argument, and that argument is the authoritative gate: WordPress removes the menu item from users who lack the capability, which is the first of two independent checks.

add_action( 'admin_menu', 'acme_ledger_register_menu' );

function acme_ledger_register_menu(): void {
    add_menu_page(
        __( 'Acme Ledger', 'acme-ledger' ),
        __( 'Ledger', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'manage_ledger',
        'acme-ledger',
        array( Ledger_Admin::class, 'render_dashboard' ),
        'dashicons-chart-bar',
        58
    );

    add_submenu_page(
        'acme-ledger',
        __( 'Entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
        __( 'Entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'manage_ledger',
        'acme-ledger-entries',
        array( Ledger_Admin::class, 'render_entries' )
    );

    add_submenu_page(
        'acme-ledger',
        __( 'Settings', 'acme-ledger' ),
        __( 'Settings', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'manage_options',
        'acme-ledger-settings',
        array( Ledger_Admin::class, 'render_settings' )
    );

    // Drop the auto-created duplicate of the top-level entry.
    remove_submenu_page( 'acme-ledger', 'acme-ledger' );
}

The menu slug becomes the hook suffix, which is how you scope asset loading. A top-level page registered as acme-ledger receives a hook suffix of toplevel_page_acme-ledger; a submenu page receives acme-ledger_page_acme-ledger-entries. Use get_current_screen() rather than string matching where you need the page identifier.

Registering admin pages, and the slug you will use for everything else

An admin page is registered as an item in a menu, and the registration gives you two things you need later: a page you can link to, and a slug that becomes a hook suffix used to scope everything else the page needs. A top-level page is registered with a menu title, an access capability, a slug and a render callback. The slug is the page's identity: it appears in the URL, it identifies the page, and — this is the part worth remembering — the slug becomes a hook suffix, so a page registered with one slug has a hook whose name contains that slug and nothing else. A submenu page is registered against a parent and produces a different suffix. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The hook suffix is the only reliable way to scope loading to your own screen, because enqueueing on a general admin hook loads your assets on every admin page — and the platform's own hook is the one that fires when your page is being rendered. The capability you declare is the capability the platform enforces, which means choosing a capability is choosing who can see the page, and it also means a page whose capability is too broad exposes its screen to users who should not have it. And the menu position is a number, so a plugin that picks an extreme position displaces everybody else's and a plugin that picks the same position as another is ordered by activation date — which means the position is a negotiation with the same character as a hook priority. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Registering the page but not scoping the assets, which loads your stylesheet and script on every admin screen, including screens where your code has nothing to contribute. Registering a page with a capability the plugin does not otherwise check, which produces a screen visible to a role that should not see it — and which is not the same as having the menu item hidden, because the URL still works. And registering the menu on the wrong hook, which produces a menu that does not appear because the registration hook fired before the menu system existed. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Derive every asset handle from the same slug constant the page is registered with, so the hook suffix, the handle and the file path cannot disagree — which is the mechanism behind an asset that loads on one page and not another for no visible reason. And give the page a capability and check it in the handler too, because the menu hides the link but the URL remains, and a page that relies on the menu for its access control is a page whose access control is a suggestion.

/* Registration. */

const REGISTER = {
  takes: ['A MENU TITLE', 'AN ACCESS CAPABILITY', 'A SLUG', 'and A RENDER CALLBACK'],
  slugIs: ['the PAGES IDENTITY', 'IT APPEARS IN the URL', 'IT IDENTIFIES the PAGE'],
  and: 'AND - THIS IS the PART WORTH REMEMBERING - the SLUG BECOMES A HOOK SUFFIX',
  so: 'A PAGE REGISTERED WITH ONE SLUG HAS A HOOK WHOSE NAME CONTAINS THAT SLUG and NOTHING ELSE',
  submenu: 'A SUBMENU PAGE IS REGISTERED AGAINST A PARENT and PRODUCES A DIFFERENT SUFFIX',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the HOOK SUFFIX IS the ONLY RELIABLE WAY TO SCOPE LOADING TO YOUR OWN SCREEN',
    detail: ['BECAUSE ENQUEUEING ON A GENERAL ADMIN HOOK LOADS YOUR ASSETS ON EVERY ADMIN PAGE',      'and the PLATFORMS OWN HOOK IS the ONE THAT FIRES WHEN YOUR PAGE IS BEING RENDERED'] },
  { c: 'the CAPABILITY YOU DECLARE IS the CAPABILITY the PLATFORM ENFORCES',
    detail: 'WHICH MEANS CHOOSING A CAPABILITY IS CHOOSING WHO CAN SEE the PAGE',
    risk: 'and IT ALSO MEANS A PAGE WHOSE CAPABILITY IS TOO BROAD EXPOSES ITS SCREEN TO USERS WHO SHOULD NOT HAVE IT' },
  { c: 'the MENU POSITION IS A NUMBER',
    detail: 'SO A PLUGIN THAT PICKS AN EXTREME POSITION DISPLACES EVERYBODY ELSES',
    collision: 'and A PLUGIN THAT PICKS the SAME POSITION AS ANOTHER IS ORDERED BY ACTIVATION DATE',
    verdict: 'WHICH MEANS the POSITION IS A NEGOTIATION WITH the SAME CHARACTER AS A HOOK PRIORITY' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'REGISTERING the PAGE but NOT SCOPING the ASSETS', symptom: 'WHICH LOADS YOUR STYLESHEET and SCRIPT ON EVERY ADMIN SCREEN', including: 'INCLUDING SCREENS WHERE YOUR CODE HAS NOTHING TO CONTRIBUTE' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING A PAGE with A CAPABILITY the PLUGIN DOES NOT OTHERWISE CHECK',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A SCREEN VISIBLE TO A ROLE THAT SHOULD NOT SEE IT',
    and: 'WHICH IS NOT the SAME AS HAVING the MENU ITEM HIDDEN, BECAUSE the URL STILL WORKS' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING the MENU ON the WRONG HOOK', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A MENU THAT DOES NOT APPEAR BECAUSE the REGISTRATION HOOK FIRED BEFORE the MENU SYSTEM EXISTED' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'DERIVE EVERY ASSET HANDLE FROM the SAME SLUG CONSTANT the PAGE IS REGISTERED WITH',
    gain: ['SO THE HOOK SUFFIX, the HANDLE and the FILE PATH CANNOT DISAGREE'],    symptom: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND AN ASSET THAT LOADS ON ONE PAGE and NOT ANOTHER for NO VISIBLE REASON' },
  { p: 'GIVE the PAGE A CAPABILITY and CHECK IT IN the HANDLER TOO',
    why: 'BECAUSE the MENU HIDES the LINK but the URL REMAINS',
    symptom: 'and A PAGE THAT RELIES ON the MENU FOR ITS ACCESS CONTROL IS A PAGE WHOSE ACCESS CONTROL IS A SUGGESTION' },
];

Do not reuse a core capability for your own pages. manage_options grants access to any plugin that has defined a page with that capability, and it is granted to administrators on single site but not by default on multisite. Define your own capability and map it to roles on activation. Chapter 11 covers the mechanics.

Page capabilities and the access guard

Access control, and the two places it has to be checked

An admin page is protected by a capability, and the protection is incomplete in a way that produces a specific and very common vulnerability. A capability is a named permission attached to a role. Each user has a set of capabilities derived from their roles, and a check asks whether the current user's set contains one. The menu item is hidden when the user lacks the capability, and hiding is not preventing. The page's URL still exists, and a request to it still runs your handler — so a user who never saw the menu item can still reach the screen and can still trigger every action it performs. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The check must be in the handler, in its first lines, before any query, before any output, before any side effect — because a check placed after the work has begun protects nothing. The check must be repeated on every action the screen performs, and the form's nonce does not substitute for it: the nonce proves the request came from your form, which a user who reached the page without the capability could still have. And capabilities are the only access control that scales, because they are what roles are made of, so a plugin that checks a role name instead of a capability cannot express what it means and will grant access to every user who acquires that role. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Relying on the hidden menu item, which is the vulnerability above and which is reported as "any subscriber can see my settings page". Checking a role name rather than a capability, which breaks the moment a site creates a custom role, and which grants the wrong access to the wrong people when it does not break. And checking the capability once in the page and not in the actions, which produces a screen the user can see and cannot use — or, worse, a screen whose form posts to a handler that checks nothing. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Check the capability in every handler that changes anything, and make the check the first statement, because a handler is the boundary and the boundary is the only place a check is guaranteed to run. And define your own capability for anything with real administrative effect, and grant it to the roles you choose — because a built-in capability like the one for managing options is either too broad or too narrow for a plugin's purpose, and a plugin that invents its own is the only one whose access model it controls.

/* What a capability is. */

const CAPABILITY = {
  is: 'A NAMED PERMISSION ATTACHED TO A ROLE',
  how: ['EACH USER HAS A SET of CAPABILITIES DERIVED FROM THEIR ROLES', 'and A CHECK ASKS WHETHER the CURRENT USERS SET CONTAINS ONE'],
};

/* The gap. */

const GAP = {
  menuHidden: 'the MENU ITEM IS HIDDEN WHEN the USER LACKS the CAPABILITY',
  isNot: 'AND HIDING IS NOT PREVENTING',
  fact: 'the PAGES URL STILL EXISTS, and A REQUEST TO IT STILL RUNS YOUR HANDLER',  consequence: ['SO A USER WHO NEVER SAW the MENU ITEM CAN STILL REACH the SCREEN',    'and CAN STILL TRIGGER EVERY ACTION IT PERFORMS'],  name: 'the PROTECTION IS INCOMPLETE IN A WAY THAT PRODUCES A SPECIFIC and VERY COMMON VULNERABILITY',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the CHECK MUST BE IN the HANDLER, IN ITS FIRST LINES',
    sequence: ['BEFORE ANY QUERY', 'BEFORE ANY OUTPUT', 'BEFORE ANY SIDE EFFECT'],
    why: 'BECAUSE A CHECK PLACED AFTER the WORK HAS BEGUN PROTECTS NOTHING' },
  { c: 'the CHECK MUST BE REPEATED ON EVERY ACTION the SCREEN PERFORMS',
    and: 'the FORMS NONCE DOES NOT SUBSTITUTE FOR IT',
    why: 'BECAUSE the NONCE PROVES the REQUEST CAME FROM YOUR FORM, WHICH A USER WHO REACHED the PAGE WITHOUT the CAPABILITY COULD STILL HAVE' },
  { c: 'CAPABILITIES ARE the ONLY ACCESS CONTROL THAT SCALES',
    why: 'BECAUSE THEY ARE WHAT ROLES ARE MADE OF',
    symptom: 'A PLUGIN THAT CHECKS A ROLE NAME instead of A CAPABILITY CANNOT EXPRESS WHAT IT MEANS and WILL GRANT ACCESS TO EVERY USER WHO ACQUIRES THAT ROLE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'RELYING ON the HIDDEN MENU ITEM', detail: 'WHICH IS the VULNERABILITY ABOVE', symptom: 'WHICH IS REPORTED AS any subscriber can see my settings page' },
  { f: 'CHECKING A ROLE NAME rather than A CAPABILITY',
    symptom: 'WHICH BREAKS the MOMENT A SITE CREATES A CUSTOM ROLE',
    and: 'WHICH GRANTS the WRONG ACCESS TO the WRONG PEOPLE WHEN IT DOES NOT BREAK' },
  { f: 'CHECKING the CAPABILITY ONCE IN the PAGE and NOT IN the ACTIONS',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A SCREEN the USER CAN SEE and CANNOT USE',
    or: 'OR, WORSE, A SCREEN WHOSE FORM POSTS TO A HANDLER THAT CHECKS NOTHING' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'CHECK the CAPABILITY IN EVERY HANDLER THAT CHANGES ANYTHING, and MAKE the CHECK the FIRST STATEMENT',
    why: 'BECAUSE A HANDLER IS the BOUNDARY and the BOUNDARY IS the ONLY PLACE A CHECK IS GUARANTEED TO RUN' },
  { p: 'DEFINE YOUR OWN CAPABILITY for ANYTHING with REAL ADMINISTRATIVE EFFECT, and GRANT IT TO the ROLES YOU CHOOSE',
    why: 'BECAUSE A BUILT-IN CAPABILITY IS EITHER TOO BROAD or TOO NARROW for A PLUGINS PURPOSE',
    and: 'and A PLUGIN THAT INVENTS ITS OWN IS the ONLY ONE WHOSE ACCESS MODEL IT CONTROLS' },
];

Hiding a menu item is a usability feature, not a security control. A user who knows the page URL can request it directly, so every page callback must repeat the capability check independently.

public static function render_entries(): void {
    if ( ! current_user_can( 'manage_ledger' ) ) {
        wp_die(
            esc_html__( 'You do not have permission to view this page.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            '',
            array( 'response' => 403 )
        );
    }

    $list = acme_ledger_get_entries(
        array( 'per_page' => 25 )
    );
    ?>
    <div class="wrap">
        <h1 class="wp-heading-inline"><?= esc_html( get_admin_page_title() ) ?></h1>
        <hr class="wp-header-end">
        <?php Ledger_Table::render( $list ); ?>
    </div>
    <?php
}

Note the wp_die() signature. WordPress 6.4 and later accept an HTML element as the message, which is why the message is passed through esc_html__() and wrapped by the caller. The third argument sets the HTTP status code, and using 403 rather than the default 0 is what makes a proxy and a monitoring system classify the response correctly.

Admin notices

Notices are printed on admin_notices above the page heading and below it respectively, and they are plain HTML with a CSS class that determines the colour. There is no notice API beyond a class and an output statement.

add_action( 'admin_notices', array( Ledger_Admin::class, 'render_notices' ) );
add_action( 'network_admin_notices', array( Ledger_Admin::class, 'render_network_notices' ) );

public static function render_notices(): void {
    if ( ! current_user_can( 'manage_ledger' ) ) {
        return;
    }

    $notice = get_transient( 'acme_ledger_notice' );

    if ( ! is_array( $notice ) ) {
        return;
    }

    echo <div class="notice notice-<?= esc_attr( $notice['type'] ) ?> is-dismissible"><p></p></div>;
    delete_transient( 'acme_ledger_notice' );
}

A transient is the correct carrier for a one-shot notice because it survives the redirect that follows a form submission and expires on its own if the notice is never displayed. The valid type values are notice-error, notice-warning, notice-success and notice-info.

Notices, and why a message that matters needs a round trip

An admin notice is a message rendered above every admin page or above one, and the difficulty is not the rendering but the timing. A notice has to survive from the request that created it to the request that displays it. Those are usually different requests, because the request that creates a notice is a form submission that ends with a redirect — the redirect exists to make the submission idempotent — and the redirect target is the request that renders the page. A message set during the first request does not exist during the second, because the first request's memory is gone. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The platform's mechanism is a transient keyed by the user, which is a short-lived stored value: the handler writes it, the redirect happens, the render reads it and deletes it. That delete-on-read is what makes it one-shot, and it is also what makes the notice disappear if the user navigates away and back — which is correct and which surprises people who expected a banner to persist. A notice must be scoped to the user, because the value lives in a shared store and an unkeyed notice is visible to whoever loads the page next, which on a busy site is a different person. And the notice type is a severity, and the platform styles it: the type is what makes it red or yellow or green and choosing the wrong one is a small credibility cost. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Setting a notice and not redirecting, which renders the notice on the submission response itself and never on the page the user lands on. Not deleting the transient after reading it, which produces a notice that reappears on every admin page load until it expires, which is the mechanism behind "I dismissed it and it came back". And putting unescaped user input in a notice, which is an injection point in the one place every administrator sees — and which is worth naming because the notice is the one piece of output a developer forgets, since notices are usually composed in the handler where the escaping discipline has slipped. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Write a small helper that sets the notice and redirects, so that the pairing cannot be forgotten — the bug is always one without the other, and the helper makes the bug unrepresentable. And scope notices to the screen when they are about that screen, because a notice about a settings save that appears on every admin page is a notice everybody learns to ignore, and ignoring notices is how the ones that matter get missed.

/* The timing problem. */

const PROBLEM = {
  has: 'A NOTICE HAS TO SURVIVE FROM the REQUEST THAT CREATED IT TO the REQUEST THAT DISPLAYS IT',
  usuallyDifferent: ['BECAUSE the REQUEST THAT CREATES A NOTICE IS A FORM SUBMISSION THAT ENDS WITH A REDIRECT',    'the REDIRECT EXISTS TO MAKE the SUBMISSION IDEMPOTENT'],  and: 'and the REDIRECT TARGET IS the REQUEST THAT RENDERS the PAGE',
  symptom: 'A MESSAGE SET DURING the FIRST REQUEST DOES NOT EXIST DURING the SECOND, BECAUSE the FIRST REQUESTS MEMORY IS GONE',
};

/* The mechanism. */
const MECHANISM = {
  is: 'A TRANSIENT KEYED BY the USER',
  detail: 'A SHORT-LIVED STORED VALUE',
  flow: ['the HANDLER WRITES IT', 'the REDIRECT HAPPENS', 'the RENDER READS IT and DELETES IT'],
  deleteOnRead: 'THAT DELETE-ON-READ IS WHAT MAKES IT ONE-SHOT',
  consequence: 'and IT IS ALSO WHAT MAKES the NOTICE DISAPPEAR IF the USER NAVIGATES AWAY and BACK',
  correct: 'WHICH IS CORRECT',  surprises: 'and WHICH SURPRISES PEOPLE WHO EXPECTED A BANNER TO PERSIST',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A NOTICE MUST BE SCOPED TO the USER',
    why: 'BECAUSE the VALUE LIVES IN A SHARED STORE',
    symptom: 'and AN UNKEYED NOTICE IS VISIBLE TO WHOEVER LOADS the PAGE NEXT, WHICH ON A BUSY SITE IS A DIFFERENT PERSON' },
  { c: 'the NOTICE TYPE IS A SEVERITY, and the PLATFORM STYLES IT',
    detail: 'the TYPE IS WHAT MAKES IT RED or YELLOW or GREEN',
    cost: 'and CHOOSING the WRONG ONE IS A SMALL CREDIBILITY COST' },
  { c: 'NOTICES ARE USUALLY COMPOSED IN the HANDLER',
    risk: 'WHICH IS the ONE PLACE AN ESCAPING DISCIPLINE HAS SLIPPED',    and: 'WHICH IS WORTH NAMING BECAUSE the NOTICE IS the ONE PIECE of OUTPUT A DEVELOPER FORGETS' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'SETTING A NOTICE and NOT REDIRECTING', symptom: 'WHICH RENDERS the NOTICE ON the SUBMISSION RESPONSE ITSELF and NEVER ON the PAGE the USER LANDS ON' },
  { f: 'NOT DELETING the TRANSIENT AFTER READING IT', symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A NOTICE THAT REAPPEARS ON EVERY ADMIN PAGE LOAD UNTIL IT EXPIRES', which: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND I dismissed it and it came back' },
  { f: 'PUTTING UNESCAPED USER INPUT IN A NOTICE', is: 'AN INJECTION POINT IN the ONE PLACE EVERY ADMINISTRATOR SEES' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'WRITE A SMALL HELPER THAT SETS the NOTICE and REDIRECTS',
    why: 'SO THAT the PAIRING CANNOT BE FORGOTTEN',
    gain: ['the BUG IS ALWAYS ONE WITHOUT the OTHER', 'and the HELPER MAKES the BUG UNREPRESENTABLE'] },
  { p: 'SCOPE NOTICES TO the SCREEN WHEN THEY ARE ABOUT that SCREEN',
    why: 'BECAUSE A NOTICE ABOUT A SETTINGS SAVE THAT APPEARS ON EVERY ADMIN PAGE IS A NOTICE EVERYBODY LEARNS TO IGNORE',
    and: 'and IGNORING NOTICES IS HOW the ONES THAT MATTER GET MISSED' },
];

Wrap every notice in a capability check. An unauthenticated or low-privilege user who can see your notice can sometimes be told about the internal state of your installation. A notice is an information disclosure surface, and the fix is one line.

Dismissible and stateful notices

Dismissible notices, and the two problems a dismissal creates

A dismissible notice needs to remember that it was dismissed, and that is where the difficulty is: the plugin has to store a preference, and a preference stored per user is not the same as a preference stored per site. A notice about a site-wide configuration belongs in the site, and a notice about something a particular administrator has decided belongs in that administrator's user record. The second is the one that needs a decision, because a per-user dismissal means the plugin is writing to the user's metadata, and a per-site dismissal means a site option. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Dismissal is state, and state has a lifetime question. A dismissal keyed on a boolean is dismissed forever, which is wrong for a notice whose content changes — a notice about a deprecation is relevant only until the plugin is upgraded, and a permanent dismissal outlives its reason. Keying the dismissal on the plugin's version is what lets a notice reappear when it is relevant again, and the cost is that the stored value is a version string rather than a flag, which is one character more to get right and is the difference between a notice that comes back at the right moment and one that never does. And the dismissal itself must be written by an authenticated action, because writing a user preference from an unauthenticated request is a write anybody can cause. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Storing the dismissal as 0 or an empty string, which is falsy and therefore indistinguishable from "never set" in a loose check, so a later version that tests for truthiness sees a dismissal that was not recorded. Not capability-checking the dismiss action, which lets any logged-in user write to any user's dismissal record if they can guess the identifier — which is the same class of bug the access-control section describes. And marking the notice dismissed before the user has actually clicked, which happens when the rendering hook records the dismissal rather than a click handler, so the notice disappears for everyone who merely loaded a page containing it — and this one is worth naming because it is invisible on a single-user site and obvious on a twenty-user one. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Key dismissal on the plugin version rather than on a boolean, so a notice can come back after an upgrade and so the stored value says why it is dismissed. And render a dismissible notice only where the dismiss action can be reached, because a notice whose dismiss control is not reachable is a notice a user cannot get rid of, which converts a notification into a permanent cost on every page load of that screen.

/* Per site or per user. */

const SCOPE = {
  perSite: 'A NOTICE ABOUT A SITE-WIDE CONFIGURATION',
  perUser: 'A NOTICE ABOUT SOMETHING A PARTICULAR ADMINISTRATOR HAS DECIDED',
  mechanism: ['the PER-USER DISMISSAL MEANS the PLUGIN IS WRITING TO the USERS METADATA', 'and the PER-SITE DISMISSAL MEANS A SITE OPTION'],
  decision: 'the SECOND IS the ONE THAT NEEDS A DECISION',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'DISMISSAL IS STATE, and STATE HAS A LIFETIME QUESTION',
    detail: 'A DISMISSAL KEYED ON A BOOLEAN IS DISMISSED FOREVER',
    example: 'WHICH IS WRONG for A NOTICE WHOSE CONTENT CHANGES',    reasoning: ['A NOTICE ABOUT A DEPRECATION IS RELEVANT ONLY UNTIL the PLUGIN IS UPGRADED',      'and A PERMANENT DISMISSAL OUTLIVES ITS REASON'] },
  { c: 'KEYING the DISMISSAL ON the PLUGINS VERSION IS WHAT LETS A NOTICE REAPPEAR WHEN IT IS RELEVANT AGAIN',
    cost: 'the COST IS THAT the STORED VALUE IS A VERSION STRING rather than A FLAG',
    and: 'WHICH IS ONE CHARACTER MORE TO GET RIGHT and IS the DIFFERENCE BETWEEN A NOTICE THAT COMES BACK AT the RIGHT MOMENT and ONE THAT NEVER DOES' },
  { c: 'the DISMISSAL ITSELF MUST BE WRITTEN BY AN AUTHENTICATED ACTION',
    why: 'BECAUSE WRITING A USER PREFERENCE FROM AN UNAUTHENTICATED REQUEST IS A WRITE ANYBODY CAN CAUSE' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'STORING the DISMISSAL AS ZERO or AN EMPTY STRING',
    detail: 'WHICH IS FALSY and THEREFORE INDISTINGUISHABLE FROM never set IN A LOOSE CHECK',    symptom: 'SO A LATER VERSION THAT TESTS FOR TRUTHINESS SEES A DISMISSAL THAT WAS NOT RECORDED' },
  { f: 'NOT CAPABILITY-CHECKING the DISMISS ACTION',
    symptom: 'WHICH LETS ANY LOGGED-IN USER WRITE TO ANY USERS DISMISSAL RECORD IF THEY CAN GUESS the IDENTIFIER',
    connects: 'WHICH IS the SAME CLASS of BUG the ACCESS-CONTROL SECTION DESCRIBES' },
  { f: 'MARKING the NOTICE DISMISSED BEFORE the USER HAS ACTUALLY CLICKED',
    detail: 'WHICH HAPPENS WHEN the RENDERING HOOK RECORDS the DISMISSAL rather than A CLICK HANDLER',    consequence: 'SO the NOTICE DISAPPEARS FOR EVERYONE WHO MERELY LOADED A PAGE CONTAINING IT',
    invisible: 'and THIS ONE IS WORTH NAMING BECAUSE IT IS INVISIBLE ON A SINGLE-USER SITE and OBVIOUS ON A TWENTY-USER ONE' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'KEY DISMISSAL ON the PLUGIN VERSION rather than ON A BOOLEAN',
    gain: ['SO A NOTICE CAN COME BACK AFTER AN UPGRADE', 'and SO the STORED VALUE SAYS WHY IT IS DISMISSED'] },
  { p: 'RENDER A DISMISSIBLE NOTICE ONLY WHERE the DISMISS ACTION CAN BE REACHED',
    why: 'BECAUSE A NOTICE WHOSE DISMISS CONTROL IS NOT REACHABLE IS A NOTICE A USER CANNOT GET RID OF',
    consequence: 'WHICH CONVERTS A NOTIFICATION INTO A PERMANENT COST ON EVERY PAGE LOAD of that SCREEN' },
];

A dismissible notice needs the JavaScript in common.js, the is-dismissible class, and a persistence mechanism. The persistence mechanism is the part that is routinely missing, without which the notice reappears on every page load.

public static function render_dismissible_notice(): void {
    if ( ! current_user_can( 'manage_ledger' ) ) {
        return;
    }

    $user_id = get_current_user_id();
    $version = Ledger_Settings::get( 'dismissed_notice_version' );

    if ( get_user_meta( $user_id, '_acme_ledger_dismissed_notice', true ) === $version ) {
        return;
    }

    ?>
    <div class="notice notice-info is-dismissible"
        data-acme-notice="setup"
        data-acme-version="<?= esc_attr( ACME_LEDGER_VERSION ) ?>">
        <p><?= esc_html__( 'Acme Ledger needs to create its tables. Run the installer?', 'acme-ledger' ) ?></p>
        <p>
            <a class="button button-primary" href="<?= esc_url(
                wp_nonce_url(
                    admin_url( 'admin.php?page=acme-ledger&action=install' ),
                    'acme_ledger_install'
                )
            ) ?>"><?= esc_html__( 'Install tables', 'acme-ledger' ) ?></a>
        </p>
    </div>
    <?php
}

Keying dismissal on the plugin version rather than a boolean is what allows a notice to reappear after an upgrade, which is when it is usually relevant. Storing 0 or an empty string means “never show again”; storing the version means “not until the next release”.

Per-screen asset loading

Loading assets only where they are needed

An admin screen's assets should load on that screen and nowhere else, and the platform gives you a precise mechanism for it that most plugins use loosely. The screen's slug, in its hook-suffix form, is the condition. A callback bound to the hook suffix fires when your page is the one being rendered, and the platform calls it with the screen identifier. Inside it you test the identifier against your page's slug and, on a match, register your stylesheet and script with the asset API. Registration is not loading — the registration records a file and its dependencies, and the platform emits the tag only if something on the page declared a dependency on it — so a script that is registered and never depended upon costs nothing. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Versioning is part of the handle, not part of the URL, so the asset cache busts when the file changes and the browser caches it when it does not, which is the whole reason the version is a parameter rather than a query string you add yourself. Dependencies are a graph and are resolved in order, so a script that calls the platform's own admin script must declare it, and declaring it is what guarantees the ordering. And loading in the footer rather than the header is a measurable win on every admin screen, because the browser can paint before the script is fetched, and the platform lets you say which. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Loading on a general admin hook instead of the screen suffix, which loads your assets on every admin page including the ones where they do nothing, and which is the mechanism behind "installing my plugin made the whole admin slow". Adding a query-string version instead of using the version parameter, which busts the cache correctly and defeats every CDN's caching rules, because most of them ignore or strip query strings. And registering the same handle twice with different files, which produces two tags for one handle on some pages and none on others, depending on which registration ran last. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Register assets inside the screen-suffix callback and nowhere else, so a screen with no assets registers nothing and a screen with assets loads exactly them — and so the answer to "why is my script loading everywhere" is a structural one. And declare every dependency you rely on, including the ones you did not write, because an undeclared dependency is a load-order bug that appears only when another plugin's version changes.

/* The condition. */

const CONDITION = {
  is: 'the SCREENS SLUG, IN ITS HOOK-SUFFIX FORM',
  how: ['A CALLBACK BOUND TO the HOOK SUFFIX FIRES WHEN YOUR PAGE IS the ONE BEING RENDERED',    'and the PLATFORM CALLS IT WITH the SCREEN IDENTIFIER'],
  inside: 'INSIDE IT YOU TEST the IDENTIFIER AGAINST YOUR PAGES SLUG and, ON A MATCH, REGISTER YOUR STYLESHEET and SCRIPT with the ASSET API',
};

/* Registration is not loading. */
const NOT_LOADING = {
  fact: 'REGISTRATION IS NOT LOADING',
  detail: ['the REGISTRATION RECORDS A FILE and ITS DEPENDENCIES',    'and the PLATFORM EMITS the TAG ONLY IF SOMETHING ON the PAGE DECLARED A DEPENDENCY ON IT'],
  consequence: 'SO A SCRIPT THAT IS REGISTERED and NEVER DEPENDED UPON COSTS NOTHING',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'VERSIONING IS PART of the HANDLE, NOT PART of the URL',
    gain: ['so the ASSET CACHE BUSTS WHEN the FILE CHANGES and the BROWSER CACHES IT WHEN IT DOES NOT'],    why: 'WHICH IS the WHOLE REASON the VERSION IS A PARAMETER rather than A QUERY STRING YOU ADD YOURSELF' },
  { c: 'DEPENDENCIES ARE A GRAPH and ARE RESOLVED IN ORDER',
    requirement: 'SO A SCRIPT THAT CALLS the PLATFORMS OWN ADMIN SCRIPT MUST DECLARE IT',
    and: 'DECLARING IT IS WHAT GUARANTEES the ORDERING' },
  { c: 'LOADING IN the FOOTER rather than the HEADER IS A MEASURABLE WIN ON EVERY ADMIN SCREEN',
    why: 'BECAUSE the BROWSER CAN PAINT BEFORE the SCRIPT IS FETCHED',    and: 'the PLATFORM LETS YOU SAY WHICH' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'LOADING ON A GENERAL ADMIN HOOK instead of the SCREEN SUFFIX',
    symptom: 'WHICH LOADS YOUR ASSETS ON EVERY ADMIN PAGE INCLUDING the ONES WHERE THEY DO NOTHING',
    which: 'WHICH IS the MECHANISM BEHIND installing my plugin made the whole admin slow' },
  { f: 'ADDING A QUERY-STRING VERSION instead of USING the VERSION PARAMETER',
    detail: 'WHICH BUSTS the CACHE CORRECTLY and DEFEATS EVERY CDNS CACHING RULES',
    because: 'BECAUSE MOST of THEM IGNORE or STRIP QUERY STRINGS' },
  { f: 'REGISTERING the SAME HANDLE TWICE with DIFFERENT FILES',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES TWO TAGS FOR ONE HANDLE ON SOME PAGES and NONE ON OTHERS',
    order: 'DEPENDING ON WHICH REGISTRATION RAN LAST' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'REGISTER ASSETS INSIDE the SCREEN-SUFFIX CALLBACK and NOWHERE ELSE',
    gain: ['SO A SCREEN with NO ASSETS REGISTERS NOTHING and A SCREEN with ASSETS LOADS EXACTLY THEM'],    and: 'SO the ANSWER TO why is my script loading everywhere IS A STRUCTURAL ONE' },
  { p: 'DECLARE EVERY DEPENDENCY YOU RELY ON, INCLUDING the ONES YOU DID NOT WRITE',
    why: 'BECAUSE AN UNDECLARED DEPENDENCY IS A LOAD-ORDER BUG THAT APPEARS ONLY WHEN ANOTHER PLUGINS VERSION CHANGES' },
];

Enqueue on admin_enqueue_scripts and branch on the hook suffix. Loading a 400 KB admin bundle on every screen in the admin, including the dashboard, for a plugin that only uses it on one page is a defect users will report as a slow site.

add_action( 'admin_enqueue_scripts', array( Ledger_Admin::class, 'enqueue_assets' ) );

public static function enqueue_assets( string $hook_suffix ): void {
    if ( ! str_contains( $hook_suffix, 'acme-ledger' ) ) {
        return;
    }

    wp_enqueue_style(
        'acme-ledger-admin',
        ACME_LEDGER_URL . 'admin/css/admin.css',
        array( 'wp-components' ),
        ACME_LEDGER_VERSION
    );

    if ( 'acme-ledger_page_acme-ledger-import' !== $hook_suffix ) {
        return;
    }

    wp_enqueue_media();

    wp_enqueue_script(
        'acme-ledger-import',
        ACME_LEDGER_URL . 'admin/js/import.js',
        array( 'jquery', 'wp-i18n' ),
        ACME_LEDGER_VERSION,
        true
    );

    wp_localize_script(
        'acme-ledger-import',
        'acmeLedgerImport',
        array(
            'restUrl'    => esc_url_raw( rest_url( 'acme-ledger/v1' ) ),
            'nonce'      => wp_create_nonce( 'wp_rest' ),
            'maxRows'    => 5000,
        )
    );
}

Prefer wp_add_inline_script over wp_localize_script for new code. The latter converts every value to a string, which destroys booleans and integers and is deprecated as a result.

wp_add_inline_script(
    'acme-ledger-import',
    'window.acmeLedgerImport = ' . wp_json_encode( $config ) . ';',
    'before'
);

Form handlers and admin-post

A form that posts to your own page reloads the page to handle the submission, which loses the transient notice and can re-run side effects. admin-post.php exists to terminate a request with a redirect after processing.

<form method="post" action="<?= esc_url( admin_url( 'admin-post.php' ) ) ?>">
    <input type="hidden" name="action" value="acme_ledger_import">
    <?php wp_nonce_field( 'acme_ledger_import' ); ?>
    <input type="file" name="entries_csv" accept="text/csv" required>
    <?php submit_button( __( 'Import', 'acme-ledger' ) ); ?>
</form>

class Ledger_Admin {
    public static function init(): void {
        add_action( 'admin_post_acme_ledger_import', array( self::class, 'handle_import' ) );
    }

    public static function handle_import(): void {
        if ( ! current_user_can( 'manage_ledger' ) ) {
            wp_die( 'Forbidden', '', array( 'response' => 403 ) );
        }

        check_admin_referer( 'acme_ledger_import' );

        if ( empty( $_FILES['entries_csv']['tmp_name'] ) ) {
            wp_die( 'No file received.', '', array( 'response' => 400 ) );
        }

        $result = acme_ledger_import_csv( $_FILES['entries_csv']['tmp_name'] );

        set_transient(
            'acme_ledger_notice',
            array( 'type' => $result['ok'] ? 'success' : 'error', 'text' => $result['message'] ),
            MINUTE_IN_SECONDS
        );

        wp_safe_redirect( admin_url( 'admin.php?page=acme-ledger-import' ) );
        exit;
    }
}

The four checks are not optional and are not interchangeable. A capability check answers who, check_admin_referer() answers whether this specific request was intended, and a file validation answer is the third. Missing exit after the redirect is a common cause of “headers already sent” fatal errors.

The admin-post path, and why it is better than your own endpoint

An admin form needs somewhere to post to, and the choice between posting to your own page file, posting to the platform's own handler endpoint, and using the REST API has real consequences for security and for code organisation. The handler endpoint exists so that an administrative action does not have to be served by a page. A form posts to it with an action name; the platform dispatches to a callback registered for that action; and the callback returns a redirect rather than a page. The action name identifies the handler, and the same request carries the nonce and the capability check. The reason this is better than posting to your own page is not that it is more secure — it is equally secure if you do the checks — but that the handler has no rendering context, so it cannot accidentally emit output, and that the dispatch is by action name, so a screen can register several independent handlers without any of them knowing about the others. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. The action name is a dispatch key and it is yours to choose, so it must be namespaced exactly as a hook is, because a collision means two plugins registering the same action and one of them handling the other's submission. The handler must never render, and must always redirect — a handler that echoes produces a response that is half output and half form, and a handler that returns nothing produces a blank page the user has to navigate back from. And the handler is the right place for work that must not be reachable by a normal page request, which is the property that makes it a boundary: a file operation, a bulk update, an import. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Verifying the nonce after doing the work, which is the ordering the settings chapter made and which is repeated here because a handler is exactly where people get it wrong. Omitting the capability check because the screen is hidden, which is the access-control gap again and which for an action endpoint is worse, because the endpoint is a URL with no menu behind it at all. And trusting that the submitted fields are the ones you expect, which for a file upload includes the size, the extension and the actual content type — and which is the same untrusted-input property every other input has. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Make every handler a fixed five-line prologue followed by one call, — nonce, capability, validate, act, redirect — so that the prologue is identical in every handler and reviewable as a pattern rather than file by file. And register every action handler from the same place you register the rest of your hooks, so the complete list of things your plugin will do in response to a request is in one place, which is the list a reviewer needs and the list an incident investigation needs.

/* The mechanism. */

const ENDPOINT = {
  exists: 'SO THAT AN ADMINISTRATIVE ACTION DOES NOT HAVE TO BE SERVED BY A PAGE',
  flow: ['A FORM POSTS TO IT with AN ACTION NAME', 'the PLATFORM DISPATCHES TO A CALLBACK REGISTERED for THAT ACTION', 'and the CALLBACK RETURNS A REDIRECT rather than A PAGE'],
  carriedBy: 'the SAME REQUEST CARRIES the NONCE and the CAPABILITY CHECK',
  betterThanOwnPage: {
    not: 'IT IS NOT THAT IT IS MORE SECURE - IT IS EQUALLY SECURE IF YOU DO the CHECKS',
    but: ['the HANDLER HAS NO RENDERING CONTEXT, SO IT CANNOT ACCIDENTALLY EMIT OUTPUT',      'and the DISPATCH IS BY ACTION NAME, SO A SCREEN CAN REGISTER SEVERAL INDEPENDENT HANDLERS WITHOUT ANY of THEM KNOWING ABOUT the OTHERS'],
  },
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'the ACTION NAME IS A DISPATCH KEY and IT IS YOURS TO CHOOSE',
    rule: 'SO IT MUST BE NAMESPACED EXACTLY AS A HOOK IS',
    symptom: 'BECAUSE A COLLISION MEANS TWO PLUGINS REGISTERING the SAME ACTION and ONE of THEM HANDLING the OTHERS SUBMISSION' },
  { c: 'the HANDLER MUST NEVER RENDER, and MUST ALWAYS REDIRECT',
    bad: 'A HANDLER THAT ECHOES PRODUCES A RESPONSE THAT IS HALF OUTPUT and HALF FORM',
    worse: 'and A HANDLER THAT RETURNS NOTHING PRODUCES A BLANK PAGE the USER HAS TO NAVIGATE BACK FROM' },
  { c: 'the HANDLER IS the RIGHT PLACE for WORK THAT MUST NOT BE REACHABLE BY A NORMAL PAGE REQUEST',
    instances: ['A FILE OPERATION', 'A BULK UPDATE', 'AN IMPORT'],    why: 'WHICH IS the PROPERTY THAT MAKES IT A BOUNDARY - A FILE IS A URL with NO MENU BEHIND IT AT ALL' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'VERIFYING the NONCE AFTER DOING the WORK', connects: 'WHICH IS the ORDERING the SETTINGS CHAPTER MADE', and: 'WHICH IS REPEATED HERE BECAUSE A HANDLER IS EXACTLY WHERE PEOPLE GET IT WRONG' },
  { f: 'OMITTING the CAPABILITY CHECK BECAUSE the SCREEN IS HIDDEN',
    detail: 'WHICH IS the ACCESS-CONTROL GAP AGAIN',    worse: 'and WHICH FOR AN ACTION ENDPOINT IS WORSE, BECAUSE the ENDPOINT IS A URL with NO MENU BEEHIND IT AT ALL' },
  { f: 'TRUSTING THAT the SUBMITTED FIELDS ARE the ONES YOU EXPECT',
    detail: 'WHICH FOR A FILE UPLOAD INCLUDES the SIZE, the EXTENSION and the ACTUAL CONTENT TYPE',
    connects: 'WHICH IS the SAME UNTRUSTED-INPUT PROPERTY EVERY OTHER INPUT HAS' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'MAKE EVERY HANDLER A FIXED FIVE-LINE PROLOGUE FOLLOWED BY ONE CALL',
    sequence: ['NONCE', 'CAPABILITY', 'VALIDATE', 'ACT', 'REDIRECT'],
    gain: ['SO THAT the PROLOGUE IS IDENTICAL IN EVERY HANDLER', 'and REVIEWABLE AS A PATTERN rather than FILE BY FILE'] },
  { p: 'REGISTER EVERY ACTION HANDLER FROM the SAME PLACE YOU REGISTER the REST of YOUR HOOKS',
    gain: ['SO the COMPLETE LIST of WHAT YOUR PLUGIN WILL DO IN RESPONSE TO A REQUEST IS IN ONE PLACE'],    and: 'WHICH IS the LIST A REVIEWER NEEDS and the LIST AN INCIDENT INVESTIGATION NEEDS' },
];

Validate uploads beyond the MIME type. A supplied $_FILES MIME type is attacker-controlled. Check $_FILES[...]['error'], enforce a size ceiling, verify the extension, and for CSV parse with fgetcsv against an expected column header. Never include a file that came out of an upload directory.

Exercises 7

  1. Register a top-level menu with two submenus and confirm the hook suffixes reported by admin_enqueue_scripts match your expectations.
  2. Attempt to load a plugin admin page as a subscriber by URL and verify the wp_die() guard returns 403 before any data is read.
  3. Implement a dismissible notice keyed on plugin version and prove it reappears after a version bump but not after a page reload.
  4. Build a CSV import through admin-post.php with nonce, capability, size, extension and column-header validation, and a success notice delivered by transient.

8 · Database Schema, dbDelta and Custom Tables

When a custom table is the right answerUsing the wpdb object correctlydbDelta and its formatting rulesCharset, collation and utf8mb4Reading and writing rowsVersioned schema migration

Post meta and options cover a great deal of ground, but a relational data model with a large number of indexed, joined and aggregated rows belongs in a custom table. This chapter covers when to make that decision, how to declare a schema that dbDelta() will accept, and how to migrate it safely.

When a custom table is the right answer

The decision is not aesthetic. It is determined by the number of rows, the number of queries required to read one logical record, and the need for indexes that the meta table cannot express.

Why a custom table is sometimes right, and the three costs it moves

The platform already has a place to put your data — post meta — and choosing a custom table instead means you have accepted three specific costs in exchange for two specific gains, and it is worth being able to state both sides because the choice is not obvious. The gain is that you control the schema exactly. You can have a column with the right type, an index that serves the query you actually run, a constraint the database enforces, and a row shape that is not a serialised string. The gain is that a query over your data is a query over your data rather than a scan of a table whose purpose is to hold a serialised value next to a key. Then the three costs, and each is a mechanism. A custom table does not participate in the platform's caching, so a value read through the platform's cache is a value you must invalidate yourself, and the invalidation is a mechanism you have to build rather than one you inherit. It does not participate in the platform's export or import, so a site's data moved by the platform's own tools moves your rows as an opaque table or not at all. And it is not covered by the platform's own backups unless you arrange it, which is a configuration question per host and a support question per user. Then the three things that follow from that trade, and each is a decision rule. If your data has one row per thing and a handful of scalar attributes, post meta is better — it is cached, exported, backed up and queried by the platform's own indexed queries, and the cost of a serialised value is invisible at that scale. If your data has many rows that are queried by several of their fields, a table is better, because the platform's meta queries require a leading key and become a scan without one, and because you cannot index a value inside a serialised string. And if your data is not really per-post at all, — records about users, about external entities, about anything that is not a post — a table is not a compromise but the obvious choice, because there is no post to attach meta to. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Creating a table for a small amount of data, which pays the three costs above for a query that would have been fast. Creating a table and then querying it through the platform's cache anyway, which gets the worst of both: the cache is not aware of the table, so it caches stale rows and misses when they change. And using a meta table for something meta was never designed for — a log of events, a queue — which grows without bound in a table the platform does not index usefully. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Choose once, per kind of data, and write the reason down, because the next person to add a feature will reach for whichever mechanism they know and the reasoning is what stops a per-post table from being slowly filled with non-post data. And keep the table's lifecycle attached to the plugin's lifecycle — created on activation, indexed on activation, and never dropped on deactivation — so a plugin that is deactivated and reactivated does not silently lose the index that made it fast.

/* The two gains. */

const GAINS = [
  { g: 'YOU CONTROL the SCHEMA EXACTLY', detail: ['YOU CAN HAVE A COLUMN with the RIGHT TYPE', 'AN INDEX THAT SERVES the QUERY YOU ACTUALLY RUN', 'A CONSTRAINT the DATABASE ENFORCES', 'and A ROW SHAPE THAT IS NOT A SERIALISED STRING'] },
  { g: 'A QUERY OVER YOUR DATA IS A QUERY OVER YOUR DATA',
    rather: 'rather than A SCAN of A TABLE WHOSE PURPOSE IS to HOLD A SERIALISED VALUE NEXT TO A KEY' },
];

/* Three costs. */
const COSTS = [

    consequence: 'SO A VALUE READ THROUGH the PLATFORMS CACHE IS A VALUE YOU MUST INVALIDATE YOURSELF',
    and: 'the INVALIDATION IS A MECHANISM YOU HAVE TO BUILD rather THAN ONE YOU INHERIT' },
  { c: 'IT DOES NOT PARTICIPATE IN the PLATFORMS EXPORT or IMPORT',
    symptom: 'SO A SITES DATA MOVED BY the PLATFORMS OWN TOOLS MOVES YOUR ROWS AS AN OPAQUE TABLE or NOT AT ALL' },

/* Three decision rules. */
const RULES = [
  { r: 'IF YOUR DATA HAS ONE ROW PER THING and A HANDFUL of SCALAR ATTRIBUTES, POST META IS BETTER',
    why: ['IT IS CACHED, EXPORTED, BACKED UP and QUERIED BY the PLATFORMS OWN INDEXED QUERIES',      'and the COST of A SERIALISED VALUE IS INVISIBLE AT that SCALE'] },

  { r: 'IF YOUR DATA IS NOT REALLY PER-POST AT ALL', instances: ['RECORDS ABOUT USERS', 'ABOUT EXTERNAL ENTITIES', 'ABOUT ANYTHING THAT IS NOT A POST'],

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'CREATING A TABLE for A SMALL AMOUNT of DATA', consequence: 'WHICH PAYS the THREE COSTS ABOVE for A QUERY THAT WOULD HAVE BEEN FAST' },

    detail: 'WHICH GETS the WORST of BOTH',

  { f: 'USING A META TABLE for SOMETHING META WAS NEVER DESIGNED FOR', instances: ['A LOG of EVENTS', 'A QUEUE'],    symptom: 'WHICH GROWS WITHOUT BOUND IN A TABLE the PLATFORM DOES NOT INDEX USEFULLY' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'CHOOSE ONCE, PER KIND of DATA, and WRITE the REASON DOWN',
    why: 'BECAUSE the NEXT PERSON TO ADD A FEATURE WILL REACH for WHICHEVER MECHANISM THEY KNOW',    and: 'the REASONING IS WHAT STOPS A PER-POST TABLE from BEING SLOWLY FILLED with NON-POST DATA' },
  { p: 'KEEP the TABLES LIFECYCLE ATTACHED TO the PLUGINS LIFECYCLE',
    detail: ['CREATED ON ACTIVATION', 'INDEXED ON ACTIVATION', 'and NEVER DROPPED ON DEACTIVATION'],    why: 'SO A PLUGIN THAT IS DEACTIVATED and REACTIVATED DOES NOT SILENTLY LOSE the INDEX THAT MADE IT FAST' },
];
RequirementStorage
Under a few thousand rows, read one at a timePost meta or option
Queryable, filterable, indexable by a site visitorCustom table
Normalised relationships between entitiesCustom tables with foreign keys
Aggregations, GROUP BY, joins across many rowsCustom table
Rows number in the millionsCustom table, and probably a separate service

Meta storage has a specific weakness worth stating plainly. A post meta query without a leading meta_key is a table scan. The wp_postmeta table is keyed on meta_id and post_id, and WordPress adds an index on meta_key only because it must. Querying by meta_value without a key forces a scan, and a scan over a million rows on a front-end request is a denial-of-service vector.

Using the wpdb object correctly

A table belongs to your plugin and must be named with your prefix. Retrieve the object with $wpdb from the global scope; never instantiate wpdb yourself, because the connection, table prefix and character set are all configured on the shared instance.

global $wpdb;

$table = $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_ledger_entries';

Two properties make this safe. The table name is composed from the site prefix at runtime, so the plugin works identically on a prefixed multisite install. The charset_collate clause is appended from $wpdb->get_charset_collate(), so the table declaration matches the database default without you repeating it.

The database object, and the three properties that make it safe

The platform's database object exists so that plugins do not talk to the database directly, and the reason it is more than a wrapper is that it carries three properties a raw connection does not. It knows the table prefix, which means a query against your table is written once and works identically on a single site, on a multisite install with a custom prefix, and on a host that prefixes for its own reasons. It knows the character set and collation, and appends them to every statement it builds, which is what prevents the class of bug where a table created with one collation is queried with another and a comparison silently returns different rows. And it formats values for you, — it applies the escaping that a prepared value needs, so a value that goes through its formatting function is a value that cannot break out of its quotes. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Table names are interpolated, values are not. A table name is derived from the prefix and cannot be attacker-controlled, so interpolating it is safe; a value can be attacker-controlled, so it is passed through the formatting function or a placeholder. Confusing which is which produces either a needless escaping of an identifier or, much worse, an interpolation of something a user typed. Preparation is not formatting, and they are different operations: formatting produces a quoted literal now, preparation produces a statement with placeholders and binds values later — and the difference matters because a formatted value is safe and a prepared statement is safe and they are not interchangeable in how they read. And every read helper has a mode, which is whether rows come back as objects or as associative arrays, and choosing the mode once per call site rather than mixing them within a function is what keeps the rest of the code from having to care. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Building a query by concatenating a value. Caching a result forever because the query took a long time, which serves a value that was correct when it was stored. And skipping the cache because "it is only one row", which is the caching chapter's argument in its smallest form: the number of rows is not the cost, the query count is. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Never build a statement by string concatenation except for identifiers you control, and mark those with a comment saying so, because that one comment is what a reviewer reads. And use the object's prepared-statement path for anything a user can influence, including values that arrive through a form, a shortcode attribute or a REST request — because the value's origin, not its type, is what determines whether it is trusted.

/* Three properties. */

const PROPERTIES = [
  { p: 'IT KNOWS the TABLE PREFIX', gain: 'WHICH MEANS A QUERY AGAINST YOUR TABLE IS WRITTEN ONCE and WORKS IDENTICALLY ON A SINGLE SITE, ON A MULTISITE INSTALL with A CUSTOM PREFIX, and ON A HOST THAT PREFIXES for ITS OWN REASONS' },
  { p: 'IT KNOWS the CHARACTER SET and COLLATION, and APPENDS THEM TO EVERY STATEMENT IT BUILDS',
    prevented: 'WHICH IS WHAT PREVENTS the CLASS of BUG WHERE A TABLE CREATED with ONE COLLATION IS QUERIED with ANOTHER and A COMPARISON SILENTLY RETURNS DIFFERENT ROWS' },
  { p: 'IT FORMATS VALUES FOR YOU',
    detail: 'IT APPLIES the ESCAPING THAT A PREPARED VALUE NEEDS',
    so: 'SO A VALUE THAT GOES THROUGH ITS FORMATTING FUNCTION IS A VALUE THAT CANNOT BREAK OUT of ITS QUOTES' },

/* Three consequences. */

    other: 'A VALUE CAN BE ATTACKER-CONTROLLED, SO IT IS PASSED THROUGH the FORMATTING FUNCTION or A PLACEHOLDER',
    symptom: 'CONFUSING WHICH IS WHICH PRODUCES EITHER A NEEDLESS ESCAPING of AN IDENTIFIER or, MUCH WORSE, AN INTERPOLATION of SOMETHING A USER TYPED' },
