  { f: 'SKIPPING the CACHE BECAUSE it is only ONE ROW', connects: 'WHICH IS the CACHING CHAPTERS ARGUMENT IN ITS SMALLEST FORM', and: 'the NUMBER of ROWS IS NOT the COST, the QUERY COUNT IS' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'NEVER BUILD A STATEMENT BY STRING CONCATENATION EXCEPT for IDENTIFIERS YOU CONTROL, and MARK THOSE WITH A COMMENT SAYING SO',
    why: 'BECAUSE THAT ONE COMMENT IS WHAT A REVIEWER READS' },
  { p: 'USE the OBJECTS PREPARED-STATEMENT PATH for ANYTHING A USER CAN INFLUENCE',

Set $wpdb->suppress_errors() only to silence a log line, never to hide a defect. Suppressing errors and continuing to run a query that failed produces a plugin that appears to work and stores nothing. If you must tolerate a missing table on an un-upgraded install, check for it explicitly with SHOW TABLES LIKE and take a defined action.

dbDelta and its formatting rules

dbDelta() compares your declarative schema against the live table and issues ALTER TABLE statements for the differences. It is a differential notifier, not a migration tool, and its parser is a set of regular expressions that are sensitive to whitespace.

There are four formatting requirements. Each field must be declared on its own line. There must be exactly two spaces between PRIMARY KEY and the column list. There must be exactly two spaces between an index name and its column list. And the index keyword must be KEY, never INDEX or UNIQUE KEY written without the intervening name.

function acme_ledger_install_schema(): void {
    global $wpdb;

    $table   = $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_ledger_entries';
    $collate = $wpdb->get_charset_collate();

    $sql = "CREATE TABLE {$table} (
    id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
    account_id bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    entry_date date NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00',
    amount decimal(19,4) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0.0000,
    currency varchar(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'EUR',
    memo text NULL,
    reference varchar(191) NULL,
    created_at datetime NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
    created_by bigint(20) unsigned NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    PRIMARY KEY  (id),
    KEY account_id (account_id),
    KEY entry_date (entry_date),
    KEY created_by (created_by),
    KEY currency_account (currency,account_id)
) {$collate};";

    require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/upgrade.php';

    dbDelta( $sql );
}

Two KEY lines above are deliberate. KEY account_id (account_id) is required for a lookup by account. KEY currency_account (currency,account_id) serves a currency-filtered account listing, where a composite index in the right column order avoids a filesort. A single-column index on currency alone would be worse than the composite for that query.

Note decimal(19,4) for money. float and double are binary floating point and cannot represent a decimal fraction exactly, so accumulating a column of them produces visible drift. decimal(19,4) is exact and is the correct column type for a monetary amount.

Schema changes that can be re-run, and the money column

Creating a table is easy; changing one safely over the life of a plugin is the harder problem, and the platform provides a specific helper for it with rules that are not optional. The helper compares the table you describe with the table that exists and issues only the differences. That means calling it with the same description twice does nothing the second time, which is what makes it idempotent, and idempotence is the whole point: an upgrade routine must be re-runnable because it may be interrupted. Then the three rules that make it work, and each has a specific mechanism. Every field must be on its own line with two keys per line, — the format the helper's parser expects, which means a single-line field list is silently not parsed and that column is not created. Each key must be on its own line, for the same reason: the parser is line-oriented, so a definition with several keys on one line does not become several columns. And the field types must be spelled the way the parser recognises, so an unusual type spelled differently is either skipped or produces a column that does not match your later queries. Then the two things that follow, and each is a consequence. A schema change is a set of steps, one per version, because the helper migrates to the description you gave it and not to a target; an installation three versions behind needs three calls with three descriptions, in order. And the index is part of the description, so an index you add later is added by a later call with an index it did not have — which is why the description in version two's routine is a full copy of version one's plus the change, not a fragment. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Writing the description on one line, which creates a table missing most of its columns and produces an error on the first query that mentions one. Using a floating-point column for money, which is the one worth naming in detail. And not re-running the routine after changing it, which leaves an existing installation on the old schema while a fresh install gets the new one, so the bug reproduces only on sites that have been around. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Use a fixed-precision decimal type for money and never a floating-point one, because a binary fraction cannot represent a decimal fraction exactly, so accumulating a column of them produces visible drift — and a ledger that drifts by a cent is a ledger nobody trusts. And store the version that created each column, in the schema itself if you can, so that an installation can be reasoned about from its own structure rather than from your memory of which release introduced what.

/* What the helper does. */

const HELPER = {
  does: 'COMPARES the TABLE YOU DESCRIBE with the TABLE THAT EXISTS and ISSUES ONLY the DIFFERENCES',
  gain: 'CALLING IT WITH the SAME DESCRIPTION TWICE DOES NOTHING the SECOND TIME',
  idempotent: 'WHICH IS WHAT MAKES IT IDEMPOTENT',
  and: 'and IDEMPOTENCE IS the WHOLE POINT - AN UPGRADE ROUTINE MUST BE RE-RUNNABLE BECAUSE IT MAY BE INTERRUPTED',
};

/* Three rules. */
const RULES = [
  { r: 'EVERY FIELD MUST BE ON ITS OWN LINE with TWO KEYS PER LINE',
    why: 'THE FORMAT the PARSER EXPECTS',    symptom: 'WHICH MEANS A SINGLE-LINE FIELD LIST IS SILENTLY NOT PARSED and THAT COLUMN IS NOT CREATED' },
  { r: 'EACH KEY MUST BE ON ITS OWN LINE',
    why: 'FOR the SAME REASON - the PARSER IS LINE-ORIENTED',
    symptom: 'SO A DEFINITION with SEVERAL KEYS ON ONE LINE DOES NOT BECOME SEVERAL COLUMNS' },
  { r: 'the FIELD TYPES MUST BE SPELLED the WAY the PARSER RECOGNISES',
    symptom: 'SO AN UNUSUAL TYPE SPELLED DIFFERENTLY IS EITHER SKIPPED or PRODUCES A COLUMN THAT DOES NOT MATCH YOUR LATER QUERIES' },
];

/* Two consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'A SCHEMA CHANGE IS A SET of STEPS, ONE PER VERSION',
    why: 'BECAUSE the HELPER MIGRATES TO the DESCRIPTION YOU GAVE IT and NOT TO A TARGET',    symptom: 'AN INSTALLATION THREE VERSIONS BEHIND NEEDS THREE CALLS with THREE DESCRIPTIONS, IN ORDER' },
  { c: 'the INDEX IS PART of the DESCRIPTION',
    consequence: 'SO AN INDEX YOU ADD LATER IS ADDED BY A LATER CALL with AN INDEX IT DID NOT HAVE',
    why: 'WHICH IS WHY the DESCRIPTION IN VERSION TWOS ROUTINE IS A FULL COPY of VERSION ONES PLUS the CHANGE, NOT A FRAGMENT' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'WRITING the DESCRIPTION ON ONE LINE', symptom: 'WHICH CREATES A TABLE MISSING MOST of ITS COLUMNS', and: 'and PRODUCES AN ERROR ON the FIRST QUERY THAT MENTIONS ONE' },
  { f: 'USING A FLOATING-POINT COLUMN for MONEY',    detail: 'FLOAT and DOUBLE ARE BINARY FLOATING POINT and CANNOT REPRESENT A DECIMAL FRACTION EXACTLY',    consequence: 'SO ACCUMULATING A COLUMN of THEM PRODUCES VISIBLE DRIFT',    verdict: 'and A LEDGER THAT DRIFTS BY A CENT IS A LEDGER NOBODY TRUSTS' },
  { f: 'NOT RE-RUNNING the ROUTINE AFTER CHANGING IT',
    symptom: 'WHICH LEAVES AN EXISTING INSTALLATION ON the OLD SCHEMA WHILE A FRESH INSTALL GETS the NEW ONE',    and: 'SO the BUG REPRODUCES ONLY ON SITES THAT HAVE BEEN AROUND' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'USE A FIXED-PRECISION DECIMAL TYPE for MONEY and NEVER A FLOATING-POINT ONE', why: 'BECAUSE A BINARY FRACTION CANNOT REPRESENT A DECIMAL FRACTION EXACTLY' },
  { p: 'STORE the VERSION THAT CREATED EACH COLUMN, IN the SCHEMA ITSELF IF YOU CAN',
    gain: 'SO THAT AN INSTALLATION CAN BE REASONED ABOUT FROM ITS OWN STRUCTURE rather than FROM YOUR MEMORY of WHICH RELEASE INTRODUCED WHAT' },
];

dbDelta never drops anything. It will add a column, add an index and change a column definition, but it will not remove a column or an index, and it will not change a column to a narrower type. Schema changes that remove data must be executed as explicit ALTER TABLE statements inside a versioned upgrade routine, which is covered at the end of this chapter.

Charset, collation and utf8mb4

Charset, collation, and the four data types that mislead people

Column types are where a plugin's data quietly becomes wrong rather than broken, and the four below account for most of it. Character set is about what bytes a string may contain; you want one that includes every character a user might type, which is why a four-byte set is the right answer rather than a conservative one — a three-byte set cannot store an emoji and will silently truncate or reject it depending on the mode. Collation is about how two strings are compared and sorted, and it is a separate decision from the charset, and it is the one that produces "why is this name sorted before that one". Then the four types, and each has a specific trap. An identifier column must be an integer type wide enough for the values, not a string, because a string identifier cannot be indexed efficiently and a truncated one collides. A date-and-time column for a business date must not be a timestamp type, because a timestamp is converted to UTC on write and back on read and is subject to a fixed upper limit — so a value outside the representable range is stored wrong, and the symptom is a date in the year 2100 that reads back as something else. A count or quantity must be an integer, because a floating-point column accepts a fractional value that the domain does not have, and the fractional value arrives from a form that accepted it. And an enumeration of a fixed set is best as a constrained integer or a bounded string, rather than as a free-form string, so the database rejects a value the code does not understand rather than storing it. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A column created with a three-byte character set, which cannot store emoji and which fails on user input nobody tested with. A collation that sorts case-insensitively when the domain is case-sensitive, which makes two distinct identifiers compare equal and produces an "already exists" error on a valid insert. And changing a table's collation without changing its data, which produces a table whose stored values and whose comparison rules disagree, and which is the schema-change case where the description must be rewritten rather than patched. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Choose the character set once, at creation, for every table and every text column, because changing it afterwards is a rewrite of every row rather than a metadata change, and because mixed sets across tables produce comparisons that behave differently depending on which side a value came from. And declare the column type to match the domain rather than to match the language, — an identifier is an identifier in every language, a date is a date, and money is a fixed-precision decimal — because a column typed after the language's convenience is a column whose constraints the application now has to enforce.

/* Charset and collation are two decisions. */

const CHARSET = {
  about: 'WHAT BYTES A STRING MAY CONTAIN',
  want: 'ONE THAT INCLUDES EVERY CHARACTER A USER MIGHT TYPE',
  which: 'WHICH IS WHY A FOUR-BYTE SET IS the RIGHT ANSWER rather than A CONSERVATIVE ONE',
  trap: 'A THREE-BYTE SET CANNOT STORE AN EMOJI and WILL SILENTLY TRUNCATE or REJECT IT DEPENDING ON the MODE',
};
const COLLATION = {
  about: 'HOW TWO STRINGS ARE COMPARED and SORTED',
  separate: 'and IT IS A SEPARATE DECISION FROM the CHARSET',
  symptom: 'and IT IS the ONE THAT PRODUCES why is this name sorted before that one',
};

/* Four types with traps. */
const TYPES = [
  { t: 'AN IDENTIFIER COLUMN MUST BE AN INTEGER TYPE WIDE ENOUGH for the VALUES, NOT A STRING',
    why: ['BECAUSE A STRING IDENTIFIER CANNOT BE INDEXED EFFICIENTLY', 'and A TRUNCATED ONE COLLIDES'] },
  { t: 'A DATE-AND-TIME COLUMN FOR A BUSINESS DATE MUST NOT BE A TIMESTAMP TYPE',
    detail: ['BECAUSE A TIMESTAMP IS CONVERTED TO UTC ON WRITE and BACK ON READ', 'and IS SUBJECT TO A FIXED UPPER LIMIT'],    symptom: 'SO A VALUE OUTSIDE the REPRESENTABLE RANGE IS STORED WRONG, and the SYMPTOM IS A DATE IN the YEAR 2100 THAT READS BACK AS SOMETHING ELSE' },
  { t: 'A COUNT or QUANTITY MUST BE AN INTEGER',
    detail: 'BECAUSE A FLOATING-POINT COLUMN ACCEPTS A FRACTIONAL VALUE THAT the DOMAIN DOES NOT HAVE',    and: 'and the FRACTIONAL VALUE ARRIVES FROM A FORM THAT ACCEPTED IT' },
  { t: 'AN ENUMERATION of A FIXED SET IS BEST AS A CONSTRAINED INTEGER or A BOUNDED STRING',
    rather: 'rather than AS A FREE-FORM STRING',
    gain: 'SO the DATABASE REJECTS A VALUE the CODE DOES NOT UNDERSTAND rather than STORING IT' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: 'A COLUMN CREATED with A THREE-BYTE CHARACTER SET', symptom: 'WHICH CANNOT STORE EMOJI', and: 'and WHICH FAILS ON USER INPUT NOBODY TESTED WITH' },
  { f: 'A COLLATION THAT SORTS CASE-INSENSITIVELY WHEN the DOMAIN IS CASE-SENSITIVE',
    symptom: 'WHICH MAKES TWO DISTINCT IDENTIFIERS COMPARE EQUAL',    and: 'and PRODUCES an already exists ERROR ON A VALID INSERT' },
  { f: 'CHANGING a TABLES COLLATION WITHOUT CHANGING ITS DATA',
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A TABLE WHOSE STORED VALUES and WHOSE COMPARISON RULES DISAGREE',    and: 'WHICH IS the SCHEMA-CHANGE CASE WHERE the DESCRIPTION MUST BE REWRITTEN rather than PATCHED' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'CHOOSE the CHARACTER SET ONCE, AT CREATION, for EVERY TABLE and EVERY TEXT COLUMN',
    why: 'BECAUSE CHANGING IT AFTERWARDS IS A REWRITE of EVERY ROW rather than A METADATA CHANGE',
    and: 'and BECAUSE MIXED SETS ACROSS TABLES PRODUCE COMPARISONS THAT BEHAVE DIFFERENTLY DEPENDING ON WHICH SIDE A VALUE CAME FROM' },
  { p: 'DECLARE the COLUMN TYPE to MATCH the DOMAIN rather than to MATCH the LANGUAGE',
    instances: ['AN IDENTIFIER IS AN IDENTIFIER IN EVERY LANGUAGE', 'A DATE IS A DATE', 'and MONEY IS A FIXED-PRECISION DECIMAL'],
    why: 'BECAUSE A COLUMN TYPED AFTER the LANGUAGES CONVENIENCE IS A COLUMN WHOSE CONSTRAINTS the APPLICATION NOW HAS TO ENFORCE' },
];

WordPress 6.7 stores core tables in utf8mb4 where the database supports it, which is a change from the historical utf8 three-byte limitation. Your custom tables must declare the same charset or emoji and certain scripts will be silently truncated at the column boundary.

$collate = $wpdb->get_charset_collate();  // utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci or equivalent

// A VARCHAR index limit only bites on MySQL 5.6 and older,
// where the 767-byte index prefix caps a 3-byte character at 191.
// Declaring 191 keeps the table portable; 255 is safe on 5.7+ with
// the DYNAMIC row format, which is the default from 5.7.9.
reference varchar(191) NOT NULL NULL DEFAULT '',

Use datetime or date and never timestamp for a business date. A timestamp column is converted to UTC on write and back on read, and is subject to the 2038 limit and to the server time zone. A date column holding a business date has no time zone semantics at all, which is what you want.

Do store a time zone-aware UTC timestamp alongside it if you must compare instants. created_at datetime written with current_time( 'mysql', true ) is UTC, and the true argument is the part that is forgotten.

Reading and writing rows

Every statement that interpolates a value uses $wpdb->prepare(). The format specifiers are the second argument, and the values are bound, not interpolated. An IN clause takes a single placeholder per element.

global $wpdb;
$table = $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_ledger_entries';

$rows = $wpdb->get_results(
    $wpdb->prepare(
        "SELECT id, account_id, amount, memo
         FROM {$table}
         WHERE account_id = %d
           AND entry_date BETWEEN %s AND %s
           AND currency = %s
         ORDER BY entry_date DESC, id DESC
         LIMIT %d OFFSET %d",
        $account_id,
        $from,
        $to,
        $currency,
        $per_page,
        $offset
    ),
    ARRAY_A
);

$count = ( int ) $wpdb->get_var(
    $wpdb->prepare( "SELECT COUNT(*) FROM {$table} WHERE account_id = %d", $account_id )
);

$placeholders = implode( ',', array_fill( 0, count( $ids ), '%d' ) );
$found = $wpdb->get_col(
    $wpdb->prepare( "SELECT id FROM {$table} WHERE id IN ({$placeholders})", $ids )
);

$wpdb->insert(
    $table,
    array(
        'account_id' => $account_id,
        'amount'     => $amount,
        'memo'       => $memo,
    ),
    array( '%d', '%f', '%s' ),
    array( 'id' )
);

$wpdb->update( $table, array( 'memo' => $memo ), array( 'id' => $id ), array( '%s' ), array( '%d' ) );
$wpdb->delete( $table, array( 'account_id' => $account_id ), array( '%d' ) );

Notice that the table name is interpolated but the values are not. This is the correct pattern: the table name is derived from $wpdb->prefix and is not attacker-controlled, and a value that reaches prepare() is bound. Interpolating a table name into prepare() is not possible because it is not a value.

Reading and writing rows, and the shape a query method should have

The database object has a small number of read and write helpers and the discipline around them is what separates a query that is correct from a query that is correct and fast. A read returns rows in one of two shapes, objects or associative arrays, and a write returns whether it affected a row and, for inserts, the identifier it created. The methods differ in whether they return one row or many, whether they return a result object or a bare value, and whether they prepare or format — and choosing between them is mostly a matter of which shape the calling code wants. Then the three things that follow, and each is a mechanism. Prefer the prepared path for anything a user can influence, because it separates the statement from the values and a value that can never become part of the statement cannot change the statement; formatting achieves the same safety for one value but reads worse and is easy to get wrong when a value is concatenated by accident. Always select the columns you need, because the object's helpers can fetch every column, a large text column on a list query is a cost multiplied by the row count, and selecting everything also makes the code brittle against a schema change that adds a column. And a write that reports success did not necessarily change anything, which is the "unchanged value" property the options chapter described arriving in a different form — an update that sets a column to the value it already has affects no rows and is not an error. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Using a helper's return value as a count when the helper returns a result object, which produces a count of one because an object is one thing — the shape of the return is a decision and reading it wrong is a bug that looks like a data problem. Not using the inserted identifier, which means the caller has to query again to find out what was created, and the second query is a race against anything else that writes concurrently. And running a query inside a loop, which is the single most common cause of a slow page in this layer and the one the platform's own query log exists to find. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Wrap each query in a named method that returns a domain value, so the SQL appears in exactly one place per operation and so the caller's code reads as domain logic rather than as database calls — which is also what makes the caching decision per operation rather than per query. And log slow queries on the development site, because the platform can record every query with its duration, and the list of the ten slowest on a real page is a shorter and more reliable list of what to fix than any amount of guessing.

/* What the helpers do. */

const HELPERS = {
  read: 'RETURNS ROWS IN ONE of TWO SHAPES - OBJECTS or ASSOCIATIVE ARRAYS',
  write: 'RETURNS WHETHER IT AFFECTED A ROW and, FOR INSERTS, the IDENTIFIER IT CREATED',
  varyIn: ['WHETHER THEY RETURN ONE ROW or MANY', 'WHETHER THEY RETURN A RESULT OBJECT or A BARE VALUE', 'and WHETHER THEY PREPARE or FORMAT'],
  choosing: 'CHOOSING BETWEEN THEM IS MOSTLY A MATTER of WHICH SHAPE the CALLING CODE WANTS',
};

/* Three consequences. */
const CONSEQUENCES = [
  { c: 'PREFER the PREPARED PATH for ANYTHING A USER CAN INFLUENCE',
    why: 'BECAUSE IT SEPARATES the STATEMENT FROM the VALUES',
    and: 'A VALUE THAT CAN NEVER BECOME PART of the STATEMENT CANNOT CHANGE the STATEMENT',
    compared: 'FORMATTING ACHIEVES the SAME SAFETY for ONE VALUE but READS WORSE and IS EASY TO GET WRONG WHEN A VALUE IS CONCATENATED BY ACCIDENT' },
  { c: 'ALWAYS SELECT the COLUMNS YOU NEED',
    detail: 'BECAUSE the OBJECTS HELPERS CAN FETCH EVERY COLUMN',
    cost: 'A LARGE TEXT COLUMN ON A LIST QUERY IS A COST MULTIPLIED BY the ROW COUNT',    and: 'SELECTING EVERYTHING ALSO MAKES the CODE BRITTLE AGAINST A SCHEMA CHANGE THAT ADDS A COLUMN' },
  { c: 'A WRITE THAT REPORTS SUCCESS DID NOT NECESSARILY CHANGE ANYTHING',
    connects: 'WHICH IS the unchanged value PROPERTY the OPTIONS CHAPTER DESCRIBED ARRIVING IN A DIFFERENT FORM',
    detail: 'AN UPDATE THAT SETS A COLUMN TO the VALUE IT ALREADY HAS AFFECTS NO ROWS and IS NOT AN ERROR' },
];

/* Three things that go wrong. */
const GO_WRONG = [
  { f: "USING A HELPERS RETURN VALUE AS A COUNT WHEN the HELPER RETURNS A RESULT OBJECT",
    symptom: 'WHICH PRODUCES A COUNT OF ONE BECAUSE AN OBJECT IS ONE THING',
    and: 'the SHAPE of the RETURN IS A DECISION and READING IT WRONG IS A BUG THAT LOOKS LIKE A DATA PROBLEM' },
  { f: 'NOT USING the INSERTED IDENTIFIER',
    consequence: 'WHICH MEANS the CALLER HAS TO QUERY AGAIN to FIND OUT WHAT WAS CREATED',
    and: 'the SECOND QUERY IS A RACE AGAINST ANYTHING ELSE THAT WRITES CONCURRENTLY' },
  { f: 'RUNNING A QUERY INSIDE A LOOP',
    rank: 'the SINGLE MOST COMMON CAUSE of A SLOW PAGE IN this LAYER',
    detail: 'and the ONE the PLATFORMS OWN QUERY LOG EXISTS to FIND' },
];

/* Two practices. */
const PRACTICES = [
  { p: 'WRAP EACH QUERY IN A NAMED METHOD THAT RETURNS A DOMAIN VALUE',
    gain: ['SO the SQL APPEARS IN EXACTLY ONE PLACE PER OPERATION', 'and SO the CALLERS CODE READS AS DOMAIN LOGIC rather than AS DATABASE CALLS'],    and: 'WHICH IS ALSO WHAT MAKES the CACHING DECISION PER OPERATION rather than PER QUERY' },
  { p: 'LOG SLOW QUERIES ON the DEVELOPMENT SITE',
    why: 'BECAUSE the PLATFORM CAN RECORD EVERY QUERY with ITS DURATION',
    and: 'the LIST of the TEN SLOWEST ON A REAL PAGE IS A SHORTER and MORE RELIABLE LIST of WHAT TO FIX THAN ANY AMOUNT OF GUESSING' },
];

Wrap reads in the object cache. A get_results() call is a direct database round trip on every invocation, bypassing the object cache entirely. Cache the result in a named group with a versioned key, and delete that group when the underlying rows change. Chapter 9 covers the invalidation pattern.

Versioned schema migration

A plugin whose schema evolves needs a version number in the database and a routine that compares it. This is the only correct place to run dbDelta() after the initial activation, because dbDelta() issues several SHOW and DESCRIBE statements and must not run on every request.

final class Ledger_Installer {
    private const DB_VERSION = '2.4.0';

    public static function activate(): void {
        self::install_schema();
        update_option( 'acme_ledger_db_version', self::DB_VERSION, true );
    }

    public static function maybe_upgrade(): void {
        $installed = ( string ) get_option( 'acme_ledger_db_version', '0.0.0' );

        if ( version_compare( $installed, '2.2.0', '<' ) ) {
            self::add_currency_column();
        }

        if ( version_compare( $installed, '2.4.0', '<' ) ) {
            self::install_schema();
            self::backfill_currency();
        }

        if ( version_compare( $installed, self::DB_VERSION, '<' ) ) {
            update_option( 'acme_ledger_db_version', self::DB_VERSION, true );
        }
    }

    private static function add_currency_column(): void {
        global $wpdb;

        $table = $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_ledger_entries';
        $column = $table . '.' . 'currency';

        if ( $wpdb->get_var( $wpdb->prepare( 'SHOW COLUMNS FROM %s LIKE %s', $table, 'currency' ) ) ) {
            return;
        }

        $wpdb->query( "ALTER TABLE {$table} ADD COLUMN currency varchar(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'EUR'" );
    }
}

add_action( 'plugins_loaded', array( Ledger_Installer::class, 'maybe_upgrade' ), 5 );

Guarding each step with a SHOW COLUMNS check makes the routine idempotent, which matters because a user may have upgraded across two plugin versions in one operation and a previous attempt may have been interrupted. The check costs one query and removes a whole class of failure.

Exercises 8

  1. Write a CREATE TABLE statement for a three-entity normalised model and prove dbDelta() reports no changes on a second run.
  2. Deliberately collapse a required two-space gap in a PRIMARY KEY definition and record the exact behaviour of dbDelta() in your debug log.
  3. Store and sum ten thousand amounts as decimal(19,4) and as double, and quantify the divergence.
  4. Implement a versioned upgrade that adds a column and backfills it, then interrupt the routine mid-run and confirm the idempotency guard allows a safe re-run.

9 · WP_Query, pre_get_posts and Query Modification

WP_Query and its argumentsThe main query and pre_get_postsSecondary queriestax_query, meta_query and date_queryTerm queries and get_termsObject caching and invalidation

The query API is the highest-traffic surface in a content plugin. This chapter covers the arguments that matter for performance, the correct way to influence the main query, and the caching discipline that keeps a custom query from being a database round trip on every page load.

WP_Query and its arguments

Key details

WP_Query accepts a large argument array. Four arguments have a disproportionate effect on performance and should be set deliberately on every query a plugin runs.

$entries = new WP_Query( array(
    'post_type'              => 'acme_entry',
    'post_status'            => 'publish',
    'posts_per_page'         => 25,
    'paged'                  => max( 1, absint( $page ) ),
    'orderby'                 => array( 'entry_date' => 'DESC', 'ID' => 'DESC' ),
    'no_found_rows'          => true,
    'update_post_meta_cache'  => false,
    'update_post_term_cache'  => false,
    'fields'                  => '',
) );

$total = count( $entries->posts );

no_found_rows => true removes the SQL_CALC_FOUND_ROWS clause and the second query that computes the total. Set it whenever you do not paginate. The two cache flags remove the meta and term priming queries when you only need the post rows, and they are safe to disable because the first code that calls get_post_meta() on a post will prime the cache on demand.

The orderby array is deterministic. Ordering only by a date column returns an arbitrary order for rows sharing the same date, and a stable secondary key is what prevents a row from appearing on two pages or on none.

Order by a column you have indexed. WordPress will not index your entry_date meta value. If orderby => entry_date is a meta value, MySQL sorts the whole result set in a temporary table, which is unbounded memory for a large archive. A dedicated indexed column in a custom table, covered in Chapter 8, is the correct structure for a sortable, paginated meta value.

The main query and pre_get_posts

Key details

The main query is constructed by WordPress before pre_get_posts fires, which is what makes it the correct and only supported point at which to alter the variables of the canonical query. Modifying query variables later by assigning to globals does not work reliably.

add_action( 'pre_get_posts', array( Ledger_Query::class, 'shape_archive' ) );

public static function shape_archive( WP_Query $query ): void {
    if ( is_admin() || ! $query->is_main_query() ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( ! $query->is_post_type_archive( 'acme_entry' ) ) {
        return;
    }

    $query->set( 'posts_per_page', 25 );
    $query->set( 'orderby', array( 'menu_order' => 'ASC', 'title' => 'ASC' ) );
    $query->set( 'meta_query', array( Ledger_Query::published_meta_clause() ) );
}

The guard at the top is the part that is always required. Without is_admin() your code runs against every admin list table query, and without is_main_query() it runs against every secondary query including the ones you wrote yourself, producing recursion.

Three further guards are worth considering. Skip the block editor’s internal post queries with $query->get( 'post_type' ) checks. Skip REST collection requests by testing defined( 'REST_REQUEST' ) && REST_REQUEST. And skip your own query by checking a query var you set yourself.

public static function shape_search( WP_Query $query ): void {
    if ( is_admin() || ! $query->is_main_query() || ! $query->is_search() ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( defined( 'REST_REQUEST' ) && REST_REQUEST ) {
        return;
    }

    $query->set( 'post_type', array( 'post', 'page', 'acme_entry' ) );
    $query->set( 'posts_per_page', 15 );

    add_filter( 'the_posts', function( array $posts ): array {
        return $posts;
    }, 10, 1 );
}

Secondary queries

Key details

A secondary query is any WP_Query you instantiate. The critical rule is that it must not run inside the loop on a singular view, because the global $post and $wp_query are left pointing at your results and the rest of the template breaks.

// Correct: run it in the footer, after the main loop has completed.
add_action( 'wp_footer', function(): void {
    $related = new WP_Query( array(
        'post_type'      => 'acme_entry',
        'posts_per_page'  => 5,
        'no_found_rows'   => true,
        'fields'          => 'ids',
        'tax_query'       => array( Ledger_Query::account_clause( $account_id ) ),
    ) );
} );

// Correct: an explicit loop with its own template.
$query = new WP_Query( array( 'post_type' => 'acme_entry', 'posts_per_page' => 10 ) );

if ( $query->have_posts() ) {
    while ( $query->have_posts() ) {
        $query->the_post();
        get_template_part( 'content', 'entry' );
    }
    $query->the_post();  // restore the global $post
}

'fields' => 'ids' is a significant optimisation when the query exists only to feed a list of links or a lookup. It selects the primary key alone and skips the entire post object hydration, including the meta and term priming that WP_Post::get_instance() triggers.

get_posts() is a thin wrapper over WP_Query that returns an array of WP_Post and sets no_found_rows and 'ignore_sticky_posts' by default. It does not set suppress_filters, so your pre_get_posts handlers still run against it. Pass 'suppress_filters' => true when you need the raw result.

tax_query, meta_query and date_query

Key details

The three clause arguments share a common shape: an array of arrays, where each inner array is a condition and the boolean keys at the top level define how those conditions combine.

$meta_query = array(
    'relation' => 'AND',
    array(
        'key'     => '_acme_entry_date',
        'value'   => $from,
        'compare'" => >=',
        'type'     => 'DATE',
    ),
    array(
        'key'     => '_acme_entry_date',
        'value'   => $to,
        'compare' => '<=',
        'type'     => 'DATE',
    ),
    array(
        'key'     => '_acme_reviewed',
        'value'   => '1',
        'compare' => 'EXISTS',
    ),
);

$tax_query = array(
    'relation' => 'OR',
    array( 'taxonomy' => 'acme_status', 'field' => 'slug', 'terms' => array( 'posted', 'settled' ) ),
    array( 'taxonomy' => 'acme_status', 'field' => 'slug', 'terms' => array( 'archived' ), 'operator' => 'NOT IN' ),
);

$date_query = array(
    'relation' => 'AND',
    array(
        'after'     => '1 year ago',
        'inclusive' => true,
    ),
    array(
        'before'    => 'today',
        'inclusive' => false,
    ),
    array(
        'year'  => 2026,
        'month' => array( 1, 2, 3 ),
        'day'   => array( 1, 15 ),
    ),
);

Mind the meta type on every comparison. A value of '1' compared with 'EXISTS' is fine, but a value of '1' compared with '=' and no type compares as the string '1' against whatever was stored. WordPress stores what it was given, so declare 'type' => 'NUMERIC' whenever the value is numeric.

Term queries and get_terms

Key details

Term retrieval has its own query class and its own cache. A taxonomy with many terms needs number and offset set explicitly, because the default returns everything and the result is then filtered in PHP.

$terms = new WP_Term_Query( array(
    'taxonomy'   => 'acme_account',
    'hide_empty' => true,
    'orderby'    => 'meta_value_num',
    'meta_key'   => '_acme_account_code',
    'order'      => 'ASC',
    'number'     => 50,
    'offset'     => $offset,
) );

$names   = wp_list_pluck( $terms->terms, 'name' );
$objects = get_terms( array(
    'taxonomy'   => 'acme_account',
    'slug'       => $slugs,
    'hide_empty' => false,
) );

$post_terms = wp_get_object_terms( $post_id, 'acme_account', array( 'fields' => 'names' ) );

'hide_empty' => true issues a NOT EXISTS subquery against wp_term_relationships. On a large taxonomy this is correct and necessary; on a small one it is unnecessary work, and omitting it is a reasonable optimisation. Decide per taxonomy and document the decision.

Object caching and invalidation

WordPress 6.7 requires a persistent object cache for multisite and strongly recommends it elsewhere. A plugin that does not use the object cache API will be slow on any site that has one, because it misses every shared cache.

public static function get_totals( int $account_id ): array {
    $key = 'totals_' . $account_id;
    $hit = wp_cache_get( $key, 'acme-ledger' );

    if ( false !== $hit ) {
        return $hit;
    }

    $totals = Ledger_Repository::compute_totals( $account_id );

    wp_cache_set( $key, $totals, 'acme-ledger', HOUR_IN_SECONDS );

    return $totals;
}

public static function invalidate_account( int $account_id ): void {
    wp_cache_delete( 'totals_' . $account_id, 'acme-ledger' );
    wp_cache_delete( 'accounts', 'acme-ledger' );
}

add_action( 'save_post_acme_entry', function( int $post_id ): void {
    $account_ids = wp_get_object_terms( $post_id, 'acme_account', array( 'fields' => 'ids' ) );

    foreach ( $account_ids as $account_id ) {
        Ledger_Query::invalidate_account( (int) $account_id );
    }
} );

A versioned key is a more robust alternative to deletion, because a delete is a write on the cache backend and a version bump is a read. Increment acme_ledger_version in a single small option, include it in every cache key, and every previously cached value becomes unreachable at once.

private static function version(): int {
    $version = get_option( 'acme_ledger_cache_version', 1 );

    return (int) $version;
}

public static function bump(): void {
    update_option( 'acme_ledger_cache_version', self::version() + 1, true );
}

$key = 'totals_' . self::version() . '_'</ . $account_id;

Register a non-persistent cache group if your values are request-specific or large. wp_cache_add_non_persistent_groups() tells a persistent object cache to skip the group entirely, which is the correct way to keep a multi-megabyte key out of Redis.

Exercises 9

  1. Shape an archive query through pre_get_posts and confirm that admin list tables, REST collections and your own secondary queries are all unaffected.
  2. Run the same query with and without no_found_rows and fields => ids and record the query count difference with SAVEQUERIES.
  3. Build a versioned cache key and prove that a single increment invalidates a hundred distinct keys.
  4. Write a tax_query with an OR relation and a NOT IN operator, and explain the resulting SQL against wp_term_relationships.

10 · Post Types, Taxonomies and Capabilities

register_post_type in fullLabels and feature supportRewrites, archives and flushingTaxonomy registrationCapabilities and map_meta_capMeta registration for the REST API

Custom content is the largest surface a plugin can add to WordPress, and the most consequential, because it determines who can do what. Registration is declarative and one-shot on init; the capability model it establishes is enforced by core on every subsequent request.

register_post_type in full

Key details

A post type registered with public => true is queryable on the front end, available in the admin, and has its own rewrite rules. Most arguments have defensible defaults, and the ones that matter most are the ones that are wrong by default for a plugin.

add_action( 'init', 'acme_ledger_register_post_type' );

function acme_ledger_register_post_type(): void {
    $labels = array(
        'name'                  => __( 'Entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'singular_name'         => __( 'Entry', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'menu_name'             => __( 'Ledger', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'add_new'               => __( 'Add entry', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'add_new_item'          => __( 'Add new entry', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'edit_item'             => __( 'Edit entry', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'new_item'              => __( 'New entry', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'view_item'             => __( 'View entry', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'search_items'          => __( 'Search entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'not_found'             => __( 'No entries found.', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'not_found_in_trash'    => __( 'No entries in the bin.', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'all_items'             => __( 'All entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'featured_image'        => __( 'Voucher image', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'set_featured_image'    => __( 'Set voucher image', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'archives'              => __( 'Entry archives', 'acme-ledger' ),
    );

    register_post_type( 'acme_entry', array(
        'labels'             => $labels,
        'public'             => true,
        'publicly_queryable'  => true,
        'show_ui'            => true,
        'show_in_menu'       => true,
        'show_in_nav_menus'  => true,
        'show_in_admin_bar'  => true,
        'menu_position'      => 58,
        'menu_icon'          => 'dashicons-chart-bar',
        'capability_type'    => array( 'acme_entry', 'acme_entries' ),
        'map_meta_cap'       => true,
        'supports'           => array( 'title', 'editor', 'author', 'revisions', 'thumbnail', 'excerpt' ),
        'has_archive'        => 'entries',
        'rewrite'            => array( 'slug' => 'entries', 'with_front' => false ),
        'query_var'           => 'acme_entry',
        'show_in_rest'       => true,
        'rest_base'          => 'entries',
        'delete_with_user'    => false,
        'can_export'          => true,
    ) );
}

Three arguments deserve a note. 'supports' => array( 'editor' ) on its own gives a title-only record with no rich text, which is usually wrong for a business document. 'delete_with_user' => false prevents a user deletion from silently destroying financial records, which is usually right. And 'show_in_rest' => true is what makes the post type available to the block editor, the REST API and any headless front end, and it cannot be added later without a flush of the rewrite rules.

Post type names are permanent. The name you pass to register_post_type() becomes the database post_type value, the meta key prefix, the REST base default and the query variable. Renaming it after release orphans every existing row, because no migration is feasible. Choose a name that is prefixed and that you would still accept in five years.

Labels and feature support

Key details

Labels are passed as an associative array rather than as a string, which is what allows full grammatical control. Omitting a label does not fall back to a translated default in most cases; WordPress uses the name and produces a grammatically wrong interface.

Feature support is additive and can be changed after registration with add_post_type_support() and remove_post_type_support(). Two supports are not reversible in practice. Removing custom-fields does not delete existing meta, and adding revisions to a post type with existing content does not create revisions retrospectively.

// Remove the custom fields panel from the editor for your post type.
remove_post_type_support( 'acme_entry', 'custom-fields' );

// Register a custom meta box. The callback returns sanitised markup only.
add_action( 'add_meta_boxes', function(): void {
    add_meta_box(
        'acme-ledger-entry-meta',
        __( 'Ledger details', 'acme-ledger' ),
        static function( WP_Post $post ): void {
            $amount = get_post_meta( $post->ID, '_acme_amount', true );
            ?>
            <p>
                <label for="acme-amount"><?= esc_html__( 'Amount', 'acme-ledger' ) ?></label>
                <input type="text" id="acme-amount" name="acme_amount"
                    value="<?= esc_attr( $amount ) ?>">
            </p>
            <?php
        },
        'acme_entry',
        'normal',
        'high'
    );
} );

The save handler must verify the nonce, verify the capability, and declare an explicit schema. The auth argument to add_meta_box() is not a security control; it only controls whether the box is shown in the UI for the current user.

add_action( 'save_post_acme_entry', function( int $post_id, WP_Post $post ): void {
    if ( defined( 'DOING_AUTOSAVE' ) && DOING_AUTOSAVE ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( ! isset( $_POST['acme_nonce'] ) ) {
        return;
    }

    $nonce = sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['acme_nonce'] ) );

    if ( ! wp_verify_nonce( $nonce, 'acme_save_entry' ) ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_post', $post_id ) ) {
        return;
    }

    if ( ! isset( $_POST['acme_amount'] ) ) {
        return;
    }

    $amount = Ledger_Money::decimal( sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['acme_amount'] ) ) );

    if ( '' === $amount ) {
        return;
    }

    update_post_meta( $post_id, '_acme_amount', $amount );
}, 10, 2 );

Rewrites, archives and flushing

Key details

Rewrite rules are compiled and stored in the wp_rewrite option. They are regenerated when a post type is registered and flushed, which does not happen on a normal page load. A new post type with a rewrite slug is therefore a 404 until a flush occurs.

// On activation, after register_post_type has run on this request.
register_activation_hook( ACME_LEDGER_FILE, function(): void {
    acme_ledger_register_post_type();
    flush_rewrite_rules();
} );

// On upgrade, and only when the rewrite version changes.
const REWRITE_VERSION = '2';

public static function maybe_flush_rewrites(): void {
    if ( (int) get_option( 'acme_ledger_rewrite_version' ) === self::REWRITE_VERSION ) {
        return;
    }

    flush_rewrite_rules( false );
    update_option( 'acme_ledger_rewrite_version', self::REWRITE_VERSION, true );
}

Flushing is expensive because it rebuilds every rule for every registered post type. Calling flush_rewrite_rules() on init on every request will melt a large site. The version guard above is the standard solution and costs one autoloaded option read.

Passing false to flush_rewrite_rules() skips the .htaccess write, which is correct unless the plugin has actually changed permalink structure. Most plugins never need the true form.

Taxonomy registration

Key details

A taxonomy registered as hierarchical behaves like categories; one registered as flat behaves like tags. The choice is semantic and permanent, because it determines whether the parent column in wp_term_hierarchy is populated.

function acme_ledger_register_taxonomies(): void {
    register_taxonomy( 'acme_account', array( 'acme_entry' ), array(
        'labels'            => array(
            'name'          => __( 'Accounts', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'singular_name' => __( 'Account', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'search_items'  => __( 'Search accounts', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'all_items'     => __( 'All accounts', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'edit_item'     => __( 'Edit account', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'add_new_item'  => __( 'Add new account', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'menu_name'     => __( 'Accounts', 'acme-ledger' ),
        ),
        'hierarchical'      => true,
        'public'            => true,
        'show_ui'           => true,
        'show_admin_column'  => true,
        'show_in_rest'      => true,
        'rest_base'         => 'accounts',
        'rewrite'           => array( 'slug' => 'account', 'with_front' => false ),
        'capabilities'      => array(
            'manage_terms' => 'manage_acme_accounts',
            'edit_terms'   => 'edit_acme_accounts',
            'delete_terms' => 'delete_acme_accounts',
            'assign_terms' => 'assign_acme_accounts',
        ),
    ) );

    register_taxonomy( 'acme_status', array( 'acme_entry' ), array(
        'hierarchical' => false,
        'show_in_rest' => true,
        'rewrite'      => 'status',
    ) );
}

Non-hierarchical taxonomies have no administrative management UI. A tag-like taxonomy that needs its own list table must be registered with a capability set and an explicit management page, or you will get a metabox on the post edit screen and nothing else.

Capabilities and map_meta_cap

Key details

Capability naming is derived automatically from the singular and plural forms of capability_type, producing fourteen primitive capabilities. map_meta_cap => true turns the meta capabilities edit_post, read_post, delete_post into a check against the object author and its status.

Meta capabilityResolves to
edit_postedit_published_posts if published and owned, edit_private_posts if private and owned, edit_others_posts if owned by another user, edit_posts otherwise.
read_postread_private_posts for a private post the user does not own, read otherwise.
delete_postdelete_published_posts, delete_private_posts or delete_posts depending on status and ownership.
// Meta capabilities must be present, because the user interface calls them.
'capabilities' => array(
    'edit_post'              => 'edit_acme_entry',
    'read_post'              => 'read_acme_entry',
    'delete_post'            => 'delete_acme_entry',
    'edit_posts'              => 'edit_acme_entries',
    'edit_others_posts'       => 'edit_others_acme_entries',
    'publish_posts'           => 'publish_acme_entries',
    'read_private_posts'      => 'read_private_acme_entries',
    'delete_posts'            => 'delete_acme_entries',
    'delete_private_posts'     => 'delete_private_acme_entries',
    'delete_published_posts'   => 'delete_published_acme_entries',
    'delete_others_posts'     => 'delete_others_acme_entries',
    'edit_private_posts'       => 'edit_private_acme_entries',
    'edit_published_posts'     => 'edit_published_acme_entries',
    'create_posts'            => 'edit_acme_entries',
),

Roles receive their capabilities on activation, and new roles must be granted them explicitly for existing sites. A plugin that adds capabilities only in the activation hook silently denies every existing user after a reinstall, because the hook does not run on a site where the plugin is already active.

Meta registration for the REST API

Meta written by update_post_meta() is not writable through the REST API unless it is registered. Registration declares the type, the sanitisation, the authorisation and the visibility, and it is the mechanism the block editor uses to discover your fields.

function acme_ledger_register_meta(): void {
    register_post_meta( 'acme_entry', '_acme_amount', array(
        'type'              => 'string',
        'description'       => __( 'Amount in minor units.', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'single'            => true,
        'default'           => '0',
        'show_in_rest'      => true,
        'sanitize_callback' => 'acme_ledger_sanitize_amount',
        'auth_callback'     => function(): bool {
            return current_user_can( 'edit_acme_entries' );
        },
    ) );

    register_post_meta( 'acme_entry', '_acme_reviewed', array(
        'type'          => 'boolean',
        'single'        => true,
        'show_in_rest'  => array(
            'schema' => array( 'type' => 'boolean', 'context' => array( 'edit', 'view' ) ),
        ),
        'auth_callback' => function(): bool {
            return current_user_can( 'edit_acme_entries' );
        },
    ) );
}

function acme_ledger_sanitize_amount( mixed $value ): string {
    $clean = preg_replace( '/[^0-9\-]/', '', ( string ) $value );

    return ( string ) intval( $clean );
}

Without an auth_callback, a registered meta key with show_in_rest is readable and writable by any authenticated user who can edit the post, which is usually correct. With one, the callback decides. Prefer omitting the callback and letting the post capability govern.

Exercises 10

  1. Register a post type with a full label set, custom capabilities and map_meta_cap, then verify the list table column, the status transitions and the author logic all behave as specified.
  2. Add a rewrite slug change, implement the version-guarded flush, and confirm no flush occurs on an unrelated page load.
  3. Register a non-hierarchical taxonomy with a management capability set and confirm that unprivileged users cannot create terms.
  4. Register a boolean meta key with a show_in_rest schema and confirm that a subscriber cannot write it through POST /wp/v2/entries/….

11 · Users, Roles and Capability Checks

The current userRoles and granting capabilitiesCapability check patternsUser meta and user queriesMultisite and network scopePseudoroles and the effective capability

Capabilities are the entire authorisation model of WordPress, and a plugin either uses them correctly or it is not secure. This chapter covers the current user API, how to define and grant capabilities across an existing install, and the multisite scope rules that most plugins get wrong.

The current user

Key details

The current user is a lazily instantiated global. Reading it before WordPress has set it up returns user zero, which has no capabilities, and this is the cause of most “works for administrators but not for subscribers” reports.

$user = wp_get_current_user();
$id   = get_current_user_id();
$lang = get_user_locale( $id );

if ( ! is_user_logged_in() ) {
    return new WP_Error( 'acme_ledger_not_logged_in', 'Not authenticated.', array( 'status' => 401 ) );
}

$email = sanitize_email( ( string ) $user->user_email );

Never read $GLOBALS['current_user'] directly. The helper exists to trigger the lazy initialisation, and it is the only supported access path. On a REST request the current user is set by the cookie or application password authenticator well before any permission callback runs.

Do not cache the user ID in a static property across a long-running process. WordPress core itself is not designed for a request that outlives a normal request lifecycle, and a worker process that keeps a stale user identity is a privilege escalation waiting to happen.

Roles and granting capabilities

Key details

A plugin that introduces capabilities must grant them to the roles it wants to use, and must do so for installations that already exist. The activation hook runs only once, which makes it the wrong and only tempting place to do this.

final class Ledger_Capabilities {
    public const ROLE_VERSION = '2.4.0';

    public static function activate(): void {
        self::add_caps();
        self::maybe_add_caps();
    }

    public static function maybe_add_caps(): void {
        if ( (string) get_option( 'acme_ledger_role_version' ) === self::ROLE_VERSION ) {
            return;
        }

        self::add_caps();
        update_option( 'acme_ledger_role_version', self::ROLE_VERSION, true );
    }

    private static function add_caps(): void {
        $caps = array(
            'edit_acme_entry', 'read_acme_entry', 'delete_acme_entry',
            'edit_acme_entries', 'edit_others_acme_entries',
            'publish_acme_entries', 'read_private_acme_entries',
            'delete_acme_entries', 'delete_private_acme_entries',
            'delete_published_acme_entries', 'delete_others_acme_entries',
            'edit_private_acme_entries', 'edit_published_acme_entries',
            'manage_acme_accounts', 'edit_acme_accounts',
            'delete_acme_accounts', 'assign_acme_accounts',
        );

        $admin = get_role( 'administrator' );

        if ( ! $admin ) {
            return;
        }

        foreach ( $caps as $cap ) {
            $admin->add_cap( $cap );
        }

        $editor = get_role( 'editor' );

        if ( $editor ) {
            foreach ( array( 'edit_acme_entries', 'publish_acme_entries' ) as $cap ) {
                $editor->add_cap( $cap );
            }
        }
    }
}

add_action( 'plugins_loaded', array( Ledger_Capabilities::class, 'maybe_add_caps' ), 4 );

Removing capabilities is your obligation too. The uninstall routine must call remove_cap() for every capability it added. Leaving orphaned capabilities behind after a user deletes your plugin is a support burden and a security smell, and the wordpress.org reviewer will check for it.

Capability check patterns

Key details

There are exactly three correct capability check forms in plugin code. Each answers a different question, and choosing the wrong one is how a plugin ends up letting any authenticated user edit any record.

// 1. Object-level: may this user act on this specific post?
if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_post', $post_id ) ) {
    return new WP_Error( 'acme_ledger_forbidden', 'Forbidden.', array( 'status' => 403 ) );
}

// 2. Type-level: may this user create entries of this post type?
if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_posts', 'acme_entry' ) ) {
    wp_die( 'Forbidden.', '', array( 'response' => 403 ) );
}

// 3. Site-level: a custom plugin capability.
if ( ! current_user_can( 'manage_ledger' ) ) {
    wp_die( 'Forbidden.', '', array( 'response' => 403 ) );
}

The edit_posts check with a post type argument is the one most often missed. current_user_can( 'edit_posts' ) without the type checks the global capability for any post type, and an editor who has edit_posts for posts but not for your post type will be allowed through.

For a user who is not logged in, current_user_can() returns false for everything except 'exist', 'read' and is_admin()-adjacent checks. A capability check is therefore also an authentication check, and you do not need a separate is_user_logged_in() guard unless the error message should differ.

// Checking someone else's permissions rather than your own.
$can_edit = user_can( $other_user_id, 'edit_post', $post_id );

// Inspect the effective capability set. WordPress 5.9 and later.
$caps = wp_get_current_user()->allcaps;

// Grant a single capability to a single user.
$user = new WP_User( $user_id );
$user->add_cap( 'manage_ledger' );

User meta and user queries

Key details

A user preference belongs in user meta, not in a plugin option keyed by user ID. The meta API gives you the cache, the revision trail and the export and erasure integration described in Chapter 17 for free.

$currency = get_user_meta( get_current_user_id(), '_acme_currency', true );

if ( ! $currency ) {
    $currency = Ledger_Settings::get( 'currency' );
}

update_user_meta( get_current_user_id(), '_acme_currency', $currency );

$users = get_users( array(
    'role'       => 'shop_manager',
    'fields'"    => 'ID',
    'number'     => 100,
    'count_total' => true,
    'meta_query'" => array( array(
        'key'"     => '_acme_currency',
        'value'"   => 'EUR',
        'compare'" => 'EXISTS',
    ) ),
) );

$count = $users->total_users;

On a multisite install, get_users() queries the whole network by default unless 'blog_id' is set. On a large network that is a performance and privacy problem, and it is a common cause of an admin page timing out after a site grows past a few hundred sites.

Multisite and network scope

Key details

Multisite introduces a second scope. Network options are shared by every site; site options are per site. Reading the wrong one produces a plugin that appears configured on one site and mysteriously unset on another.

// Site-level option, per blog.
$enabled = get_option( 'acme_ledger_enabled' );
update_option( 'acme_ledger_enabled', true );

// Network-level option, shared by every site.
$key = get_site_option( 'acme_ledger_license_key' );
update_site_option( 'acme_ledger_license_key', $key );

// Capabilities in the network administrator role.
$network_admin = get_role( 'administrator' );

if ( $network_admin ) {
    $network_admin->add_cap( 'manage_network_ledger' );
}

// Iterate every site on the network.
$site_ids = get_sites( array( 'fields' => 'ids', 'number' => 0 ) );

foreach ( $site_ids as $site_id ) {
    switch_to_blog( $site_id );
    Ledger_Installer::maybe_upgrade();
    restore_current_blog();
}

switch_to_blog() changes the $wpdb prefix, the options table and the current post type registry context, and it is not free. Never call it on a front-end request. On a network activation, wrap the per-site loop in a check for is_multisite() and a capability check for manage_network_plugins.

register_activation_hook( ACME_LEDGER_FILE, function(): void {
    if ( is_multisite() && get_site_option( 'acme_ledger_network_wide' ) ) {
        $site_ids = get_sites( array( 'fields' => 'ids', 'number' => 0 ) );

        foreach ( $site_ids as $site_id ) {
            switch_to_blog( $site_id );
            Ledger_Capabilities::add_caps();
            Ledger_Installer::install_schema();
            restore_current_blog();
        }

        update_site_option( 'acme_ledger_network_wide', true );

        return;
    }

    Ledger_Capabilities::add_caps();
    Ledger_Installer::install_schema();
} );

Pseudoroles and the effective capability

A pseudorole is a filter on user_has_cap that grants a capability conditionally. It is the correct mechanism for a rule that depends on context rather than on a role, and it is the only correct way to add a temporary grant to a single request.

add_filter( 'user_has_cap', function( array $caps ): array {
    if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_acme_entries' ) ) {
        return $caps;
    }

    if ( get_current_user_id() !== (int) get_post_meta( get_the_ID(), '_acme_owner_id', true ) ) {
        return $caps;
    }

    $caps['edit_others_acme_entries'] = true;

    return $caps;
}, 10, 1 );

Modifying the capability array inside map_meta_cap or user_has_cap is powerful and dangerous. The filter runs on every capability check, including checks core performs internally, so an overly broad grant leaks authorisation to unrelated subsystems. Scope the condition as narrowly as possible and document the rule in the readme, because it changes what your plugin’s capability names mean.

Test with a real role matrix. Write an integration test that, for each role, records whether each capability resolves to true. Run it on every release. A change to map_meta_cap or to a capability name in a new WordPress release will fail the matrix immediately, and the failure will otherwise reach production.

Exercises 11

  1. Add a plugin capability that grants on activation and re-grants on upgrade, then prove the second path by uninstalling, reinstalling and reactivating over an existing install.
  2. Write a role-and-capability matrix test covering administrator, editor, author, contributor, subscriber and a custom role.
  3. Demonstrate that current_user_can( 'edit_posts' ) and current_user_can( 'edit_posts', 'acme_entry' ) return different values for a deliberately restricted role.
  4. Implement network activation on a three-site multisite install and verify the table exists on all three with no front-end switch_to_blog call.

12 · The REST API

register_rest_route and the namespaceArgument schema and validationpermissions_callbackResponses and errorsExtending WP_REST_ControllerCollections, pagination and schema

The REST API is the supported integration surface for a WordPress plugin, and it is where the security model is enforced most strictly. A route without a permissions_callback is a hard error, and validation that happens in the callback instead of the schema is validation you will forget somewhere.

register_rest_route and the namespace

Key details

Routes are registered on rest_api_init, which fires once per REST request. The namespace must contain at least two segments separated by a slash, and the route is appended to it. The third argument is an array of endpoint definitions or, for a single endpoint, the definition itself.

add_action( 'rest_api_init', array( Ledger_REST::class, 'register_routes' ) );

public static function register_routes(): void {
    register_rest_route( 'acme-ledger/v1', '/entries', array(
        array(
            'methods'             => WP_REST_Server::READABLE,
            'callback'            => array( self::class, 'get_items' ),
            'permission_callback' => array( self::class, 'get_items_permissions_check' ),
            'args'                => self::collection_params(),
        ),
        array(
            'methods'             => WP_REST_Server::CREATABLE,
            'callback'            => array( self::class, 'create_item' ),
            'permission_callback' => array( self::class, 'create_item_permissions_check' ),
            'args'                => self::create_params(),
        ),
    ) );

    register_rest_route( 'acme-ledger/v1', '/entries/(?P<id>[\d]+)', array(
        array(
            'methods'             => WP_REST_Server::'GET, PATCH, DELETE',
            'callback'            => array( self::class, 'get_item' ),
            'permission_callback' => array( self::class, 'get_item_permissions_check' ),
            'args'                => array( 'id' => self::id_param() ),
        ),
        array(
            'methods'             => WP_REST_Server::EDITABLE,
            'callback'            => array( self::class, 'update_item' ),
            'permission_callback' => array( self::class, 'update_item_permissions_check' ),
            'args'                => self::update_params(),
        ),
        array(
            'methods'             => WP_REST_Server::DELETABLE,
            'callback'            => array( self::class, 'delete_item' ),
            'permission_callback' => array( self::class, 'delete_item_permissions_check' ),
            'args'                => array( 'force' => array( 'type' => 'boolean', 'default' => false ) ),
        ),
    ) );
}

Note the fourth argument is not passed, so routes are additive. Passing true as the fourth argument replaces any route already registered at the same path, which is a useful escape hatch and a dangerous thing to do to another plugin.

Name the namespace after your plugin slug with a version suffix. The version is not enforced by WordPress, so bump it when a breaking change is made, and document that the previous version remains available for a defined period.

Argument schema and validation

Key details

Argument definitions are JSON Schema fragments. They drive validation, sanitisation, coercion and the automatically generated OPTIONS response, which is what makes a route discoverable from a client library.

private static function create_params(): array {
    return array(
        'account_id' => array(
            'description'       => __( 'Term ID of the ledger account.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'type'              => 'integer',
            'required'          => true,
            'minimum'           => 1,
            'sanitize_callback' => 'absint',
            'validate_callback' => function( mixed $value ): bool {
                return term_exists( (int) $value, 'acme_account' );
            },
        ),
        'amount' => array(
            'description' => __( 'Decimal amount with up to four decimal places.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'type'        => 'string',
            'required'    => true,
            'pattern'      => '/^-?[0-9]{1,15}(\.[0-9]{1,4})?$/',
            'max_length'  => 20,
        ),
        'currency' => array(
            'type'        => 'string',
            'enum'        => array( 'EUR', 'USD', 'GBP', 'JPY' ),
            'default'     => 'EUR',
        ),
        'memo' => array(
            'type'              => 'string',
            'max_length'        => 500,
            'sanitize_callback' => 'sanitize_textarea_field',
        ),
        'status' => array(
            'type'"       => 'string',
            'enum'"       => array( 'draft', 'posted', 'settled' ),
            'default'"    => 'draft',
            'sanitize_callback' => function( mixed $v ): string {
                return sanitize_key( ( string ) $v );
            },
        ),
    );
}

Sanitise and validate are not the same. sanitize_callback normalises a value into an acceptable form. validate_callback decides whether the value is acceptable at all and returns a boolean. Register both where both are needed, and never let a validate_callback return false silently — the API will still pass the original value to your callback, because a false validation only logs.

permissions_callback

Key details

Since WordPress 5.5, every route must declare a permission callback. Omitting it, or returning a value that is not true, produces a rest_forbidden error rather than a bypass, so the failure mode is safe but the endpoint is unusable.

public static function get_items_permissions_check( WP_REST_Request $request ): bool|WP_Error {
    if ( ! is_user_logged_in() ) {
        return new WP_Error(
            'acme_ledger_rest_not_logged_in',
            __( 'Authentication is required.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            array( 'status' => 401 )
        );
    }

    if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_posts', 'acme_entry' ) ) {
        return new WP_Error(
            'acme_ledger_rest_forbidden',
            __( 'You are not allowed to read entries.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            array( 'status' => rest_authorization_required_code() )
        );
    }

    return true;
}

public static function update_item_permissions_check( WP_REST_Request $request ): bool|WP_Error {
    $post = get_post( (int) $request['id'] );

    if ( ! $post || $post->post_type !== 'acme_entry' ) {
        return new WP_Error( 'acme_ledger_rest_not_found', __( 'Entry not found.', 'acme-ledger' ), array( 'status' => 404 ) );
    }

    if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_post', $post->ID ) ) {
        return new WP_Error( 'acme_ledger_rest_cannot_edit', __( 'You are not allowed to edit this entry.', 'acme-ledger' ), array( 'status' => 403 ) );
    }

    return true;
}

Return a WP_Error with the most specific status code you can: 401 when authentication is missing, 403 when it is present but insufficient, 404 when the resource should not be disclosed. Returning 403 for a resource that does not exist confirms its absence to an unauthenticated caller, which is a minor information disclosure that some compliance regimes care about.

Responses and errors

Key details

A callback returns data, a WP_REST_Response, or a WP_Error. Returning a bare array is the simplest correct form; the response object adds status codes, headers and link headers.

public static function create_item( WP_REST_Request $request ): WP_REST_Response|WP_Error {
    $post_id = Ledger_Repository::create_entry( array(
        'account_id' => (int) $request->get_param( 'account_id' ),
        'amount'     => ( string ) $request->get_param( 'amount' ),
        'currency'   => ( string ) $request->get_param( 'currency' ),
        'memo'       => ( string ) $request->get_param( 'memo' ),
    ) );

    if ( is_wp_error( $post_id ) ) {
        return $post_id;
    }

    $response = new WP_REST_Response( self::prepare_item( $post_id ), 201 );
    $response->header( 'Location', rest_url( 'acme-ledger/v1/entries/' . $post_id ) );

    return $response;
}

private static function prepare_item( int $post_id ): array {
    $post = get_post( $post_id );

    return array(
        'id'         => $post->ID,
        'account_id' => (int) get_post_meta( $post_id, '_acme_account_id', true ),
        'amount'     => (string) get_post_meta( $post_id, '_acme_amount', true ),
        'date'       => get_post_time( 'c', true, $post ),
        'link'       => get_permalink( $post ),
    );
}

Escape nothing in the response. The REST API serialises to JSON, and JSON is not HTML, so applying esc_html() to response data produces double-encoded output that the client must decode twice. Sanitise on input, validate on input, and return raw values.

Extending WP_REST_Controller

Key details

A class extending WP_REST_Controller gives you a documented structure with nine methods to override. The class is the right choice once a plugin exposes more than one route, because it keeps registration, permission logic and serialisation together.

final class Ledger_Entries_Controller extends WP_REST_Controller {
    public const REST_BASE = 'entries';
    public const NAMESPACE_NAME = 'acme-ledger/v1';

    public function register_routes(): void {
        register_rest_route(
            self::NAMESPACE_NAME,
            '/' . self::REST_BASE,
            array(
                array(
                    'methods'             => WP_REST_Server::READABLE,
                    'callback'            => array( $this, 'get_items' ),
                    'permission_callback' => array( $this, 'get_items_permissions_check' ),
                    'args'                => $this->get_collection_params(),
                ),
                array(
                    'methods'             => WP_REST_Server::CREATABLE,
                    'callback'            => array( $this, 'create_item' ),
                    'permission_callback' => array( $this, 'create_item_permissions_check' ),
                ),
            )
        );
    }

    public function get_items_permissions_check( $request ) {
        return current_user_can( 'edit_posts', 'acme_entry' ) || is_wp_error(
            rest_ensure_response( new WP_Error( 'acme_ledger_rest_forbidden', __( 'Forbidden.', 'acme-ledger' ), array( 'status' => 403 ) )
        );
    }

    public function get_collection_params(): array {
        return array_merge( parent::get_collection_params(), array(
        'status' => array(
            'description'" => __( 'Limit results by status.', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'type'"        => 'array',
            'items'"       => array( 'type' => 'string', 'enum' => array( 'draft', 'posted', 'settled' ) ),
            'default'"     => array( 'posted' ),
        ),
        ) );
    }
}

add_action( 'rest_api_init', function(): void {
    ( new Ledger_Entries_Controller() )->register_routes();
} );

Collections, pagination and schema

A collection endpoint must paginate. The X-WP-Total and X-WP-TotalPages headers are how clients discover the extent of the data, and omitting them makes pagination impossible for a consumer that did not hard-code the total.

public static function get_items( WP_REST_Request $request ): WP_REST_Response {
    $page     = max( 1, (int) $request->get_param( 'page' ) );
    $per_page = min( 100, max( 1, (int) $request->get_param( 'per_page' ) ) );

    $query = new WP_Query( array(
        'post_type'      => 'acme_entry',
        'post_status'"    => 'publish',
        'posts_per_page'" => $per_page,
        'paged'          => $page,
        'fields'"          => 'ids',
        'orderby'"       => 'date',
        'order'"         => 'DESC',
    ) );

    $items = array_map( array( self::class, 'prepare_item' ), $query->posts );

    $response = rest_ensure_response( $items );
    $response->header( 'X-WP-Total', (int) $query->found_posts );
    $response->header( 'X-WP-TotalPages', (int) $query->max_num_pages );

    return $response;
}

public static function get_item_schema(): array {
    return array(
        '$schema'"    => 'http://json-schema.org/draft-04/schema#',
        'title'"      => 'entry',
        'type'"        => 'object',
        'properties'" => array(
            'id'"         => array( 'type'" => 'integer', 'context'" => array( 'view', 'edit' ), 'readonly'" => true ),
            'amount'"     => array( 'type'" => 'string', 'context'" => array( 'view', 'edit' ) ),
            'account_id'" => array( 'type'" => 'integer', 'context'" => array( 'view', 'edit' ) ),
            'currency'"   => array( 'type'" => 'string', 'context'" => array( 'view', 'edit' ) ),
        ),
    );
}

The schema callback is passed as the callable value of the schema key, alongside the array of endpoint definitions rather than inside one of them. This is exactly what core does: WP_REST_Posts_Controller registers with 'schema' => array( $this, 'get_public_item_schema' ). A common error is to place schema inside a single endpoint definition, where it is silently ignored.

Cache the generated schema in a property. WP_REST_Controller declares protected $schema for exactly this purpose, and populating it once removes the schema generation from every request. The only wrinkle is that WordPress 5.4 and later may pass a WP_REST_Request to the schema callback, so declare the parameter as optional.

Declare context on every property. The context array is what tells a client which representation is which. A property with no context key appears in the response regardless of the requested context, which leaks write-only or privileged fields to read-only consumers.

Exercises 12

  1. Implement a full CRUD route set under acme-ledger/v1 with a controller class and inspect the auto-generated OPTIONS response for schema correctness.
  2. Remove the permission_callback from one endpoint and record the exact status code and message WordPress returns.
  3. Return 404 instead of 403 for an entry owned by another user and justify the change on information-disclosure grounds.
  4. Add a schema with per-property contexts and confirm a context=embed request omits the privileged fields.

13 · AJAX and the admin-ajax Debate

The admin-ajax registration modelPassing data to the scriptA complete handlerThe REST alternativeFront-end and unauthenticated requestsResponses, errors and debugging

Two mechanisms can serve an asynchronous request: admin-ajax.php and the REST API. They are not interchangeable, they have different security defaults, and choosing wrongly is the source of a large fraction of plugin vulnerabilities.

The admin-ajax registration model

Key details

A request to admin-ajax.php carries an action parameter. WordPress fires the hook named wp_ajax_{$action} for a logged-in user and wp_ajax_nopriv_{$action} for everyone else. A request whose action has no registered callback returns the literal string 0 and a 400 status.

add_action( 'wp_ajax_acme_ledger_search', array( $this, 'ajax_search' ) );
add_action( 'wp_ajax_nopriv_acme_ledger_search', array( $this, 'ajax_search_denied' ) );

public function ajax_search_denied(): void {
    wp_send_json_error(
        array( 'message' => __( 'Authentication required.', 'acme-ledger' ) ),
        401
    );
}

Registering only the authenticated hook is a deliberate decision and it is the safe one: an unauthenticated request for that action receives 0 and learns nothing. It is not a security control, though, because the hook name is public. The security control is the nonce and capability check inside the handler, and it must be there regardless of which hooks you registered.

admin-ajax.php does not load the admin. It bootstraps WordPress and nothing else. Functions such as get_current_screen(), wp_enqueue_media() and most of wp-admin/includes/ are unavailable unless you require the file yourself. Requiring an admin file inside an AJAX handler is acceptable, but importing the whole admin bootstrap is not, because it will emit output and break the JSON response.

Passing data to the script

Key details

The script needs a URL, a nonce and a small configuration object. The correct modern mechanism is wp_add_inline_script with wp_json_encode, which preserves types and avoids the string coercion of the older helper.

wp_enqueue_script( 'acme-ledger-admin', ACME_LEDGER_URL . 'admin/js/admin.js', array( 'wp-api-fetch' ), ACME_LEDGER_VERSION, true );

$config = array(
    'ajaxUrl'  => admin_url( 'admin-ajax.php' ),
    'restUrl'  => esc_url_raw( rest_url( 'acme-ledger/v1' ) ),
    'restNonce' => wp_create_nonce( 'wp_rest' ),
    'ajaxNonce' => wp_create_nonce( 'acme_ledger_search' ),
    'perPage'"  => 20,
    'canExport' => current_user_can( 'manage_ledger' ),
);

wp_add_inline_script(
    'acme-ledger-admin',
    'window.acmeLedgerConfig = ' . wp_json_encode( $config ) . ';',
    'before'
);

Generate a distinct nonce action string per handler. A single shared nonce lets one compromised handler forge a request to any other handler, which defeats the purpose of having nonces at all.

A complete handler

Key details

A handler is a public HTTP endpoint. It needs the same four checks as an admin form handler: verify the nonce, verify the capability, validate and sanitise the input, and escape the output.

public function ajax_search(): void {
    check_ajax_referer( 'acme_ledger_search', 'nonce' );

    if ( ! current_user_can( 'edit_posts', 'acme_entry' ) ) {
        wp_send_json_error( array( 'message' => __( 'Insufficient permissions.', 'acme-ledger' ) ), 403 );
    }

    $term    = sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['term'] ?? '' ) );
    $page    = max( 1, absint( $_POST['page'] ?? 1 ) );
    $per_page = min( 50, max( 1, absint( $_POST['per_page'] ?? 20 ) ) );

    if ( '' === $term || mb_strlen( $term ) > 100 ) {
        wp_send_json_error( array( 'message' => __( 'Invalid search term.', 'acme-ledger' ) ), 400 );
    }

    $query = new WP_Query( array(
        'post_type'      => 'acme_entry',
        's'"                => $term,
        'posts_per_page'   => $per_page,
        'paged'"            => $page,
        'post_status"'      => 'publish',
        'no_found_rows'    => false,
        'update_post_meta_cache' => false,
    ) );

    $results = array_map( static function( WP_Post $post ): array {
        return array(
            'id'    => $post->ID,
            'title' => get_the_title( $post ),
            'url'   => get_permalink( $post ),
        );
    }, $query->posts );

    wp_send_json_success( array(
        'items'      => $results,
        'total'      => (int) $query->found_posts,
        'totalPages' => (int) $query->max_num_pages,
    ) );
}

Every one of the wp_send_json_* functions terminates the request by calling wp_die() internally, so there is no need for an explicit exit and adding one is redundant. What you must not do is emit any output before them, because the first byte written locks in a non-JSON content type.
