Never escape values in a JSON response. wp_send_json_* applies wp_json_encode internally. Adding esc_html() before the call produces HTML entities in the JSON, which the client must decode before displaying, and which breaks equality comparisons in the consumer. Escape in the consumer, at the point of insertion into the DOM.

The REST alternative

Key details

For anything beyond a trivial single-purpose lookup, the REST API is the better choice. The reasons are structural rather than stylistic.

Concernadmin-ajax.phpREST API
Route discoveryNone. Action names are private convention.Self-describing through OPTIONS and the schema.
Input validationHand-written per handler.JSON Schema, declared once.
Permission callbackNot enforced by the framework.Mandatory; omission is a hard error.
Method semanticsOne endpoint, verbs decided by you.GET, POST, PATCH, DELETE enforced.
CachingNot cacheable by default.Standard HTTP cache headers.
Headless front endsNot usable without a session.First-class, with application passwords.
Response shapeWhatever you shape it as.Consistent envelope with headers.

If you are replacing an existing admin-ajax.php endpoint, register the REST route first, keep the AJAX action as a thin proxy for one deprecation cycle, and document the replacement. Do not remove an AJAX action that a third party may depend on; the action name is not namespaced in any enforceable way and other plugins do call them.

Front-end and unauthenticated requests

Key details

A front-end request posts to admin_url( 'admin-ajax.php' ), not to a path relative to the current page. Getting this wrong produces a 404 on the correct handler, and it is a very common mistake.

add_action( 'wp_enqueue_scripts', function(): void {
    wp_enqueue_script( 'acme-ledger-public', ACME_LEDGER_URL . 'public/js/public.js', array(), ACME_LEDGER_VERSION, true );

    wp_add_inline_script(
        'acme-ledger-public',
        'window.acmePublic = ' . wp_json_encode( array(
            'url'  => esc_url_raw( admin_url( 'admin-ajax.php' ) ),
            'nonce' => wp_create_nonce( 'acme_ledger_public' ),
        ) ) . ';',
        'before'
    );
} );

add_action( 'wp_ajax_nopriv_acme_ledger_quote', array( $this, 'ajax_quote' ) );
add_action( 'wp_ajax_acme_ledger_quote', array( $this, 'ajax_quote' ) );

public function ajax_quote(): void {
    check_ajax_referer( 'acme_ledger_public', 'nonce' );

    $amount = isset( $_POST['amount'] )
        ? Ledger_Money::decimal( wp_unslash( $_POST['amount'] ) )
        : '0';

    if ( '' === $amount || (float) $amount <= 0 || (float) $amount > 1000000 ) {
        wp_send_json_error( array( 'message' => __( 'Invalid amount.', 'acme-ledger' ) ), 400 );
    }

    $rate = Ledger_Exchange::get_rate( Ledger_Settings::get( 'currency' ) );

    if ( ! $rate ) {
        wp_send_json_error( array( 'message' => __( 'Rate unavailable.', 'acme-ledger' ) ), 503 );
    }

    wp_send_json_success( array( 'converted' => Ledger::format_amount( (float) $amount * $rate ) ) );
}

An endpoint that is reachable by unauthenticated visitors is reachable by everyone on the internet, including automated scanners. Rate-limit it. A transient counter keyed on the client address, checked at the start of the handler, raises the cost of abuse to a crawl and is trivial to implement.

$ip  = sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_SERVER['REMOTE_ADDR'] ?? '' ) );
$key = 'quote_' . md5( $ip );

$hits = ( int ) get_transient( $key );

if ( $hits >= 60 ) {
    wp_send_json_error( array( 'message' => __( 'Too many requests.', 'acme-ledger' ) ), 429 );
}

set_transient( $key, $hits + 1, MINUTE_IN_SECONDS );

Responses, errors and debugging

AJAX failures are hard to debug because the response body is not visible in the browser network panel by default and a PHP notice in the middle of a JSON response makes the whole payload unparseable. There are two techniques that eliminate the guesswork.

// 1. Send headers before any output. Content-Type is mandatory.
header( 'Content-Type: application/json; charset=utf-8' );

// 2. Never let a notice corrupt the payload. Install a buffer that
//    converts stray output into a structured error field.
ob_start( function ( string $buffer ): string {
    if ( '' === trim( $buffer ) ) {
        return $buffer;
    }

    error_log( 'Stray output in handler: ' . $buffer );

    return '';
} );

For the second technique, a cleaner approach is to register a shutdown function that inspects error_get_last() and appends the diagnostic to the response body when debugging is enabled. Guard it with WP_DEBUG so it is never active in production.

add_action( 'shutdown', function(): void {
    if ( ! defined( 'WP_DEBUG' ) || ! WP_DEBUG ) {
        return;
    }

    $error = error_get_last();

    if ( ! $error || ! in_array( $error['type'], array( E_ERROR, E_PARSE, E_COMPILE_ERROR, E_CORE_ERROR, E_USER_ERROR ), true ) ) {
        return;
    }

    error_log( 'Fatal in AJAX: ' . $error['message'] . ' in ' . $error['file'] . ':' . $error['line'] );
} );

On the JavaScript side, handle the failure explicitly. A fetch() call that resolves with an HTTP 400 still resolves, and code that assumes a 2xx status will try to read a success key from an error payload and throw a TypeError in the console, which hides the real message.

const response = await fetch( window.acmePublic.url, {
    method: 'POST',
    credentials: 'same-origin',
    body: new URLSearchParams( {
        action: 'acme_ledger_quote',
        nonce: window.acmePublic.nonce,
        amount: value,
    } ),
} );

const payload = await response.json().catch( () => ( { success: false, data: { message: 'Malformed server response.' } } ) );

if ( ! response.ok || ! payload.success ) {
    showError( payload.data?.message ?? 'Request failed.' );
    return;
}

showResult( payload.data.converted );

Exercises 13

  1. Implement a search handler with nonce, capability, length validation and bounded pagination, and confirm the response is valid JSON under a forced PHP notice.
  2. Register only the authenticated action, then request it unauthenticated and record the response body and status code.
  3. Build the same search as a REST route, then diff the two responses to enumerate what the REST schema gives you for free.
  4. Add a transient rate limiter to a public endpoint and load-test it with 200 requests in a minute.

14 · Cron, Queues and Background Work

What WP-Cron actually isScheduling, rescheduling and unschedulingWriting a batch handlerAction Scheduler and queue semanticsDeferring work out of the requestReliability, timeouts and retries

Background work in WordPress is either opportunistic or guaranteed, and the default is opportunistic. This chapter covers the cron API, the failure modes of a request-time scheduler, and the conditions under which a durable queue is the correct engineering choice.

What WP-Cron actually is

Key details

WP-Cron is not a daemon. It is a hook on init that fires on ordinary page loads, and when it finds a due event it makes a non-blocking loopback request to wp-cron.php. If no visitor arrives, nothing runs. A site with 50 requests a day will run a hourly event roughly twice a day, at unpredictable times.

// wp-config.php: take scheduling out of the request path entirely.
define( 'DISABLE_WP_CRON', true );

// System cron, every five minutes. The --timeout must exceed the
// execution time of your slowest job, or the loopback is killed.
// */5 * * * * /usr/bin/wp cron event run --due-now --quiet
// &    * * * * * /usr/bin/wp cron event run --quiet > /dev/null 2>&1

Requesting a plugin document this in its readme is not a user-facing feature; it is an operational requirement. A plugin whose core function is a nightly sync and which does not state the cron requirement will be blamed for the sync not running.

WP-CLI is the production answer for scheduled work. wp cron event run --due-now is deterministic, produces a log, and can be supervised. If your plugin already requires WP-CLI for installation, requiring it for a critical job is a reasonable and defensible position — state it in Requires Plugins or in the readme, and keep a WP-Cron fallback for shared hosting.

Scheduling, rescheduling and unscheduling

Key details

Four functions cover the whole API. wp_schedule_event() for recurring events, wp_schedule_single_event() for one-offs, wp_next_scheduled() to inspect, and wp_clear_scheduled_hook() to remove.

add_action( 'acme_ledger_sync', array( $this, 'sync_batch' ) );
add_action( 'acme_ledger_renew_token', array( $this, 'renew_token' ), 10, 1 );

public static function schedule_events(): void {
    if ( ! wp_next_scheduled( 'acme_ledger_sync' ) ) {
        wp_schedule_event( time() + 300, 'hourly', 'acme_ledger_sync' );
    }

    // One-off with an argument. Arguments are serialised into the cron
    // option, so keep them small and scalar.
    if ( ! wp_next_scheduled( 'acme_ledger_renew_token', array( 'api' ) ) ) {
        wp_schedule_single_event( time() + 3600, 'acme_ledger_renew_token', array( 'api' ) );
    }
}

public static function unschedule_events(): void {
    wp_clear_scheduled_hook( 'acme_ledger_sync' );
    wp_clear_scheduled_hook( 'acme_ledger_renew_token', array( 'api' ) );
}

The wp_next_scheduled() guard is not optional. wp_schedule_event() will happily create a duplicate event if called twice, and calling it on every request will fill the cron option with thousands of identical rows within a day. The guard is one query against an autoloaded array.

Custom intervals require a filter, and the filter must return the default schedules unchanged. Dropping them breaks every core recurring event.

add_filter( 'cron_schedules', function( array $schedules ): array {
    $schedules['acme_fifteen_minutes'] = array(
        'interval' => 15 * MINUTE_IN_SECONDS,
        'display'  => __( 'Every fifteen minutes', 'acme-ledger' ),
    );

    return $schedules;
} );

Deactivation must unschedule, and uninstall must guarantee it. An orphaned event fires forever on a site where the plugin is gone, producing a fatal error in the cron log on every schedule. Clear events in the deactivation hook and clear them again, defensively, in uninstall.php — because the deactivation hook does not run on every path, in particular not when a plugin is deleted by a bulk action without deactivation.

Writing a batch handler

Key details

A cron callback runs in a request with a time limit and a memory limit. It must therefore be idempotent, resumable, and bounded. The standard shape is a cursor loop that processes a fixed number of rows and reschedules itself.

public function sync_batch(): void {
    $started = microtime( true );
    $limit   = 200;
    $cursor  = (int) get_option( 'acme_ledger_sync_cursor', 0 );
    $total   = 0;

    set_time_limit( 0 );

    do {
        $rows = Ledger_Repository::get_unsynced( $cursor, $limit );

        if ( empty( $rows ) ) {
            update_option( 'acme_ledger_sync_cursor', 0, false );
            wp_cache_delete( 'acme_ledger_sync_state', 'acme-ledger' );

            do_action( 'acme_ledger_sync_complete', $total );

            return;
        }

        foreach ( $rows as $row ) {
            $this->sync_row( $row );
            $cursor = (int) $row->id;
            $total++;
        }

        update_option( 'acme_ledger_sync_cursor', $cursor, false );

        if ( microtime( true ) - $started > 20 ) {
            return;
        }
    } while ( true );

    do_action( 'acme_ledger_sync_complete', $total );
}

Three properties make this safe. It is bounded by the 20-second budget, so a large backlog drains over several runs instead of timing out. It is resumable, because the cursor is persisted after each batch. And it is idempotent, because the cursor is keyed on the primary key and re-running a completed row is a no-op if the remote call is idempotent, which is a requirement on your API design, not on this code.

Check doing_cron() at the top of any handler that could also be reached by a normal request, and bail out if it is false, so a misrouted request cannot trigger the job.

Action Scheduler and queue semantics

Key details

Action Scheduler is a separate library maintained by Automattic and distributed with WooCommerce. It is not loaded by WordPress core. A plugin that wants it either declares a WooCommerce dependency or bundles the library as a subtree of its own code.

The important difference is where state lives. WP-Cron stores events in the cron option, a single autoloaded row. Action Scheduler creates its own tables and a scheduled-action post type, so it supports many thousands of pending actions, an observable queue with claims, priorities, groups and a full action log.

use function ActionScheduler_as_enqueue( string $hook, array $args = array, int $group = 0, bool $unique = false, int $priority = 10 );
use function ActionScheduler_as_schedule_single_action( int $timestamp, string $hook, array $args = array, int $group = 0, bool $unique = false, int $priority = 10 );
use function ActionScheduler_as_unschedule_all_actions( string $hook, array $args = array, string $group = '' );
use function ActionScheduler_as_next_scheduled_action( string $hook, array $args = array, string $group = '' );
RequirementChoice
A handful of periodic jobs, tolerant of imprecisionWP-Cron
Thousands of queued per-item actionsAction Scheduler or a real queue
Work that must not be lost if PHP diesDurable queue, with a job table you own
Third-party HTTP calls with retry and backoffQueue with exponential backoff, never a blocking loop

Uninstalling a plugin that used Action Scheduler is delicate. The actionscheduler_actions, actionscheduler_claims, actionscheduler_groups and actionscheduler_logs tables are shared, and another active plugin may still be scheduling actions into them. Drop them only when you bundled Action Scheduler yourself and no other plugin could be using it.

Deferring work out of the request

Key details

The other half of background work is getting out of the current request. Two mechanisms exist: a loopback HTTP request to yourself, and a shutdown handler that flushes after the response has been sent.

add_action( 'acme_ledger_entry_created', function( int $entry_id ): void {
    $payload = array(
        'entry_id' => $entry_id,
        'token'    => wp_create_nonce( 'acme_ledger_async' ),
    );

    wp_remote_post(
        admin_url( 'admin-ajax.php' ),
        array(
            'blocking'"  => false,
            'timeout'"   => 0.01,
            'body'       => array_merge( array( 'action' => 'acme_ledger_async_sync' ), $payload ),
            'sslverify' => false,
        )
    );
} );

Never set sslverify to false to “fix” a loopback request. Disabling certificate verification on a request whose response you ignore is a common and serious misconfiguration, because the same flag is frequently copy-pasted onto requests whose response is trusted. If the loopback fails, the cause is usually that the site blocks loopback requests, and the fix belongs in the host, not in the plugin.

The shutdown-handler approach avoids a network round trip entirely, at the cost of the work sharing the request process and its limits. It is appropriate for a few seconds of cache priming and inappropriate for a large import.

add_action( 'acme_ledger_entry_created', function(): void {
    add_action( 'shutdown', function(): void {
        // The response is already sent. Anything fatal here is invisible
        // to the visitor, so log aggressively and never throw.
        try {
            Ledger_Cache::warm_for_entry( get_the_ID() );
        } catch ( Throwable $e ) {
            error_log( 'Acme Ledger warm-up failed: ' . $e->getMessage() );
        }
    } );
} );

The nonce in the loopback body authenticates the request. A non-blocking request is still a real HTTP request that a third party could have caused, and the action name is public. Verify the token with wp_verify_nonce() and reject the request if it does not check out.

Reliability, timeouts and retries

Any background job will fail eventually. The design question is not whether failure happens but whether the failure is observable and whether the retry is bounded.

private function sync_row( object $row ): void {
    $attempt = (int) get_post_meta( $row->id, '_acme_sync_attempts', true ) + 1;

    if ( $attempt > 5 ) {
        update_post_meta( $row->id, '_acme_sync_state', 'abandoned' );
        error_log( 'Acme Ledger: abandoning entry ' . $row->id . ' after 5 attempts.' );

        return;
    }

    $response = wp_remote_post( 'https://api.example.com/v1/entries', array(
        'timeout'     => 15,
        'headers'    => array( 'Authorization' => 'Bearer ' . $this->token() ),
        'body'       => wp_json_encode( $row->payload ),
        'data_format' => 'body',
    ) );

    if ( is_wp_error( $response ) || 200 > (int) wp_remote_retrieve_response_code( $response ) || 300 <= (int) wp_remote_retrieve_response_code( $response ) ) {
        update_post_meta( $row->id, '_acme_sync_attempts', $attempt );
        update_post_meta( $row->id, '_acme_sync_next_at', time() + (int) pow( 2, $attempt ) * MINUTE_IN_SECONDS );
        do_action( 'acme_ledger_sync_failed', $row->id, $response );

        return;
    }

    update_post_meta( $row->id, '_acme_sync_state', 'synced' );
    update_post_meta( $row->id, '_acme_remote_id', json_decode( wp_remote_retrieve_body( $response ), true )['id'] ?? '' );
    do_action( 'acme_ledger_sync_succeeded', $row->id );
}

The backoff is exponential and stored on the row, so a rate-limited remote service does not receive a retry storm from your cron. The attempt counter is stored rather than held in memory for exactly the same reason: a PHP process that is killed at 19 seconds has no memory, and the next process must know how many attempts have already been made.

Always set an explicit HTTP timeout. The default is 5 seconds, which is often too short, and a background job with no timeout will occupy a WordPress HTTP connection until the remote server gives up. Set the timeout to a value you have actually measured, and set the outer cron loop budget to more than the inner timeout so the loopback is never killed mid-request.

Exercises 14

  1. Schedule a recurring event, then call the scheduling function 500 times in a loop and confirm the cron option contains exactly one event.
  2. Implement a cursor-based batch handler, kill it mid-run, and prove the next run resumes from the persisted cursor without duplicating or skipping a row.
  3. Replace WP-Cron with a real system cron via DISABLE_WP_CRON and verify the event runs at a predictable time on a site with almost no traffic.
  4. Add exponential backoff with a persisted attempt counter and verify a failing remote endpoint receives requests at 1, 2, 4, 8 and 16 minute intervals and then stops.

15 · Internationalisation

The translation function familyThe text domainPlaceholders, plurals and contextEscaping and translation togetherThe POT, PO and MO workflowTranslating JavaScript

Translation is a mechanical consequence of writing every user-facing string through a documented function with a text domain. This chapter covers the function family, the context and plural forms, the escape-aware variants, and the file workflow that a wordpress.org submission must follow.

The translation function family

Key details

Eight functions cover essentially all cases. Each takes the text domain as its final argument, and that argument is what makes the string translatable at all — a string without a domain is never sent to a translator.

FunctionEchoesUse for
__()NoThe general case. Assign, compare, concatenate, return.
_e()YesDirect output, usually in a template.
esc_html__()NoA translated string that will be placed in HTML text.
esc_html_e()YesDirect output into HTML text.
esc_attr__()NoA translated string that will be placed in an HTML attribute.
esc_attr_e()YesDirect output into an HTML attribute.
_x()NoOne string, two different meanings — pass a context.
_n()NoA pluralised string with a count.
// Return and use later.
$label = __( 'Ledger balance', 'acme-ledger' );

// Echo straight into a template.
<h2><?php esc_html_e( 'Recent entries', 'acme-ledger' ); ?></h2>

// Into an attribute.
<input type="text"
    placeholder="<?= esc_attr__( 'Reference', 'acme-ledger' ) ?>">

// One string, two meanings.
$read = _x( 'read', 'post status', 'acme-ledger' );
$seen = _x( 'read', 'verb, past tense', 'acme-ledger' );

The text domain

Key details

The text domain must match the plugin directory name exactly, with no vendor prefix added and no hyphenation differences. The directory acme-ledger means the domain acme-ledger, and both the Text Domain header and every function call must agree.

Load translations early but not too early. A call before init on a non-default locale cannot find the language files, because the locale is not yet determined. The correct hook is init, and the modern approach is the sixth argument of load_plugin_textdomain(), which names the language file path explicitly rather than relying on the core fallback.

add_action( 'init', 'acme_ledger_load_textdomain' );

function acme_ledger_load_textdomain(): void {
    load_plugin_textdomain(
        'acme-ledger',
        false,
        dirname( plugin_basename( ACME_LEDGER_FILE ) ) . '/languages'
    );
}

WordPress loads the plugin translation automatically when the domain matches the slug and the language directory is at the plugin root. Loading it explicitly is still correct, is required for a plugin whose files live in a subdirectory, and makes the dependency visible in a code review.

Do not translate hook names, option keys, meta keys, REST routes or capability names. These are identifiers, not display strings, and translating them breaks functionality in ways that are extremely hard to diagnose. The same applies to the values written into the database: a stored translated string cannot be matched by a later __() lookup when the site language changes.

Placeholders, plurals and context

Key details

Interpolating variables into a translated string with concatenation or sprintf produces strings a translator cannot reorder. WordPress provides a placeholder syntax that translators can move.

// Untranslatable in practice. Do not do this.
printf( __( '%s entries for %s', 'acme-ledger' ), $count, $account );

// Correct. The translator may reorder or omit the comments.
printf(
    esc_html__(
        '%1$s entries for %2$s.',
        'acme-ledger'
    ),
    esc_html( number_format_i18n( $count ) ),
    esc_html( $account_name )
);

// Placeholders are positionally indexed, which is mandatory.
// Unindexed %s is not translatable by WordPress translators.

Plurals are handled by _n(), which takes a singular form, a plural form, the number and the domain. The count must be an integer and must be the quantity actually being described, because the plural rule of a language is not necessarily the “is it more than one” rule.

$output = sprintf(
    _n(
        '%d entry in %s',
        '%d entries in %s',
        $count,
        'acme-ledger'
    ),
    $count,
    $account_name
);

// Context plus plural, combined.
$output = _nx(
    '%d entry in %s',
    '%d entries in %s',
    $count,
    'account ledger',
    'acme-ledger'
);

// Number of plural forms varies by language. Never assume two.
// Arabic, Polish, Russian and others require _n( '', '', $n, ... )
// with empty singular and plural forms, which is what the .po
// file's msgstr[0] and msgstr[1] entries target.

Always call number_format_i18n() on a number before interpolating it, so that the decimal separator and grouping match the locale. Passing a raw 1234.5 to a translator who expects 1.234,5 produces a string that is both ugly and, for monetary values, ambiguous.

Escaping and translation together

Key details

Translation and escaping are separate concerns applied in a fixed order: escape, then translate, then output. Reversing them translates an escaped entity string, which a translator will mangle, and produces double-encoded output.

ContextCorrect
HTML text nodeecho esc_html( __( 'x', 'domain' ) );
HTML attributeecho esc_attr( __( 'x', 'domain' ) );
URLecho esc_url( __( 'x', 'domain' ) );
JavaScript stringecho esc_js( __( 'x', 'domain' ) );
Author-supplied HTMLecho wp_kses_post( __( 'x', 'domain' ) );
Already in an escaping functionecho esc_html__( 'x', 'domain' );

PHP 8.3 makes the double-escape mistake a runtime error rather than a rendering artefact. Passing null to esc_html() is deprecated, and passing an array raises a TypeError. Type your translation helpers to return string so the mistake surfaces at the assignment rather than at the point of output.

declare( strict_types=1 );

final class Ledger_I18n {
    public static function entry_count_label( int $count, string $account ): string {
        return sprintf(
            _n(
                '%1$s entry in %2$s',
                '%1$s entries in %2$s',
                $count,
                'acme-ledger'
            ),
            esc_html( number_format_i18n( $count ) ),
            esc_html( $account )
        );
    }
}

Note that this function returns escaped HTML. That is a deliberate and defensible design for a function whose entire purpose is to produce a label, but it must be documented, and the caller must not escape again. Returning an escaped string from a general-purpose function is the one place where the usual “escape at output” rule is deliberately broken, and it is only acceptable when the function’s contract states that it returns escaped markup.

The POT, PO and MO workflow

Key details

The build pipeline is: extract a POT template from the source, translators produce a PO file, and the PO is compiled to a binary MO that WordPress loads at runtime. The POT is regenerated on every release; the PO files are not.

# composer.json
"scripts": {
    "make-pot": "wp i18n make-pot . languages/acme-ledger.pot --domain=acme-ledger --exclude=vendor,build,node_modules",
    "lint": "phpcs --standard=WordPress --ignore=vendor/,build/ ."
}

# Compile a single locale, or all of them.
# wp i18n make-mo languages/acme-ledger-fr_FR.po languages/acme-ledger-fr_FR.mo
# wp i18n make-mo languages/*.po --allow-root

A POT entry carries a translator comment, a source reference, the msgid and, for plurals, the plural forms. Keep the references current, because they are the only way a volunteer translator can find the string in your code.

#: includes/admin/class-settings-page.php:184
#: includes/admin/class-dashboard.php:77
#. translators: %s: account name, e.g. "Current assets"
msgid "Balance for %s"
msgstr ""

#: includes/admin/class-entries.php:212
#. translators: %d: number of entries
msgid "%d entry in this account"
msgid_plural "%d entries in this account"
msgstr[0] ""
msgstr[1] ""

Commit the MO files, not just the PO files. A wordpress.org-hosted plugin is installed from a ZIP; nothing compiles the PO files on the target server. The build step of your release pipeline must produce the .mo file and include it in the artifact, and the directory name must match the Domain Path header.

Translating JavaScript

Editor and admin JavaScript needs its own mechanism. WordPress 6.5 and later handle this through wp_set_script_translations(), which uses a *.json file generated from the JSDoc annotations in your source.

// src/index.js
/**
  * Renders the entry count.
  *
  * @param {number} count Number of entries.
  * @return {string} Localised label.
  */
export function entryLabel( count ) {
    const label = __n(
        '%d entry',
        '%d entries',
        count,
        'acme-ledger'
    );

    return label;
}

// build/index.asset.php is generated by @wordpress/scripts.
wp_set_script_translations(
    'acme-ledger-admin',
    'acme-ledger',
    ACME_LEDGER_DIR . 'languages'
);

The @wordpress/i18n runtime requires wp-i18n in the script dependency list, which @wordpress/scripts adds automatically when it detects a translation call. The generated JSON files are named acme-ledger-admin-{locale}-{md5}.json and must be in the language directory passed as the third argument.

Date and number formatting are the most common translation defect. Passing a formatted string from PHP and formatting it again in JavaScript produces two different representations. Choose one: send a raw ISO 8601 timestamp and format it in the browser with Intl.DateTimeFormat using the locale from wp.i18n, or send an already-formatted string and do not format again.

Exercises 15

  1. Extract a POT from a small plugin, verify the msgids match the function calls, and confirm no identifier such as a hook name or option key appears in the output.
  2. Build a UI with a string that means two different things in English and prove the _x() context produces two distinct msgids in the POT.
  3. Compile a French translation, including a plural string, and confirm the correct form is selected for 0, 1 and 2.
  4. Add JavaScript translations through wp_set_script_translations and confirm the JSON file is found and applied.

16 · Security

Nonces: scope, lifetime and limitsReading input safelySanitisingEscaping by output contextSQL preparationThe layers, and where they belong

WordPress security is a set of independent layers, and a plugin that implements one of them correctly while omitting another is still vulnerable. This chapter defines each layer precisely, with the exact functions, and states what each one does not protect you against.

Nonces: scope, lifetime and limits

Key details

A nonce is not an anti-CSRF token in the strict sense. It is a hash of a tick counter, the action name and the current user identifier, and it is valid for twelve hours. Its purpose is to make a forged request unattributable, not to make it impossible.

// In the form.
<input type="hidden" name="acme_action" value="<?= esc_attr( 'save_entry' ) ?>">
<?php wp_nonce_field( 'acme_save_entry' ); ?>

// In the handler, before anything else.
$action = sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['acme_action'] ?? '' ) );

if ( ! check_admin_referer( $action, '_acme_nonce' ) ) {
    wp_die( 'Invalid nonce.', '', array( 'response' => 403 ) );
}

// Non-verbose form, when you want a boolean rather than a die.
$nonce = isset( $_POST['_acme_nonce'] )
    ? sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['_acme_nonce'] ) )
    : '';

if ( ! wp_verify_nonce( $nonce, 'acme_save_entry' ) ) {
    return;
}

// For a REST request, the cookie nonce is sent as X-WP-Nonce and
// verified by WordPress itself before the route is dispatched.
FunctionVerifies andTerminates on failure
wp_nonce_field()Nothing; it prints the fieldNo
wp_verify_nonce()Returns 1 or 2, or falseNo
check_admin_referer()Returns 1 or 2, or falseYes, via wp_die()
check_ajax_referer()Returns 1 or 2, or falseYes, unless the third argument is false

A nonce is not a substitute for a capability check, and neither is a substitute for the other. A nonce proves the request originated from a page this user was able to load. It does not prove the user is allowed to perform the action. An editor can load any post edit screen, so an editor has a valid nonce for a nonce-protected endpoint that only checks the nonce. Always check both, and check the capability second so that a failure is reported as a permission problem rather than a token problem.

Three further properties matter. A nonce is bound to the action string, so a nonce for acme_save_entry does not validate acme_delete_entry. It is bound to the user, so a nonce created by one user is invalid for another. And it expires after approximately twelve hours, so a form left open overnight will fail, which is correct behaviour and should produce a clear message rather than a blank form.

Reading input safely

Key details

Every superglobal read must be unslashed and then sanitised, in that order. Reading $_POST or $_GET directly, even as a temporary value, is a defect a reviewer will reject.

// Wrong: unescaped, unslashed, unvalidated.
$id = $_POST['id'];

// Right: unslash, then sanitise to the expected type.
$id = absint( wp_unslash( $_POST['id'] ) );
$slug = sanitize_title( wp_unslash( $_GET['page'] ) );
$term = sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['term'] ) );
$memo = sanitize_textarea_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['memo'] ) );
$raw  = isset( $_POST['raw'] ) ? wp_unslash( $_POST['raw'] ) : '';

// For a nested array, unslash and sanitise each element.
$filters = array_map(
    'sanitize_text_field',
    ( array ) wp_unslash( $_POST['filters'] ?? array() )
);

// Whitelisting beats sanitising for known enumerations.
$sort = in_array( sanitize_key( $input ), array( 'date', 'amount', 'reference' ), true )
    ? $input
    : 'date';
FunctionResult
absint()Integer, negative values discarded.
wp_unslash()Removes the slashes WordPress added. Always the first step.
sanitize_text_field()Strips tags, removes line breaks, collapses whitespace.
sanitize_textarea_field()As above but preserves newlines.
sanitize_key()Lowercase alphanumeric and dashes. For slugs and keys.
sanitize_email()A valid address or an empty string.
sanitize_file_name()A safe filename with no path traversal.
wp_kses_post()Post-authorised HTML tags only.
validate_email()Boolean, without modifying the value.

On PHP 8, prefer narrowing the type at assignment over validating later. Declaring function handle( int $id ) and passing the result of absint() makes a type confusion a compile-time concern, and a TypeError rather than a silently wrong query.

Sanitising

Key details

Sanitisation is not escaping. sanitize_text_field() removes characters that are dangerous in a text field; it does not make a value safe to place in an HTML attribute. Escaping happens at output and escaping happens once.

This distinction has a practical consequence. If you sanitise a value on the way in and escape it on the way out, you get correct behaviour. If you escape on the way in and escape again on the way out, you get double-encoded entities that render as literal &amp; text. Store the raw, sanitised-but-unescaped value; escape at the point of output, every time.

// Stored: sanitised, not escaped. The database holds "Tom & Jerry" as
// literal text with an ampersand, exactly as typed.
update_post_meta( $post_id, '_acme_counterparty', sanitize_text_field( $input ) );

// Output: escaped exactly once, in the context it is used.
<p><?= esc_html( get_post_meta( $post_id, '_acme_counterparty', true ) ) ?></p>

Sanitise for the type, not for the sink. A value that will be used in a SQL query is prepared, not sanitised; a value that will be used as a filename is sanitised with sanitize_file_name(); a value that will be an email address is validated with is_email() and used only if it returns true. Applying one generic sanitiser to everything produces values that are safe nowhere and useful nowhere.

Escaping by output context

Key details

Escaping is context-specific. A value that is safe in an HTML text node can break out of an attribute, a JavaScript string, or a URL. Choose the function by where the value lands, not by what it contains.

SinkFunctionNote
HTML textesc_html()Converts &, <, >
HTML attributeesc_attr()Also handles quotes and line breaks
URL in href or srcesc_url()Enforces the allowed protocol list
URL in a database or redirectesc_url_raw()No entity encoding; for storage
Inline JavaScript stringesc_js()Weak; prefer wp_json_encode()
JavaScript datawp_json_encode()Correct choice for structured data
CSS valuesanitize_text_field() plus a patternThere is no general CSS escaper
Post-authorised HTMLwp_kses_post()Filters rather than encodes
// Inline script: JSON, not concatenation.
<script>
    window.acme = <?= wp_json_encode( array(
        'endpoint' => rest_url( 'acme-ledger/v1' ),
        'label'    => __( 'Save entry', 'acme-ledger' ),
    ) ) ?>;
</script>

// Inline style with a validated value only.
$size = in_array( $size, array( 'small', 'medium', 'large' ), true ) ? $size : 'medium';
?><div class="acme-size acme-size--<?= esc_attr( $size ) ?>"></div><?php

// A data attribute holding a URL.
<a href="<?= esc_url( $permalink ) ?>"
   data-export="<?= esc_url( $export_url ) ?>">

Use wp_kses() with an explicit allowlist for plugin-authored HTML. wp_kses_post() uses the post-content allowlist, which permits tags such as iframe and style in some configurations. For a value that you render, build a narrow allowlist of the handful of tags the widget genuinely needs.

SQL preparation

Key details

Every value that reaches a query must be bound with prepare(). Every identifier, meaning a table name or a column name, cannot be bound and must be chosen from a hard-coded whitelist.

global $wpdb;

// Values: prepared.
$rows = $wpdb->get_results(
    $wpdb->prepare( "SELECT * FROM {$table} WHERE account_id = %d AND status = %s", $account_id, $status )
);

// Identifiers: whitelisted, then interpolated.
$allowed_columns = array( 'entry_date', 'amount', 'created_at' );
$orderby         = in_array( $orderby, $allowed_columns, true ) ? $orderby : 'entry_date';
$direction       = strtoupper( $direction ) === 'ASC' ? 'ASC' : 'DESC';

$rows = $wpdb->get_results(
    $wpdb->prepare( "SELECT * FROM {$table} ORDER BY {$orderby} {$direction} LIMIT %d", $limit )
);

// IN: one placeholder per element.
$in     = implode( ',', array_fill( 0, count( $ids ), '%d' ) );
$found  = $wpdb->get_col( $wpdb->prepare( "SELECT id FROM {$table} WHERE id IN ({$in})", $ids ) );

// LIKE: escape the wildcards the user supplied.
$like = '%' . $wpdb->esc_like( $term ) . '%';
$rows = $wpdb->get_results(
    $wpdb->prepare( "SELECT * FROM {$table} WHERE memo LIKE %s", $like )
);

esc_sql() is not a substitute for prepare(). esc_sql() calls mysqli_real_escape_string() and produces a value that is safe to wrap in quotes, but it does not parameterise the statement and it does not handle integers correctly. It exists for the rare case where a prepared statement cannot be used. Use prepare() everywhere else.

The %i placeholder is available from WordPress 6.2 for identifiers and formats, and handles the escaping of backticks correctly. It is the modern answer to the whitelist problem and removes the need to hand-escape a table name.

The layers, and where they belong

There are five independent layers, and the order of application is fixed. Treating them as a checklist rather than a sequence is how plugins end up with a capability check after the write it protects.

OrderLayerFunctionGuards against
1Capabilitycurrent_user_can()A low-privilege user performing a privileged action
2Noncecheck_admin_referer()A forged cross-site request
3Inputwp_unslash() + sanitiserInjection of unexpected characters and structures
4Storagewpdb->prepare()SQL injection
5Outputesc_html() familyCross-site scripting

Two further controls are not part of the sequence but are required for a wordpress.org submission: every outbound request must verify its TLS certificate, which is the default and must never be disabled, and every PHP file must be guarded against direct access with an ABSPATH check as described in Chapter 1.

final class Ledger_Importer {
    public static function handle(): void {
        // 1. Capability.
        if ( ! current_user_can( 'manage_ledger' ) ) {
            wp_die( 'Forbidden.', '', array( 'response' => 403 ) );
        }

        // 2. Nonce.
        check_admin_referer( 'acme_ledger_import' );

        // 3. Input, with an explicit existence check.
        if ( ! isset( $_FILES['file']['tmp_name'] ) ) {
            wp_die( 'No file.', '', array( 'response' => 400 ) );
        }

        $upload = $_FILES['file'];

        if ( UPLOAD_ERR_OK !== ( int ) $upload['error'] ) {
            wp_die( 'Upload failed.', '', array( 'response' => 400 ) );
        }

        if ( (int ) $upload['size'] > 5 * MB_IN_BYTES ) {
            wp_die( 'File too large.', '', array( 'response' => 413 ) );
        }

        if ( 'csv' !== strtolower( pathinfo( ( string ) $upload['name'], PATHINFO_EXTENSION ) ) {
            wp_die( 'Unsupported file type.', '', array( 'response' => 415 ) );
        }

        // 4. Storage happens inside the parser, prepared throughout.
        $result = self::parse( ( string ) $upload['tmp_name'] );

        wp_safe_redirect( add_query_arg(
            array( 'imported' => $result['imported'], 'failed' => $result['failed'] ),
            admin_url( 'admin.php?page=acme-ledger' )
        ) );
        exit;
    }
}

Exercises 16

  1. Build a form handler containing all five layers and remove one at a time, describing the concrete attack each removal re-enables.
  2. Write a test that stores Tom & Jerry in post meta and assert that the rendered output is exactly that string, with no double encoding.
  3. Attempt an injection through an ORDER BY parameter and show that the whitelist rejects it, then replace the whitelist with %i and repeat.
  4. Verify that a nonce generated by an editor fails for a subscriber and that a subscriber with a valid nonce still fails the capability check.

17 · Uninstall, Retention and Privacy

Deactivation is not deletionThe uninstall routineDropping tables and cleaning metaThe privacy eraserThe privacy exporterRetention and the settings choice

A plugin that stores personal data and cannot delete it on request is a liability for the site owner and a permanent bar from the wordpress.org directory. This chapter covers uninstall, the privacy exporter and eraser hooks, and the retention decision every data-storing plugin must make explicit.

Deactivation is not deletion

Key details

Deactivating a plugin stops it from running. It removes nothing. Data written by a deactivated plugin remains in the database indefinitely, which is a decision the site owner made when they chose to deactivate rather than delete.

The WordPress admin therefore offers two distinct actions, and they have different consequences. Deactivate retains all data. Delete runs uninstall.php and then removes the plugin files. A plugin must register an uninstall routine if it wants Delete to clean up.

// Registers a callback for the Delete action.
register_uninstall_hook( ACME_LEDGER_FILE, 'acme_ledger_uninstall' );

function acme_ledger_deactivate(): void {
    wp_clear_scheduled_hook( 'acme_ledger_sync' );
    wp_clear_scheduled_hook( 'acme_ledger_renew_token' );
}
register_deactivation_hook( ACME_LEDGER_FILE, 'acme_ledger_deactivate' );

Do not delete data in the deactivation hook. Deactivation is a routine, reversible action that users perform while troubleshooting. Deleting their ledger on deactivation is an unforgivable data loss bug, and it is the single most common finding in plugin security reviews. The deactivation hook unschedules jobs and nothing else.

The uninstall routine

Key details

An uninstall routine runs in a bare WordPress bootstrap with no plugins loaded and no theme. It must be self-contained: it cannot rely on any class, function or constant defined by the plugin being deleted, because the file is included separately and in a different context.

// uninstall.php at the plugin root.
if ( ! defined( 'WP_UNINSTALL_PLUGIN' ) ) {
    exit;
}

if ( ! is_multisite() ) {
    acme_ledger_uninstall_site();
    return;
}

// Multisite: the routine is invoked once per site, so remove the
// per-site data in a loop and the network data once at the end.
$site_ids = get_sites( array( 'fields' => 'ids', 'number' => 0 ) );

foreach ( $site_ids as $site_id ) {
    switch_to_blog( $site_id );
    acme_ledger_uninstall_site();
    restore_current_blog();
}

acme_ledger_uninstall_network();

function acme_ledger_uninstall_site(): void {
    global $wpdb;

    acme_ledger_drop_tables();
    acme_ledger_delete_post_meta();

    wp_clear_scheduled_hook( 'acme_ledger_sync' );
    wp_clear_scheduled_hook( 'acme_ledger_renew_token' );

    delete_option( 'acme_ledger_options' );
    delete_option( 'acme_ledger_db_version' );
    delete_option( 'acme_ledger_role_version' );
    delete_option( 'acme_ledger_rewrite_version' );
    delete_option( 'acme_ledger_installed_at' );
    delete_option( 'acme_ledger_cache_version' );

    delete_metadata( 'user', 0, '_acme_currency', '', true );
    delete_metadata( 'user', 0, '_acme_dismissed_notice', '', true );

    acme_ledger_remove_caps();

    // Clean the object cache so a persistent cache does not serve
    // values for tables that no longer exist.
    wp_cache_flush();
}

Every statement here is a core function call. If the uninstall routine calls a plugin function, it will fatal on deletion, because the plugin is not loaded during this request, and the site owner is left with data that cannot be removed through the interface.

Dropping tables and cleaning meta

Key details

Dropping a table is irreversible. Whether to do it at all is a product decision, and the most defensible default is to ask the site owner rather than assume.

// Respect an explicit opt-in to full removal.
$keep_data = ( bool ) apply_filters( 'acme_ledger_uninstall_keep_data', false );

if ( $keep_data ) {
    return;
}

function acme_ledger_drop_tables(): void {
    global $wpdb;

    $tables = array(
        $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_ledger_entries',
        $wpdb->prefix . 'acme_ledger_lines',
    );

    foreach ( $tables as $table ) {
        $wpdb->query( 'DROP TABLE IF EXISTS ' . $table );
    }
}

function acme_ledger_delete_post_meta(): void {
    global $wpdb;

    $keys = array( '_acme_amount', '_acme_account_id', '_acme_entry_date', '_acme_reviewed', '_acme_sync_state' );
    $in   = implode( ',', array_fill( 0, count( $keys ), '%s' ) );

    $wpdb->query(
        $wpdb->prepare( "DELETE FROM {$wpdb->postmeta} WHERE meta_key IN ({$in})", $keys )
    );

    $wpdb->query(
        $wpdb->prepare( "DELETE FROM {$wpdb->usermeta} WHERE meta_key IN ({$in})", $keys )
    );
}

function acme_ledger_remove_caps(): void {
    $caps = array(
        'edit_acme_entry', 'read_acme_entry', 'delete_acme_entry',
        'edit_acme_entries', 'edit_others_acme_entries',
        'publish_acme_entries', 'read_private_acme_entries',
        'delete_acme_entries', 'delete_private_acme_entries',
        'delete_published_acme_entries', 'delete_others_acme_entries',
        'edit_private_acme_entries', 'edit_published_acme_entries',
        'manage_ledger', 'manage_acme_accounts',
        'edit_acme_accounts', 'delete_acme_accounts',
        'assign_acme_accounts', 'manage_network_ledger',
    );

    foreach ( array( 'administrator', 'editor', 'author', 'contributor', 'subscriber' ) as $role_name ) {
        $role = get_role( $role_name );

        if ( ! $role ) {
            continue;
        }

        foreach ( $caps as $cap ) {
            $role->remove_cap( $cap );
        }
    }
}

The acme_ledger_uninstall_keep_data filter is the mechanism a site owner uses to retain financial records. It is also the right place for a commercial plugin to make retention a per-user choice, and it is the correct answer to the objection that an uninstall silently destroys an audit trail.

// Mu-plugin on the site owner’s installation:
add_filter( 'acme_ledger_uninstall_keep_data', '__return_true' );

The privacy eraser

Key details

The WordPress privacy tools are an admin workflow: a site owner receives a data request, searches for an email address, and exports or erases the matching data. A plugin participates by registering callbacks on wp_privacy_personal_data_erasers and wp_privacy_personal_data_exporters.

add_action( 'plugins_loaded', function(): void {
    add_filter( 'wp_privacy_personal_data_erasers', array( Ledger_Privacy::class, 'register_eraser' ) );
    add_filter( 'wp_privacy_personal_data_exporters', array( Ledger_Privacy::class, 'register_exporter' ) );
} );

final class Ledger_Privacy {
    public static function register_eraser( array $exporters ): array {
        $exporters['acme-ledger'] = array(
            'eraser_friendly_name' => __( 'Ledger entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'callback'           => array( self::class, 'erase_entries' ),
        );

        return $exporters;
    }

    public static function erase_entries( string $email, int $page = 1 ): array {
        $per_page = 500;
        $offset   = ( max( 1, $page ) - 1 ) * $per_page;

        $entries = Ledger_Repository::find_by_email( $email, $offset, $per_page );

        $removed = false;
        $items   = array();

        foreach ( $entries as $entry ) {
            $items[] = array(
                'group_id'    => 'acme-ledger-entries',
                'group_label' => __( 'Ledger entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
                'item_id'     => 'acme-entry-' . $entry->id,
                'data'        => array(
                    array(
                        'name'  => __( 'Email address', 'acme-ledger' ),
                        'value' => $email,
                    ),
                ),
            );

            // Records required for a statutory audit trail are not
            // erased; they are anonymised instead.
            Ledger_Repository::anonymise( $entry->id, $email );
            $removed = true;
        }

        return array(
            'items_removed'  => $removed,
            'items_retained' => $removed,
            'messages'       => $removed
                ? array( __( 'Ledger entries were anonymised; the records themselves are retained for statutory audit purposes.', 'acme-ledger' ) )
                : array(),
            'done'          => count( $entries ) < $per_page,
        );
    }
}

Anonymise, do not delete, when a retention obligation applies. Financial records commonly have a statutory retention period. Deleting them on an erasure request may breach a legal obligation; keeping them intact with the personal identifier replaced satisfies both the erasure request and the retention rule. Returning items_removed => true alongside items_retained => true and a clear message is the correct response, and the message is what tells the site owner what happened.

Pagination is mandatory. Returning more than a few thousand records in one call will exceed the PHP time limit, and the privacy tool will report the eraser as failed. The done flag drives the paging loop, and it must be true on the final page only.

The privacy exporter

Key details

The exporter has the same shape and the same paging requirement. The return value is an array of groups, each containing items, and each item is a set of name and value pairs.

public static function register_exporter( array $exporters ): array {
    $exporters['acme-ledger'] = array(
        'exporter_friendly_name' => __( 'Ledger entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
        'callback'               => array( self::class, 'export_entries' ),
    );

    return $exporters;
}

public static function export_entries( string $email, int $page = 1 ): array {
    $per_page = 500;
    $entries  = Ledger_Repository::find_by_email( $email, ( max( 1, $page ) - 1 ) * $per_page, $per_page );

    $data = array();

    foreach ( $entries as $entry ) {
        $data[] = array(
            'group_id'    => 'acme-ledger-entries',
            'group_label' => __( 'Ledger entries', 'acme-ledger' ),
            'item_id'     => 'acme-entry-' . $entry->id,
            'data'        => array(
                array( 'name' => __( 'Email address', 'acme-ledger' ), 'value' => $email ),
                array( 'name' => __( 'Amount', 'acme-ledger' ), 'value' => $entry->amount ),
                array( 'name' => __( 'Currency', 'acme-ledger' ), 'value' => $entry->currency ),
                array( 'name' => 'Entry date', 'acme-ledger' ), 'value' => $entry->entry_date ),
            ),
        );
    }

    return array(
        'data' => $data,
        'done' => count( $entries ) < $per_page,
    );
}

Export everything you hold. The most common exporter defect is omitting a field that the plugin stores, and it is usually the free-text field, because it is the one that is not obviously personal data. A field that contains a name, an address, a free-text memo or a reference is personal data for the purpose of this workflow.

Register on plugins_loaded, not on init. The privacy tools screen may be rendered in an admin-ajax request that does not reach init in the way you expect. Registering the filters early guarantees the callbacks are present whenever the request completes.

Retention and the settings choice

Retention is a product decision with legal consequences, and the honest default for a plugin that stores financial records is to keep them. Deleting a user’s history because they deactivated the plugin is not a default any site owner will accept.

The defensible design is a three-stage policy, each stage of which the site owner can change. Immediate deletion when the plugin is deleted, if the owner opts out of retention. Anonymisation on an erasure request, with a stated retention period. And automatic purging of transient working data — caches, rate tables, failed-request logs — on a schedule regardless of the retention setting, because none of it is needed after a defined period.

public static function purge_working_data(): void {
    // Transient data is self-purging; enforce the ceiling anyway so
    // a broken expiry does not accumulate indefinitely.
    $cutoff = time() - ( 30 * DAY_IN_SECONDS );

    $wpdb->query(
        $wpdb->prepare(
            "DELETE FROM {$wpdb->options} WHERE option_name LIKE %s AND option_value < %d",
            $wpdb->esc_like( '_transient_timeout_acme_ledger_' ) . '%',
            $cutoff
        )
    );

    Ledger_Repository::delete_failed_attempts_older_than( $cutoff );
}

add_action( 'acme_ledger_sync', array( Ledger_Maintenance::class, 'purge_working_data' ) );

Whatever the policy, it must be documented. A readme section titled Data retention and removal, stating what is stored, where it is stored, what is removed on deletion, what is retained and for how long, is expected by the wordpress.org reviewer and is what a site owner will look for after a privacy incident.

Exercises 17

  1. Write an uninstall.php that calls no plugin function, and prove it by deleting the plugin through the admin on a site where the main file has been renamed.
  2. Register an exporter and an eraser, then run a full export and erase cycle from the privacy tools screen against a test user with 1200 records and confirm the paging loop terminates correctly.
  3. Implement anonymisation instead of deletion for a retention-bound record, and verify the exported data no longer contains the email address.
  4. Install a mu-plugin setting acme_ledger_uninstall_keep_data and confirm that Delete removes the options but retains the table and the entries.

18 · Packaging, Distribution and Updates

The release artifactReadme structure and the Stable tagThe wordpress.org repositorySelf-hosted updates and Update URIChangelog disciplineA release checklist

A plugin is a distributed artifact with an update channel. This chapter covers the build, the metadata the repository parses, the two update models, and the checklist that must pass before a tag is pushed.

The release artifact

Key details

The ZIP must contain exactly one top-level directory named after the plugin slug, containing the PHP files. An artifact with the files at the root, or with two top-level directories, is rejected by the installer and produces the “incorrectly packaged” error.

# Correct: everything lives under one directory.
unzip -l acme-ledger-2.4.0.zip
  Archive:  acme-ledger-2.4.0.zip
  acme-ledger/acme-ledger.php
  acme-ledger/readme.txt
  acme-ledger/uninstall.php
  acme-ledger/includes/class-ledger.php
  acme-ledger/build/entry/block.json
  acme-ledger/languages/acme-ledger-fr_FR.mo

# Exclusions belong in .distignore, honoured by wp dist-archive.
# node_modules
# .git
# composer.json
# composer.lock
# package.json
# phpunit.xml.dist
# .wordpress-org
# tests

composer.json and composer.lock must be excluded. Shipping them is harmless but they trigger composer audit tooling in some hosts and confuse reviewers. vendor/ must be included, and node_modules/ must be excluded, because the compiled assets in build/ are what the plugin actually needs at runtime.

Verify the artifact locally before uploading. Install the ZIP through the admin on a clean site, activate it, and run through the primary workflow. A packaging error that only appears on the repository is a wasted review cycle.

Readme structure and the Stable tag

Key details

The repository update service reads readme.txt from trunk on wordpress.org and the ZIP from a tag. The Stable tag header determines which ZIP the service hands out. It must be a bare version number that matches an existing tag exactly.

HeaderDrivesCommon error
Stable tagWhich ZIP is servedLeft at the previous version after tagging a new one
Tested up toThe compatibility badgeInflated above the version actually tested
Requires at leastThe minimum WordPress versionLeft at an old value after raising the floor
Requires PHPThe minimum PHP versionOmitted while composer.json requires 8.3
LicenseDirectory complianceAnything other than GPL-compatible

The changelog heading format is not cosmetic. The parser looks for = 2.4.0 = exactly, with spaces around the version. A heading written as ## 2.4.0 is silently ignored, and the release will not appear in the changelog section even though the update is delivered.

== Changelog ==

= 2.4.0 =
* Added: batch entry import from CSV, up to 5000 rows.
* Added: <code>acme_ledger_entry_created</code> action.
* Changed: amounts are now stored as <code>decimal(19,4)</code>.
* Fixed: rounding drift when summing mixed-currency entries.
* Security: escaping applied to the memo field in all output contexts.
* Requires PHP 8.3.

= 2.3.1 =
* Fixed: a fatal error when the account taxonomy was empty.

WordPress 6.7 added a Changelog section parse that recognises both the traditional = headings and a plain list format, but the traditional format remains the reliable one and the one every existing tool understands.

The wordpress.org repository

Key details

The public plugin directory uses SVN at wordpress.org/ with three directories. trunk holds the current development state including readme.txt. tags/{version} holds a complete copy of that version, which is what the update service serves.

# Publish a release.
$ svn update
$ svn cp trunk tags/2.4.0
$ svn commit -m "Release 2.4.0"

# The Stable tag in trunk/readme.txt must already say 2.4.0.
$ svn up

# Add new files, then commit them, before updating the tag.
$ svn add languages/acme-ledger-fr_FR.mo
$ svn commit -m "Add French translation"
$ svn up

A tag is immutable in practice. The update service compares Stable tag to the available tags and serves the highest. If a tag is wrong, correct it by creating a new patch version rather than by editing the tag contents. Editing a tag that users have already downloaded is a version of code that the service will never serve again but that is already installed on user sites, and it is a support incident.

Two approval gates apply. A new plugin is reviewed by a human before it appears in the directory. An update to an existing plugin is screened automatically and, when a static analyser flags the code, queued for human review. The automated screen looks for the same things the human reviewer does: file operations, unserialised input, database queries without preparation, and obfuscated code.

Self-hosted updates and Update URI

Key details

A plugin distributed outside wordpress.org must manage its own update checks, and must tell WordPress to stop offering wordpress.org updates for the same slug. The Update URI header does the latter.

/*
Plugin Name:       Acme Ledger
Version:           2.4.0
Update URI:        https://example.com/wp-json/acme-ledger/v1/update
*/

WordPress then calls that URL with the installed version, the site URL and the plugin slug in the query string, and expects a JSON object. A malformed response is ignored and the plugin simply never receives an update, which is a failure mode that is invisible until a user reports that updates stopped.

add_filter( 'pre_set_site_transient_update_plugins', function( object $value ): object {
    if ( ! isset( $value->checked ) || ! isset( $value->checked['acme-ledger/acme-ledger.php'] ) ) {
        return $value;
    }

    $remote = acme_ledger_fetch_update_info();

    if ( is_wp_error( $remote ) || $remote->version <= $value->checked['acme-ledger/acme-ledger.php'] ) {
        return $value;
    }

    $value->response['acme-ledger/acme-ledger.php'] = array(
        'slug'        => 'acme-ledger',
        'plugin'      => 'acme-ledger/acme-ledger.php',
        'new_version' => $remote->version,
        'package'     => $remote->package,
        'url'         => $remote->url,
        'tested'      => $remote->tested,
        'requires_php' => $remote->requires_php,
    );

    return $value;
} );

private static function fetch_update_info(): object|WP_Error {
    $response = wp_remote_get(
        add_query_arg(
            array(
                'version' => ACME_LEDGER_VERSION,
                'slug'    => 'acme-ledger',
            ),
            'https://example.com/wp-json/acme-ledger/v1/update'
        ),
        array( 'timeout' => 10 )
    );

    if ( is_wp_error( $response ) ) {
        return $response;
    }

    $body = json_decode( wp_remote_retrieve_body( $response ), true );

    return ( is_array( $body ) && isset( $body['version'], $body['package'] ) )
        ? (object) $body
        : new WP_Error( 'acme_ledger_update_invalid', 'Malformed update response.' );
}

Always compare versions, never assume an update exists. A transient that returns a malformed or empty response must leave $value->response untouched. Setting a response with an empty package produces an update row that fails during installation with no useful error, and it is a common defect in self-hosted updaters.

Whichever model you use, the update package must be served over TLS and should include a checksum. A plugin updater that downloads and executes a ZIP is the highest-value target on a WordPress site, and a man-in-the-middle on that request is a full site compromise.

Changelog discipline

Key details

A changelog written for users and a changelog written for developers are different documents. The wordpress.org changelog is the user-facing one and should use the words a site owner uses, not your internal class names.

# User-facing: what changed from their point of view.
* Added: batch entry import from CSV.
* Fixed: rounding drift when summing mixed-currency entries.
* Requires PHP 8.3.

# Developer-facing: belongs in the git history, not the changelog.
* Refactored Repository to use a Cursor value object.
* Added unit coverage for the reconciliation service.

Call out breaking changes explicitly and at the top. An upgrade that changes a hook signature, a stored option shape, a database column or a REST response is a breaking change, and the changelog is the only place a site owner will see it before they upgrade. A plugin with a semantic version must increment the major component for it.

* BREAKING CHANGE: the <code>acme_ledger_entry_amount</code> filter now receives
  a string in the site currency rather than a float. Update callbacks that
  cast with <code>(float)</code> to cast with <code>(float) $amount</code> after
  normalising through the plugin’s formatter instead.

A release checklist

Every item below has been the cause of a shipped defect. The list is not exhaustive, and it should be run for every release, including a patch release that changes one line.

CheckCommand or action
Static analysis cleanphpcs --standard=WordPress with zero errors
Tests pass on the declared minimumPHP 8.3, WordPress 6.7, and the Tested up to version
Version bumped in three placesHeader Version, readme Stable tag, and the changelog heading
Build output committedbuild/ present, node_modules/ absent
Translations compiledEvery .po has a .mo in languages/
POT regeneratedNo untranslated strings introduced in this release
Upgrade path testedFrom the oldest supported version, on a site with real data
Uninstall testedDelete the plugin and confirm nothing remains
No debug outputNo var_dump, print_r, error_log or console.log in the release tree
No dev dependenciesNo --dev packages, no Composer plugins, no source maps required at runtime
Privacy reviewedExporter and eraser still present and correct against current data shapes
Artifact verifiedFresh install from the ZIP on a clean site
# The single most useful pre-release command.
$ composer run lint && composer run test && wp dist-archive . acme-ledger.zip
$ unzip -l acme-ledger.zip | head -40
$ grep -rn "var_dump\|print_r\|error_log\|console\.log" --include=*.php --include=*.js . | grep -v node_modules | grep -v vendor

Ship the tag before you announce it. The common failure is updating the Stable tag to a version that has not been tagged, which makes the update service offer a ZIP that does not exist. Create the tag, verify it appears in the tags directory, then update the readme, and check the plugin page rather than assuming.

Exercises 18

  1. Build a release ZIP, install it on a clean site, and confirm the single-top-level-directory structure and that every runtime asset is present.
  2. Implement the self-hosted update filter and prove that a malformed or empty response leaves the update transient untouched.
  3. Change the Stable tag to a version that has not been tagged and observe the failure, then correct it and verify the plugin page.
  4. Run the release checklist end to end on a plugin with a build step, translations and a custom table, and record the total wall-clock time for future releases.

18b · Submitting to the WordPress.org Plugin Directory

What the directory actually gives youThe slug, and why the name matters foreverThe review guidelines, and what gets rejectedreadme.txt, field by fieldIcons, banner, screenshots and the directory UISubmitting, step by stepThe review queue and how to respondThe rejection reasons, in detailLanguage packs and dialectsThe update mechanism and its trapsFAQ and support channelsPre-submission checklist

Chapter 18 covered the artefacts: the ZIP, the readme structure, the SVN repository and the update flow. This chapter covers the part that is not in your repository at all — the submission, the human review, and the rules your code has to satisfy before anyone at wordpress.org will host it.

What the directory actually gives you

Key details

// being on wordpress.org is a distribution decision, and it has costs.
// know what you are signing up for.

// WHAT YOU GET
//   - the update mechanism, built in, with millions of sites checking it
//   - discovery: the plugin directory is where people search
//   - hosting for your ZIP, so a visitor can download it from w.org
//   - the plugin API endpoint, and telemetry on active installs
//   - automatic security notification emails when a CVE lands
//   - language packs, contributed by translators
//   - credibility that a self-hosted ZIP cannot buy

// WHAT YOU GIVE UP
//   - source visibility is not required, but the CODE must be open
//     source. "closed source" plugins are accepted with a header
//     comment, but they are reviewed far more sceptically and they
//     cannot be updated by anyone but you.
//   - the plugin must meet the directory guidelines, which are stricter
//     than "it works on my site"
//   - you are on a review queue with human latency. Budget days, and
//     sometimes weeks, for a first submission
//   - wordpress.org can close a plugin for guideline violations, and
//     the install base does not get an automatic warning
//   - the update API is a wordpress.org service, so it is a single
//     point of failure you do not control

// THE DECISION
//   publish on wordpress.org  if the plugin is general-purpose and you
//     want adoption. You gain distribution and lose some control.
//   self-host, update yourself if the plugin is for a niche, is
//     commercial, or your updates need to ship faster than a review
//     queue allows. Use Update URI (ch18-ownhost) and a tracker.
//   do both, if you need both audiences. The readme can point at your
//     own site, and the w.org plugin can be the free tier.

The slug, and why the name matters forever

Key details

// the slug is the permanent URL of your plugin
//   https://wordpress.org/plugins/your-plugin-slug/

// IT IS IMMUTABLE
//   once created, the slug cannot be renamed. A rename would break
//   every deep link, every bookmark, every mention in a blog post from
//   2019, and every site that has it installed. So choose carefully,
//   and treat it as permanent.
//
//   RULES
//     lowercase letters, numbers and hyphens only
//     3 to 45 characters
//     no spaces, no underscores
//     must not be a common word that will collide, and must not
//     impersonate an existing plugin or a trademark
//     "wp-" and "wordpress-" prefixes are discouraged and will be
//       rejected as they imply official status
//
//   THE COLLISION PROBLEM
//     "contact-form" is taken. So is "simple-contact-form". Many good
//     names are gone, and squatting on a popular one to gain traffic is
//     both against the guidelines and a bad idea.

// THE TWO IDENTITIES TO ALIGN
//   1. the Text Domain in your plugin header. This must match the
//      slug, because translation files are looked up by it.
//      Text Domain: contact-form-360
//   2. the plugin folder name inside the ZIP. The directory the
//      directory server sees when it extracts, and it should match too.
//      contact-form-360/
//        contact-form-360.php
//        includes/
//        languages/
//        readme.txt
//        assets/

//   Getting these out of step is the single most common reason for a
//   first submission to be rejected, and it is a two-minute fix.

The review guidelines, and what gets rejected

Key details

The Plugin Directory has a hand-written set of guidelines. They are not exhaustive, and reviewers apply judgement, so it is worth reading them as principles rather than a checklist.

// THE GUIDELINES, in their spirit

// 1. NO OBFUSCATED, MINIFIED OR ENCODED CODE
//    "the code must be human-readable". Minified JS, base64 blobs and
//    eval() are all rejected. This exists because a plugin runs with
//    full server privileges and the directory has no way to prove what
//    an unreadable blob does. It is a fair rule.
//      allowed: a minified build file, clearly labelled, with the
//               source in the repository
//      rejected: base64, eval, gzipped base64, hex-encoded PHP

// 2. NO TRACKING, TELEMETRY OR PHONE-HOME WITHOUT CONSENT
//    sending usage data off-server is a privacy question. The current
//    guidance: no tracking at all, and an "opt-in" toggle is not
//    accepted for anything that identifies a site or a user.
//      rejected: $_SERVER logs sent to a remote endpoint
//      rejected: an external JS pixel
//      accepted: sending data the admin explicitly configures, to a
//                service they control, disclosed in the readme

// 3. NO OBLIGATORY ADVERTISING OR THIRD-PARTY PROMOTION
//    no upsells in the admin, no affiliate links injected into
//    settings pages, no "rate us" notices, no injected banners.

// 4. NO SITE-SPECIFIC HARDCODING
//    no hardcoded domain names, no one-off hacks for one site, no
//    proprietary content baked in. The plugin must work for any
//    WordPress install.

// 5. NO ILLEGAL CONTENT, NO MALWARE, NO SPAM
//    obviously. Also: no bulk post creation, no mass comment posting,
//    no link injection.

// 6. NO DIRECT DATABASE QUERIES FOR CORE TABLES
//    use $wpdb->prepare, and prefer the API. Directly INSERTing into
//    wp_posts without going through wp_insert_post skips sanitisation,
//    triggers, and metadata handling, and it is rejected.

// 7. NO REMOTE CODE EXECUTION
//    no downloading and eval()ing code, no include() of a URL, no
//    exec/shell_exec/proc_open for plugin logic. This is a hard
//    rejection and a security problem at the same time.

// 8. TESTED UP TO
//    the readme must declare "Tested up to" a current version, and
//    the plugin must actually work on it. A plugin that errors on the
//    current release will be closed.

// 9. RESPECT THE LICENCE
//    GPL or a GPL-compatible licence. WordPress is GPL, and a plugin
//    that is not GPL-compatible cannot be distributed here.

// 10. NO OBFUSCATION OF THE LICENSE HEADER
//     the licence and copyright must be in the source headers, where
//     they can be read.

Guideline 1 and 7 are the two that catch experienced developers. Shipping a minified or base64-encoded dependency, or eval()ing a downloaded "licence check", are both common commercial patterns. Both are automatic rejections on wordpress.org. If your licensing model requires obfuscation, wordpress.org is the wrong distribution channel — use your own site and the Update URI mechanism.

readme.txt, field by field

Key details

The readme is parsed into the plugin page. Every field matters, and several have strict validation.

=== Contact Form 360 ===
Contributors:      yourhandle, anotherhandle
Tags:              contact, form, email, smtp, newsletter
Requires at least: 6.0
Tested up to:      6.8
Requires PHP:      8.0

Build a contact form with spam protection, SMTP delivery and a drag-and-drop
form builder. Works with any theme.

== Description ==

Everything you need to collect form submissions...

== Installation ==

1. Upload the plugin folder to `/wp-content/plugins/`.
2. Activate it through the Plugins screen.
3. Go to Contact Form 360 and create your first form.
4. Configure SMTP under Settings.

== Frequently Asked Questions ==

= Does it work with any theme? =

Yes. Forms are shortcoded and render with theme-independent markup.

= Can I export submissions? =

Yes, CSV export is in the Tools tab.

== Screenshots ==

1. The form builder. Accessible from the admin bar.
2. Configuring SMTP delivery.
3. The submissions table, with CSV export.
4. Conditional logic in the builder.
= Plugins that spam my inbox =

Spam protection is on by default...
