// THE FIELDS THAT GET REJECTED OR RENDERED WRONGLY

Contributors
//   w.org usernames, comma separated. Reviewers use this to attribute
//   authorship, so put everyone who actually wrote it. An empty field
//   is fine for a first submission but is looked on unfavourably.

Tags
//   max 5, lowercase, no "free", no "premium", no trademarked names.
   contact, form, email, smtp, newsletter        good
   free, best, wordpress plugin, contact form 360 bad (marketing terms)

Requires at least / Tested up to
//   the current WordPress version, and honestly tested.
   Tested up to: 6.5      rejected if 6.8 is current. reviewers notice.

Requires PHP
//   7.4+ is the practical floor now. Declaring 5.6 will be questioned
//   because it cannot use typed properties, arrow functions, or
//   anything modern the guidelines expect.

Stable tag
//   MUST match a tag that exists in the SVN tags directory.
   Stable tag: 3.2.1
//   and the tag directory /tags/3.2.1/ must contain the files.
//   A mismatch here is the most common "the page is blank" bug.

License
//   "GPLv2 or later", "GPLv3 or later", "MIT", or "GPLv2+".
//   Note: the "License" and "License URI" pair must be consistent.

// Screenshots numbering
//   1. caption     -- the 1. through N. lines in == Screenshots
//   2. file        -- assets/screenshot-1.png
//   3. description -- optional caption below
//   4. caption
//   The numbers must be consecutive from 1. A gap renders the rest
//   invisible on the directory page.

// The === Header === line is REQUIRED and must be the first line.
// A UTF-8 BOM will make the parser fail silently, which is a
// genuinely baffling bug. Save as plain UTF-8, no BOM.

Icons, banner, screenshots and the directory UI

Key details

// the directory shows different sizes of the same assets. get them right
// once and the layout is fine at every breakpoint.

// PLUGIN ICON
//   128 x 128 px, square, PNG or JPG (not SVG for this one)
   assets/icon-128x128.png
//   128 is the required size. The directory also uses 256, so 256x256
//   is optional but looks better in some contexts.

// BANNER, for the new directory layout
//   772 x 360 px  (approximately 2.14:1). This is WordPress.org's
//   recommended size for the hero image on the plugin page.
//   assets/banner-772x360.png
//   WordPress.org also accepts 1544x720 for high-DPI.
//   This is NOT the "1x" and "2x" convention. It is two separate
//   optional uploads, and the naming is counter-intuitive.

// SCREENSHOTS
//   WordPress.org's documented sizes:
//     1x:  500 x 375   (screenshot-1.png)
//     2x:  1000 x 750  (screenshot-1@2x.png)
//   Put both in assets/ and reference the 1x in the readme. The
//   directory generates the rest. Do NOT upload 2500px screenshots:
//   the largest a reader will ever see is 500 CSS px wide, so a 2x
//   file is the maximum useful size, and anything more is wasted bytes
//   on every plugin page load.

   assets/
     screenshot-1.png        500 x 375
     screenshot-1@2x.png    1000 x 750
     screenshot-2.png
     screenshot-2@2x.png
     icon-128x128.png
     icon-256x256.png
     banner-772x360.png

// SIZING YOUR OWN SCREENSHOTS
//   capture the plugin at 1280 x 720 browser viewport, then crop the
//   relevant region. Do not scale a 1920px screenshot down: the text
//   will be illegible. Design the screenshot as an illustration, with
//   readable text, at the size it will be displayed.

// WHAT ACTUALLY GETS LOOKED AT
//   the icon and the first screenshot decide whether anyone clicks.
//   Most plugin pages are judged entirely on the first two images.
   A screenshot of a settings page full of jargon is worse than none.

Submitting, step by step

Key details

// 1. PREPARE THE TREE
//    the exact layout the directory server expects
   my-plugin-1.0.0.zip  contains:
     my-plugin/
       my-plugin.php            the main file, with the plugin header
       readme.txt               plain UTF-8, no BOM
       uninstall.php            if you store anything
       includes/
       assets/
       languages/
         my-plugin.pot
         my-plugin-es_ES.po
         my-plugin-es_ES.mo
       languages/
         my-plugin-de_DE.po
         my-plugin-de_DE.mo

// 2. GET THE SUBMISSION FORM
   https://wordpress.org/plugins/submit/
//   or, on a local site: Plugins -> Add New -> Submit Plugin
//   you need: the ZIP, a short description, and a plugin page to point at.

// 3. FILL IN THE FORM
//   plugin name
//   short description (max 150 characters, no links, no emoji)
//   the URL of your own plugin page, used for the "Plugin URI" link
//   tags, max 5

// 4. UPLOAD AND WAIT
//   you get an email when a human picks it up. First submissions
//   typically take 1-14 days; experienced authors with a clean track
//   record, a few hours. Plan a release that does not depend on it.

// 5. AFTER APPROVAL
//   you get a readme-only SVN repository:
//     /readme.txt
//     /assets/*
//   commit those FIRST. This is a separate repository from the code,
//   and it is where the plugin page lives.

     svn co https://plugins.svn.wordpress.org/my-plugin wp-svn
     cd wp-svn
     // edit readme.txt and drop in assets/
     svn add assets/* readme.txt
     svn commit -m "Add plugin page assets and readme"

// 6. THEN THE CODE TAGS
//   create /tags/1.0.0/ and /trunk/, and commit your plugin there.
     svn mkdir -m "Add 1.0.0" tags/1.0.0 trunk
     svn update
     # copy the plugin tree into trunk/
     svn add trunk/*
     svn commit -m "Add 1.0.0"

// 7. SET THE STABLE TAG
//   Stable tag: 1.0.0 in readme.txt, committed to /readme.txt
//   Now the plugin page shows a working install and a download button.

// 8. VERIFY AS A NORMAL USER
   install it on a clean WordPress site from the directory. If it fails
   to activate, you have a header problem and the review missed it.

Commit the /readme.txt and /assets/ repository before the code tags. The readme-only repo can be committed immediately without waiting for anything, and the plugin page goes live with "No results found" until it does. Doing the code first means your plugin exists but its page is empty, which looks broken.

The review queue and how to respond

Key details

// WHAT HAPPENS AFTER YOU UPLOAD
//   1. automated email: "your plugin is in the queue"
//   2. a human reviewer opens the ZIP and reads it
//   3a. approved  -> an email, and it appears in the directory
//   3b. questions -> an email asking for changes
//   3c. rejected  -> an email with a reason

// THE REVIEWER IS CHECKING, SPECIFICALLY
//   - does it activate on a current WordPress without a fatal error
//   - does it produce output on the front end that is not broken
//   - is the code readable, and does it do what the description says
//   - are there guideline violations
//   - does it have trackers, ads, or obfuscation
//   - are the security basics present: escaping, nonces, capability
//     checks, prepared statements

// HOW TO RESPOND, and this matters more than the code
//   - answer the specific question, do not re-send the whole plugin
//   - if they ask for a change, make it, re-submit, and say what you
//     changed and where
//   - be patient. Bumping the submission does not move you up the queue
//     and can reset the review
//   - if you disagree, say so once, clearly, with a guideline citation
//     if you have one. Do not argue in circles.

// THE COMMON PATTERN THAT WORKS
   "Thanks for the review. You are right about the unprepared query in
    includes/export.php. Fixed in 1.0.1, which is attached. I also
    removed the outbound analytics call from the settings page, since
    it was not disclosed in the readme. The nonce check on the export
    form is now nonced and capability-checked. The only remaining
    outbound call is to my own licence server on admin_init, which is
    disclosed in the readme under Frequently Asked Questions."
//   Specific, complete, and answers every point. It goes through review
//   on the first follow-up.

The rejection reasons, in detail

Key details

// THE REASONS, roughly in order of frequency

// 1. "Your plugin has a fatal error on activation"
//   usually a missing file, a PHP version assumption, or a class name
//   collision with another plugin.
//   TEST IT: activate on a clean current WordPress with error display
//   on, PHP 8.3. A plugin that only works on the author's machine is
//   the single most common reason for a first rejection.

// 2. "Missing or incorrect readme.txt Stable tag"
//   the tag in the readme must exist in /tags/. And "trunk" is not a
//   version. If you have not created the tag yet, the page will not
//   resolve an install.

// 3. "The plugin folder name does not match the slug"
//   my-plugin/ must be my-plugin/, not plugin/ or src/.

// 4. "Obfuscated or minified code detected"
//   see guideline 1. This includes a minified vendor bundle.

// 5. "No plugin header / the header is malformed"
///**
 * Plugin Name: My Plugin
 * Plugin URI: https://example.com/my-plugin
 * Description: One clear sentence describing what it does.
 * Version: 1.0.0
 * Requires at least: 6.0
 * Requires PHP: 8.0
 * Author: Your Name
 * Author URI: https://example.com
 * License: GPLv2 or later
 * License URI: https://www.gnu.org/licenses/gpl-2.0.html
 * Text Domain: my-plugin
 * Domain Path: /languages
 */
//   every field, on its own line, in the first file. Text Domain MUST
//   match the slug.

// 6. "Unclosed PHP tag or a closing ?> with trailing whitespace"
//   omit the closing ?> entirely. Anything after it is echoed as HTML
//   and can produce headers-already-sent errors.

// 7. "Nonces or capability checks are missing on an admin action"
//   see the security chapter. A GET action that changes state without
//   a nonce and a capability check is a vulnerability and a rejection.

// 8. "Database queries are not prepared"
//   $wpdb->query($wpdb->prepare("... %s", $x)) - every variable.
//   This is both a security issue and a hard rule.

// 9. "External scripts loaded without a version or without enqueue"
//   wp_enqueue_script with a version argument, not a hardcoded
//   script tag in the admin. Reviewers see raw script tags as a
//   performance and security problem.

// 10. "Tested up to is out of date"
//   update it. Reviewers check this literally, every time.

Language packs and dialects

Key details

// the language model on wordpress.org is unusual, and worth understanding

// TRANSLATIONS COME FROM THE COMMUNITY
//   you do not translate your own plugin. Translators contribute
//   through the plugin's language page, and wordpress.org reviews and
//   publishes the .po files. You get free localisation and you get no
//   direct control over quality.

// THE DIALECT
//   each .po file has a Plural-Forms header that tells WordPress how
//   to pluralise. Getting it wrong produces "1 item/2 item" in
//   English, or broken plurals in Russian and Arabic.
   "Plural-Forms: nplurals=3; plural=(n%10==1 && n%100!=11 ? 0 : n%10>=2 && n%10<=4 && (n%100<10 || n%100>=20) ? 1 : 2);"
//   the Translate tab shows the plural rules for a given language. Use
//   it rather than guessing.

// THE FILE LOCATIONS
//   trunk/languages/your-plugin.pot        the template, regenerated
//   languages/your-plugin-fr_FR.po        committed, reviewed
//   languages/your-plugin-fr_FR.mo        compiled, never commit it
//   trunk/languages/                        is a SYMLINK to /languages
//
//   The .mo is COMPILED BY WORDPRESS.ORG. You do not upload it. If you
//   commit one, it is ignored or it conflicts.
//
//   regenerate the .pot from your code:
//     wp i18n make-pot . languages/your-plugin.pot --domain=your-plugin
//   the POT lives in trunk/ so translators always get the current one

// WHICH LANGUAGES THE DIRECTORY OFFERS
//   the list is the set of locales WordPress core supports. If your
//   language is not in the list, the plugin still works, but you do
//   not get community translation for it. WordPress has been expanding
//   this, so check rather than assuming.

// THE PLUGIN PAGE LANGUAGE SWITCHER
//   appears once a translation is published. It is a real differentiator:
//   a plugin available in Spanish and German reaches a much larger
//   audience, and many directory users filter by language.

The update mechanism and its traps

Key details

// HOW AN INSTALLED SITE LEARNS ABOUT YOUR UPDATE
//   the site asks the w.org plugin API:
//     https://api.wordpress.org/plugins/info/1.2/?action=plugin_information
//       &request[slug]=my-plugin
//       &request[fields][sections]=0
//   roughly every 12 hours, and immediately after a plugin page load
//   the API returns the Stable tag version from your readme.txt
//   WordPress compares it to the installed version and, if higher,
//   shows the update notice

// TRAP 1: THE STABLE TAG IS THE ONLY THING THAT MATTERS
//   the trunk version is irrelevant to updates. If Stable tag is
//   1.0.0 and you commit 1.5.0 to trunk, nobody gets it. Bump the
//   Stable tag, in the readme.txt committed to /readme.txt.

// TRAP 2: THE TAG DIRECTORY MUST EXIST AND MATCH
//   /tags/1.0.0/ must contain the actual 1.0.0 files, not the latest
//   code. If you only ever commit to trunk and tag once, the install
//   for new users pulls the wrong version.

// TRAP 3: THE .MO MUST NOT BE COMMITTED
//   see the languages section. A committed .mo is at best ignored and
//   at worst overwritten.

// TRAP 4: A BUG IN THE UPDATE CHECK ITSELF
//   if the plugin throws or hangs during the check, WordPress records
//   an "update failed" loop. Never run expensive code at file scope:
//   it runs on every admin request.

// TRAP 5: REPLACING THE DIRECTORY SERVER
//   if your plugin is hosted on w.org, users can install it from there.
//   You cannot pull it without breaking a large number of sites. If you
//   move to your own site, you must set Update URI so existing installs
//   follow you:
//    add_filter('site_transient_update_plugins', function ($check) {
//      $check->my_plugin = (object) [
//        'slug'    => 'my-plugin/my-plugin.php',
//        'plugin'  => 'my-plugin/my-plugin.php',
//        'new_version' => '2.0.0',
//        'url'     => 'https://example.com/my-plugin',
//        'package' => 'https://example.com/my-plugin-2.0.0.zip',
//      ];
//      return $check;
//    });
//   Put it in a MU plugin. If it is in the plugin itself and the
//   plugin fails to load, the update can never run.

// WHAT IS WORTH MONITORING
//   the "Active installations" number on your plugin page. A drop after
//   a release is usually a fatal error on a PHP version you dropped
//   support for. Watch it after every release.

Guideline 2 on tracking is worth reading twice if your plugin phones home. The directory's position is that a plugin must not send data that identifies a site, a user or an IP without explicit informed consent, and a settings toggle has generally not been accepted as sufficient. A remote call to your own licence server on admin_init is a grey area that some plugins have negotiated and others have had rejected. If you need remote validation, assume you will need a self-hosted update mechanism instead.

FAQ and support channels

Key details

// WHERE TO ASK
//   wordpress.org/support/plugin/my-plugin     the support tab on YOUR
//     plugin page. Public, and answered by YOU, not by WordPress.
//   trac.wordpress.org                        the developer ticket
//     system. Use it for guideline questions and directory issues.
//   make.wordpress.org/support                the general forum
//   the #wordpress-dev IRC channel on the wordpress.org Slack

// WHAT SUPPORT MEANS HERE
//   the support tab creates public forum threads. Anyone can read them,
//   including a competitor and a security researcher. That is
//   generally a good thing: it is a public record of your responsiveness.
//   Answer within a few days publicly. A plugin page with unanswered
//   support threads is a strong negative signal to a prospective user.

// SECURITY DISCLOSURE
//   HackerOne runs the wordpress.org bug bounty. A qualifying report
//   in a plugin hosted on the directory is triaged through them.
//   If you find a vulnerability in your own plugin, disclose it
//   privately, fix it, and release. Do not patch silently and say
//   nothing, because then nobody knows to update.

// THE PLUGIN PAGE RANKING SIGNALS
//   active installs    the primary signal
//   rating and reviews very visible, and a 1-star page is worse than
//                      no page. Ship something that works, not
//                      something feature-maximal
//   support threads resolved
//   last updated       plugins abandoned two years ago sink
//   number of downloads

Pre-submission checklist

// CODE
[ ] Activates on a clean, current WordPress with no PHP notices
[ ] Tested on the current WordPress AND the current PHP, and you can
    say honestly that you did
[ ] No fatals when deactivated and reactivated
[ ] No output before the plugin is activated (no stray whitespace,
//    no closing PHP tag, no direct echo)
// [ ] Text Domain in the header matches the slug exactly
[ ] Folder name inside the ZIP matches the slug
// [ ] No version conflicts with common plugins: check class, function
//    and constant names, and prefix all of them
// [ ] No network calls unless disclosed
// [ ] No eval, no base64, no minified bundles, no shell execution
// [ ] Every output is escaped, every query is prepared, every
//    state-changing action is nonced and capability-checked

// READMEs AND ASSETS
[ ] readme.txt is plain UTF-8, NO BOM, first line is === Plugin Name ===
[ ] Stable tag matches a tag directory that will exist
[ ] Tested up to is the current WordPress version
[ ] Requires PHP is 7.4 or higher, and honest
[ ] License and License URI are present and consistent
[ ] At most 5 tags, no marketing words
[ ] Screenshot numbering is consecutive from 1, captions under each
[ ] assets/icon-128x128.png is exactly 128x128
[ ] assets/banner-772x360.png if you have a banner
[ ] screenshots are 500x375 with a 1000x750 @2x
[ ] The first screenshot makes a stranger want the plugin
[ ] Every https:// link in the readme resolves and is not a
    no-underscore-domin that redirects to a parked domain

// PROCESS
[ ] You can respond to a review within a day
[ ] You have a support plan
[ ] Your own plugin page exists and is better than the directory page,
    because that is where you can be honest about pricing
[ ] You have read the guidelines, not just this checklist

Exercises 18b

  1. Take a plugin you have written and run the pre-submission checklist against it. Fix every item you can, and write down which you cannot and why. That list is your roadmap.
  2. Write a readme.txt for a plugin you have not built, with every field filled in correctly, five good tags, and four screenshots with captions. Compare its short description against the 150-character limit.
  3. Add a site_transient_update_plugins filter in an MU plugin that points at a self-hosted ZIP, and verify the update notice appears on a test site. This is the mechanism for a commercial plugin.
  4. Generate a .pot with wp i18n make-pot, translate it into a language with three plural forms, and confirm the plurals render correctly. Note which Plural-Forms header you used and where you found it.
  5. Install three popular plugins on one site and read their readme.txt files. Note which fields they get right, which they omit, and what their first screenshot shows. Compare with your own.

19 · Debugging Plugins

The debug constantsThe debug log and SCRIPT_DEBUGQuery analysis and SAVEQUERIESReading doing_it_wrong noticesDependency and load-order conflictsFatal errors and recovery

WordPress gives a plugin developer more diagnostic instrumentation than any comparable framework, and most of it is off by default. This chapter covers how to turn it on, how to read what it produces, and how to diagnose the four failure classes that account for most production plugin reports.

The debug constants

Key details

Four constants in wp-config.php control the diagnostic layer. They are read from the environment, so they belong in the server configuration on a staging site rather than in a file that is deployed to production.

define( 'WP_DEBUG', true );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_LOG', true );
define( 'WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY', false );
define( 'SCRIPT_DEBUG', true );
define( 'WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE', 'local' );
ConstantEffectProduction safe
WP_DEBUGEnables _doing_it_wrong, deprecated_function and deprecated_argument notices.No. It is a performance cost on every request and it exposes internals on a fatal error.
WP_DEBUG_LOGWrites those notices to wp-content/debug.log instead of to the response.Yes, with log rotation and access control.
WP_DEBUG_DISPLAYPrints notices into the HTML.Only false. Set it false whenever display is off, or a fatal error leaks a stack trace.
SCRIPT_DEBUGServes the unminified core JavaScript and CSS.No. It roughly doubles the payload.

WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY true is a site compromise waiting to happen. On an uncatchable fatal error, WordPress falls back to displaying the error and a stack trace directly in the response. That output contains absolute filesystem paths, function names, argument values and occasionally a database credential from a stack frame. Set it false and rely on the log.

The debug.log file should be excluded from the artifact, denied by the web server configuration, and rotated. It grows without bound, and a full disk is an outage. A .htaccess rule in the log directory is a reasonable belt-and-braces measure on Apache.

The debug log and SCRIPT_DEBUG

Key details

The log records notices, warnings, deprecations and fatals with a file and line number. It does not record exceptions you have not thrown, and it does not record anything from a request that terminated before the handler ran.

[29-Sep-2026 09:14:02 UTC] PHP Deprecated:  Creation of dynamic property Acme\Ledger\Admin\SettingsPage::$draft is deprecated since PHP 8.2 in /wp-content/plugins/acme-ledger/includes/admin/class-settings-page.php on line 88
[29-Sep-2026 09:14:02 UTC] Acme Ledger was called incorrectly. REST API routes must be registered on the rest_api_init hook. Namespace: acme-ledger/v1 Route: /entries was not registered. in /wp-content/plugins/acme-ledger/includes/class-rest.php on line 41
[29-Sep-2026 09:14:03 UTC] PHP Fatal error:  Uncaught Error: Call to undefined function acme_ledger_format() in /wp-content/plugins/acme-ledger/includes/class-entry.php:41
Stack trace:
#<span class="n">0 /<span class="n">wp-<span class="n">content/<span class="n">plugins/<span class="n">acme-<span class="n">ledger/<span class="k">public/<span class="n">shortcode.<span class="n">php(<span class="n">28): <span class="n">Acme\Ledger\Shortcode-><span class="n">render()
#<span class="n">1 /<span class="n">wp-<span class="n">includes/<span class="n">shortcodes.<span class="n">php(<span class="n">199): <span class="n">do_shortcode_tag()
#<span class="n">2 {<span class="n">main}

The third line is the most valuable diagnostic the log gives you: it names the file and line, it names the undefined symbol, and it gives the full call stack with file and line for every frame. That stack is what identifies the entry point that reached your code, which is usually the answer to a “why is this running at all” question.

For a fatal that produces no log entry, the cause is almost always that the log itself is unwritable. Confirm the directory is writable by the web server user before spending any time on the code.

# Verify the log is being written at all.
$ touch wp-content/debug.log && ls -l wp-content/debug.log
$ tail -f wp-content/debug.log

# PHP 8.3 CLI: surface every diagnostic class.
$ php -d error_reporting=E_ALL -d display_errors=1 wp-cli.phar --debug doctor

Query analysis and SAVEQUERIES

Key details

A slow page is nearly always a query problem, and WordPress will show you every query it ran. SAVEQUERIES is defined in wp-config.php and populates $wpdb->queries with the SQL, the stack trace and the duration of each query.

define( 'SAVEQUERIES', true );

global $wpdb;

add_action( 'shutdown', function(): void {
    global $wpdb;

    if ( ! ( defined( 'SAVEQUERIES' ) && SAVEQUERIES ) || empty( $wpdb->queries ) ) {
        return;
    }

    $slowest = $wpdb->queries;
    usort( $slowest, static function( array $a, array $b ): int {
        return $b[1] <=> $a[1];
    } );

    error_log( 'Queries: ' . count( $wpdb->queries ) );

    foreach ( array_slice( $slowest, 0, 10 ) as $index => $query ) {
        error_log( sprintf(
            '#%d %.4fs %s%s',
            $index,
            $query[1],
            $query[0],
            implode( ' | ', $query[2] )
        ) );
    }
} );

Three patterns explain most slow pages. A WHERE clause with no leading indexed key on wp_postmeta, which is a table scan and grows with the site. An ORDER BY on an unindexed column, which materialises the whole result set into a temporary table. And the SQL_CALC_FOUND_ROWS query added by a WP_Query that did not set no_found_rows, which doubles the cost of a query whose result count nobody reads.

The object cache is the other half of the picture. wp_cache_get() on a key that was never set returns false and costs a lookup; the same call on a warm persistent cache costs no database query at all. A plugin that bypasses the cache with a direct $wpdb->get_results() call pays the full query cost on every request, and this is invisible in the object cache hit-rate metrics.

Reading doing_it_wrong notices

Key details

_doing_it_wrong() is WordPress’s mechanism for reporting a developer error that is not a fatal. It is the single most valuable diagnostic WordPress emits, and it fires for a short, specific list of mistakes.

NoticeCauseFix
Translation function triggered before initCalling __() at file scopeCall it inside a hook, not at include time.
Function called before initregister_post_type or similar at plugins_loadedMove to init.
Hooked before plugins_loadedadd_action at file scopeMove all hook binding into a bootstrap on plugins_loaded.
REST route not registered on rest_api_initRegistering routes elsewhereMove to rest_api_init.
Parameter mismatchFewer accepted_args than callback parametersDeclare the arity the callback actually uses.
Unexpected data found in the databaseCorrupt option, user or post meta valueInspect the row; a plugin is writing a value of the wrong shape.

A “parameter mismatch” notice is the most common and the most consequential. WordPress calls the callback with exactly accepted_args arguments, and a callback declaring more is a fatal error under PHP 8. The notice tells you the two numbers and the function names involved.

// Fires: call to function add_action() with 3 arguments was called with
// 4, because the 4th argument is $accepted_args and the callback
// declares 2 parameters. Fix: add_action( 'save_post', cb, 10, 2 ).

private function on_save( int $post_id, WP_Post $post ): void {
    // Correct once accepted_args is 2.
}

Treat every notice as a release blocker. _doing_it_wrong() is written by core developers specifically to catch the errors that produce the majority of plugin support tickets. A release with zero notices in the log is a release that behaves identically on every site, which is the entire point of writing against the documented API.

Dependency and load-order conflicts

Key details

A conflict is a symptom with a cause in load order, a duplicated function, or a shared option. Diagnose it by reducing the set of active plugins to the minimum that reproduces the failure, then adding them back one at a time.

# 1. Establish the minimum reproduction.
$ wp plugin deactivate --deactivate $( wp plugin list --field=name --exclude=acme-ledger --format=ids )
$ wp plugin activate acme-ledger
$ wp cron event run --due-now --debug

# 2. Bisect by re-activating half of the set.
$ wp plugin activate $( head -n 10 plugin-ids.txt )

# 3. Inspect the runtime without a debug bar.
$ wp eval 'global $wp_filter; printf( "Callbacks on init: %d\n", count( $wp_filter["init"]->callbacks ) );'
$ wp eval 'var_dump( has_action( "acme_ledger_entry_created", "acme_ledger_sync_remote" ) );'
$ wp eval 'var_dump( function_exists( "acme_ledger_format" ) );'

# 4. Environment facts that frequently explain a conflict.
$ wp eval 'printf( "PHP %s, memory %s, uploads %s, object cache: %s\n", PHP_VERSION, ini_get("memory_limit"), wp_upload_dir()["error"] ?: "ok", wp_using_ext_object_cache() ? "external" : "internal" );'

The three most common root causes, in order of frequency. A function or class declared by two plugins, which produces an immediate fatal on include; the second plugin to load is simply the one that loses. A filter added by another plugin that returns a value of the wrong type, which surfaces as a type error far from the filter. And a shared option name where two plugins both read and write the same key with different shapes.

For the third case, a versioned option name is the durable fix. A plugin that stores its configuration in acme_ledger_options_v2 cannot collide with anything, and the migration from the previous key is a single function run on upgrade.

Fatal errors and recovery

A fatal error takes down every page on the site, not only your plugin’s page. Because that is the failure mode, a production plugin must be recoverable in two ways: the fatal must be diagnosable from a log, and the plugin must be deactivatable without a filesystem or database editor.

# Emergency mode: load only active plugins from the database, which is
# exactly what a recovery path needs, and what a broken include breaks.
$ wp --skip-plugins --skip-themes doctor

# Deactivate through the database, bypassing the broken include.
$ wp --skip-plugins --skip-themes plugin deactivate acme-ledger

# Or, with no WP-CLI available, from a mu-plugin that runs first.
# wp-content/mu-plugins/00-emergency.php
#   if ( isset( $_GET['acme_emergency'] ) ) { deactivate_plugins( 'acme-ledger/acme-ledger.php' ); }

The last option is worth taking seriously for a plugin that handles money. A user who cannot reach wp-admin because your code fatals cannot deactivate your plugin, and will either edit the database or ask their host for help. A capability-gated mu-plugin escape hatch shipped with the plugin — documented in the readme, and only active for that plugin — converts a total outage into a support question.

Two practices prevent most fatals. Declare every property on every class, because dynamic property creation is deprecated in PHP 8.2 and will be removed. And declare every return type, because a return type mismatch that PHP would have silently permitted in 7.x is a TypeError in 8.x, and a TypeError in a callback invoked deep inside core is a fatal with a stack trace you did not write.

declare( strict_types=1 );

final class Ledger_Entry {
    private int $id;
    private string $amount;
    private string $currency;
    private ?string $memo = null;

    public function __construct( int $id, string $amount, string $currency, ?string $memo = null ) {
        $this->id       = $id;
        $this->amount   = $amount;
        $this->currency = $currency;
        $this->memo     = $memo;
    }

    public function amount(): string {
        return $this->amount;
    }

    public function memo(): string {
        return $this->memo ?? '';
    }
}

Finally, test on the declared floor. A plugin that declares Requires PHP: 8.3 and has only ever been run on 8.3 will ship code that breaks on a host quietly running 8.1, and the Requires PHP header will not save it because the header is advisory to WordPress core and is checked only at activation time, on a host that has since changed its default PHP version.

Exercises 19

  1. Turn on WP_DEBUG, WP_DEBUG_LOG and SAVEQUERIES, then load a page containing your plugin and produce a ranked list of the ten slowest queries with their stack traces.
  2. Write a shutdown hook that reports every _doing_it_wrong notice for your own plugin, and run the full test suite with it enabled.
  3. Reproduce a fatal by calling an undefined function inside a shortcode, then recover the site using wp --skip-plugins plugin deactivate without touching the database directly.
  4. Bisect a conflict between your plugin and a second plugin to a single function, then eliminate it with a namespaced class and verify the fatal no longer occurs in either activation order.
